PREFACE. 


Circumstances have occurred, during the pub- 
lication of the Dialogues on Prophecy, which 
render it proper to give an account of their 
origin and progress. 

About three years ago, some friends, who had 
been often in the habit of conversing together on 
the future destinies of the Church, their country, 
and the world, thought that it would be expedi- 
ent to unite with themselves a few other Chris- 
tians in searching the Scriptures, that they 
might ascertain how far they coincided in un- 
derstanding the mind of the Spirit on these mo- 
mentous subjects. The secular concerns of 
those who resided in London, as well as the pa- 
rochial duties of Country Clergymen and other 
Ministers, rendered impracticable such an asso- 
ciation there, as should lead to any beneficial 
result : it was therefore resolved to invite the 
attendance of as many as had published on, 
or as were known to have had their minds par- 
ticularly directed to, the prophetic Scriptures, 
without excluding those of any sect or party 
in the Church whatever, at the house of some - 
mutual friend in the country, where unbroken 

a 


ii PREFACE. 


retirement might afford the leisure wach the 
Metropolis denied. 

In order that the attention of those assembled 
might be confined within some limits, the heads 
of a few questions to be discussed were sub- 
mitted to them : and upon the following leading 
and essential points a perfect unanimity was ul- 
timately found to prevail : 

1. That the present Christian dispensation is 
not to pass insensibly into the millennial state 
by gradual increase of the preaching of the 
Gospel ; but that it is to be terminated by judg- 
ments, ending in the destruction of this visible 
Church and polity, in the same manner as the 
Jewish dispensation has been terminated. 

2. That during the time that these judgments 
are falling upon Christendom, the Jews will be 
restored to their own land. 

3. That the judgments will fall principally, if 
not exclusively, upon Christendom, and begin 
with that part of the Church of God which has 
been most highly favoured, and is therefore most 
deeply responsible. 

4, That the termination of these judgments is 
to be succeeded by that period of universal 
blessedness to all mankind, and even to the 
beasts, which is commonly called the Millen- 
nium. 

§. That the second Advent of Messiah pre- 
cedes or takes place at the commencement of the 
Millennium. 

‘6. That a great period of 1260 years com- 
menced in the reign of Justinian, and termi- 


PREFACE. lil 


nated ! the French Revolution; and that the 
vials of the Apocalypse began then to be poured 
out; that our blessed Lord will shortly appear, 
and that therefore it is the duty of all, who so 
believe, to press these considerations on the at- 
tention of all men. 

As. far as these points were doctrinal, it was 
considered that the Church had been so long 
employed in contending for other branches of 
the truth as it is in Jesus, that she had suffered 
them to get a little out of sight; and it was 
therefore judged advisable to recall her attention 
to them. A publication in the form of Dia- 
logues was suggested, that being the shape 
in which discussions had been most successfully 
conducted upon all subjects, from the days of 
Plato, Tully, and Lucian, down to those of Bun- 
yan, Littleton, and Horne Tooke. The conver- 
sations .which had just been held seemed to 
point out a convenient basis: but the published 
Dialogues have borne little or no resemblance to 
these real conversations : much that was spoken 
was not printed; and much that was printed 
was never spoken. Fictitious names were 
assumed, and sentiments put into the mouths of 
the supposed collocutors, without any reference 
to the real opinions of any one who was actually 
present. Yet the editor could searcely call 
himself the author, as all that was valuable was. 
suggested by others, or taken from old standard 
divines : he knew that he was propagating no- 
thing new nor heterodox, and in honest simpli- 
city he believed that he had only to excite the. 

a2 


iv PREFACE. 


attention of Christians to subjects which, though 
they might have forgotten, they were much 
better acquainted with than himself. 

It was for this reason that the first two 
volumes did not take so diffuse and argumenta- 
tive a form, as it was subsequently found ne- 
cessary that the last should assume. Their 
office was not designed to convince, but to arouse 
those whom it was supposed needed only to be 
awakened. The prophetic chords were written in 
score, and it was presumed that the notes ne- 
cessary to complete the harmony could be rea- 
dily supplied by every reader. But the expec- 
tation was groundless. Several other works 
upon the subject of unfulfilled prophecy made 
their appearance, or were called into notice, 
about the same period, and then it was for the 
first time perceived, with wonder and grief, that 
the doctrines advanced, in them and in the 
Dialogues, were charged with being “ novel,” 
and of ‘‘ modern invention.” This charge was 
not made in one, or only in some, of those pub- 
lications which arrogate to themselves to be the 
sole legitimate teachers and judges of theology, 
called “ Religious Magazines,” and contradicted 
in others ; but they all, of every party, creed, 
and denomination, united in one concurrent 
testimony that these opimions were novel and 
heretical. 

But this is not all. In the course of the dis- 
cussion, several other vital and essential points 
of Christian doctrine were alluded to, and neces- 
sarily so, since Divine Truth is one, and not 


PREFACE. Y 


made up of a bundle of detached and indepen- 
dent doctrines. They who had attacked the 
interpretation of unfulfilled prophecy offered in 
the Dialogues, arraigned these vital truths also ; 
and hence it was discovered, that in the midst 
of a widely spread, and increasing numerical 
assent to the form of sound words, the ideas of 
which these words were the symbols, had been 
completely abandoned by the leaders of that 
portion of the church which calls itself the most 
orthodox, and, par excellence, THE RELIGIOUS 
WORLD. : 

This, however is not the place for entering 
into a discussion of these subjects. The end of © 
all things is at hand. The declaration of one 
branch of truth is as accurate a test as the decla- 
ration of any other, whereby to discover those 
whose hearts are disposed by the Lord to be in 
unison with His will.- In the early ages it was 
a declaration of the true nature of the person of 
His son, which separated His people from a 
world lying in wickedness. In the time of the 
Reformation, it was the declaration, that men 
are justified by faith, and not by works, which 
drew out from the great Apostacy, those who 
were written in the Lamb’s book of life. In 
the present days, the literal accomplishment of 
his revealed word seems to be the criterion of 
real conversion to Him, and thus that which is 
the test of a standing or falling church in one 
age, ceases to be the test of it in another. The 
doctrine of justification by faith is not preached 
clearly in ten Evangelical pulpits in Lon- 


Vi - PREFACE. 


don: and the most barefaced heresies re- 
specting the human nature of our Imma- 
nuel are beginning to be promulgated. The 
great Apostacy, however, which is foretold to be 
the last, is that against the kingship of Christ, 
in rebellion against which, the Evangelical party 
is raging as hotly as the most open infidels. The 
second and eighty-second Psalms are fulfilling 
to the very letter. Christ the king of the na- 
tions, and kings ruling in his name, and as 
trustees of him, though the basis of ' every 
European constitution, is now openly laughed 
at. Heretics, blasphemers, and apostates, are 
maintained by the Evangelical party, to be as 
fit legislators for a Christian nation, as men who 
are partakers of the Spirit of Christ; so that 
the salt having lost its savour, there is not 
a hope for the world, not a possibility of 
righteous government, but by Christ himself 
coming and taking to himself that sovereignty 
out of which he is cast by the universal consent 
of men, be they of the religious or of the infidel 
world. The Kingship of Messiah seems now 
to be one chief doctrine to shew us who is on 
the Lord’s side. They who are His will be led 
to embrace it to the saving of their souls ; and 
false professors will be hardened more and more 
in their hearts against Him, while their lips 
speak of nothing but universal philanthropy, 
Christian devotedness, meek humility, and 
brotherly love; and while their lives pass in one 
continual bustle for the circulation of Bibles and 
Tracts, sending out missionaries, and emanci- 


e> 


PREFACE. Vil 


pating the oppressed of mankind. Oh, itis a 
tremendous delusion: the last, and therefore 
the highest finished of all Satan’s devices; 
working through the most amiable of our 
species ; for the most cruel, most bitterly cruel 
part of the delusion is, that it works by means 
of the gentlest, the meekest, the most lovely, 
the most benevolent of men. Nothing but in- 
cessant, and earnest prayer to God, and an 
honest determination to take his Word alone 
for our guide, even if it cost us the cutting off 
of our hand, or casting out an eye, and rejecting 
our dearest and most admired friend, can save 
us from the snare; and if a name for it must 
be found, it will not be easy to find one more 
appropriate than that of UNSCRIPTURAL GOOD 
NATURE, OF UNSANCTIFIED BENEVOLENCE. 

The ancient bonds which held Society to- 
gether are severed. Duty to a body is evapo- 
rated out of men’s minds. They see no one- 
ness of the church of Christ, and therefore no 
oneness of the mimic church of antichrist. 
There is a mingling of contrarieties, and con- 
fusion of principles every where prevalent. In- 
stead of an individual looking upon himself as 
a part of a great whole, and thereby cultivating 
a catholic, and universal spirit for the body of 
which he is a component part, each looks upon 
himself with a personal, selfish, and narrow 
feeling, as if his great concern should be for 
self; and self-interest is therefore the only 
guide. Stern, forbidding, self-denying duty is 
heard of no more. All reverence for kings as 


vii ` PREFACE. 


delegates of Christ’s kingly office, for pastors 
as the ministers of Christ, the office bearers in 
his church, and teachers of the people, is gone ; 
and these latter are listened to only in so far as 
they can make eloquent displays, or will preach 
such doctrines as the congregation will conde- 
scend to hear. There is not principle enough 
in either church or state to rouse men to per- 
form one great and noble exertion for any thing 
in which they do not see themselves personally 
interested: so that in self-devotion and disin- 
terestedness they are far below the heathen of 
antiquity. This has been engendered, fostered, 
and matured by the theology of the last 
thirty years, to shake men out of which 
ought to be the continued effort of all whom 
God has enabled to see the evil and mourn 
over it. 

It cannot be a matter of surprise to any one 
who will exercise his common sense, that Satan 
should find it easy to make use of a church 
which has got into this state, as a cat’s paw, 
with which to bring his own purposes to pass. 
Hence {t is that the church is foremost in all 
the evil works which have been above enume- 
rated. But by far the greatest and most im- 
portant in that fearful catalogue, is the heresy 
which denies that the Son of God became in- 
carnate in flesh like our’s, and asserts that he 
took a better kind of flesh; that his human 
nature, (that is, that part of the God-man 
which was creature,) was not upheld in its 
purity by the power and work of the Holy 


PREFACE. ix 


Ghost carried on in it, but was essentially holy, 
pure, immaculate, and immortal, per se, and 
consequently in no one respect similar to human 
nature, or similar even to any creature whatever. 
One writer, indeed, has surpassed even this 
absurdity, by denying not only that the man- 
hood of Christ was similar to that of his mother, 
but alleging as a reason, that no animal par- 
takes of the nature of his mother, any more than 
an oak tree partakes of the nature of the 
ground in which its seed has been planted : an 
assertion as false in physiology, as it is in 
divinity. 

This heresy against the proper humanity of 
Christ, is of far more importance than any of, 
or than all, the absurdities which have been 
broached against the doctrine of the kingdom of 
our Lord. Itis a heresy with which the church 
has not been infested since the destruction of 
the Greek branch of it, by the Saracens; and 
that judgment may teach us its exceeding hate- 
fulness in God’s sight. It is a heresy springing 
out of the church itself, and therefore far more 
subtle than the counterpart to it, which denies 
the proper divinity of Christ, and which is found 
chiefly amongst men who make no profession of 
orthodoxy. Those who profess this latter, the 
orthodox have never been slack in excommuni- 
cating ; and all believers who will deliver their 
own souls, must cast out those who profess the 
former also. As yet, only two clergymen of the 
church of England have been found publicly to 
espouse it; and it will make less progress there, 


x PREFACE. 


because, for the most part, they are better im- 
structed : in the church of Scotland it has been 
avowed by two also; but among the heads of 
the dissenters it is making much more fearful 
progress. There is, however, still a consider- 
able body of the people who are sound, and to 
those, and for those, the servants of God must 
now direct their labours. All that has been 
hitherto done, respecting the doctrine of the 
Kingdom, is as nothing in comparison of what 
remains to be done for this. The doctrine of 
the kingdom may be compared to the outfield 
which has been reclaimed from the uncultivated 
waste which the church had allowed it to be- 
come, whilst the doctrine of the human nature, 
which the Son of God assumed, is as the citadel 
of divine truth itself. If this goes, all goes. 
If Christ did not take our nature, but a better 
nature, then is that better nature, and not our 
nature, redeemed from the curse under which it 
lay, and been reconciled to God. If the Holy 
Ghost did not preserve the humanity of Jesus, in 
obedience to his Father’s will, then have we no 
ground to expect that He will preserve our vile 
bodies in obedience to our fathers. If the Son 
of Man, Jesus of Nazareth, did not live a life 
of faith, and if he did not experience all the 
temptations which we feel, then is his life, and 
his resistance of evil no example to us. Be- 
fore such a question as this, in comparison with 
such a stake, and with such an issue, all other 
things are trifling ; and to this fight let every true 
hearted soldier of Christ forthwith gird himself. 


PREFACE. Xi 


“ Beloved, believe not every spirit, but try the 
spirits whether they are of God, because many 
false prophets are gone out into the world. 
Hereby know ye the spirit of God ; every spirit 
that confesseth that Jesus Christ is come in 
FLESH is of God: and every spirit that 
confesseth not that Jesus Christ is come in 
FLESH, is not of God: and THIS IS THAT 
SPIRIT OF ANTICHRIST, whereof ye have heard 
that it should come ; and EVEN NOW, ALREADY 
IS IT IN THE WORLD.” The Bible knows no- 
thing of two kinds of flesh, one immutable, 
immortal, and incapable of acting otherwise 
than in conformity with the will of the Cre- 
ator, and another flesh mutable, mortal, and 
capable of acting contrary to the will of 
God; it knows but of one kind of flesh, and 
it speaks awfully of those who deny that in 
that one kind the Son of God came; and who, 
be it observed, have gone out from the church 
into the world. ‘‘ Ye are of God, dear children, 
and have overcome them ; because greater is 
he that is in you, than he that is in the world. 
THEY ARE OF THE WORLD, therefore speak they 
of the world, and the WORLD HEARETH THEM, 
We are of God; he that knoweth God heareth 
us; he that is not of God heareth not us. 
HEREBY KNOW WE THE SPIRIT OF TRUTH, 
AND THE SPIRIT OF ERROR. 1 John iv. 1—6.

DIALOGUES 


ON 


Prophecy. 


CONVERSATION THE FIRST. 


Doctrine of Scripture concerning the Times of the Gentiles, 
and the Olject and End of the present Dispensation. 


Philalethes.—I nave been reading lately the 
works of Mr. Cuninghame and Mr. Frere on the 
Prophecies, and find it very perplexing that they 
should differ, not only from each other, but also 
from Mr. Faber; so that it seems as if no good 
could result from studying that subject. 

Anastasius.—It will not be denied, that a large Deft A Ea 
majority of the sacred writings consists of pro- 77. 3)... AS 
phecies ; to say, then, that no good can result a 
from studying the prophecies, implies a supposi- 
tion that a considerable portion of the Bible is 
useless; and even if the discrepancies between 
the writers you have named, were so great as you 
suppose, it would be more probable that they had | 
treated the subject in an unsatisfactory manner, 
than that the subject itself was unworthy of 
study.

(p EATA 


stl oe Ona, afar 
ee sa! ae egal ying zas

yuy P hilalethes.—lt ‘has occurred to me ‘that the 
object of prophecy was not to make men know, 
beforehand, the events which were about to take 
place, but only to shew, by the event, the fore- 
knowledge of God. 

Anastasius.—This is certainly not a sufficient 
motive for declaring unaccéfnplished events ; for 
it does not require a revelation from the Deity to 
prove, that if there be a God, that God must have 
a knowledge of the future effects and operations 
of the works of his own hands. But, instead of 
considering this point abstractedly, we have only 
-to refer to the prophecies which have been already 
“fulfilled, to see that such was by no means the 
object with which they were given; but in order 
that men might believe what God declares, and 
act accordingly. I would recommend you to read. 
upon this subject, a very able work by Mr. Da- 
vison, ‘‘on the Structure of Prophecy.” 

Philalethes.—1s that the best work upon unful- 
filled prophecies ? 

Anastasius.—It is not accordant with Mr. Da- 
vison’s plan, to enter into that branch of the sub- 
ject, nor do the other works which you have 
named, give a general and comprehensive view of 
the whole prophetic Scriptures. Mr. Faber’s are 
very elaborate works, which display much erudi- 
tion; and he has been of great use to the church, 
by keeping its attention alive upon points on 
which it had gone almost completely asleep, not- 
‘withstanding the labours of Mede, Sir Isaac 
‘Newton, and Bishop Newton. Mr. Cuninghame 
and Mr. Frere professedly confine themselves to 


3 


the chronological prophecies of Daniel: and . the. 
Apocalypse. Mr. Fry indeed, in his book on the 
Second Advent of ‘our blessed Lord, has followed 
the Sacred Scriptures seriatim upon that single 
point; and all the other writers have: confined 
themselves to some one branch, such as Thruston, 
Woodhouse, Cooper; Gisborne, Bayford, &c. &c. 
But the person who has given the most concise, 
and, at the same time, the most enlarged view of 
those unfulfilled prophecies which pervade the 
whole Bible, is Mr. Lewis Way, in his Letters 
under the signature of Basilicus. 
Philalethes——Why do you prefer the Letters of 
Basilicus, to all the other larger works which you: 
have named 2? es O 
Anastasius.—Because it dovetails, as if were, 25 0-707 441 
the last chapters of the Apocalypse into the first 4.77. 4.4. plm 
chapters of Genesis, and shews the beauty, unity, 
and consistency of the mind of the Holy Spirit .. 
to be equally infused through every part of the- 
sacred volume. Men study the prophecies, as 
too many do other parts of Christian doctrine, as 
if they were unconnected and insulated facts. They 
are like persons who look through the windows of 
a building which contains a very large and -com- 
plicated piece of machinery; by dint of some 
pains they arrive at the knowledge of the uses of 
all that they see, but, for want of being able to 
get inside the building, they cannot perceive how 
all the various movements are connected, so as to: 
produce one grand, and consistent, and uniform: 
operation. Thus some are so wholly intent upon 
the doctrine of justification, others upon the sanc-. 
B2 


4 


tifying influence of the Holy Spirit, others upon 
the decrees and unchangeableness of the purposes 
of Jehovah, that the whole of the contents of the 
sacred volume seems, in their eyes, to be included 
in one or more of these separate doctrines; 
whereas, no Christian is well and scripturally 
versed in one branch of divine truth, but in pro- 
portion as he perceives its harmony and connexion 
with all others. | 

_Philalethes.—But you surely do not mean that 
the study of the prophecies is as essential a 
branch of theology as the doctrine of justification, 
and the other great and leading doctrines which 
_ you have mentioned ? 

Anastasius.—God has united them in his word, 
and what he has joined together let not man put 
| asunder. 

Philalethes.—Do you consider, then, that any 
practical good is likely to result from the study of 
the prophecies ? 


Sluijs wo  Anastasius.—Every portion of divine truth 


z RA a Tas IESO 


- which is received into the heart, produces a cor- 
responding practice. Systems and creeds in theo- 
logy may be learned by the intellect, as readily as 
systems of chemistry, botany, or mineralogy ; and 
. so acquired only, will produce no effect. The 
activity of the mind of the present generation, has 
caused many to acquire the Christian scheme of 
salvation in this and in no other way, and hence 
the large apparent extension of religious profes- 
sion. But so hostile is the heart and affections of 
the natural man to those things which God de- 
lights in, that no one can acquire such a love for 


9 


5 


the essential peculiarities of the religion of the 
despised Nazarene, the crucified God-man, as to 
have the current of his thoughts, principles, and 
course of life changed and influenced by them, 
except through the almighty spirit of Omnipotence 
itself. 

Philalethes.—This I firmly believe, and expe- 
rimentally know, as far as it respects the funda- 
mental truth of salvation by the cross of Christ; 
but what good purpose do you expect further 
to be accomplished by the study of the pro- 
phecies ? 


Anastasius:—For every delusion which Satan 46 Ax... 


introduces into the world, there is a specific anti- ie 
dote in some particular portion of Divine Truth. .. 


. /

alatanana bral f

{ur gn He ae 
lá 


After the whole Roman Empire had become nomi-~~~--4~7 


nally Christian, the world was over-run with the 
superstition, idolatry, and self-righteousness which 
had been set up by various Bishops of Rome. 
The especial truth for the overthrow of that delu- 
sion, was the doctrine of justification by faith 
alone. To preach this doctrine the reformers 
were raised up; upon this point the whole diffe- 
rence between the Christian and all other reli- 
gions essentially turns; and therefore, this point 
is justly termed, Articulus stantis vel cadentis 
Ecclesie ; upon this foundation the whole body 
of Protestant churches was founded and stands. 
But superstition and self-righteousness constitute 
no longer the elements of the prevailing delusion 
of these days, with which Satan draws men’s souls 
to perdition: scepticism, infidelity, the deification 


6 


of the intellect ‘of man, reasoning pride, disbelief 
in the Word of -God,-is the grand sin of these 
days: to meet this error, it is obviously useless to 
preach any particular mode of salvation, because 
men doubt the efficacy of all: in the former case, 
they were at least:alive to the danger of offending 
God, and anxious in their endeavours to pacify him, 
and only mistook the mode by which this was to be 
accomplished ; whereas, now they doubt the fact 
‘of his anger, and consequently the necessity of any 
mode: of reconciliation. The grand truth, there- 
fore, to bring forward, is the standing miracle of 
the past and present condition of the Jews; pro- 
phecies fulfilled already, and those which are to be 
fulfilled hereafter: thereby shewing, from the 
analogy of judgments which, having been pre- 
dicted, did afterwards come to pass, the certainty 
and awfulness of those which are yet to come; 
that many may be roused from their lethargic se- 
curity, and awake, not to the tremendous realities 
of a despised indignation, but to the papeeree 
glories of an eternal blessedness. 
Philalethes.—This will indeed be a delightful 
‘result, and if you can make this clear, no doubt 
‘will be very effectual to the conversion of infidels, 
‘and must at events be inoffensive to them. 
TD eas Dae Anastasius —The analogies of former times 
Ld Ax puget. would lead us rather to expect that the bold de- 
is gees m'w- clarations of the revealed purposes of God will 


| 
| 
lyh an v flow ad Ai 4 

[ase ae ‘excite a very bitter persecution on the part of the 
: 


‘infidel philosophers of the day. 
` Philalethes.—How can this be? the philosophi- 


i 


7 


‘cal party always professes to:be perfectly tolerant 
towards all creeds and systems alike. | 
Anastasius.—Whilst our blessed Lord was upon 
earth, his principal persecutors were the self-righ- 
teous Pharisees; his conversations with them 
turned chiefly upon the necessity of inward purity, 
in contradistinction to their specious outward ap- 
pearances: meanwhile, although the philosophi- 
cal Sadducees sometimes argued with .him, they 
do not seem to have had their enmity roused into 
any overt acts. No sooner, however, was he risen 
again, than they, finding their unbelief in a future 
‘state and in the immortality of the soul directly. 
attacked by the preaching of the Apostles, became 
in their turn the bitterest persecutors. Thus now, 
as long as we insist solely upon the doctrines 
which have been preached ever since the Reforma- 
tion, and which affect only the self-righteous for- 
malists, the sceptics tolerate and ridicule both 
‘parties alike; but no sooner shall the preachers of 
the Gospel make the grand truths of the return 
of the Jews to their own land, and to God's favour ; 
the judgments about to come upon the world, in 
consequence of the infidelity of its inhabitants ; 
the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ to set up his 
kingdom upon the earth, &c. &c. the prominent 
subjects of their preaching, than the pretended 
toleration of sceptics will be proved to be a de- 
lusion, and an imposture, like every other pre- 
tence to virtue which is not acknowledged as the 
alone production of the Spirit of God. The 
sceptical revolutionists of France were the most 


8 


blood-thirsty demons which ever broke loose upon 
mankind; and the great and last tribulation upon 
the saints of the Most High seems to be described 
as that which is to be brought upon them by the 
infidel faction. Bishop Horsley, in his Commen- 
tary on the Psalms, has some admirable remarks 
upon this subject. 

Philalethes.—You have excited a great interest 
in my mind, and I entreat you to let me become 
your scholar, and that you will yourself instruct 
me in the prophetic parts of PENRUUS, which you 


_ seem to have much studied. 


Anastasius-—When Mr. Whitefield was re- 


Vw Pury? ot flee ss 
i. A EA a É. /aptoached by a scoffer at his labours, who said; 


nina ln 


“there is one of your converts, Mr. Whitefield, 

going to be hanged,” Mr. Whitefield replied, “ you 
ure right, my friend, he is only one of my converts, 
but if he had been one converted by the Holy 
Spirit, he would not have been going to be hanged.” 
I shall be very sorry to be your only, or even your 
chief instructor in the prophetic Scriptures, but I 
shall be happy at all times “ to give a reason of 
the hope that is in me,” and to lay before you the 
passages in the word of God on which that hope is 
founded. If it shall please the Holy Spirit, whose 
office it exclusively is to seal the truth as it is in 
Jesus upon the hearts of his followers, to convince 
your mind as he has convinced mine, glory be to 
Him alone. But as it will and ought to be more 
satisfactory to you to have the truth con- 
firmed out of the mouth of many witnesses, I 
pray you to enquire of our friend and brother 


9 


in the Lord, Aristo, whether his opinion coin- 
cides with that which I have been now stating 
to you. : 

Philalethes.—I know full well that it is the Holy 
Spirit alone which can impress the truths which 
we read in the word of God upon our hearts, in 
such a way as to make us believe them to the sav- 
ing of the soul; but tell me, Aristo, whether you 
think there is the same ground to look for the 
teaching of the Spirit in the investigation of the 
prophecies ? 

Aristo.—One of the purposes for which our 
blessed Lord promised that the Holy Spirit should 
dwell in, and abide with his followers, is, that he 
should ‘‘show them things to come.” John xvi. 13. 
On this office gf the Holy Spirit rests the sanction 
of all meetings. and discussions held for the mutual 
edification of each other in the prophetic Scrip- 
tures; and whoever, in faith and reliance upon 
this office, will assemble together, to redeem from 
neglect that important part of the word of God, 
and make their only view the investigation of truth, 
need not doubt but that God will bring such good 
purpose to full accomplishment. 

Anastasius—The things which are to come 
upon the earth are revealed in various ways; 
namely, by declaration, by examples, and by 
figures. The periods when they are to take place 
are pointed out by signs in the political frame- 
work of human society, and by chronological dates. 
The things which are to take place have reference 
to the church of Christ, to the Jewish people, to 
Christendom, and to the world at large. 


4 p ? 
oe j w- J af (ae gex 


10 


Philalethes.—If the guides be so clear, how 
can we account for the errors into which most 
persons have fallen who have, made the prophetic 
Scriptures peculiarly their study ? 

Anastasius.—The errors into which these have 


vinta “or, fallen have arisen partly from their want of know- 


2 
vere” aa 


ledge in other branches of divine truth, such as 
the extent of man’s fall and apostacy from God, 
the ground-work and means of man’s redemption, 
&c. &c.; for no part of revealed truth can be 
distorted, either by exaggeration or suppression, 


‘without introducing confusion into the whole; and 


partly from attempting to interpret exclusively by 
one of the modes, without taking in the others as 
correctives. Thus the ancient Millenarians, and 
the more recent fifth monarchy men, were right in 


their fact of a period of blessedness, which is to 


last for a thousand years, being promised, and 
that an universal kingdom shall be set up, in 
which a king shall reign in righteousness, the 
meek shall inherit the earth, there shall be no more 
war nor misery, and the knowledge of the Lord 
shall be over all the world; but they were wrong 
in not looking to the analogy of former times, and 
the almanack which is given in the chronological 
prophecies of Daniel and St. John, by which they 
would have learned that a long period must in- 
tervene before that time should arrive: the latter 
were also wrong in their theology, by not feeling 
that their own views of the subject were perverted 
by worldly ambition ; that their hearts were not 
in subjection to the teaching of the Spirit; and by 
not knowing what manner of spirit they were. of. 


}1 


The fifth monarchy men are said also (by Russell, 
who copies Hume without quoting him, who cites 
Thurloe, who does not say so) to have asserted 
that the saints were to reign until Messiah comes, 
whereas it is universally declared, that during the 
whole of this dispensation they are to be trampled 
upon, and submissive to the powers that be. 
Aristo.—This branch of revealed truth, as well 
as others, must be assaulted by Satan; and if he 
has been more busy in perverting it, we may rest 
assured that it is because of its distinguished im- 
portance. His mode of attack has been by teach- 
ing things to come something like, yet essentially 
different from, those which the Holy Spirit teaches: 
thus, at the first advent of our adorable Saviour, 
many were looking for the things to come which 
were taught by Satan. The Jews were looking 
for a temporal kingdom then; the heathen were 
looking for some great conqueror or governor, 
whom Virgil, and other poets, thought they had 
found in Augustus; while very few were looking 


for the things pointed out by the Spirit. But 


no prophecy of the Scripture is of any private 
(i. e., as I conceive, single, partial, and limited) 
interpretation ; for the prophecy came not in old 
time by the will of man, but holy men of God 
spake as they were moved by the Holy Ghost. 
2 Pet. i. 20,21. And not only is prophecy of no 
private application, as it respects a private indi- 
vidual, but neither is it of partial application as it 
relates to time; for it partakes of the omniscience 
‘of the Holy Spirit itself.

ju. Aristo—The phrase which you have used,

Anasfasius.—From the passage to which you

Aristo.—In this passage of Luke, our Lord speaks 
of some things tirst of all which are not the signs 
of the end, and against considering which as such 
he warns his disciples; the necessity of such a

therefore, he adds, ver. 9. “the end is not evdews,

of the Romans would stand after the reign of the 
seven wicked emperors; and the Jews knew that,

Judaism. Against this he warns them in ver. 12. 
He mentions the times of the Gentiles as a period 
with which the Jews were well acquainted. It is 
evident that the prophecies of Daniel were then

in ver. 27, the Prophet sees the Gentiles brought

is apparent; so that ver. 10, 11, 12, are dove- — 
tailed into Dan. vii. by the times. In ver. 25, 
after the end, then the true signs appear. The 
fig-tree, which had long been bound up by the 
frost, begins to put forth leaves ; and if the words 
in- ver. 32, “till all are fulfilled,’ were rendered 
«till all be in being,” which is more correct, 
the passage would be clearer still. In all such 
predictions our blessed Lord does not speak as: 
the living Word, but as the Prophet anointed by 
the Spirit. |

Aristo.—The present dispensation is the dis- ee EES 
pensation of the Spirit. This object is set forth %2 Qu Slak 
at large in the third chapter of St. John. Unless a 
man enter into this present spiritual dispensation 
upon earth, he cannot enter into the kingly dispen- 
sation: This dispensation of the Spirit was the 
earthly things, ver. 12., of which our blessed. Lord 
was speaking to Nicodemus; and he rebukeshim for 
coming to seek the knowledge of heavenly things 
before he was acquainted with earthly things, “God 
sent not his Son into the world to condemn the 
world; but that the world through him might be 
saved,” ver. 17.; and accordingly it is said, in 
Matt. xii. 32. “that whosoever speaketh a word 
against the Son of Man it shall be forgiven him ; 
but whosoever speaketh against the. Holy Ghost, 
it shall not be forgiven him, neither in this. world, 
nor in the world to come :” sre ev raro ry awe, ete ev. 

Tr -peddovrt, so that it appears that our blessed. 
Lord’s: personal ministry might be rejected, and. 


16 


yet that sin might be pardoned ; but the rejection | 
of the spiritual dispensation, which is the present, 
will hever be forgiven, for no one can enter the 
kingdom of Messiah hereafter, who has not been 
first born into the present earthly or spiritual 
dispensation here. Although all judgment is com- 
mitted to Christ, John v. 27, yet he did not then 
come to judgment, but, on the contrary, refused it 
when he was requested to take the office of judge 
upon him, both by the man, in Luke xii. 14., and 
also by the scribes and pharisees in the case of 
the woman taken in adultery, John viii. See also 
John xviii. 36. and observe the emphatic »». In 
the 16th chapter of John, to which I have already 
alluded, the office of the Spirit to the church is 
contrasted with his office to the world. He is to 
comfort the church, to guide her into all truth, to 
show her things to come, to glorify Christ, &c. &c. 
He is to reprove and convict the world of the 
sin of unbelief in the name of Jesus, of righteous- 
ness, and of judgment; so that this spiritual 
dispensation, like all other of God’s dispensations, 
is one of mercy and judgment united. The dis- 
pensation of earthly things is the testimony to 
the incarnation of God. We read in Luke i. 35. 
that the Holy Ghost came upon the Virgin Mary, 
and fashioned the human body of Immanuel within 
her womb. ‘‘ God gave not the Spirit by measure 
unto him,” John iii. 34.; and the Spirit dwells in 
the church, which is Christs body, as its com- 
forter, and preserver, and guide into all holy con- 
formity to the will of the Lord. The object of this 
dispensation is farther insisted upon in Acts xv, 13. 


17 


wherein James declares, that ‘‘ God at the first 
did visit the Gentiles to take out of them a people 
for his name ;” for as the Jews had not only re- 
jected Christ's personal ministry, but further de-

not performed by the Holy Spirit, but by the

glo§ous name should be preserved: after this it 
is said, ver. 16., he ‘‘ will return and build again 
the tabernacle of David which is fallen down, and 
build again the ruins thereof, and set it up.” 

Philalethes.—In what sense do you use the term 
church ? 

Aristo.—I1 cannot define the term better than I 
find it in the XIXth Article of the Church of Eng- 
land, ‘‘ the visible church of Christ is a congrega- . 
tion of faithful men, in the which the pure word — 
of God is preached, and the sacraments be duly 
administered according to Christ’s ordinance,” &c. 
If you drive me to explain the word church by a 
single word, I would say, ‘‘ Christendom.”

Psalm cvi. 8., cxv. 1. and in many other places. 
The object of the Jewish dispensation was, that 
God might preserve a memorial of himself amongst 
his degenerate and rebellious creatures, and ac- 
cordingly that nation was separated from all the 
other nations of the earth, and to their custody was 
confided all the revelations of his will, which he 
at any time vouchsafed to give to mankind. ‘‘ What 
advantage hath the Jew? or what profit is there 
of circumcision? Much every way: chiefly be- 
cause that unto them were committed the oracles 
of God.” Rom. i. 1. This was done in order 
that he might choose out of them a people to bless 
“ with all spiritual blessings in heavenly things in 
Christ Jesus, according 5 hath chosen them in

cepted in the beloved.” Ephes. i. 3—6; “ and

The tables, however, seem now to be turned, and

declared that God will restore the Jews to their

tery” which is declared, as “ finished” in Rev. x. 7.. 
at the sounding of the seventh angel. The same 
mystery is spoken of in Rom. xi. the substance of 
which is contained in the second chapter of the

Philalethes.—It is clear, from the popular ob- 
jections which are made to the labours of the So- 
ciety for promoting Christianity amongst the Jews, 
and from the total supineness of all the established 
Churches of Christendom to their spiritual im- 
provement, that there is very little practical ex- 
pectation at present of such fulfilment of the times 
of the Gentiles, as Anastasius has now laid before 
us; but do you, Aristo, think that this unbelief 
has been always prevalent ? 

Aristo.—The Church of Rome has not been in- 
different to the subject, for she has proceeded 
against the poor Jews with that same insatiable 
thirst for human blood, which has ever charac- 
terized that wicked apostacy. It appears, how- 
ever, from the Apostle’s argument in the eleventh 
chap. of his Epistle to the Romans, that no sooner 
had the Gentiles been received into the communion 
of the church of Christ, than they began to boast, 
like Elias of old, that they were the exclusive 
possessors of this salvation ; whereas the apostle 
declares, that there was still a remnant preserved 
amongst the Jews, according to the election of 
grace. The diminishing of the Jews is said to be 


21 


the cause of the riches of the Gentiles; but the 
Jews are not said to be eradicated; and the re- 
turn of the Jews to God’s favour, is said to bea 
source of increased blessing to the Gentiles. The 
Gentiles are there warned, that if they remain not 
in the faith which he has taught them, they will 
be cut off; and not only ecclesiastical history, but 
every day’s experience shows us churches cut off, 
and théir candlestick removed for their apostacy ; 
witness the seven churches of Asia, mentioned in 
the Apocalypse: witness the church of Rome, 
the Greek church, the greater part of the Protes- 
tant churches on the Continent, especially that of 
Geneva. Where can we find amongst them justi- 
fication by faith alone, without works, preached 
fully and unequivocally? Where can we find it 
clearly held, that “ God made Christ to be sin for 
us, that we might be made the righteousness of 
God in him,” 2 Cor. v. 21., that which Augustine 
well expresses by the double transfer, namely, all 
that we are to Christ, and all that Christ is to us ? 
also Irenæus, who says ‘‘ he became what we are 
to make us what he is;” and finding not this, how 
can we find that love towards him, and zeal for his 
service, and anxiety to tell petishing men of this 
great salvation, which distinguished the first 
Christians, and still distinguishes the few who 
receive it? Thus this is still a dispensation of 
judgment. The present is also a dispensation 
of separation, whereas the dispensation which 
is to come, will be a dispensation of union. Thus 
God is doing on the great stage of the world, that © 
which he is doing in each particular individual. 


22 


First, we see a nation separated, then a family, 
then an individual; and the effect of the preaching 
of his doctrine is to make separation. ‘‘ This 
is the judgment,” «perc (namely, that which is 
effected by the Spirit,) “ that light is come into 
the world, and men loved darkness rather than 
hight, because their deeds were evil: for every one 
that doeth evil hateth the light, neither cometh to 
the light, lest his deeds should be reproved; but 
he that doeth truth cometh to the light, that his 
deeds may be made manifest that they are 
wrought in God.” John ii. 19—21. The mani- 
festation of Christ by the Spirit, has a repulsive 
as well as an attractive power ; and no one can be 
his disciple, who does not leave father, mother, 
wife, children, lands, houses, &c. &c., yea all that 
binds us down to this present dispensation and 
order of our being, and enter singly and naked 
into that other dispensation, in the same manner 
as we made our first entrance into this. 
Philalethes.—It has often appeared to me, that 
the character of the dispensation and office of the 
Spirit had been much undervalued by the church, 
for it seemed as if one part were entirely absorbed 
by the work of justification, atonément, righteous- 
ness, &c. whilst the other part confounded the 
work of the Spirit with that of the Redeemer, 
making an infused righteousness, and running 
into a kind of mystical view, which confused all 
the doctrines of grace. Now, let me ask you how 
you consider this dispensation is to end? Are the 
great exertions made by our Bible, and Mission- 
ary, and other religious Societies, to end in pro- 


23 


ducing a reign of blessedness, or at least a far 
greater extension of Christianity, than any thing 
we have yet witnessed? | 

Aristo.—Let us first hear what Anastasius says 
to this matter, for it is tender ground. 

Anastasius.—lt is tender ground with respect 
to the most valuable part of the community, - 
namely, those whose benevolence and love of 
God, has led them to promote and labour in the 
cause of our religious institutions; but I have too 
sincere a belief in their devotion to God, than 
to suppose that they will be offended, when it is 
shown to them from Scripture that they have 
taken partial and imperfect views of their duty. 
I will endeavour to illustrate what I mean by the 
analogy of the great types of our Immanuel, David 
and Solomon, A promise was given to David 
that Solomon should sit upon his throne, that 
in his days should the righteous flourish, and 
abundance of peace should be upon the earth. 
Nevertheless, some persons laboured to make 
Adonijah king, and either despised the blessings 
promised under Solomon’s reign, or expected to 
enjoy them equally by means of Adonijah. But 
their efforts were vain; for the decree had gone 
forth that these blessings should only be brought 
about by, and in, the reign of Solomon. Thus, 
now, during the David, or militant state of the 
church, a reign of blessedness is promised to be 
brought about by and during the reign of Christ 
upon the throne of David; but all who expect 
that before he comes ‘the knowledge of the Lord 
shall cover the earth, as the waters do the sea,” 


24 


are acting upon the principle of those who set up 
Adonijah of old. The duty of Christians is to 


bear witness of the truth, and to send the glad 
tidings of a free salvation to the utmost boun- 
daries of the earth; and their object should be 
to gather out of the nations all that have a will 
to hear the joyful sound; but when they talk 
of converting the heathen, of overthrowing popery, 
and rooting out infidelity, they are aiming at ac- 
complishing that which they have no promise to 
plead for, but on the contrary, that which they 
are expressly told shall be performed by another. 

Aristo.—I fully subscribe to all that Anastasius 
has said; but to come more directly now to the 
question of Philalethes, it will perhaps be more 
satisfactory to examine it under the following 
form; namely, whether the present dispensation 
is to end in the blindness of the Gentiles? or in 
an efflorescence, as it were, of fuller light?’ Now 
all are agreed that the present dispensation will 
‘come to a close at the second coming of Christ; 
let us therefore examine what he says respecting 
the state of things which will be upon the earth 
at the time when he is to arrive, and see whether 
there is any warrant in Scripture to conclude that 
religion will be in a more flourishing condition 
than it is at present. The parable of the tares 
amongst the wheat, Matt. xiii, 24—30. 36—43. 
seems expressly designed to teach us what will 
be the state of the professing church at the time 
of the end signified by the figure of a harvest; 
the same idea is conveyed by the bad fishes 
which are caught by the preaching of the Gospel, 


25 


and only separated at the last when drawn into 
shore. The expression likewise, ‘‘ Nevertheless, 
when the Son of Man cometh, shall he find faith 
on the earth,” Luke xviii. 8. evidently implies, 
that he will find very little; but I admit that 
it possibly relates to faith in his second coming, 
- which seems at present to be at fully as low an 
ebb as was faith in his first coming, at the time 
when that event took place. Our blessed Lord 
expressly declares, that as it was in the time of 
Noah, so shall it be at the time of his coming; 
and no one can suppose for a moment that the 
knowledge of the true God was widely diffused 
in Noah’s days. His servants too are described 
as beating one another, and even of that small 
band who do profess to be looking for his coming, 
one half is represented as slumbering, and the: 
other half as asleep. Nor is there one statement 
of an opposite character, and which would lead us 
to suppose that there will be a general diffusion 
of vital religion at the time of his coming, The 
only way to justify the present prevailing opinion 
of the effects which are to be brought about by 
religious societies, is to suppose that after this 
extensive knowledge has been generated, there 
will be a subsequent falling away. But, surely, we 
ought to have some decided authority before we are 
called upon to believe this; for it is obvious that 
nothing of this sort was revealed to the Apostles, 
since they have given us no intimation of it; 
whereas, on the contrary, the words of our Lord 
always imply wickedness lasting to the time of 
the end, as at Matt. xiii. 24—30. 47, and at 


26 

Luke xviii. 8. and prove that the state of the world 
at the last day, will be one of great unbelief and 
ungodliness ; and warns, not only:his immediate 
disciples, but all, who in subsequent ages should 
believe on him through their word, that they 
would be beaten for their testimony, according to 
the genius of the age, and custom of the times in 
which their witness shall be testified, some by the 
jaws of lions, some by the stake, some by scourg- 
ing, and some by cruel mockinps, satire, ridicule, 
and scorn. 

Evander.—In confirmation of this opinion, we 
should also bear in mind that the end of this 
dispensation is revealed, as to be brought about sud- 
denly, which is entirely opposed to the idea of 
gradual improvement. | 

Philalethes.—From whence does our brother 
Theophilus think that we ought to date the com- 
mencement of the time of the Gentiles ? 

Theophilus.—-In one sense the times of the Gen- 
tiles may be said to be anterior to those of the 
Jews, for the origin of both may be traced up 
to Paradise itself. Here man was created king 
and priest. ‘‘ Power (Elohim) said, let us 
make man in our image, after our likeness; and 
let them have dominion over the fish of the 
sea, and over the fowl of the air, over the cattle, 
and over all the earth, and over every creeping 
thing that creepeth upon the earth. So Power 
created man in his own image; in the image 
of Power created he him; male and female 
created he them: and Power blessed them, 
and Power said unto them, be fruitful, and 


27 


multiply, and replenish the earth and subdue 
it; yea, have dominion over the ‘fish of the 
sea, and over the fowl of the air, and over every 
living thing that creepeth upon the earth,” Gen. 
i. 26.; upon which passage you may consult the 
able dissertation of the late Mr. Tilloch. Here 
then Adam was created in the image of Power, 
and had power and dominion over all the 
creatures in the world. This dominion, however, 
he lost when he sinned, and Satan usurped that 
power which Adam had, and became the prince 
or ruler of the world. In the same day in which 
the first man fell and lost his empire, God re- 
vealed another dispensation, in which that power 
should be recovered. In the first it was in man 
alone; in the other it is to be in the God-man; in 
the first it was lost; in the second it is to be 
recovered, and so secured that it can never be 
lost again. But it was to be fought for, and won, 
in the same flesh in which it was lost, and this is 
what the prophets desired to search into when 
they testified of the sufferings of Christ, and of 
the glory which should follow. Thus, at the con- 
clusion of the ¢zmes of the Gentiles, man will be 
In possession again of that throne which Adam 
had. 

Philalethes.—You consider then that the domi- 
nion of Adam extended over all the earth, whilst, 
if I-am correct in the inference I am about to 
draw, the subsequent dominions promised at 
various periods, were of a more limited extent ? 

Theophilus.—Exactly so: as you will perceive 
by consulting the ixth ne of Genesis ; where 


28 


to Noah it is only promised that the fear and

we see daily exercised over them, we may per-

= condition, is to be entrusted with power. But 
let us pass on to the other great covenant-head of 
God’s promises, Abraham. In Gen. xii, “ the 
Lord said unto Abram, get thee out of thy country, 
_ and from thy kindred, and from thy father’s house, 
unto a land that I will shew thee; and I will

and make thy name great; and thou shalt be æ 
blessing; and I will bless them that bless thee, 
and curse him that curseth thee; and in thee shall 
all families of the earth be blessed.” This pro-

together with some remarkable additions, ver. 13. 
s Know of a surety that thy seed shall be a

also that nation whom they shall serve, will I 
judge; and afterward they shall come out with

xxii. after the sacrifice of Isaac, God renews the 
promise, that his seed should possess the gate of

‘nations of the earth be blessed, ver, 18. Our 
blessed Lord says, that Abraham rejoiced to see 
his (Christ’s) day, he saw it and was glad. John 


29 


viii. 56. Abraham saw in Isaac the atonement, 
the resurrection, and the possession of the gate of,

xi. 21. In the wilderness of Sinai, God repeated 
the promise that Israel should be a kingdom of - 
priests, Exod. xix. 6. Balaam is constrained to 
confess, that when the Lord beholds no iniquity 
in Jacob, nor perverseness in Israel, that the Lord 
his God is with him, and the shout of a king 
among them, Numb. xxiii. 21. ; and, again, xxiv. 7. 
that this king shall be higher than Agag, and his 
kingdom shall be exalted. The subject of Hannah’s

also, for the first time, called the anointed, z. e. 
the Christ, 1 Sam. 11. 10.; and the promise is again 
repeated by Nathan to David, in 2 Sam. vii. 12.; 
from whence, (in order to avoid the quotation of 
above fifty intermediate passages, all bearing upon 
the same point,) 1 will bring you at once down to 
Acts ii. 30. where the Apostle declares Christ .to 
have been the substance of all these promises, 
who was to be raised up a king upon this throne,

of the restitution of all things, aype xpovuwy aroyasacews

Theophilus.—Undoubtedly I do; but here let

I no where find the times of the Gentiles men-

Theodorus.—We may look at the times of the 
Gentiles as divided into four great epochs, of 
1, merey, 2, trial, 3, judgment; and 4, mercy 
again. Mercy came when salvation by the atone- 
ment, and justification by the righteousness of 
another, was offered to every Gentile who had the 
will to receive it. Their time of trial has lasted 
ever since; and when their hour of trial is over, 
the time of their judgment commences before 
Christ, the judge of all men: after the Jews are 
restored, another time of mercy will arrive to 


a2 


the then Gentile world. We may observe the same 
mode of treatment displayed by God towards 
the Jews. Jn the calling of Abraham, and in all 
his dealings with them, up to the establishment of 
the Mosaic institutions, we may see his loving- 
kindness and mercy. Their time of trial was 
during the whole government of their kings, the 
preaching of their prophets, and the personal 
ministry of the Son of God. The time of their 
judgment began by their absolute rejection of 
him, and their final dispersion, and has continued 
ever since.. The time of mercy again will be that 
of their restoration. 

Philemon.—Certainly the dealings of God with 
the visible Gentile church, will be precisely similar 
to that which they were with the visible Jewish 
church. The Jewish church had apostatized at 
the first coming of Christ, which apostacy was 
foretold; and the Gentile church has apostatized 
as much now, so that she bears every one of the 
marks described in the Revelation, under the 
figure of the mother of harlots and abominations ; 
which apostacy was equally foretold in the Apoca- 
lypse, and in the Epistles to the Thessalonians : 
and this is the cause of the Apocalypse being so 
little relished by the church. Both apostacies are 
judged, but the judgment on the latter will be 
more severe, as the privileges which have been 
abused are greater. Our brother Josephus knows, 
practically, the sufferings of the Jewish church 
for her apostacy, and has been an eye-witness of 
the apostacies and cutting off of sundry Gentile 
churches.

Basilicus.—All commentators on Scripture in 
general, have erred in some material instances,

pressed the second advent into the first, and lost

sight of the great outline in confining their specu-

Now to arrive at any satisfactory conclusion re-

sidered as a whole, and men must be found of 
minds sufficiently large, or must have their minds 
enlarged to this degree of expansion before they 
can satisfactorily take in, or examine the essential

for the recapitulation of all under Him as their

carrying on, under the’ present dispensation, or 
evangelical ceconomy, and the consummation of 
which is its object and end. Respice finem is the 
motto for all other speculations ; but neghge finem 
seems to have been the watch-word to Jew and 
Gentile, as to the expectation of their respective 
churches. ‘The children of Israel could not 
steadfastly look to the end of that which is 
abolished ;” and the children of our Israel cannot 
steadfastly look to the end of that ministration 
which is at last to succeed the former in glory. 
There is a vail untaken away in the reading of the

ing cast over all nations, which is still upon the 


35 


hearts of Christians themselves in the reading of

times, or the times of the Gentiles are fulfilled. 
Hence arises the indistinct confusion with many 
real believers, as to the spiritual and personal 
kingdoms of the Saviour. 

Philalethes—Is not Mede a good writer upon 
this subject ? 

Sophron. —Mede is the most valuable writer we 
have upon all the promises of God which were 
unfulfilled in his time. Respecting the subject

has an admirable passage, wherein he considers 
the times of the Gentiles under a two-fold aspect,

concerns of mankind can only be classed, either

rendered tributary to Shalmaneser, king of As- 
syria: this event took place about the year 728 
before Christ: if to this number be added 1792

Anastusius.—The structure of the book of Isaiah 
is sadly overlooked, whereas if it were better 
attended to, great light would be thrown upon. 
much that is now obscure. The first chapter con- 
tains a description of the spiritual diseases of the 
visible Jewish church. The second chapter begins 
a series of prophecies, and notes also the chrono- 
logy, or date, when they are to take place; the 
various terms of ‘‘ in the last days,” “in the day 
when the Lord alone shall be exalted,” &c. &c., 
marking the epoch when we are to look for their. 


37 


fulfilment. From the 13th to the 23d chapters 
are contained ten woes, denounced upon ‘ten 
kingdoms which bordered upon the Holy Land, 
but which have manifestly never yet received a full 
accomplishment ; and, be it remembered, that ten 
plagues came upon Egypt before the inhabitants 
would allow the people of God to remove out of it ; 
and that the last kingdom, or power, which Daniel 
declares shall oppress the saints, is divided into 
ten parts. But to return to Isaiah; at the 24th 
chapter, and from thence to the 35th chapter, is 
a description of the judgments which God shall 
‘bring upon the whole earth, and at the same 
time an exhortation to the church, and to the Jews 
to rejoice. 

Sophron.—We may rest assured, that as the 
Jews, who are the types to us Gentiles in all 
things, had a baptism of water at the Red Sea, 
and a baptism of blood in the days of Titus; so 
the Gentiles have had an individual baptism in 
water at their entrance into the church, and shall 
yet have a baptism of blood in the day of 
„Armageddon. 

Philalethes.—Will the judgments you suppose

Theophilus.—We shall gain some insight into 
this most important question, by referring to the 
object of all God’s visitations upon mankind ; and 
we shall find, upon examination, that whenever he 
has specially interfered, it has always been in 
order to work deliverances for his own people. 


38 


The purport of our blessed Lord’s last sermon 
was to instruct his followers, that although they 
would be persecuted by the world, they would 
not be deserted by him. All God’s judgments 
are in mercy towards his people, and he never 
has risen up for them until they have long cried 
to him for relief. 

Crito.—It does not appear probable that the 
spiritual church will receive- any more damage 
than the Israelites did in coming up out of 
Egypt. The phrase ‘‘ the times of the Gentiles,” 
has a two-fold application. In reference to the 
Jews as a nation, it designates that time during 
which the Jews will be under the domination of 
the four Gentile monarchies, symbolized by the 
great image and beasts of Daniel. In reference 
to the Jews as the Church of God, the phrase 
describes that time during which the gospel of the 
kingdoms is taken from them and given to the 
Gentiles. 

Aristo.—The true church will not suffer any 
loss by the pouring out of the judgments; but it 
may have to suffer tribulation. Just as the spiri- 
tual church of the Jews suffered a change, but no 
loss, when that nation was cut off. Its state is 
analogous to the Israelites passing out of Egypt. 
The fruitless branches, both of Jews and Gen- 
tiles, will be cut off. It was the Israelites multi- 
plying, which enraged Pharaoh, and it is the 
increase of the church that enrages Satan. The 
church was much more awake some time ago 
than it is now. Our duty is to hate the world, 
and to forsake the world, and this duty is quick- 


39 


ened by the study of prophecy, and therefore the 
prophetic view is the practical view of the Holy

servants, and not Kings, during the whole of this 
- dispensation. The church has been receiving 
blessings from God in an especial manner for 
these last thirty years, which have been felt by 
all the world through the light that she has thereby 
shed around her. The wrath of the world will 
always be excited against her in exact proportion 
to the brightness with which her light shines. 
We know, individually, that the more we possess 
of the Holy Spirit, the more the world hates us, 
and. gnashes on us with their teeth, as our blessed 
Lord himself was more ill-treated than any other 
person. The church will, no doubt, receive in- 


creased light upon the subject of the end of the

and excited to it shortly before the time of its 
consummation, in order that she may be readj 
for the change, enter into her ark, be saved from

same body, even Christ’s body, and it is only by

= Josephus.—It is a remarkable feature in the

_ Philalethes.—Our present business is, no doubt, 
with the end of the times of the Gentiles; but I

Aristo.—Our brother Sophron has very well 
referred us to the Assyrian captivity as the first 
period of the church becoming subject to the 
world, and thence, no doubt, one interpretation of 
_ the expression may be derived; and we may 
fairly say that the times of the Gentiles commence 
with the times of the Gentile oppression. In these 
last days the Gentile dispensation has been forming 
the elements of the man of sin, and there can be 
no doubt but that the Scriptures, und voce, declare, 
that the judgment on the Gentiles will be much 
more severe than that which fell upon the Jews. 
As the prophets of old knew not the time when 
Christ would appear the first time, so neither did 
the apostles know when he would appear the 
second time; but Paul knew that before he would 
come in glory a man of sin was to be revealed ; 
and Paul knew enough of Satan’s devices to 
be aware that he would erect a figment very 
like the true Christ. At the first, Satan set up 


41 


a sensual Pope; one addressed to the senses ; 
magnificent cathedrals, splendid paintings and 
statues, delightful music, gorgeous dresses, poetic 
ritual, and immense wealth. The wide circulation 
of the Scriptures, increasing information, advance- 
ment of science, the scoffs and ridicule of philoso- 
phers, and the desolations of conquering armies, 
have together nearly destroyed this sensual pope. 
Satan has therefore now betaken himself to set up 
another, an intellectual pope, o avopoc, 2 Thess. ii. 
who is being produced out of the Protestant 
churches. This pope will receive nothing upon 
the bare testimony of God’s word, but he must 
have his reason convinced : it is the very opposite 
of faith: his appearance is the 4 arosracıa par 
excellence, the apostacy: he sits in the intellect, 
the noblest part of man, where God alone ought 
to dwell, and shows himself as the only object to 
which all things must bow. Satan has an inlet 
into the intellect of man, as well as into the senses 
of man; he could not, therefore, be contented 
with a mere sensual man of sin, but he must have 
an intellectual man of sin also; and the last great 
antichristian confederacy will be a denial of the 
divinity of our Lord. This is the natural and | 
inevitable consequence of all education which is 
not founded upon the doctrines of Christianity, 
and in this must all schemes for the instruction 
of the people end, such as those of Owen and the 
Mechanics Institutions. | 
Josephus.—It is a curious fact, in corroboration 
of this, that Mr. English, the American, who had 
turned Mahometan, and whom I met in Egypt,

told me that he had renounced Christianity in

7  Aristo.—Nothing can be so opposed to the dis- 
position of faith, as that which is-only to be con- 
vinced by external evidences. It is, in fact, saying 
that we will not believe God unless He can bring 
a voucher for the truth of what He says. It is a 
remarkable feature of this man of sin, (the law- 
less one,) that he not only sets the laws of God at 
defiance, but those of man also, which might be 
abundantly proved by areference to the works of 
the most popular infidel writers of the present 
: day. Infidels are necessarily under no subjection 
to the laws, any longer than it is their interest to 
be so, for they cannot respect the institution of 
kings, rulers, and magistrates as of God’s appoint- 
ment. The effect, however, will be very different 
according as infidelity is diffused amongst the 
higher or lower ranks of society. If confined to 
the higher, they will still make use of religion, 
even whilst they despise its realities, as a conve- 
nient engine for the government of the State: but 
if diffused generally amongst the lower, they will 
look upon all the institutions of religion and de- 
grees in society, as instruments of injustice and 
oppression, and will infallibly, sooner or later, 
destroy them. 
Theodorus.—This is quite true, and might of 
itself convince us, even if the testimony of God

Aristo.—Prophecy is the great sign to the

Philalethes.-Do you not consider that the de- 
struction of Babylon will take place at the end of 
the present dispensation ? 

Aristo.— Undoubtedly it is so declared in Isaiah, 
Jeremiah, and the Apocalypse. The true mean- 
ing of Babylon, or that which it symbolizes, is the 
prison house of the church. The captivity of the 
church of old by the king of Babylon was 
permitted, amongst other reasons, in order to 
furnish a name and a language to the future 
captivities. Wherever the spirit of binding down 
and oppressing the church of Christ is found, 
there is Babylon. I do not deny, indeed, that the 


44 


centre and seat of this spirit is in Rome; but 
as Christians have been persecuted in old times 
in the East Indies, in America, and in Asia, 
though we neither know the names nor number 
of the sufferers, Babylon may include the whole 
world. The importance of events which are 
nearer home, make us sometimes apply the term 
to the Papal church only, and the victims to those 
who kave been butchered by the Inquisition alone. 
The judgment on Babylon will be tremendous 
and awful in the extreme. The natural heart is 
inclined to shudder on reading the account ‘of the 
torments which will be brought upon her; we are 
too deeply infused with sin, our natural hearts are 
yet too unsanctified to make us rejoice as we 
should otherwise do in the destruction of the ene- 
mies of a thrice holy God; but let not our un- 
holiness lead us to doubt the reality of that de- 
struction, and the greatness of the danger which 
awaits every inhabitant of Babylon. Let it rather 
stimulate us to cry without ceasing, to every one, 
to come out of her, that they may not be partakers 
of her plagues.

CONVERSATION THE SECOND. 


Practical Relations of the Times of the Gentiles, &c. &c. 
to the Church. 


Philalethes.—I gather from all you have seve- 
rally said, in our last most interesting conversation, 
that you are agreed that the object of this dis- 
pensation was either as a punishment upon the 
Jewish church, or as a favour conferred upon the 
Gentile church; and that in either point of view it 
is to end in severe and awful judgments upon 
Christendom. But I am somewhat surprised at 
finding that you treat this subject as a practical 
one, whereas I have always heard it spoken of 
as a pure theory, without any pragucal results 
whatever. | 

Theophilus.—This is ever the case: men who 
will not take the trouble of informing themselves 
about the matter in question, pronounce dogmati- 
cally upon the effects of that of which they con- 
fess they are ignorant. You may consider the 
duties arising out of this view of Scripture truth, 
under two leading divisions of, Ist, duties gene- 
rally of the Christian church; 2d, duties of indivi- 
duals. These may be also subdivided into duties, 
Ist, as ministers, 3d, as laymen; and these again 
with reference, Ist, to the church, 2d, to the 
world; and all of us ought to desire and aim 
after a better realization of our characters of fel- 
low-workers together with our Lord (1 Cor. iii. 9.) 


46 


either watching in the outer court, as it were, 
for our Lord’s appearance, or entering more 
earnestly into the Holy of Holies, by private me- 
ditation and prayer; beseeching him especially to 
take from us that timidity which paralyzes our 
_efforts, and give us boldness and activity in his 
service. The first duty in this, and all other 
things, is faith: looking for his coming, whilst 
infidels are crying, ‘‘ where is the promise of his 
coming? for since the fathers fell asleep all things. 
continue as they were from the beginning of the 
creation.” 2 Pet. iii. 4. Secondly, Watchfulness ; 
“ Behold I come as a thief, blessed is he that 
watcheth, and keepeth his garments, lest he walk 
naked, and they see his shame.” Rev. xvi. 15. 
This has reference to the Levites who were ap- 
pointed to watch in the temple. If they were 
- found asleep they had their garments cut off, and 
were compelled to wear them in their curtailed 
condition all the next day, that their want of 
fidelity in their charge might be apparent to all. 
See also Mark xiii. 33—37. and Rev. i. 3. in all 
which places you will find exhortation to watch- 
fulness coupled with the second coming of Christ. 
Thirdly, Active zeal; this is the characteristic of 
the persons described in Rev. xiv. 6. “ the angel / 
flying ;” and in these days to be evinced by assist- 
ing in the labours of our various religious societies. 
Fourthly, Deadness to the world; for it is mani- 
fest that they who really believe the present dis- 
pensation to be so nearly at a close, that its consum- 
mation may be expected at any moment, can no 
more rivet their affections and interests to the 


47 


things of the present scene, than they could build, 
and plant, and dance, upon ground during its 
agitation by an earthquake. 

Philalethes.—It is quite obvious that a practical 
belief in the near approaching end of the present 
dispensation wouid produce deadness to the world, 
because we find that such was the effect produced 
by the preaching of the Apostles, as we learn from 
the whole tenor of Paul’s two Epistles to the 
Thessalonians, and from Gibbon’s sneering con- 
fessions (vol. ii. chap. xv.); and before our brother 
Theophilus proceeds, let me ask Evander what he 
has found in the Scriptures, respecting the duties 
of the church at this particular juncture. 

Evander.—I will tell you with pleasure: but 
you must bear with me if I bring forward opinions 
which will not fit in to ordinary systems: yet, 
surely, our business is not to support Calvinism, 
nor Arminianism, nor any other ism, but to be 
guided by that which is written in the word of 
God. I find every where in Scripture a judgment 
according to works, and I think it highly neces- 
sary to bring forward- this view, in order to pro- 
mote the holiness of the Christian’s walk. It will 
not, of course, be requisite to shew that I am not 
alluding to the mode of a sinner’s acceptance with 
God; but leaving this milk of Christian doctrine, 
I am speaking of the highest point of a Christian's 
attainment in this world, his walk with God in all 
holy obedience ; and I find that in almost all the 
passages in which the end of this dispensation is 
spoken of, a reward according to works is spoken 
of in connection with it. ‘ The Son of Man shall 


48 


come in the glory of his Father, with his angels, 
and then shall he reward every man according to 
his works.” Matt. xvi. 27. Again, in chap. xxv. 
the parable of the unprofitable servant is evidently 
spoken by our Lord, of those who are his servants 
in the highest acceptation of that term. See, too, 
Luke xix. 12, &c. But not only is a reward ac- 
cording to works spoken of as connected with 
our blessed Lord’s second coming, but an exhorta- 
tion to be found walking worthily at the time 
when it shall take place, is constantly given when 
that subject is spoken of. Examine, therefore, 
the following passages for yourself, and the Lord 
give you a right understanding in all things. Eph. 
v. 5—7.; Phil. in. 17—21.;°> Col. i. 21—23.: 
iii, 22—25.; 1 Thess. ii. 11, 12. 19. ; ii. 11—13.;

John i. Ep. m. 2, 3. All which passages are 
clearly addressed to those within, exclusively. | 
Philalethes.—These passages, I have no doubt, 
refer to the rewards and stations which the Great 
King will assign, in his future kingdom, to his fol- 
lowers, according to the attainments they: shall 
have made in spirituality of mind, and conformity 
to Him during the present dispensation.” May I 
now beg our brother Theophilus to proceed from 
the point at which I interrupted him. _ | 
Theophilus. —I was proceeding to remark upon 
the duties which seemed to belong to us as minis- 
ters, amongst which duties I place, first, diligent 
study of the Scriptures, and prayer for peculiar 
instruction in the prophetic Scriptures, grounded 


49 


upon ‘the little that is known upon them by the 
church at large, and grounded also upon the nature 
of the instruction required. We find from 1 Pet. i. 
“That the prophets have enquired and searched 
diligently—searching what, or what manner of 
time the spirit of Christ which was in them did 
signify, when it testified before hand—the glory 
that should follow.” 10,11. The same Apostle 
says again to us, “ we have not followed cunningly 
devised fables, when we made known unto you 
the power and coming of our Lord Jesus Christ— 
we have also a more sure word of prophecy ; 
whereunto ye do well that ye take heed, as unto a 
light shining in a dark place, until the day dawn, 
and the day star arise in your hearts.” 2 Pet.1. 
16—19. Not only have we this direction, but we 
have-also the example of Daniel, Elias, Jere- 
miah, men of like passions with ourselves, yet to 
whose prayers remarkable answers were granted. 
Daniel set his face unto the Lord God, to seek by 
prayer and supplication, with fasting, and sack- 
cloth, and ashes ; and whiles he was speaking in 
prayer, the man Gabriel came to him, and said, 
O Daniel, I am now come forth to give thee skill 
and understanding ; at the beginning of thy sup- 
plications the commandment came forth, and I am 
come to shew thee. ix. 3—23. In Jeremiah xxxii. 
is a striking example of the earnestness of that 
prophet’s supplications, and of the instruction in 
the impending destinies of his people which the

ii, 12, there appears very distinct directions for 
. fasting and prayer upon subjects immediately re-

lating to our present discussiqn. It is mentioned:

shortly after his people had set apart a particular 
day in the week, on which to unite in imploring.

The second duty is to preserve a meek spirit on 
the subject of the prophetic Scriptures in our con- 
versations, and other communications with Chris- 
tians; to be on our guard to confine our public 
statements to points which can be maintained by 
direct and plain passages of Scripture, and not use 
analogies and types as the basis of arguments, 
but only as illustrations; and to avoid dogmatizing, 
or speaking with unseemly asperity of those who 
have no light upon the matter, because the church, 
like other persons, when asleep, does not like to 
be aroused. Some fathers have been so conscious 
of the ill-humour with which many people awake, 
when suddenly aroused, that they have caused 
their children to be awoke only by the sounds of 
the most plaintive and melodious music. ` 

The third duty is to confine our discussions 
with the church to those great outlines on which 
the majority of Christians seem to be wholly in the 
dark. Many spiritual men are to be found, who 
have never given the subject a thought; and if 
particulars are entered upon, some one of these is 
fastened upon and reproved, and the attention 1 is 
diverted from the general subject. 

The fourth a is to speak more ee and. 


ol 


fearlessly on the sins of the world, and on the sins 
of the church. Real Christian boldness in re- 
proving sin is always mixed with mournings for 
the sin complained of. Scolding sinners, or even 
vehemence in reproving sin itself, does not arise 
from the movements of the Holy Spirit, but from 
pride. Christian abhorrence of sin arises from a 
real and spontaneous, and not affected horror of 
the effects of sin in calling down the indignation of 
Omnipotence, and in destroying the souls of men. 
Although Jeremiah was perpetually denouncing 
the coming of the day of wrath, he was earnest in 
praying to God to pardon the people, and so as it 
_were to falsify his own predictions; unlike Jonas, 
a prophet shewn to us perhaps as a warning, 
rather than an imitation. ‘‘ I have not hastened 
from being a pastor to follow thee,” says he, 
“ neither have I desired the woeful day.” xvii. 16. 
Ezekiel also, when he saw the vision of the 
slaughtermen, fell upon his face, and cried, and 
said, ‘“ Ah, Lord God! wilt thou destroy all the 
residue of Israel in thy pouring out. of thy fury 
upon Jerusalem ?” ix. 8. And it seems as if our 
blessed Lord could not get through all the woes 
which he was denouncing in Matt. xxi. without 
breaking out into that beautiful and pathetic la- 
mentation for Jerusalem recorded in ver. 17. It 
is one of the most god-like of all graces to weep at 
the thoughts of the miseries arising from sin, and 
to pour out our souls unto God in prayer, and in 
expressions of sorrow for the sufferings of others 
on account of their transgressions. Thus did 
Ezra ix. and Nehemiah, chap. i. : Let it be al-

ways remembered, that the church is not repre-

ments are being poured out, but only after all 
things are consummated, and the mystery of God’s
