Fifthly. We must not suffer our views of the 
fulfilment of the prophecies to modify, in the. least, 
our actings on our commission as ministers of the 
Gospel, heralds of glad tidings, and preachers of 
salvation. Thus in Rev. xiv. the angel that goes 
forth pronouncing, that God's judgments are come, 
still goes forth preaching the Gospel. From Rev. 
xix. 1—6., there is great reason to hope, that 
many persons will have been brought in, even in 
_ the eleventh hour, and perhaps by seeing set be- 
fore them the judgments now taking place in the 
world. The boast of ministers of the Gospel must 
still. be, that which constituted the boast of the 
Apostle Paul, when he said, ‘‘ God forbid that I 
should glory save in the cross of our Lord Jesus 
Christ,” for a greater work has been done for us 
there than can ever be done for us again. One 
device of Satan will, no doubt, be to represent the 
cross as alittle thing; as it were only the begin- 
ning of the work of Christ for us. -Whereas, that 
is the grand alpha and omega of all our salvation: 
the most marvellous manifestation of the love of 
God, even of our Saviour, is, that he was slain 
for us. 

Sixthly. Let us sit daily more loose to the 
‘world; have as few wants as possible; gratify 
them as little as possible, and devote our sub- 
stance more to the relief of the necessitous, and 


53 

to the promotion of the preaching of the Gospel in 
falling Babylon: the warnings of our blessed Lord 
throughout the Evangelists are very strong against 
those of his servants being found, at his coming, 
charged with surfeiting, drunkenness, and cares 
of this world. It is said, that many. Jews in Po- 
land have long been expecting to be summoned 
back to Jerusalem, and keep all their property in 
moveables, so that they are ready to set out at a - 
few hours notice. 

Seventhly. We must be particularly on our 
guard against the wiles of Satan; this doctrine is 
one of peculiar power against his kingdom, and 
therefore he will set himself with peculiar malice 
to destroy it, and chiefly, no doubt, by exciting 
the opposite of a spirit of love between those 
Christians who receive it, and those who are as 
yet unenlightened in it. Where a heart is sur- 
rounded by love to the brethren, there he cannot 
so easily penetrate, and the more Satan sees men 
on the Lord’s side, the more will he be active in 
assaulting them. 

Eighthly. Patience ; we are come to the aol 
when the patience of the saints will be peculiarly 
tried; we shall have to imitate the example of 
the Apostles, who, after they had been ill-treated, 
did not resent their wrongs, and excite a spirit of 
discontent and radicalism amongst the people; 
but being let go, they went immediately to their 
own company. To his saints, the Lord saith, 
“ because thou hast kept the word of my patience, 
I also will keep thee from the hour of temptation, 
which shall come upon all the world, to try them

that dwell upon the earth : behold I come quickly :

. Anastasius.—Nothing can be more useful than 
the practical observations: and enumerations of 
duties with which our dear brother Theophilus 
has favoured us. It is truly grievous to see how 
little Christian sympathy and love is excited for 
those who are suffering most from the obloquy of 
the professing world, and whose only crime is 
their zeal and their sincerity. But such a spirit 
of cold calculating selfishness has palsied of late 


. the professors of religion, that there is no longer 


an holy emulation amongst them, who can do 
most for the service of the best of Masters; but 
all the anxiety is to avoid obloquy, to go with 
the stream, and keep to the happy mediocrity of 
knowledge, faith, and practice which characterizes 
that which is ambiguously termed, the religious 
world. I would just call your attention to the 
distinction to be made in the word crown, when 
in the original it is expressed by segavoc, and when 
by anpa. The latter is part of the insignia of a 
reigning monarch: it is the former of which Paul 
speaks, when he says, “ henceforth there is laid 


55 


‘up for me a crown,” segavoe: “ chaplet,” would 
perhaps convey the idea better in English, although 
from the custom of placing chaplets on the heads 
of victors, in various competitions, whether of 
athletic or scientific contention, being discontinued 
in this country, it would not be easy to convey the 
idea in a corresponding term. 

Aristo.—1 cordially subscribe to all that our 
beloved brother Theophilus has: said of the duties 
of every one who is really influenced by the be- 
lief, that the second coming of our blessed Lord 
draweth nigh. It appears to me to be quite evi- 
dent, that the object for which any portion of 
truth is at any time revealed or given, so as to 
be felt, is in order that error may be convicted. 
As soon as Satan the deceiver had come into the 
world, it became necessary for Christ, the truth, 
and the revealer of truth, to come, in order to cast 
him out. The errors into which the church has 
at present fallen may be classed under three 
heads. Ist. That there are to be no judgments 
at the period when the times of the Gentiles are 
said to be fulfilled. 2. That a period of great 
blessedness is about to be brought i in immediately. 
3. That the millenial state is to be brought about 
by natural means: such as the diffusion of know- 
ledge, commerce, peace, civilization, improved 
views of political justice, jurisprudence, political 
economy, arts, &c. &c. From these three er- 
rors have arisen three kinds of evil fruits; from 
the first, a total misapprehension of the actual 
state of Christendom; and from not perceiving 
that it is ripe for judgments, there is no union in . 


56 


-” 


the church respecting its hopes and fears; for it 
is not place, nor discipline, nor forms, nor ordi- 
nances, nor any of these kinds of things which can 
unite the whole church together, nor any thing 
but a community of hope and of fear. From the 
second error, namely, that there are to be great 
blessings expected immediately, there arises an 
exaggeration of the favour which God is .shew- 
ing to the church. This is the leading error of 
all our Bible and Missionary Societies, which in- 
duces the managers of these bodies to keep back 
the lets and hindrances they meet with in their 
course from the knowledge of the church; con- 
cealment leads, in its turn, to a spirit of flattery 
and adulation, and this draws after it worldly 
accommodation ; for wherever there is an extrava- 
gant hope, there is an extravagant desire excited 
to gratify that hope. If your hope be higher than 
the truth, you will have a desire higher than a 
right desire; you will have a lust; for what is a 
lust but an extravagant desire; and you will strive 
to gratify that lust by any means, however unscrip- 
tural; this is the secret of all that expediency-work 
which is so prevalent, so lavishly applauded in 
these Societies, although so contrary to all godly 
simplicity, honesty, and scriptural injunction. The 
third error takes away the glory from Christ; it 
is an error similar to what the error of the 
Jews would have been, had they expected 
a Gospel to come by the instruction of Jewish 
teachers, without a personal coming of Christ. 
Thus Christ the Mediator is left out of the means, 
_ and the means are looked to without the Media- 


97 


tor, and Christ is dethroned from his mediatorial 
throne ; whereas no mean is a mean of grace with- 
out the honour of it being referred to Christ the 
Mediator. Hence, they have no object on which 
to roll the honour of success, and therefore they 
divide it amongst one another; the Bible Society 
compliments the Missionary Society; this com- 
pliments the Bartlett’s Buildings Society, and each, 
though professing to give all the glory to God, 
virtually says of itself, “ by the strength of my 
hand have I done it, and by my wisdom, for I 
am prudent; and my hand hath found as a 
nest the riches of the people; and as one ga- 
thereth eggs that are left, have I gathered all 
the earth, and there was none that moved 
the wing, or opened the mouth, or peeped.” 
Isaiah x. 13. 

: Philalethes —This is certainly the most chris- 
tian, and the most just way of accounting for the © 
various faults which the managers of these so- 
cieties have committed ; for it 1s obvious that their 
wish has been to do that which appeared to them 
to be most for the glory of God, and for the 
salvation of the souls of men; but it is most true 
that they have been instrumental in creating a 
very false impression of the extent of religion, 
both in Great Britain and on the Continent of 
Europe. Tell us then our duty with respect to 
these societies. | 

Aristo.—I1 will state my judgment therein to 
you with great pleasure; but I will first suggest 
to you a second use of truth, which is to direct our 
own conduct; and as our dear brother Theophilus 


08 


omitted, in his divisions of practical duties, to 
allude to those which are peculiarly applicable to 
laymen, I will here mention a few. In this matter 
we must look for examples in the history of the 
church; and we there find, that whenever God 
had an important movement for his church to 
make, he has always had a man of power and 
policy, as well as a man of piety, in times of her 
emergency; and that he raised up powerful lay- 
men to protect his ministers in fulfilling the work 
he had assigned to them. The present duty of 
laymen is clearly to protect, as far as in them lies, 
those ministers who shall faithfully fulfil the 
offices which our brother Theophilus so well laid 
down as their peculiar duty. Thus, at the first 
Exodus of his people, God raised up Moses, a man 
learned in all the learning of the Egyptians, who 
had been brought up in the king’s palace, with the 
rank and title of the king’s son, to protect the 
interests of his people, and finally to lead them 
out, with the assistance of his brother Aaron the 
priest, in defiance of all the opposition which the 
king could make. Thus also was Nehemiah, the 
king’s cup-bearer, raised up to succour the zeal 
of Ezra in rebuilding the temple, and leading back 
the people from the Babylonish captivity. Many 
other instances might be cited to shew that God 
has at all times, when there was occasion for 
them, raised up men of power, as well as spiritual 
men, for the guidance and protection of his church, 
although at other times his flock is not found among 
the rich and noble. (1 Cor. i. 26.) In more mo- 
-dern times we observe that the same line of con- 


59 


duct has been preserved. The Lord Mayor of 
London, and John of Gaunt, sat by the side of 
Wickliffe, and so protected him, when he would 
otherwise have most certainly been condemned. 
Lord Cobham was honoured in being allowed to 
bear testimony to the truth as it is in Jesus, even 
unto death, before either Cranmer, or Latimer, 
or Ridley. Luther owed his preservation re- 
peatedly tọ Frederick of Saxony. In Scotland 
the sacrament of the Lord’s Supper was admini- 
stered in the houses of Lords Glencairn and Tor- 
phichen, and other noblemen, before it was per- 
mitted to be administered in any of the churches. 
Subsequently, in the times of the non-conformists, 
we find almost all their books (which are the spi- 
ritual food of the church at the present day, and 
which make all modern works on divinity appear 
but puerile drivelling in comparison to them) 
dedicated to some person of rank and influence 
who had protected them during their persecu- 
` tion. More lately still, Burchardt, the first mis- 
sionary who publicly sold the Gospel of Jesus 
Christ in the towns of Mahometans, was sent 
out by Mr. Drummond, from Geneva; and Wolff, 
the first converted Jew who ever established 
himself permanently at Jerusalem, on purpose 
to preach the faith in Jesus of Nazareth as 
essential to the salvation of his countrymen, 
was sent out from England by two laymen, Mr. 
Drummond, and Mr. Bayford; and wherever . 
Wolff went he found supporters and friends in 
the most influential laymen he met with, such as 
Mr. Salt, the British Consul at Alexandria, and 


60 


Mr. Stratford Canning, the British Ambassador 
at Constantinople. In the 12th, 13th, and 14th 
chapters of the Ist Epistle to the Corinthians, we 
are informed that God distributes his various gifts 
to different members of his church for the benefit 
of his whole mystical body; and aecording to this 
arrangement, whilst he has commanded his mini- 
sters so to devote themselves to the study of his 
word as to become wholly withdrawn from the 
things of this world, and thereby become lacking 
in worldly wisdom, and policy, and skill, he raises 
up powerful laymen to protect them; and he is 
now working amongst the higher orders in this 
country, in order to provide, as it were, a nest for 
his turtle dove: - although Satan, therefore, will 
raise up as much opposition now to the doctrine of 
the second advent, as he did to the doctrine of the 
first advent at the time when that took place, God 
will have now, as he had then, his Josephs of 
Arimathea, his Nicodemuses, and his centurions, 
yea, even those from among the very household of 
Cesar, to do his bidding for his church’s service. - 

Anastasius.—It has been commonly remarked, 
and much insisted on by Lord Clarendon, that 
priests have ever made but indifferent statesmen ; 
and it appears from the Ecclesiastical History of 
the Old Testament, that the union of these two 
characters was not God’s manner of procedure. 
The state of religion, relatively, between the 
higher and lower orders in England, has often 
struck me as very remarkable, in comparing 
what it was during the 18th, and what it is 
in the 19th centuries. From the time of the 


61 


accession of the house of Hanover, ta nearly the 
end of the reign of George ITI, real Christianity 
seems to have become quite dormant, if not ex- 
tinct. The short, though brilliant career of White- 
field and Wesley, scarcely form an exception to 
this observation. If there were, as it seems now 
that there certainly must have been, any live 
embers of the holy fire, they were amongst the 
poorest classes, and entirely smothered by the 
rubbish of this world ; whilst a scarcely disguised 
deism, or an almost acknowledged infidelity pre- 
vailed throughout the higher orders. The class of 
infidel books favoured this state of things; they 
almost wholly consisted of the half-learned, or half- 
metaphysical and voluminous works of Shaftes- 
bury, Bolingbroke, Bayle, Frederick of Prussia, 
Voltaire, Rousseau, &c. &c., and consequently 
were never the common reading of the lower or 
middling classes in England. For the last twenty 
years, however, the style of writing has changed, 
and infidelity is now chiefly disseminated in such 
publications as have the most rapid sale amongst: 
the lowest orders; whilst, on the other hand, 
some of the boldest and most conscientious fol- 
lowers of the despised religion of Jesus Christ 
are to be found in the very highest ranks in this 
country. The profession of religion has been 
found to be lucrative, and therefore all its pro- 
fessors have been accused of insincerity by in- 
fidels; but infidelity is now become lucrative ; 
many persons gain a livelihood by writing for it, 
by vending its publications, by spouting in taverns 
in defence of it, and by railing against Christianity. `

Aristo.—This is truly an awful state for a 
Christian community to be in, and I know too 
well the correctness of what Anastasius has said, 
to feel otherwise than fearfully anxious upon the 
subject. The land seems ripening for destruc- 
tion; the vessels of wrath seem preparing them- 
selves, fitting themselves, for the day of wrath. It 
is a wise way of studying the subject of the 
judgments which are impending upon the Gentile 
church to look at the former judgments with 
which God hath in old times visited the earth; 
and not to multiply examples, more than there be 
need for, let us examine these four; Ist, the deluge ; 
2d, the destruction of Canaan; 3d, captivity of 
Israel and Judah; 4th, the destruction of Jerusalem. 

. Before the Ist, certain prophets were raised up, 
such as Enoch and Noah, to give timely warning, 
and, as it were, to sound the note of preparation. 
Before the 2d, Abraham and the patriarchs were 
long preachers of righteousness, and men whose 
lives testified that God was with them of a truth. 
Before the 3d, the great prophets Isaiah, Jeremiah, 
Micaiah, Hosea, Joel, Amos, Micah, Habakkuk, 
Zephaniah, were all raised up immediately before 
that captivity, in order that they might, in their: 
predictions of the one and subsequent recovery, 
shadow forth God’s typographical providence of 
the great captivity in which the church has been 
since the days of Adam, and out of which it hath 
yet to be redeemed: thus the Bible may be said 
to be the history of the church in a state of 
captivity, with her hopes and her fears, and- her 
anticipations of future deliverance. Before the 4th, 


63 


it was the peculiar office of our blessed Lord and 
the Apostles, to testify to the Jews the destruc- 
tion of Jerusalem; he came, as it were, to avert the 
judgment ; peradventure they might reverence the 
Son, though they would not obey the voice of the 
prophets. ; 

The cause of the deluge is given in Gen. vi.; 
we are there told, that the children of God were 
not careful to keep themselves distinct from the 
children of ungodly men, but mingled with them, 
intermarried with them, became earthly, cum- 
bered with much serving, and so the distinction 
between the church and the world was lost. This 
error is a very common one down to the present 
day; and does not always (nor does it necessarily 
follow from the context that it did so in ante- ` 
diluvian times,) arise from any thing but a motive, 
which may be called good in a certain sense; the 
error is that of endeavouring to include the world 
within the bosom of the church, for its own bene- 
fit; God’s command ever has been, and ever is to 
his children, “ come ye out from among them, and 
be ye separate ;” but the children are ever thinking 
that, by mixing with the world, they can entice 
the world to become children of God; the plan 
fails, as all plans must which do not emanate 
from God, and still more such as are adopted in 
direct defiance of his commands. - This is one’ of 
the essential errors of popery, which is an at- 
tempt to embrace the world:in the church: so 
there is in England a tendency in the church to 
take up with temporal rank, . peerages, wealth, &c. 
as a bribe, and on condition of her acknowledging 


64 


all the members of the political state as members 
of the church of Christ. The history given of the 
state of society, in the beginning of this chapter, 
runs over a very long period, and describes it as it 
existed probably for above 1,000 years before the 
flood came. In the earlier period of this de- 
generated state of the church Enoch was raised: 
his character is shortly and emphatically described 
in chap. v. 22. 24, ‘And Enoch walked with 
God :” whole volumes could not say more for any 
man than that ‘‘ he walked with God.” We are 
told by Jude that Enoch foretold the coming judg- 
ment, and he gave to his son the name of Methn- 
selah, which signifies, ‘‘When he is dead it shall 
come,” or, ‘‘at his death it shall be sent,” ex- 
pressive of his faith in the certainty of what God 
had declared by him coming to pass. Accordingly, 
the same year that Methuselah died did the de- 
luge come. During 100 years did the old man wit- 
ness the preparations of his grandson, in making that 
ark wherein his family of eight persons were alone, 
of all the inhabitants of the earth, preserved alive. 
Perhaps it is fanciful, but at least it is harmless, 
and it is certainly pleasing, to see in the extreme 
age to which Methuselah was permitted to draw 
on his life, an emblem of the extreme reluctance 
of God to bring down his threatened judgments 
on the world. Enoch is the type of what we 
ought now to be, and we ought, on no account, to 
be deterred from speaking of, and warning men 
concerning the judgments which we believe to be 
coming now; and thus Enoch’s preaching is the — 
model of what our preaching ought to be, as it is 


a 


65 


recorded by Jude, ver. 14, 15.; and we learn from 
the example of Enoch, that it is not only our duty 
to testify as he did, but also “ to walk with God” 
like him. The crimes against which Enoch is said 
particularly to have testified, are such as were 
analogous to the sins of Cain, Balaam, and Core. 
“The way of Cain” was not only a murderous 
way, but an infidel way; the way .which the 
spirit of infidelity takes; yet so little disgust does 
the character, the demoniacal character of Cain 
excite now, that two of our best modern poets, 
Byron and Shelly, have selected him as a subject 
on which their morbid geniuses delighted to ex- 
patiate. The way of Balaam, as we learn from 
2 Pet. ii. 15., was the way of the spirit of avarice 
in the church: and we hear now of men, whose 
professions would lead us to hope better things of 
them, speculating in chapels, and counting the 
value of a ministry by the number of the congre- 
gation, or by the amount of money the preacher 
can collect. “The way of Core” was insubordina- 
tion both in church and state; and never was that 
spirit more rife than in the present day. Eccle- 
siastical discipline has altogether ceased, and the 
fear of being priest-ridden has driven men head- 
long into the opposite error. The state shows no 
reverence to the church as an institution of God, 
although it makes use of her as an engine of go- 
vernment: and thus the children of the state, 
being taught to imbibe a want of deference to the 
church, carry the same principle against all con- 
stituted authority. These three evil spirits. are 
well handled by Mr. Lewis Way, in a sermon 
F 


66 


preached before the Continental Society: against 
these, in all their modifications, Enoch prophecied 
an impending judgment. ‘The judgments of God 
are not brought upon sins of one day, but on a 
long series of iniquittes. The present, also, is a 
time of “hard speeches,” as it was when Enoch 
witnessed against it; and in the coming of the 
then judgments was Enoch’s faith tried. Three 
eminent servants of God testified their faith in the 
coming judgment of the deluge, and God justified 
their faith in three remarkable ways: Enoch was 
translated ; Methuselah died just before the de- 
struction came; and Noah was saved out of it: 
and thus it will be at the last judgment; as it was 
in the days of Noah, so shall it be in the days 
when the Son of Man cometh again. As Enoch 
-was caught up to heaven, so ‘‘ we Gentiles, which 
‘are alive and remain, shall be caught up together 
with them in the clouds, to meet the Lord in the 
air.” 1 Thess. iv. 17. As Methuselah was “ taken 
away from the evil to come,” (Isa. lvii. 1.) so shall 
many saints fall asleep in Jesus before his wrath 
Is poured out; according as he says by Isaiah 
xxvi. 20: ‘Come, my people, enter thou into thy 
chambers, and shut thy doors about thee ; hide 
thyself as it were for a little moment, until the 
indignation be overpast: for, behold, the Lord 
cometh out of his place, to punish the inhabitants 
of the earth for their iniquity; the earth also shall 
-disclose her blood, and shall no more cover her 
slain.” As Noah was preserved in the ark, so 
‘shall the Jewish church be preserved in the wil- 
:derness, into which she shall be taken, (Cant. 


67 


viii. 5; Hos. xi. 14, &c.) there re-betrothed, and 
kept from the antichristian tribulation. Enoch’s 
translation was doutbtless out of great trouble, 
brought upon him by his faithfulness in witnessing 
of the coming judgments; and when Christ takes 
up his people to meet him in the air, it will be out 
of great and severe danger. As the judgment drew 
‘near, Noah, a different person from Enoch the first 
witness, was raised up immediately hefore the 
deluge. Let us now see his character in Gen. 
vi. 8, 9. “Noah found grace in the eyes of the 
‘Lord.—Noah was a just man and perfect in- his 
generations, and Noah walked with God.” Gene- 
rally, when God has an important. work to da, he 
raises up some one of the seed of his children for 
the purpose: and let us ever bear in mind, from 
this history, that we cannot be preachers of righ- 
teousness by mixing with the ungadly. ‘“Naman 
that warreth entangleth himself with the affairs of 
this life, that he may please him who hath chosen 
him.to be a soldier ;”—-therefore let us “‘ endure 
all things for the elects’ sake, that they may also 
obtain the salvation which is in Christ Jesus with 
eternal glory.” 2 Tim. 11.4. 10. ‘* If God spared 
not the old world, but saved Noab, the eighth per- 
son, a preacher of righteousness, bringing in the 
flood upon the world of the ungodly,” (2 Pet. 
ii. 5.) assuredly it is our great business now to 
separate ourselves, to shed tears even while we do 
it, but to declare fully, loudly, and unreservedly, | 
the awfulness of the coming judgments. The pre- 
sent age has obscured the holiness of God by false 
views of his.mercy ; it is.our part, therefore, espe? 
F2 


68 
cially to testify of his holiness. It is well said by 
Young, that “a God all mercy is a God unjust :” 
and what the world means by a God of mercy, is 
a God who will allow men to sin as they list, and 
not punish them for it; so that they desire no 
better amusement than to sin, provided only they 
can be freed from the certainty or the apprehen- 
sion of the consequences. But we must remem- 
ber that itis not by the spirit of holiness, but by 
the spirit of pride, that we are testifying of God’s 
holiness, if we are not full of tenderness and love 
in the manner of our testimony. It is the Spirit 
of God alone which can enable us to do this: 
even Jeremiah flinched from the fearful office of 
proclaiming the full extent of the then impending 
visitations. The object of Noah being raised up, 
and his office before God, we find stated in Heb. 
xi. 5. “By faith Noah, being warned of God of 
things not seen as yet, moved with fear, prepared 
an ark to the saving of his house, by which he 
condemned the world, and became heir of the 
righteousness which is by faith;” it is therefore 
the office of us, who believe in this coming judg- 
ment, now to bear witness that the grandeur of | 
this world is passing away, not by changing our 
conditions, but by shewing that we care nothing 
about them. Such, then, being Noab’s office, we 
have Noah’s work described in the same chapter 
of Gen. vi. 14—22; and ‘‘according to‘all that 
God. commanded him, so did he.” The ark is. the 
church, and all the works of the church are like 
the planks of the ark, which keep those who are 
within safe, and keep the danger out. Reading 


69 


the Scriptures, prayer, frequenting. public wor- 
ship, observing family and private devotion, re- 
verencing ministers, attending to the ordinances, 
&c. &c. these things, unless they are looked upon 
as of God’s appointment, will not convey to us the 
blessings which God will only communicate by l 
these means. The church, in the present day, is 
the true ark, and unless. we reverence the ap- 
pointed offices in her, she cannot prove the means 
of deliverance to us. Ministers should be espe- 
cially diligent in preaching the word ; administer- 
ing the ordinances, and fulfilling every part of the 
ministry ; and in showing themselves dutiful to the 
church : for nothing will tend so much to spread 
abroad a spirit of irreverence to her, as any re- 
missness on the part of her ministers : whereas 
even the outer court is sanctified by that which is 
within : if the ministers, who are the salt, lack 
saltness, how can the earth be salted? The 
preacher of righteousness, Enoch, walked with 
God, and was the translated one: the preacher 
of righteousness, Noah, walked with God, and 
was the saved one; and it is only by our treading 
in their steps that we ourselves can escape. 
Josephus.—1 feel the justness of the observa- 
tions of our dear brother Aristo-so forcibly, that 
I earnestly pray for more zeal in the service of my 
Lord and Master. I have many times proved that 
the more fearlessly we act, the more safe we are ; 
and the more worldly prudence and management 
we use, the more danger we run. All schemes for 
the civilization or instruction of men which have 
been resorted to in the East, except the preaching 


of the Gospel, have only ended in making them 
change one folly for another: some Musselmen 
Rave changed their creed in order to drink gin; 
some Jews in order to eat pork; and some Papists 
in order to avoid the tyranny of the priests. 
Crito.—T he expression of the Apostle in } Thess. 
iv. 17. “We which are alive and remain shall be 
caught up in the clouds to meet the Lord in the 
air,” seemis to refer rather to the third and last act 
of Christ’s coming to judgment, namely, at the 
end of the world. The event which we are to 
look for at the intermediate act of Christ’s coming’ 
to judgment at the end of the Gentile dispetisa~ 
tion, I conceive will be a remarkable presérvation 
of the church, like that of Lot from the destruction 
of Sodoni, and that of the chufch at Pella at 
the destruction of Jerusalem. Then he will send 
forth his angels, and gather together his elect, 
perhaps by an invisible ministry, though in the 
case of Sodom, their ministry was visible. In re- 
ference to this deliverance, the church is directed 
to remember Lot’s wife, that is, to let no worldly 
considerations prevent their embracing that only 
hope of escape which will then be offered to them. 
For this, I conceive, the Christian ministry should 
now prepare the church, as Christ prepared his 
church by similar injunctions : were the church at 
this time to be delivered from destruction, by 
being caught up into the air, there would be no 
need to caution them to remember Lot’s wife; 
such a caution can only have reference to a tem- 
poral deliverance, which they may embrace and 
be saved, ór neglect and perish. Hence arises 


71 


the importance of preaching the coming of Christ, 
and of preparing the church for that great event. 
Basilicus.—There must needs be great defect 
in the practice of the church, as long as there pre- 
vails so much error in its principles; and, at the. 
present day, the practical duties and relations 
arising from a due distinction between the spiritual 
and personal kingdom of the Saviour are small, 
irrelevant, or wholly overlogked, in comparison to 
the result which would ensue from referring them 
all, (not to the day of death, but) as the Apostles 
uniformly did, to the blessed hope, and glorious 
appearance of the triumphant Jesus: and thus it 
is that God hath concluded both Jew and Gentile 
in unbelief: the Jew as to the humiliation, and 
the Gentile as to the exaltation of Messiah in the 
scene of his sufferings. | 
Sophron.—There is a remarkable difference be- 
tween the fasting and praying of the first churches 
and those of the present day : we talk of religion, 
and study theology on sofas, whilst they learned 
it in the sight of racks and martyrdom, Our pre- 
sent duty clearly lies in a more strenuous preach- 
ing of the Gospel than ever, and the more as we 
see the judgments drawing nearer; and our en- 
couragement in so doing is to be found in Rev. 
vil. 14., which seems to describe those who are 
converted during the great, the last tribulation. 
We must take care in looking for, and in preaching. 
the crown, that we do not lose sight of the cross. 
Neither are these days in which laymen ought to 
be content with sending out labourers, they onghs. 
to labour as preachers themselves. 7

: Anastasius.—Not only before the establishment 
of the Levitical priesthood was the head of every 
family a priest in his own house; but in subse- 
quent times God has been pleased to honour lay- 
men by being his prophets. Joshua, David, Solo- 
mon, Isaiah, Daniel, and many others, might be 
mentioned ; and in these latter days, Sir I. Newton, 
Mede,Cuninghame, Frere, Bayford, Brown,Tilloch, 
&c. have been blessed in having their minds en- 
lightened in the prophetic parts of the Holy Scrip- 
tures. That this is a very distinguished honour, . 
may be learned from | Cor. xii. 28., where the 
Apostle places the understanding of prophecies as- 
the second highest office in the Christian church. 
Laymen should principally bear witness of the 
truth, in places to which ministers either cannot 
have access, or from which- the clergy ought to. 
exclude themselves, as being of too secular a 
character. In the 4th chapter of the Acts, bold- ` 
ness is three times mentioned, as an accompani- 
ment of the Holy Ghost in the hearts of believers. 
All Bible and Missionary Societies ought to be 
encouraged to cry, ‘Fear God, and give glory. 
to him, for the hour of his judgment is come,” . 
Rev. xiv. 7., a passage which the Societies are 
very fond of applying to themselves; but the last 
clause of it, they seem by common consent to 
overlook. It is our especial duty to encourage all 
Societies for preaching the Gospel on the Con- 
tinent of Europe, the great scene of the apoca- 
lyptic drama, and to show our brotherly love to 
those faithful followers of the Lamb, who have 
been driven out of .the Swiss National Churches, |

on acoount of the testimony which they have

sin is causing, and will cause upon the earth, so

| Isocrates—We should be on this, as on all 
ether subjects, cautious in stating any thing 
that we do not see clearly spoken in the word of 
God; but having once made ourselves satisfied 
of that, no pretext of caution should make us 
withhold it from the world. 

Anastasius.—This is quite true, and it is im- 
possible too strongly to condemn the spirit which 
prompted Mr. Cecil to withhold much truth that 
he knew from the flock over which the Holy 
Ghost had made him overseer in St. John’s Chapel : 
a system that proves an utter misapprehension of 
that which ought to be the principal point in all 
_ that we say, and in all that we do, namely, a mani- 
festation of the glory of God in Jesus Christ. It 
is an unsound view, which makes the exclusive 
object to be the salvation of sinners, of which the 
preaching of Christ is to be the means ; the great 
object should be the testifying of the glory of 
Christ, of manifesting which glory the salvation 
of sinners will be the means. Ezekiel trembled 
at the commission which was. given him; being

Theophilus—lIt is certainly the duty of laymen 
to testify of the truth of God in places where 
ministers cannot enter. Two laymen were honoured 
by being enabled to stand by our Lord when his 
disciples forsook him, and who took down his 
body from the cross: they seem also to have been 
naturally timid men, for Nicodemus came to our 
Lord by night, and Joseph of Arimathea was not 
in the council at the time he was condemned. 
When nobody would go near the cross of the de- 
spised Nazarene but women and the common 
people, the Lord sent these two timid men to 


75 


make this bold confession of their attachment to 
him who hung there, before Pilate. This was a 
time of difficulty and danger to them, and it is in 
such times that the Lord chiefly makes use of 
laymén; and may all laymen who love Jesus 
Christ remember this. Peculiar boldness is re- 
quited in ministers at this time, particularly in ` 
testifying of the sins of the church, joined with 
mtich tenderness, that they may not be found iti 
the act of smiting their fellow-servants. “< Lift 
up thy voice like a trumpet,” is the motto for 
ministers at the present day. Laymen ought to 
be much in prayer for their ministers. There is 
ho doubt a great deal of secret love going on, and 
a ‘great part of the joy of the saints hereafter will 
eonsist of the sécret love and prayer being re- 
vealed thet, which has been offered for each 
other now. The doctrine of the kingdom will be 
a great means of making men sit loose to the 
world. Modern religious societies have been the 
cause of making ministers think more about money 
than they did formerly; for it is not now so 
much a question concerning him, ‘is he pious, 
or not?” as “what can he give, or collect ;” and 
the test of the piety of a parish, is the amount 
that it sends to the Societies. This has led to 
many clergymen taking pupils, and neglecting their 
flocks, and thus a worldly spirit has crept in 
amongst them; and as the church becomes more 
simple, Christians will cease to imitate mere 
worldly moralists, in doing all things which are 
tiot positively sinful. Laymen may assist much 
in this work by resisting late hours, which are a 


- 76 

great drawback to spirituality; late hours either 
obstruct tamily worship altogether, or cause it to 
take place when the bodies and spirits of the 
worshippers are too fatigued to be so employed 
to any profit, and nothing is given to God but the 
refuse of exhausted nature. It is a great point 
for the true church to come out from all worldly 
conformity and half-measures, and say at once, 
we do not look for Satan to help us. We must 
hold by. the Societiés, although Satan has intro- 
dued great evil into some of them. Zech. viii. 20, 
is strong in their favour. 

` Philalethes.—It is surely an unsound reason for 
withdrawing from Societies, that the managers of 
them conduct business badly. The majority of 
evil doers always accuse the few who stand on the 
Lord’s side with being the troublers of Israel, 
as was the case with Elijah and the Prophets of 
Baal, Micaiah, and many others. 

Aristo.—It is the duty of ministers, when S52 
cieties behave ill, to act as Paul did. at Antioch, 
when all the apostles, and even Barnabas, had gone 
back to Judaism ; he withstood them, and we know 
not with what effect at that time; but afterwards 
he caused the matter to be brought before the 
church collectively. In like manner, we ought to 
protest against the misdeeds of Societies, repeat- 
edly and continually, but never leave them, unless 
they make our adherence to them a compromise 
of duty and conscience. It is impossible to give 
a distinct rule, when caution and when boldness 
should be used respectively; the spirit of a man 
cannot be walled up and fenced by words and 


77 


rules; and we must trust and rely upon the Holy 
Spirit to direct us at each time to say what is most 
fitting for the occasion. 

Anastasius.—The plan which is recommended 
by our brother Aristo, seems to be that which 
was acted upon by Mr. Haldane and Mr. Drum- 
mond, with respect to the Apocryphal question in 
the Bible Society; for it appears from some of 
their publications, that they for several years re- 
monstrated with the Secretaries, and with the. 
Committee in private, before the subject was 
brought before the body of subscribers. 

Philalethes.—You told me, Aristo, that you con- 
sidered the deluge, the destruction of Canaan, the 
captivities of the Jews, and the taking of Jeru- 
salem, as types of the judgments impending over 
Christendom ; but you only explained the tirst of 
these types; will you have the kindness to enter 
into some farther particulars on the second of 
these ? 

Aristo.—I will do so with great pleasure; and 
let me beg you to bear in mind, that these great 
events are not erected into types by me, but, that 
the Holy Ghost hath directly referred to them as 
the types of the last and great destruction, as you 
will see in the case of the deluge, at Luke xvii. 26. ; 
2 Peter ii. 5.; 1 Peter v. 13.; where the Apostle 
calls Rome, Babylon; and Rev. xi. 8.; where 
the city is called Sodom and Egypt; and for the 
destruction of the nations of Canaan being a 
type, we have, if possible, still fuller testimony, 
in every one act which befel the Israelites, during 
the first Exodus, from the Passover to the fall of 


78 


Jericho, being quoted by the apostles for this 
purpese; as Pharaoh, cloud, Red Sea, manna, 
Jordan, &c. &c. therefore we are quite certain, 
that to consider in this light, is according to the 
mind of the Holy Spirit. Abraham, who was to 
possess the land of Canaan, was brought into it to 
be a witness for righteousness. His removal from 
Ur, (though it might be primarily for his own good, 
and the trial of his faith in order that he might 
become the type of the church, to whom it is the 
first commandment to hate and forsake our fa- 
ther’s house, that is, the whole present humanity 
and affections ef man; although this, I say, was 
the first motive in the command which he re- 
ceived, yet it) was intended also as an aet of 
mercy to the land of Canaan, in sending to re- 
side amongst them a man who “walked with 
-God,” as a witness to it for righteousness; in like 
manner as- Enoch and Noah had been witnesses 
in their days before the flood. Let us take notice 
from this example, that if we would be witnesses for 
God we must forsake ourselves; ponder upon this, 
my dear brethren, and consider what a thing it is 
to have God alone always before us in every thing 
that we do, and have no secret reference to any 
plan or purpose of our own; it is a glorious, but it 
‘is a mighty and an awful character, to be a -witness 
for ‘God. _ As Paul could boast of having begotten 
sons through his testimony of the Gospel, 1 Cor. 
iv. 14, 15.; 1 Tim. i. 2.; Tit.i.4.; so‘had Abram 
**gotten souls in Haran,” Gen. xii. 5. “and into 
‘the land of Canaan they eame.” Here he is said 
to have been “confederate,” to have joined himself 


79 


to certain men in the land, no doubt the few who 
were faithful in it, xiv. 13. The Christians who 
have gone out tn these latter days, as witnesses 
against Babylon, have, in like manner, joined 
themselves to, and become confetlerate with, all 
the faithful men they found in it; thus did Mr. 
Haldane and Mr. Drummond at Geneva, the chief 
city of “the God-denying apostacy.” (Bishop of 
Salisbury). God gave to Abram high honour in 
the sight of the people of the land, by delivering the 
nations through his means; and, perhaps, if all his. 
servants possessed the faith and devotedness of 
Abram, God would always give them honour in 
the sight of their enemies. Abraham would have 
none of the spoil, which teaches us how pure all 
servants of the Lord ought to be; especially we 
must avoid deriving gain from our service of God. 
It is not to be denied that England, with all her | 
manifold transgressions, nevertheless possesses a 
greater number of persons professing. the faith of 
Abram, than any other country in Christendom. 
Let us trust that this faith, which has been put 
into our hearts, is the earnest of greater blessings 
in store for this land ; and let us not forget the 
signal deliverances which the Lord has already 
effected for, and by, us during the last 30 years. 
The nations of Babylon have been delivered by 
our hands, as the nations of Sodom and Gomorrah 
were delivered by the hands of Abraham; and to 
make the parallel more complete, we would not take 
the smallest share of the spoil. I do not stop to 
enquire into the wisdom of this, in a political or 
economical view, either in the case of England, or 


80 


in the case of Abram; I only state the fact as an 
important feature in the similitude. Although the 
punishment of the Canaanites must have been in 
God’s eye, when he called Abram, Gen. xii. 7. he 
delayed to inflitt it until their iniquity was full, 
xv. 16. God gave a warning, first in the destruction 
of the cities of the plain, and even to these was Lot 
sent as a witness, so that he would not take, as it 
were, the first ripe grape of the vintage of wrath, 
without having given them a witness, 2 Pet. ii. 8. 
and thus God will always speak before he brings 
down a judgment. ‘‘ Shall there be evil in the 
city, and the Lord hath not done it? surely the 
Lord will do nothing, but he revealeth his secret 
unto his servants the prophets. The lion hath 
roared, who will not fear? the Lord God hath 
spoken, who can but prophecy?” Amos iii. 7, 8. ; 
therefore God will not bring any judgments, with- 
out revealing the same to the children of Abraham, 
his faithful servants. At the same time that 
Abraham got the account of the judgment coming 
on Sodom, he received likewise a more clear and — 
precise annunciation of the birth of Isaac; and we 
- shall find corresponding instances of ‘the divine 
procedure throughout the prophets, in that 
wherever a tiding of judgment is made, a reve- 
lation of a new coming blessing is made also. 
When Abraham heard the tidings against Sodom, 
he forthwith betook himself to intercede for the 
righteous, but for the righteous only, within the 
city, so that it is not only necessary to be on our 
guard against harsh and irascible feelings in 
‘denouncing the judgments of God against sin, buf 


81 


also against the false humanity of natural feelings, 
which would make us confound the wicked with 
the righteous. Our present duty-is to intercede 
for the righteous in Babylon, and to send preachers 
of righteousness into her, to warn them to come 
out of her; and if I were required to express in 
one word the present duty of the church, I would © 
say it was Intercession. Let us likewise be 
comforted in the thought that God does not re- 
fuse us because there is dross in the gold; we do 
not duly appreciate our liberty before God in 
pleading for others, but are too much straitened 
by a sense of sin. Yet we have striking ex- 
amples of the power and efficacy of pleading with 
God; witness Exod. xxxii. 11—14.; Numb. xiv. 
12—20.; James v. 13—16. In these passages we 
have instances of intercession, judgment, and in- 
tercession again, an order which is usually pre- 
served, so that wrath is supported on either side 
by love ; thus love rises, like a tide, into indigna- 
tion and zeal; it is very hot, but sinneth not, and 
settles down again into the low waters of ten- 
derness, 

For the sake of Lot the judgment was delayed 
on Sodom, Gen. xix. 22. The angels were sent 
to Lot, and Abraham entertained them by the way. 
Let us learn, too, from hence, the duties of hospi- 
tality to strangers. ‘Be not forgetful to enter- 
tain strangers, for thereby some have entertained 
angels unawares.” Heb. xiii. 2. and use hospi- 
tality not grudgingly. Abraham accompanied the 
angels, “to bring them on their way ;” and if he 
had not given them this Scotch convoy, he would 

@ 


82 


not have had the intimation of the coming destruc- 
tion on the cities of the plain. Lot’s intercession 
likewise saved. the town of Zoar. God took Abra- 
ham after this and led him about until the iniquity 
of Canaan was full. He saved Abimelech’s house, 
and blessed it for Abraham’s sake, notwithstanding 
Abraham's sin; we may learn hence not to be dis- 
couraged by a sense of sin from praying for others, 
but rather take it as a token for good, that God 
has made us sensible of it; here, too, again, Abra- 
ham had honour in the sight of the king. 

After Abraham’s death, God took Israel into 
Egypt, and Egypt was preserved for the church's 
sake, and by the church, even by Joseph, a type 
of the head of the church, who was cast out by 
his brethren; and from all these examples of the 
servants of the Lord being cast into tribulation, 
we may learn, that if we are to be faithful wit- 
nesses for God, we must lay our account to be 
obliged to forsake all our worldly delights. In 
Egypt, also, did the church get honour in the 
sight of both king and of people, in saving it from 
famine. Afterwards the king oppressed the - 
church and commanded the midwives to slay the 
male children of the people; but they feared God, 
and disobeyed the king; by which the Lord again 
interposed for the salvation of the church. Exodus 
i. 17.20. Laymen may take example from this; 
for it may come to pass, that Satan may prevail 
upon future kings, and future governments here, 
to destroy the church, although we are now in full 
enjoyment of Christian privileges.

83 


Heb. xi. we learn, that Moses was a great man, 
and had attained to an exalted station in the court 
of Pharaoh. He knew that the Lord would de- 
liver his people, and although he had not received 
the commission to undertake it, yet, calculating 
that the 400 years of their predicted captivity, 
Gen. xv. 13., was expired, and thinking, as was 
natural to a learned and powerful man, that he 
could accomplish the deliverance by his own 
might, being also fully persuaded of the promise, | 
he waited not for the directions of God to com- 
mence the undertaking. At first the people re- 
jected Moses as their deliverer, ver. 25.; so at 
his first appearance did they reject Christ, and 
would not suffer him to rule over them; but as 
Moses afterwards brought them out of Egypt, so 
the same Jesus will come again, after having, in 
the mean time, as Moses did, gotten himself 
children from among the Gentiles, to deliver his 
people. The deeper theology, which represents: 
Christ as the sinner, though sinless, is correct; 
at his first coming he was the substitute and 
suffered; but at his second, he will deliver and 
reign. The coming of Moses from Sinai is not a 
type of Christ’s coming from Bethlehem, but of 
his coming in glory, as was also his own transfi- 
guration on Mount Tabor. The coming from Beth- . 
lehem was in too mean a manner to be the anti- 
type of the wonders of Sinai. In the coming of 
Christ in glory, will be fulfilled'the prophecy of a 
. prophet being raised up like unto Moses, and him 
hey shall hear. We fail of attaining to the full 
lory of the Gospel, if we overlook. Christ’s second

coming. For however we love the cross, it is not 
Christ’s revelation complete; there is a glory 
higher than the cross, which will carry: the Chris- 
tian to higher elevation. 

‘When at length, by the command of God, 
Moses did go to Pharaoh, he asked him to let the 
people depart and. serve the Lord; this Pharaoh 
refused to allow them to do, and God began to 
plague him with gradual judgments: nor was his 
final destruction accomplished until after many 
and reiterated warnings. From Exod. xxiii. 20. 
we find that the Lord had long ago written the 
death-warrant of Canaan, and hence all that in- 
tervened between. the giving of the promise to 
Abraham, and the fulfilment of its accomplish- 
ment, was but an instance of the patient waiting 
of God. Even Jericho had a warning, for Rahab 
was not only saved by it herself, but renders her 
testimony that the whole land had heard of God’s 
dealings for his people, and that therefore Jehovah 
assuredly is the God of heaven above, and of the 
earth beneath, Joshua ii. 11. In like manner the 
Moabites had warning by Balaam; affording 
further proof that the Lord proceeds with great 
long-suffering to the judgments, but, at length, 
the cup being full, he assuredly sends them. 
From all these cases, and from many more which 
might be brought forward, we learn to look with 
the eyes of enlightened Christians upon the great 
historical and polifical events which we daily see 
taking place around us; we are taught by the 
Bible, that God’s providences, which we find in 
the world, are only the word of God in act; that 


85 


is, the enacting of that which in the Bible is de- 
clared. In preaching concerning the judgments, 
we ought to receive God’s truth as Abraham did ; 
that is, all combined; the wrath and the promise ; 
then betake ourselves to intercede, as Abraham 
did, and then proceed to declare it with boldness 
and wisdom, with consideration towards those to 
whom we speak. If a minister declare God’s 
truth, however ill he does it, yet he is innocent of 
the blood of the people ; and we may rest assured, 
that for one error which arises from preaching 
too boldly, there are an hundred errors arising 
from preaching too timidly. | 
Philalethes.—Do you think it probable that the 
last great tribulation will be one which will be 
common both upon Jew and Gentile ? 
_ Anastasius.—The persecutions which Satan has 
raised up, whether by heathen powers or by the 
papacy, have had for their object the destruction 
of the spiritual church of Christ: but as this 
church is invisible, his agents have often missed 
their intended objects, and sometimes fallen upon 
wrong. There is a very. beautiful story founded 
upon the heathen persecution of the early Chris- 
tians, entitled Valerius, said to be written by Mr. 
Lockhart, which I recommend for your amuse- 
ment. Since the preaching of the servants of 
Christ among the Waldenses, Lollards, Protest- 
ants, &c. and the national existence of the Jews, 
have been the only things which prevented the 
absolute dominion of the Popes, and therefore 
drew down persecution from them; so the testi- 
mony of Christians to the coming of Christ to 


86 


judgment, and the fulfilment of the promises given 
to the Jews, will be the grand obstacle to the 
universality of the scepticism of these days, and 
will consequently draw down persecution from the 
infidels upon both Jew and Christian. When the 
time was arrived for the Jews to enter into the 
promised land, Caleb and Joshua gave a good 
report of it: the people, however, called out to 
have the men stoned who gave this testimony. 
So now that the time draws near for the Jews to 
enter once more into that same land out of which 
they never shall be - more: ejected, the cry is 
every where raised against those who declare 
this truth. In like manner “it came to pass, when 
Jeremiah had made an end of speaking all that 
the Lord had commanded him to speak unto. all 
the people, that the priests and the prophets, and 
all the people took him, saying, Thou shalt surely 
die. Why hast thou prophesied in the name of 
the Lord, saying—this city shall be desolate 
without an inhabitant? and all the people were 
gathered against Jeremiah in the house of the 
Lord.” Jer. xxvi. 8, 9. And thus those Mini- 
sters who shall be found to warn the church 
boldly and unequivocally of the coming judg- 
ments upon Christendom, will assuredly undergo 
the same persecution that they did, who. pro- 
claimed the impending visitations on Judæa.

CONVERSATION THE THIRD. 


Doctrine of Scripture concerning the present and future 
Condition of the Jews. 


Philalethes.—Iwn our first conversation you ex- 
plained the word ovyrsAna to mean “the simul- 
taneous closing of more things than one;” will 
you have the kindness to mention some of those 
things which you consider simultaneous with the 
fulfilment of the times of the Gentiles ? 

Anastasius.—That which runs principally pa- 
rallel with the times of the Gentiles, we may 
naturally expect to find will be the times of the 
Jews; and it is delightful to find, that the atten- 
tion of the church is now drawing towards the 
ancient people of God. A change is also taking 
place in the sentiments which the Jews entertain 
towards the Christians, and towards the object of 
the Christian hope. This is doubtless the budding 
of the fig-tree, an emblem of the Jewish nation, 
which has assuredly borne no fruit to its Lord 
during the whole of this dispensation, agreeably 
to the curse which was passed upon it, and which 
was to last «c rov awva. Matt. xxi. 19. But let us 
hear Philemon.upon their present and future con- 
dition, for he has studied the Scriptures which 
relate peculiarly to the Jews far more be auously 
than I have. 


88. 


Philemon.—In directing your attention to those 
parts of holy writ, which refer exclusively to the 
_ present and future condition of the Jews, it is 
very important for you to remark the distinction, 
which is therein preserved in speaking of the ten, 
and of the two tribes. This distinction is ob- 
servable as early as in Deut. xxviii. 36—48, 
where Moses foretels the captivity of the ten 
tribes: the passage is strictly applicable to the 
ten tribes going into captivity in Assyria, which 
was their final overthrow, for at that time they 
had a king; but the passage is wholly inappli- 
cable to the destruction of Jerusalem by Titus, 
which was the final overthrow of the two tribes, 
because, amongst other reasons, at that period 
they had no king: and, secondly, it is declared, 
that in their captivity they shall serve gods of 
wood and stone, which the ten tribes did, whereas 
the two tribes have never become idolaters. 

The destruction of the two tribes is described 
from the 49th to 58th verses of the same chapter, 
and again by our Lord in Luke xxi.; and with how 
awful a minuteness it was fulfilled, may be seen by 
consulting the history of the destruction of Jeru- 
salem by Josephus. The same distinction is con- 
tinued in various parts of the prophets, such as 
Ezek. xxxvii. 16. ‘* Moreover, thou son of man, 
take thee one stick, and write upon it, For Judah 
and the children of Israel, his companions: then 
take another stick, and write upon it, For Joseph, 
the stick of Ephraim, and for all the house of 
Israel, his companions.” In Isa. xi. 12, it is said 
that the Lord “ will assemble the outcasts of Israel, 


89 


and the dispersed of Judah, from the four corners of 
the earth,” where the retarn of both the ten tribes 
and the two tribes is spoken of : and in Hos. xiv. 8, 
‘‘ Ephraim’, (the ten) is described as saying, 
‘s What have I to do any more with idols?” from 
whence we may infer, that these will continue © 
idolaters until the time of their restoration; and 
this may serve to indicate to us, that we have 
to seek them among the heathen nations. As this 
distinction, then, is kept up in Scripture through- 
out, it is very necessary to preserve it in our 
own minds during our consideration of the present 
and future condition of the Jewish people. With 
respect to their present condition, it is indeed one 
of awful though righteous severity; every denun- 
ciation which was ever made against them has 
been accomplished, usque ad unguem. I have al- 
ready pointed out to you that in Deut. xxvii. 
See again that in Isa. vi. 9, 10. ‘* Go and tell this 
people, Hear ye, indeed, but understand not; and 
see ye, indeed, but perceive not: make the heart 
of this people fat, and make their ears heavy, and 
shut their eyes; lest they see with their eyes, 
and hear with their ears, and understand with 
their heart, and be healed.” This awful denun- 
ciation was completed by our blessed Lord him- 
self, (Luke viii. 10.) where he tells his disciples, 
“To you it is given to know the mysteries of the 
kingdom of God, but to others in parables, that 
seeing they might not see, and hearing they might 
not understand.” See again Hos. iii. 4. ‘ The 
children of Israel shall abide many days without 
aking, and without a prince, and without a sacri- | 


90 


fice, and without an image, and without an ephod, 
and without teraphim,” remarkably fulfilled in that 
they are the only people on earth, who have not 
the possibility of exercising their own religion. It 
is another remarkable feature in their history, that 
God has constrained them to become the unwil- 
ling witnesses to the truth of his Gospel, as he 
made Balaam of old the unwilling prophet of good 
to ‘his people: and the truth of God is further 
made manifest by the way, in which they are 
maintained as a distinct and peculiar people. 
Papists and Turks have equally endeavoured to 
extirpate them, but in vain; as the Jews whom 
Mr. Wolff met with in Arabia heroically exclaimed 
to him, ‘‘ Oppression cannot bow us, ner tyranny 
shake us,” according as it was predicted by 
Balaam, ‘‘ The people shall dwell alone, and shall 
not be reckoned amongst the nations,” Numb. . 
xxiii. 9; and again by Jer. xxxi. 36, that the seed 
of Israel should not cease from being a nation 
before the Lord for ever. The judgments upon 
the Jews, however, are for a limited period, ` 
namely, only “until the times of the Gentiles are 
fulfilled,” Luke xxi. 24; and this is to be under- 
stood with respect both to their temporal and 
Spiritual adversities. Many passages in holy writ 
cannot have any application but to the lineal de- 
scendants of Abraham, and neither have, nor can 
have, been hitherto fulfilled. One of the most 
striking of these is in Deut. xxx. 1—5, “ And it 
shall come to pass when all these things are come 
upon thee, the blessing and the curse, which I have 
set before thee, and thou shalt call them to mind 


91 


among all the nations whither the Lord thy God 
hath driven thee; and shalt return unto the Lord 
thy God, and obey his voice, according to all that 
I command thee this day, thou and thy children 
with all thine heart and with all thy soul: that 
then the Lord thy God will turn thy captivity, and 
have compassion upon thee, and will return and 
gather thee from all nations whither the Lord thy 
God hath scattered thee. If any of thine be driven 
out into the outermost parts of heaven, from 
thence will the Lord thy God gather thee, and 
from thence will he fetch thee: and the Lord thy 
God will bring thee into the land which thy fathers 
possessed, and thou shall possess it; and he will 
do thee good, and multiply thee above thy fa- 
thers:” the concluding verses of the preceding 
chapter bring their then future history down to 
the dispersion by the Romans, and therefore the 
passage in question must relate to their future ` 
temporal prosperity. In Ezek. xxxvi. also, both 
temporal and spiritual blessings are promised, the 
former from ver. 1. to 24. the latter from 24. to 
the end. Itis often said to be a matter of little 
importance whether the Jews will be restored to 
their own land or not, a sentiment against which 
we cannot too strongly protest; for since the 
truth of God is pledged in the matter, the fulfil- 
ment of the prophecy is of infinite importance to 
every individual believer. The dealings of God 
with his ancient people are precisely similar to 
his dealings with his spiritual church; and if God 
did not restore his backsliding people, it would 
seem,. that his promises might fail with respeot 


92 


to his backsliding church, and hence the want of 
fulfilment would shake the rock, upon which our 
hope and faith are built. It is to be observed, that 
the prophecies of their conversion, and of spiritual 
blessings, are carefully distinguished from those, 
which we have been just considering, which relate 
only to their temporal advantage. These spiritual 
blessings are spoken of in Deut. xxx. 6. and Ezek. 
xxxvi. 24. tothe end. Four rules have been laid 
down to enable us to distinguish those prophecies, 
which are still clearly unfulfilled. Ist, When 
Israel and Judah are both separately mentioned, 
as Jer. xxxi. 31. and Ezek. xxxvii. 16. 2dly, 
When permanent blessings are promised, as in 
Ezek, xxxix. 25—29. 3dly, When these blessings 
are spoken of as enjoyed under king David, Ezek. 
xxxvii. 24; Hos. iii. 5. 4thly, When they are 
connected with the conversion. of the world, 
Psalm cii. 13—15. 

Philalethes.—In what manner do you sup- 
pose that their restoration will be brought 
about ? 

Philemon.—It appears very clearly from Isaiah 
xviii., (which I recommend you strongly to study, 
with Bishop Horsley’s comment on that passage,) 
as well as from xlix. 22, that their temporal resto- 
ration will be brought about by providential cir- 
cumstances, and mainly through the instrumen- 
tality of a maritime nation. But with respect to 
their national conversion, it seems as if that would 
take place by the personal interposition and ap- 
pearance of Messiah their King, in proof of which, 
I would refer you to Isa. lix. 20. and Rom. xi. 26. 


93 


It is a great mistake of the Christian church to 
imagine, that the whole nation is under blindness, 
whereas there has always been, and is now, a 
remnant according to the election of grace; for 
only some of the branches are said to be broken 
from the olive-tree, ver. 17, and blindness is 
said to have happened only in part unto Israel, 
Rom. xi. 25, and this is to last: no longer, as 
I observed before, than until the time of the 
Gentiles be fulfilled. It was the opinion of M ede, 
that the conversion of St. Paul is a type of 
the manner of the conversion of Israel, (see B. V. 
chap. 11.) This opinion receives considerable 
support from the language of the apostle himself, 
in speaking of that event to his favourite proselyte 
Timothy, 1 Ep. i. 16. It is a true saying, and 
worthy of being universally received, that Jesus 
Christ came upon the earth to save sinners, of 
whom I am the first; but on this account I re- 
ceived mercy, that in me first, Jesus Christ might 
indicate all long-suffering for the type of those 
about to believe on him to eternal life. 
Philalethes.—Stop one minute till I ask Anas- 
tasius to explain to me the justness of this transla- 
tion. ; 
Anastasius. I see nothing in the translation 
given by Philemon, essentially different from that of 
our English authorized version, although he makes 
it somewhat more forcible. As I mentioned to you 
before, I would never think of establishing any 
truth by a mere verbal criticism; an error which - 
Holloway, and some of the most ingenious of the 
Hutchinsonian writers have committed; but if we 


94 
can render the meaning of the original passage 
more clear by such criticisms, it is certainly wise 
so to make use of them, and not to be deterred 
by the anathemas of the timid or the indolent; 
for whatever on this, or on any other subject, 
compels men to think, that is to labour, will 
always arouse their hostility. On this account, a 
work entitled Paleeoromaica, which appeared three 
years back, was most vehemently attacked: it 
abounded, however, with excellent verbal criti- 
cisms, although the hypothesis of the writer was 
untenable to the extent, to which he endeavoured 
to carry it. I suppose the word which chiefly 
staggers you in the translation is ‘‘ type,” for 
‘pattern ;” yet, in fact, they are very much the 
same: the original is irorvrwow, and if you consult 
your Scapula, he will tell you, that the meaning 
of the verb trorvrdw, is to inform, delineate, sub- 
ject to the eyes, represent, inform of the appear- 
ance of a thing. But as I will not build much 
upon a verbal criticism, neither will I build much 
more on the ipse dixit of a Lexicographer, however 
learned, as I fully admit the force of Payne 
Knight’s remark to Price, that “ a dictionary is 
the remembrancer of the scholar, but the oracle of 
the dunce.” |

Anastasius.— Philalethes will admit these paral- 
lels drawn by Mede to be very striking, and they 
are highly necessary to be impressed upon the 
church at this time, which is running clean con- 
trary to them all. If the world would see with its 
eyes, and hear with its ears, a very striking testi- 
mony is now afforded to it every day by the con- 
duct of those Jews, who do become converted. 
Let us suppose, that a man knows no more of reli- 
gion than that there are two sets of opinions pre- 
valent in the Christian church; the one founded 
upon the doctrine of human desert, as the ground 
of reconciliation with God, and that this opinion 
is supported by all papists, and by all that party 
in England, which calls itself the High Church | 
party; the other opinion, founded upon the me- 
rits of Christ, as the sole ground of acceptance 
with God, and of final salvation, which opinion 
is supported by all the Reformers, and by all that 
party in England which is called Evangelical: the 
question then is, which of these two sets of opi- 


97 


nions do the Jews embrace when they become 
Christians ? 

Philalethes.—Invariably the latter; but may 
not that arise from the latter party being the only 
one, which busies itself in the conversion of the 
Jews? | 

Anastasius.—If this were the reason, still the 
very fact on which the ‘reason is founded, is a 
farther proof of the soundness and influential 
character of the latter opinions: but I doubt 
whether the most enlightened converts have been 
produced through any such instrumentality; as 
for example, Lacunza, the Spanish Jew, whose 
valuable work Mr. Irving has lately translated, 
and Wolff. What do you say, Philemon, to the 
timezof the conversion of the two, and of the ten 
tribes, between whose destinies you have so 
properly desired us to observe the distinction ? 

Philemon.—We are told in Zech. xii. 7. ‘* that 
the Lord will save the tents of Judah first,” and 
it appears farther, from the concluding verses of 
the same chapter, as if their restoration would 
precede their conversion. In Ezek. xxxvi. 24, 25., 
also it is said, “ I will take you from among the 
heathen, and gather you out of all countries, 
and will bring you into your own land; THEN 
will I sprinkle clean water upon you,” &c. &c. It 
is farther declared, that the place of the Lord's 
appearance to them shall be Mount Olivet; “ Be- 
hold the day of the Lord cometh—and his feet 
shall stand in that day upon the Mount of Olives,” 
Zech. xiv. 4.; the same mount from which he 
finally quitted the earth after his resurrection, 

H 


98 


Acts i. 12.; and it is promised in verse 11, that 
he shall return to the earth in like manner as he 
quitted it. We learn from Ez. xxxviii. 18—23. 
that “ it shall come to pass at the same time when 
Gog shall come against the land of Israel, saith the 
Lord God, and my fury shall come up in my face 
—and I will plead against him with pestilence, and 
with blood; and J will rain upon him and upon 
his bands, and upon the many people that are 
with him, an overflowing rain, and great hailstones, 
Jire, and brimstone; thus will I magnify myself, 
and sanctify myself, and J will be known in the 
eyes of many nations; and they shall know that I 
am the Lord.” The angel tells Daniel, speaking 
of the wilful King, xi. 44, that “he shall go 
forth with great fury to destroy and utterly to 
make away many—yet he shall come to his end, 
and none shall help him, and at that time shall 
Michael stand up, the Great Prince which stand- 
eth for the children of thy people, and there shall 
be a time of trouble such as never was since there 
was a nation,” &c. xii. 1. See also Zech. xiii. 8, 9., 
xiv.; fromall the above passages it appears, that the 
immediate object of the appearance of Messiah as 
King, will be to deliver the Jews, when in their own 
land, from some confederated host of enemies as- 
sembled to drive them out of it; perhaps excited by 
avarice and by jealousy of the riches of the peo- 
ple which they shall. have taken back with them, 
having spoiled the Gentiles as they did the Egyp- 
tians of old, ver. 14. The *“ time of trouble” is 
clearly that of the Jewish people, and such as never 
was since the world began, or shall be to them 


99 


again. In this awful extremity, when there ap- 
pears no hope, but that Christ should come to 
punish them, will he manifest himself, not to 
punish, but to deliver, as he did of old when they 
were pent in by the army of Pharaoh behind, and 
by the Red Sea before them. This will be the 
dignus vindice nodus. It seems also from Isaiah 
lxvi. 19, 20., that those Jews who escape this time 
of trouble, will be sent out as Missionaries to the 
ten tribes, and also to the heathen, ‘I will send 
those that escape of them unto the nations—that 
have not heard my fame, neither have seen my 
glory, and they shall declare my glory among the 
Gentiles; and they shall bring all your brethren 
for an offering unto the Lord out of all nations—to 
my holy mountain, Jerusalem, saith the Lord— 
&c. ;” the consequence of which will be, that the 
heathen will be converted and brought to worship 
the Lord at Jerusalem. Zech. xiv. 16.; 1. 10 
—13. : 
Philalethes.—But how does this agree . with 
the judgments, which you, Anastasius, said in 
our first conversation, and which was confirmed 
by our brethren, were about to come on the 
world ? 

Anastasius.—You will observe in the passage 
from Mede, quoted by Philemon, that under his 
6th head, that accurate interpreter of Scripture 
limits the application of the word nations, in the 
time of the apostles, to those nations which were 
within orbis Romanus ; and abundant proof may 
- be adduced to show, that the word world is gene- 
rally synonimous with, and limited to, the Roman 

H 2 


100 


empire. Thus we are told, that a decree went 
out from Cæsar Augustus, that all the world 
should be taxed, Luke ii. 1., whereas it is‘ quite 
clear, that the decree could only include the in- 
habitants of the Roman empire. The British Isles 
are always called the extremity of the world by 
Latin poets and geographers: ultimos orbis Bri- 
fannos ; penitus toto divisos orbe Britannos, &c. &c. 
Besides, the geography of the Bible extends no 
farther than.the Roman empire. In Daniel's vision 
he saw the four great universal monarchies, which 
were to oppress the people of God, and the last 
is indisputably the Roman, beyond the bounds 
of which, therefore, we have no authority to tra- 
vel. The Bible divides all history with reference 
to the Jews into five great empires; the Assy- 
rian, Babylonian, Medo-Persian, Grecian, and 
Roman; and God has been pleased to limit the 
extent of our learning from heathen sources to the 
knowledge of the several dynasties, and revolu- 
tions, and manners of these people: whilst con- 
cerning the more extensive and populous nations 
of Asia and Africa, we possess no information 
whatever. This has been doubtless so ordered, 
that we may perceive the truth of the predictions 
of his holy Word, even out of the mouths 
of its enemies: and hence we may see the true 
use of such learning, and how valueless it is 
when pursued as an end, or merely as a mean 
of cultivating intellectual tastes and sentiments. 
The Apocalypse of St. John is limited to the 
Roman Empire, nor is there a trace of any country 
beyond it. Itis upon this Roman empire, now 


101 


‘called Christendom, or at farthest upon Christen- 


‘dom and Turkey together, the two legs of Daniel’s 


fourth monarchy, that we expect the judgments to 
come ; and therefore, even supposing that a deluge 
of fire were to overwhelm Christendom, as it did 
the cities of the plain of old, still the heathen 
nations, which are vastly more numerous and po- 


‘pulous than those of Christendom, would afford a 


large sphere for the missionary labours of the re- 
stored Jews, who will be sent into the field of the 


world at the eleventh hour, having stood idle all 


the rest of the day, because no man hath hired 
them. 
Philemon.—The time subsequent to the restora- 
tion is also the period when those predictions shall 
be fulfilled, which promise the Jewish church her 
precedence over an evangelized world. You will 


-do well to study Bishop Horsley’s sermons on the 


xlvth Psalm with reference to this subject, a writer 
who cannot be too much read. In Micah iv. 8. it 
is written, ‘“ And thou, O tower of the flock, the 


strong hold of the daughter of’ Zion, unto thee 


shall it come, even the first dominion,” &c.; and 
in Isa. ii. 2, “ It shall come to pass in the last 


days, that the mountain of the Lord’s house shall 


be established in the top of the mountains, and 


shall be exalted above the hills; and all nations 


shall flow into it; and many people shall go and 
say, Come ye, let us go up to the mountain of the 
Lord, to the house of the God of Jacob; and he 
will teach us of his ways, and we will walk in his 
paths; for out of Zion shall go forth the law, and 


‘the word of the Lord from Jerusalem.” This will . 


102 


be the period for which the whole creation groans 
with longing; and then shall the earth be full of 
the knowledge of the Lord, as the waters cover 
the sea. 

Philalethes—How often I have heard that pas- 
sage quoted at meetings of the Bible and. Tract 
Societies, as if the blessedness spoken of was to 
be brought about before the Jews were restored 
to their own land! | 

Anastasius —That you certainly have; but do, 
I beseech you, allow the Prophet Isaiah to know 
the time, about which he himself says he is writ- 
ing, and turn to chap. xi. where this 50 often mis- 
quoted passage occurs. You will there find that 
at the time when this prediction is to be fulfilled, 
“in that day it shall come to pass, that the Lord 
shall set his hand again the second time to recover 
the. remnant of his people,” &c. &c. ver 11.: but 
the persons who misquote this text in their 
speeches, virtually say, that this is not to take 
place in that day, but that the knowledge of the 
Lord is to cover the earth before that day, and 
that after the earth is so covered, then the Jews 
are to be recovered the second time. Neverthe- 
less, let you and I trust the word of God, and 
cease from all commentators, who would explain 
away the plain and obvious meaning of the sacred 
volume.

. Anastasius.—As the Jewish nation, throughout 
its history, from the calling of Abraham down to its 
final possession of the promised land under Mes- 
siah the King, is typical of the invisible church, 
it is often difficult to distinguish, when the pro- 
phets are speaking of one or other exclusively. 
A promise was given to Abraham of a blessing, 
which should come upon his posterity; and the 
Apostle explains this to the Galatians to mean 
not his children after the flesh, but his children 
after the promise; or in other words, those who 
possess the faith of Abraham. Thus, whilst the 
Jews were united in one nation, the church was 
united in one congregation ; now that the Jewish | 
nation is scattered and outcast, the true believers 
are scattered, and outcast, up and down all Chris- 
tendom. The time of the restoration of the Jewish 
people to their land will also be the time when the 
bride, the Lamb’s wife, will be betrothed to her 
Lord; and these two events are consequently 
frequently blended together. If the church, the 
bride, is caught up and saved out of great tribula- 
tion, the Jewish nation is also spoken of as re- 
stored during a time of great trouble: and this 
last great trouble is likened to that, which befel 
them in Egypt and in Babylon, of which places 
they are constantly reminded in the promises, 
which assure them of their second restoration. In 
Isa. lii. the church is called upon to put on her 
“beautiful garments,” the glorified bodies with 
which true believers are to be clothed upon, (2 Cor. 
v. 2.) for that the time is arrived, when nothing 
that is uncircumcised and uaclean, nothing that

loveth and maketh a lie, like Satan, Rev. xxi. 15, 
shall any more enter into the holy city: she is

syrian oppressed them without cause; now,

phemed.” In ver. 9. it is said, that “the Lord
