= Basilicus.—The restoration of the Jews is a 
point, which till of late has been very generally 
overlooked, and this for want of considering the ` 
history and the mystery of the literal Israel, 
although it is distinctly considered in this light by 
Moses in the last chapter of Deuteronomy; by 


= David in many Psalms, particularly cvii. and by 


St. Paul in his Epistles to the Romans, Corin- 
thians, and Hebrews. The present condition of the 
Jews is, of all the three dispersions, the most hu- 
miliating ; and the future condition will, according 
to Scripture, far exceed all the preceding in glory, 

and issue in that theocracy, of which former dis- 

pensations are only typical, in the person of Him 
who is the prophet like unto Moses; the king 
like unto Solomon; and a priest after the order 
of Melchizedeck, that is, a priest on a throne, 
sitting, and ruling over the spiritual Israel, of 
which the literal, being only a figure for the time, 
had a priest, who was not a king, and kings, who 
did not pertain unto the priesthood. But he who 
was counted worthy of more glory than Moses, 

who is greater than Solomon, and who is the High 
Priest of our (Christian) profession, will then 
unite the prophetical, royal, and sacerdotal cha- 

racter and office in one, The pretence of effecting 


106 


which combination, constitutes the very essence of 
that threefold Anti-christ, the Dragon, the Beast, 
and the False Prophet, which is to be destroyed - 
at the brightness of his coming. 

Philalethes.—1n what particulars, then, do you 
suppose, that the future restoration of the Jews 
will be like their coming up from Egypt ? 

Anastasius.——One very striking similitude is that, 
which has been already hinted at by Philemon, in 
the passage which he quoted from Mede, namely, 
that as Paul heard the voice and saw the appear- 
ance of our Lord, whilst none of the bystanders 
did, so the Jews will see an appearance of our 
Lord for their conversion, which no other people — 
will perceive. This is exactly what took place 
on the coming up out of Egypt. ‘The Lord went 
before them by day in a pillar of cloud, to lead 
them in the way, and by night in a pillar of fire, to 
give them light, to go by day and night: he took 
not away the pillar of the cloud by day, nor the 
pillar of fire by night, from before the people.” 
Exod. xiii. 21, 22. ‘* And the angel of God which 
went before the camp of Israel removed and went 
behind them, and the pillar of the cloud went from © 
before their face and stood behind them; and it 
came between the camp of the Egyptians and the 
camp of Israel; and it was a cloud and darkness 
to them, but it gave light by night żo these; so 
that the one came not near the other all the night.” 
Exod. xiv. 19. It seems, therefore, that as the 
appearance of our blessed Lord, at the first 
coming out of Egypt, was to the people of Israel 
alone, and that the Egyptians perceived him not, 


107 


so he will appear again to deliver them in a way 
which they will perceive, but which will not be 
visible to the nations, who obstruct their return. 
This view seems to be further strengthened by 
remembering, that our Lord’s first coming in the 
flesh was to the Jewish nation alone; he was not 
sent but to the lost sheep of the house of Israel, 
nor did he direct his apostles to go amongst the 
Gentiles, until the Jews had repeatedly refused 
the offer of his salvation. Thus his second com- 
ing in the flesh will be to them alone, and for 
the purpose of leading them in person into their 
own land. It is very striking to observe the 
various particulars, in which it is promised, that

Hartley has three striking Corollaries to his 
Eighty-third Proposition relating to the restoration 
of the Jews: 1. “ May not the two captivities of 
the Jews, and their two restorations, be types of 
the first and second death, and of the first and 
second resurrections? 2. Does it not appear 
agreeable to the whole analogy of the word and 
works of God, that the Jews are types both of 
each individual in particular, on the one hand, 
and of the whole world in general, on the other? 
&c. &c. 3. As the downfall of the Jewish state, 
under Titus, was the occasion of the publication 
of the Gospel to us Gentiles, so our downfall may 
contribute to the restoration of the Jews, and 
both together bring on the final publication and 
prevalence of the true religion. Thus the type, 
and the thing typified, will coincide; the first 
fruits and the lump, be made holy together. 
Vol. ii. p. 375.

I 


114 
mon has shown to be in the Scriptures of God, 
is from the opinions prevalent respecting the Jews, 
‘even amongst the supportors of the Societies 
formed exclusively for their benefit. In the first 
chapter of Zechariah, there is a vision, which was 
given to the prophet, in the second year of the 
reign of Darius Hystaspes, which contains an epi- 
tome of all the Lord’s dealings with his people, 
from their first punishment under the Assyrians to 
their final restoration to his favour. The vision 
begins at the 7th verse, and is continued with its 
explanation down to the end-of the second chap- 
ter; it was given during the rebuilding of the 
temple. The prophet saw by night “a man 
_ riding upon a red horse,” called afterwards, ‘the 
angel of the Lord,” verse 12.; the same person 
whom Joshua saw as ‘‘the Captain of the Host’ 
of the Lord,” ver. 13. Christ the avenger is here 
typified, the red colour of his horse bespeaking 
him as the same who cometh with dyed garments 
from Bozrah, and blood upon his apparel: the 
“ red horses speckled and white,” like the descrip- 
tion of himself in Cant. v. 10., ‘‘ruddy and white,” 
mark the tempering of judgment with mercy. 
s Myrtle trees” are every where used as an em- 
blem of the church, in the midst of which our 
blessed Lord is represented here standing, as he 
is amongst the lampstands in Rev. 1. 13. The 
red horses are described as ministering spirits 
“sent to walk to and fro upon the earth.” They 
report, that “the earth sitteth still, and is at rest :” 
this deceitful repose, in which the world will be 
caught by the last great and sudden destruction, 


115 
is spoken of in other places as immediately pre- 
ceding the last great earthquake, and as the time 
of ease enjoyed by a woman, directly before she 
is seized with the pangs of labour. In ver. 12. 
Messiah is represented as interceding for his an- 
cient people. The prophet then sees the four 
Gentile tyrannies, which have oppressed the Jews, 
and which were figured to Nebuchadnezzar as 
four metals composing one colossal image, and to 
Daniel as four savage beasts, under the symbol of 
four horns: afterwards he sees, under the symbol 
of four carpenters, the ministers of God’s vengeance 
on these tyrannies. By the 4th and Sth verses of | 
chapler ii., we have the date of the accomplish- 
ment of the prophecy fixed for the time when 
Jerusalem will be inhabited again, and ‘‘the Lord 
will be the glory in the midst of her.” In ver. 6, 
the four winds represent the last judgments, being 
the same emblem as they are represented by in 
Rev. vii. 1., and xvi. 17.; and the concluding 
_ verses again bind the period of fulfilment to a time 
subsequent to the manifestation of Messiah in 
glory in Jerusalem. Although the last great de- 
struction on the Gentiles is represented under the 
figure of an earthquake, yet we must remember, 
that earthquakes are made up of several repeated 
- shocks, with pauses between. The French revo- 
lution was a great and sudden shock, which shook 
the whole frame-work of kingly authority to its 
very centre. The shock on the financial power of 
England, during the winter of 1825, was another. 
Yet the nation sees not the hand of the Lord in 
these things, any more than Pharaoh did of old.

Josephus.—I once asked an old Rabbi in the 
East to describe to me the present: state of: his 
brethren; he replied, “we can only use the lan- 
guage of Abraham, where he said, A horror of 
great darkness fell upon me.” The root of all 
their calamities now is that which has ever been, 
“ going about to establish their own righteousness, 
and not submitting themselves to the righteous- 
ness of God ;” and with all their Talmudical know- 
ledge, ‘‘ they are ever learning, and never able to 
come to a knowledge of the truth.” There has 
always been a remnant amongst them “ according 
to the election of grace.” About one hundred ~ 
years ago 300 Jewish families were converted by 
reading the Gospel. The most interesting sight 
I ever witnessed, was that of 100 Jews in Persia 
calling out, ‘‘ what shall we do to be saved 2” and 
there is a large number of Jews at this moment, in

Theophilus:—The key chapter to the whole = 
ject of the present dispensation, as far as it is 
connected with the Jews, is Deut xxxii. From 
the 21st verse we learn, that this is a dispensation 
of jealousy. By receiving a Gentile church, we 
are told by the apostle who cites this passage, 
that God would provoke them to jealousy, and — 
we see, that his divine procedure of offering salva- 
tion to the Gentiles, has ever been peculiarly irri- 
tating to them. In the Acts, and Rom. xi., we 
find, that whenever Paul met with much success 
amongst the Gentiles, the Jews cried out, “away 
with such a fellow from the earth.” Before Paul 
could turn to the Gentiles, he was obliged to turn 
away from the Jews; he could not contemplate 
the pleasure of saving the souls of the heathen 
without being most painfully reminded, that he 
was, as it were, abstracting so much from his own 
nation. But we, on the contrary, have the glorious 
satisfaction of knowing, that to preach to the Jews, 
it is not necessary for us to turn from the heathen : 
for that, on the contrary, the heathen will never be 
effectually blessed, until the Jews become their 
missionaries, having been first converted. Al- 
though this dispensation is one of jealousy, the 
next will be one of unity and love, where all will 
be one in Christ Jesus. There are three signs of 
approaching blessings coming shortly to the Jews. 
Ist, The Lord hath put it into the hearts of his 
faithful people to favour the Jews; a proof of the 
time appointed for that to be done having arrived ; 


118 


Psalm cii. 13. 2dly, The standard is lifted up 
for them, as predicted in Isaiah lxii. 10—12., and 
xi. 10, 11, in which former passage, you will ob- 
serve the expression, ‘‘unto the end of the 
world,” and remember Mede’s explanation of it. 
And 3dly, The Jews are beginning to confess 
their iniquity, which, when they do in the midst of 
their dispersion, God has promised to restore 
them; Lev. xxvi. 40—42. The principal duty 
of the ruling powers in England now, is neither to 
embrace the false Babylonish church, nor throw 
stumbling blocks in the way of God’s ancient 
people. 

Aristo.—The Jews are the only people in the 
world who have received a grant of lands and 
country from God himself; their restoration to it 
is certain, for it is their inheritance, and they have 
a right to take possession of it. Gen. xv. 18. 
and 7. This chapter contains a type of their dis- 
pensation, from ver. 9. to the end. Their sacri- 
fices, and their privileges, upon which the fowls 
of the air descended, (the symbol for evil spirits 
made use of by our Lord in the parable of the 
sower,) and which they wished to defile, driven 
away by faith; the sun setting at length upon 
them, and a consequent horror of great darkness 
falling upon the people ; the fiery trials they were 
to pass through, and the lamp, the fire of persecu- 
tion ; still the covenant and promises remaining, 
and an assurance of future possession given ; all 
are contained in this remarkable passage. At 
ver. 18. ‘“ the Lord made a covenant with Abraham, 
saying, unto thy seed have I given this land, from 


119 


the river of Egypt unto the great river, thc river 
Euphrates ;” and in order to ensure their posses- 
sion of it, it is.said in Isaiah xxvii. 12. “it shall 
come to pass in that day, that the Lord shall beat 
off from the channel of the river unto the stream of 
Egypt, and ye shall be gathered one by one, O ye 
children of Israel ;” that is, ye shall go back into 
your land, as people usually return at evening to 
their own houses: and in Amos ix. 14, 15, it is 
said, ‘I will bring again the captivity of my peo- 
ple Israel, and I will plant them upon their land, 
and they shall no more be pulled up out of their 
land which I have given them, saith the Lord 
God.” Notwithstanding their dispersion, the Jews 
are still a people in covenant with God. The for- 
mer covenant is broken, but they have the pro- 
mise of a new one, which shall never be broken. 
This is clearly expressed by many figures, parti- 
cularly by that of a married wife, put away indeed, 
but not divorced, and afterwards received again 
into favour. For to the Jewish church, “ Thus 

saith the Lord, where is the bill of your mother’s © 
divorcement, whom I have put away?” Isaiah 
l. 1. ‘Fear not; for thou shalt not be ashamed, 
neither be confounded: for thou shalt not be put 
to shame; for thou shalt forget the shame of thy 
youth, and shalt not remember the reproach of thy 
widowhood any more: for thy Maker is thy hus- 
band, the Lord of Hosts is his name, and thy re- 
deemer the Holy One of Israel; the God of the 
whole earth shall he be called. For the Lord 
hath called thee as a woman forsaken and grieved 
in spirit, and a wife of youth, when thou wast 


120 


refused, saith thy God.” Isa. liv. 4—6. ‘ Thou 
shalt no more be termed forsaken, neither shall 
thy land any more be termed desolate—for the 
Lord delighteth in thee, and thy land shall 
be married.” Isa. lxii. 4. As Abraham is the 
father and type of each believer, and of the 
. spiritual church, so Sarah, as his wife, is a 
type in another sense of the true church 
also. As soon as Messiah is revealed in the 
Psalms to David as king, a queen is revealed 
likewise. Sarah did not bring forth the child of 
joy, until the seed was preserved in another; and 
so the Jewish people is not brought forth; that 
birth which is to be the means of ushering in 
joy to all lands, until a spiritual seed has been 
gathered out of the Gentiles ; thus, whilst Sarah’s 
barrenness represents the depressed. state of the 
Jews, Isaac's birth represents their restoration. 
The Song of Solomon is alone applicable to the 
Jewish church, for she alone is the sister and 
spouse of Jesus. of Nazareth, Her deliverance 
from her widowed state is beautifully expressed 
by ‘‘ rise up, my love, my fair one, and come 


away; for lo the winter is past, the rain is over 


and gone; the flowers appear on the earth, the 
time of the singing birds is come, and the voice 
of the turtle is heard in our land; the fig-tree 
putteth forth her green figs, and the vines with 
the tender grape give a good smell,” ii. 11. Surely 
the Jewish fig-tree is now budding, and the 
harvest is near, and the Sun of Righteousness 
is about to arise. It is she “ that comes up out 
of the wilderness leaning on her beloved.” viii. 5. 


121 


It is she alone of all visible churches of whom 
it shall ever be said, “Thou art all fair my love, 
there is no spot in thee.” iv. 7. 

Philalethes.— But surely we are justified by the 
Apostle’s expressions in Eph. v. 27, to apply this 
passage to every believer. l 

Anastasius.— Undoubtedly we are, but by giving 
a spiritual interpretation to a passage, we are not 
justified in denying its literal meaning; because 
the Apostle tells us that the story of Hagar and 
Ishmael is an allegory, we are not justified in 
denying that there was really such a place as 
Arabia, or such individuals as these; and it is 
worthy of remark, that the same persons who 
object to see any literal meaning in the pro- 
phecies, usually also object to see any spiritual 
meaning in the histories; and in both which they 
surely are to be condemned, | 

Aristo.—This arises from poor and partial views, 
which will be mended by more extensive infor- 
mation in the Scriptures, of which there is a 
grievous lack just now amongst professors of re- 
ligion. Some sturdy disputants still maintain that 
the promises of the restoration of the Jews to 
their own land, only means taking the elect to 
heaven ; but leaving these, let me refer you to the 
beautiful passage in Cant. iii. 11. where the Jewish 
church is represented as putting the crown of her 
own kingdom on the head of her king. The 
present condition of the Jews is clearly set forth 
in Hos. ii. 2. where God declares she is repu- 
diated, and at ii. 4. she is said to “ abide,” or 


122 


wait for her king in that widowed state many 
days. But all this time it is promised, that 
“ that which cometh into your mind shall not be 
at all, that ye say, we will be as the heathen, as 
the families of the countries, to serve wood and 
stone,” Ezek. xx. 32., and accordingly she never 
has’wed herself to any other God. Observe, too, 
what is said about the wilderness. Before the 
Jews of old were put into possession of the pro- 
mised land, the Lord led them into the wilder- 
ness; and it is promised, in this same chapter 
of Ezekiel, that the Lord will do this again, v. 35. 
“I will bring you into the wilderness of the 
people, and there will I plead with you face to 
face,” and in Hos. ii. 14. ‘I will allure her and 
bring her into the wilderness, and speak comfort- 
ably unto her; and I will give her her vineyards 
from thence, and the valley of Achor for a door 
of hope; and she shall sing there as in the days 
of her youth, and as in the days when she came 
up out of the land of Egypt.” 
Philalethes.—What.is the meaning of giving the 
` valley of Achor for a door of hope ? 
Anastasius.—Near.the valley of Achor stood 


ancient Jericho, the first town within the promised — 


land which the people took. This town fell at 
the sound of the seventh trumpet. Near it also 
were the famous vineyards of Engedi (Cant. i. 14.); 
it was the first land on which the Israelites en- 
camped after having passed through Jordan, and 


it was here where Achan and the Babylonish ~ 


garment, and all that he had was burned. The

place, therefore, was a striking earnest of their

of permanent favour, which is also expressed in

and the glory of the Gentiles like a flowing

Philalethes.—What promises are there respect- 
ing the pouring out of the Spirit upon the 
Jews ? 

Aristo.—The outpouring of the Spirit upon the 
Jews is generally typified under the figure of ‘‘ the 
latter rain.” This is said, in Jer. mi. 1—3., to 
have been withholden from Judah on account of 
her sins, who is commanded, in Zech. x. 1., to ask 
for it again. The former rain came upon the Jews 
alone at Pentecost, by which the church has been 
_ nourished, and has continued to grow ever. since ; 
we Gentiles are but, as it were, the first-fruits from 
the former rain; but when the latter rain will 


. come, then will come the fulness of the harvest of 


the world. If there be any great ingathering from 
among the Gentiles before the vintage, it will be 
through the Jews, and therefore the hope of the 
church is from showing mercy to the Jews. Micah 
vii. It seems, however, that before this is brought 
about, Elias will first come. Then ‘the mighty 
God,” which is the God of war, will be manifested; 
and afterwards “ the Prince of peace.” As David 
and Solomon, in their several histories, are made 
to afford a language in which to express Christ, 
the mighty warrior and the Prince of peace, and 
as the captivities in Egypt and Babylon furnish a 
language for the expression of the great deliverance 
of the church from the world, so John the Baptist 
came in order to furnish a language by which to 
express the coming again of Elias. Isaiah xl. al- 


125 


though it is applied to John, has an ultimate ap- 
plication to Elias. A prophet was obliged, during 
some time of his office, to predict that which 
would be accomplished immediately, as well as 
that which was to have a later fulfilment, other- 
wise the people would have had no means of judg- 
ing of a true or of a false prophet, according to the 
rule given by Moses, Deut. xviii. 22. Probably 
the lamp of faith in the church shall have so nearly 
waned out, that Elias will come to preserve the 
little salt which will be left. The expression of 
our Lord, in Matt. xi. 14., “ if ye can receive it,” 
&c. I take to mean, if ye can understand the aye 
tery of this matter. 

_ Philalethes.—I was much interested in the ex-

and with Christian candour, ask whether you

= Crito.—Indeed I do, completely ; and I think, 
moreover, that the four carpenters will very 
likely, from Daniel x. 20., be spiritual agents, 
appointed to instigate one power against the 
other. With respect to “the earth sitting still 
and being at rest,” this is precisely its present 
case: the prophetical date for it is between 
the sixth and seventh vial, which I take to 
be between the thirty and forty-five yenta of 
Daniel.

Philalethes—What will the Jews be called 
after they are converted? will they become Chris- 
tians, or remain Jews ? 

Isocrates.—They who are converted in this dis- 
pensation, have necessarily been called Christians, 
although it does not appear that they have gene- 
rally amalgamated themselves very cordially with 
any Christian sect. But they who will be con- 
verted as a nation at the coming of Messiah, will, 
doubtless, preserve that distinction of name which 
they had before. 
= Philalethes—Do you also, Aristo, agree with 
our brother Anastasius in the importance of the 
Jews and Continental Societies ? 

Aristo.—Most assuredly I do: and if I have 
any hope derived from Scripture, that England 
will fare better than the other toes of the image 
when the stone begins to smite them, as indeed it 
` seems already to have begun to do, it is on account 
of her being made the witness to the Jews and to 
Babylon. But I trust, as it is said in Isa. lx. 12. 
‘* the nation and kingdom that will not serve thee 
shall perish,” that England, or at least Englishmen, 
are serving the Jews, and doing them good service 
00. Taking it for granted that Bishop Horsley’s, 
correction of our version of Isaiah xviii. is right, 
observe the connection between the first verse of 
this chapter, and the twelfth verse of the pre- 
ceding; in the one it is, ‘“ woe to the multitude of 
many people—the nations—which God shall re- 
buke,” &c. whilst it is, “‘ Ho to the protecting 
land” which is called upon to bring the scattered 


127 


people as a present to Jehovah of Hosts on Mount 
Zion. Perhaps Messiah will not lead the 
whole political nation of the Jews into the 
land, but only his elect remnant out of them, 
after he has pleaded with them in the wilder- 
ness. 
Philalethes.—Is not the history of Job con- 
sidered to contain the history of the Jews ? 
Aristo.— Both the Books of Job and Esther 
seem to be histories of individuals, the circum- 
stances of whose life are made to shadow forth 
the history of the Jewish nation. The wealth, 
~ prosperity, and favour, shewn to him by God at 
the commencement of Job’s life; his subsequent 
desertion by God; the crown taken from his 
head; the taunts of pretended friends; his 
complaint and self-justifying spirit; the mes- 
senger who at last tells him of the Lord 
having found a ransom for him; his consequent 
recognition of God’s holiness, which he could not 
attain to, and of his own sinfulness as compared 
to it; his restoration to God’s favour, and enjoy- 
ment of far greater prosperity than he had at first; 
are so obviously typical of the history of the 
Jewish people, that they have been generally 
acknowledged. The history of Esther is not 
always so considered, but it nevertheless admits 
of an equally clear application. 
Anastasius.—However the modern custom of 
not considering the histories of individuals as 
typical may be defended, it will be difficult to 
oppose the concurrent testimonies of the first 


128 

ages of the church; and it is clear that the Jews 
themselves think the history of Esther something 
more than an amusing tale, for they keep the feast 
of Purim in commemoration of it to thisday. A 
very good explanation of the typical character of 
Job has lately appeared inthe Jewish Expositor 
for 1826. |

CONVERSATION THE FOURTH. 


Practical Duties of the Church, arising from the present and 
future Condition of the Jews. 


Philalethes—What do you. consider to be the 
duty of Christians with reference to the Jews ? 

Anastasius.—I think the first duty is that 
pointed out in Joel ii. 17, “ Let the priests, the 
ministers of the Lord, weep between the porch 
and the altar, and let them say, Spare thy people, 
O Lord, and give not thine heritage to reproach, 
that the heathen should rule over them ; wherefore 
should they say among the people, where is their 
God ; then will the Lord be jealous for his land, 
‘and pity his people:” the promise of hearing and 
answering this prayer is continued through se- 
veral verses. Like all Christian works, that of 
prayer is fallen into sad neglect in these days, 
even amongst spiritual people; yet the efficacy of 
itis not only repeatedly insisted on in Scripture, 
but the universal testimony of those who have 
made use of it confirm the same truth. With 
respect to public duties we shall perceive wherein 
these lie, by calling to mind the public duty 
which the Jewish church had to perform, and 
for refusing to do which she was ‘cast off from 
God's favour. God chose the family of Abraham 

K 


130 
from out of all the families of the earth, in order 
that it might be the depository of his great and 


glorious name of Jehovah; and farther, that it 
might be the witness to the truth of his 


‘< Oracles,” in contradistinction to the lying - ” 


Oracles of the priests of the groves, whether of 
Baal-peor or Delphi. This duty the Jews did 
perform for a long season; at length God had a 
new name to record, even Jah Osea, Jesus, God 
with us, God in our nature, the incarnate God; 
this name they rejected, and refused to bear wit- 
ness to. God therefore was compelled to choose 
a church out of the Gentiles, in which to record 
his new name, and which might bear witness to 
the truth of his revealed word, in which that name 
is written, in contradistinction to all pretended. 
revelations, such as the Apocrypha of the Papists, 
the Koran of the. Mohammedans, the Shasters of 
the Hindoos, the Sacred Books of the Persians, 
Chinese, &c. Hence we see the great sin of the | 
Committee of the Bible Society, in tampering — 
with the Word of Inspiration; and hence we see 
that the great public duty of every Christian, is to 
bear record to the true, and to testify against pre- 
tended revelation. 

Philemon.—It is of the utmost consequence that 
all missionaries, and other persons who preach to 
the. Jews, should correctly understand the things 
which they have a right to expect from Scripture. 
The prophets foretold a suffering and glorified 
Messiah; the apostles preached the. sacrifice of 
Jesus, and the splendour of his second coming ; 
but it 1s become the fashion amongst moderns to 


131 


confine their statements to the former, and with- 
hold the latter character of Messiah altogether. 
Mr. Faber and Mr. M‘Neile have placed the sub- 
ject on a more correct footing by their sermons 
preached before the Jews’ Society; and it is ne- 
cessary to set before the Jewish nation a crucified 
Saviour for them now, and a glorious King who is 
to come to them hereafter. The direction given 
by the Prophet Isaiah is very clear, lxii. 11: ‘‘ Say 
ye to the daughter of Zion, behold thy salvation 
cometh; behold his reward is with him, and his 
work before him.” Christianity must be pre- 
sented before the Jews in all its loveliness, for 
they have been accustomed to view it only in 
papists and persecutors. 

Anastasius. —When Wolff first began to search 
after Christ, he made inquiries of some Swiss 
Protestants concerning him: they, however, gave 
him such an account as convinced him they were 
wrong, and he applied to the excellent and pious 
Count Stollberg, who was a Papist: the Count 
gave him good instruction, but told him that the 
errors he had heard concerning Christ were the 
doctrines of the Protestants. This was true, as 
far as it relates to Geneva, and the other Pro- 
testant, Swiss, and German Universities, and 
marks the necessity of real Christians exerting 
themselves with increased energy, since the Pro- 
testants abroad are more likely to repel conver- 
sions to Christianity than to encourage them by 
their theological opinions. 

Philemon.—It is also very important to use the 
doctrine of the past and present condition of the 

K 2 


132 


J ews as a warning to the present Christian church. 
“She enjoys every external privilege that the Jewish 
_church had, at the time of her rejection. Christ’s 
coming in humiliation was then declared, and she 
disbelieved it. Christ’s coming in glory to set 
up the throne of David is now declared, and the 
Christian church disbelieves that. Both churches 
were equal in privileges, and therefore both are 
equal in guilt; and both will be alike partakers of 
judgment. The Jewish church exhibits, in a very 
striking manner, the long-suffering of God, and is 
brought forward as an illustration of it in Rom. x. 
21. She proves likewise the wonderful foreknow- 
ledge of God, together with the infinite wisdom of 
God in over-ruling all for good ; she shews from her 
fall the evil of unbelief, and the danger of losing 
our privileges; and from her restoration, the 
loving-kindness of God: for the whole universe 
-will be benefited by the display of all God's dis- 
pensations, first in the Jews, secondly in the Gen- 
tiles, and thirdly in the Jews again. Our dear 
brother Theophilus has turned his attention much 
to the best mode of preaching to the Jews, and 
perhaps he will tell us what he considers the 
most effectual method of awakening their at- 
tention. : 
Theophilus.—I have always considered that, as 
our Lord commanded the apostles to preach the 
Gospel to the Jews first, beginning at Jerusalem, 
so it was the duty of a follower of Jesus Christ to 
make the preaching to the Jews the subject of his 
especial regard. However bad we may find the 
present state of, the Jews, it cannot be worse 


133 


than the scriptural representation of it. As, how- 
ever, it is not easy to gain access to them in any 
considerable numbers, it is only by making use of 
missionaries expressly for them that we can ap- 
proach them; on which account it appears to be 
of the first importance to promote the success of 
the Jews’ Society: In preaching upon those parts 
of the revealed will of God that are contained in 
the prophetic Scriptures, we shall be constantly 
accused of a tendency to act in some manner at 
variance with the plain declarations of Scripture, 
which command us to call on sinners to repent 
and believe the Gospel. It is needless to prove 
the falsehood or the folly of the accusation, but 
it is well to bear in mind that such charges will be 
made, and to take care that we do not in our mini- 
stry afford any pretext to justify them. Of all 
converts to the Gospel, Jews are the most likely 
to fall back after having made a profession, and 
on this account they should be treated with the 
utmost tenderness. 

Basilicus.—The practical relation of the present 
state of the Jews and the church, and therefore to 
individual believers, is the hope that notwithstand- 
ing the darkness of their natural condition, their 
bondage and captivity under Satan, and their per- 
verseness, backsliding, and impenitence under 
their present privileges, and trials, God will in his 
mercy finally deliver those who are Israelites in- 
deed out of all their troubles: and the practical 
duty in the mean time is most assuredly to ad- 
vance the temporal and spiritual improvement of 
those who are enemies for their sakes, and repre- 


134 


sentatives of their own conduct, and character, and 
expectation, under all the vicissitudes of the 
Christian life. 

Philalethes.—Do you think, Crito, that there is 
any direct indication in Scripture of the exertions 
now making for the Jews? 7 
| Crito. —As there is no reason whatever for 
doubting that the various Bible Societies are re- 
presented by the angel of the everlasting Gospel 
in Rev. xiv. 6, and that before the year in which 
it was established “the hour of God’s judgment 
was come; so the Jews’ Society, which first pro- 
claimed the recovery of God’s ancient people, 
and their establishment on the downfall of Baby- 
lon, is as evidently announced by another angel in 
ver. 8. The third angel that follows, and warns - 
the world that “ if any man worship the beast and 
his image, and receive his mark in his forehead, 
or in his hand, the same shall drink of the wine of 
the wrath of God,” &c. &c. indicates, in an equally 
clear manner, the office and duty of the Conti- 
nental Society. It is remarkable, however, that — 
where this same subject 1s resumed in the xviiith 
chapter, the two last angels are again mentioned 
in nearly similar terms, whilst. the first angel is 
omitted altogether. I trust this is not indicative 
of the worldly spirit which has been manifested by 
the former Society being about to produce a with- 
drawing of God’s favour from it, and consequently 
its downfall. It is, however, further remarkable, 
that the same class of persons which have supported 
the Jews’ Society, have been also the warmest 
friends of the Continental Society, and vice versd. 


135 


Anastasius.—I can mention some additional 
' striking proofs of this: for instance, both So- 
cieties had for seven years the same President; 
many of the agents of the one have been at dif- 
ferent times the agents of the other: one of the 
Secretaries of the Continental Society was after- 
wards editor of the Jewish Expositor; at its 
earliest formation, when it was apprehended that 
it might become the means of giving a more cor- 
rect view of the state of religion on the Continent 
of Europe than the public were in the habit of 
receiving from other quarters, and when it con- 
sequently had to encounter an opposition which 
strongly proved its spiritual character, and when 
its name was cast out as evil by many with 
large phylacteries in our modern Sanhedrims, the 
Jews’ Society fearlessly and freely allowed it the 
use of its Committee room. These two Societies 
are unquestionably the great duties of the Chris- 
tian church at the present time; but it is lament- 
able to reflect how much fashion, rather than duty, 
is followed even in religion; and more than one 
clergyman has avowed to me that he felt the scrip- 
tural importance of the Continental Society, but 
was afraid to recommend it from his pulpit, or 
allow others to do so, because it was not the 
fashion amongst his brethren. | 

Thales.—It is highly necessary that something 
should be done to sift and try how much there is 
of gold amidst all the present extensive mass of 
religious profession; and I much fear that our 
friend Anastasius has furnished us with a fearfully 
accurate test. We may all take warning from 


136 

the present state of the Jews in this matter, which 
is that of their table having become a snare and 
a trap, mistaking their shadows for substance, 
whilst their hearts continue fat, their ears heavy, 
and their eyes dim. God in his infinite mercy, in 
his dear Son, grant that this may not be the case 

with a large portion of the professing world! and 
assuredly there is ground for alarm, when expe- 
diency is contended for against principle, when 
the protection of infidels is preferred in religious 
works to the efforts of the poor and despised 
brethren of Jesus Christ, and when such large 
sums are raised readily for Societies so conducted, 
whilst the more spiritual ones can scarcely carry 
on their work. 

| Anastasius.—It is certainly very awful to think, 
that, in the whole of the Apocryphal controversy, — 
many persons preferred to corrupt the word of 
God in order to please Papists and Neologists, 
although, by so doing, besides the other evils 
arising therefrom, an additional stumbling-block 
was thrown in the way of God’s ancient people. 
I have known some instances where persons who 
have been asked to speak at Bible Societies have 
been requested to abstain from making any allu- 
sion to the distinguishing excellencies of the 
Christian religion, for fear of offending the com- 
pany assembled. 

Philalethes.—But do you not think that the va- 
rious religious societies have been instrumental in 
increasing the spread of true religion, ane of bro- 
therly love ? 

Anastasius.—I have no doubt but that some 


137 


persons who have attended these Societies, in 
ignorance of all the essentials of religion, have 
heard things which have been made useful to them 
afterwards, and that so far the number of profes- 
sors of religion has increased. But I am sure that 
they have been also the means of keeping back 
many who would otherwise have made greater 
advances both in knowledge of the mysteries of 
godliness, and in all holy conformity to the: com- 
mands and example of our blessed Lord. With 
respect to the increase of brotherly love, it ap- 
pears that they have tended to repress the folly of 
sectarian jealousy ; but they have not tended to 
make their admirers prefer the despised and 
unlettered followers of Jesus to wealthy, titled, 
and learned men of the world. But let us, who 
see the errors into which others have fallen, pray 
to God that we, who seem to ourselves to stand, 
may not fall into the like, and that He will shew 
to them the evil of their ways, and give to us all 
power, with meekness and firmness, to. confess 
Jesus Christ before men, that at his second 
coming he may confess us before = heavenly 
Father. 

Eugenio.—I cordially join in that prayer, but 
must always insist, not only in protesting against 
the conduct alluded to, but, in my own particular 
circumstances, in acting directly at variance with 
it. I feel, as a layman, that I have no opportunity 
of testifying of the grace which God has conferred 
upon me, but at those meetings which I am in- 
vited to attend; and therefore, though I obey the 
summons to serve the Societies most willingly, 


138 


I wish, by God’s grace, to remember before all 
things that I am a servant of the Lord Jesus 
Christ. | 

Polydorus.—I am delighted with the sentiments 
of Eugenio; they remind me of a wise saying of 
Pascal’s, that ‘‘he is the most original character 
who will act up to the maxims in which we all are 
agreed.” Such a personal testimony being given 
by many Christians before the Jews, would lead 
them soon to see the difference between those 
who were really deserving of the name, and those 
-papists and persecutors who only take that name 
in vain. It seems very extraordinary that, in our 
preaching to the Jews, it should ever have become 
a question, whether or not we should speak to 
them of Messiah the Prince, as well as of Mes- 
siah the Paschal Lamb. | 

Crito.—As the great national conversion of 
them is said to take place upon the manifestation 
to them of Christ in his glory, so we may rest 
assured, that conversion of individuals of that 
nation is more likely to be produced by the 
‘preaching of the glory of Christ, than by any 
other means. 

Philalethes—You mentioned, Anastasius, that 
it was our duty to pray for the restoration of the 
Jews, and you founded that duty upon a passage 
in Joel: have you any other authority from Scrip- 
ture fur similar purposes ? 

Anastasius.—It is advisable to call upon the 
Jews also to set about praying for their own: re- 
turn, and to make use of such portions of Scrip- 
ture as the 3d and 5th chapters of Lamentations of 


139 


Jeremiah for the purpose: and to exhort them 
likewise to pray for an outpouring of the Spirit 
upon their nation, and to accomplish the blessings 
which Jehovah has promised to send upon them. 
We should also set ourselves to find out what laws 
there are which affect them, and endeavour to pro- 
cure the remission of any evils under which they 
Jabour. It is to be lamented, that the Jews’ So- 
ciety is precluded by its constitution from afford- 
ing any aid to the temporal distresses of that na- 
tion, and it is therefore the more gratifying to 
find that another Society, under the name of the 
Philo-Judzan Society has been lately established, 
from which such objects will not be excluded. 
Nor is there more danger of being imposed upon 
by false professors amongst the Jews, than by 
any other class of false professors of religion, if 
we pursue a very obvious line in the administra- 
tion of temporal relief: to the converts, which is 
never to give it, in any case, except where the 
man’s trade was necessarily injured by his becom- 
ing a Christian. There is great danger in being 
found persecuting the Jews, and one of the causes 
of the judgments on the Gentile nations, is their 
having oppressed that nation, as it is said in 
Obad. 10. 15. “ for thy violence against thy brother 
Jacob, shame shall cover thee, and thou shall be cut 
off for ever; but thou shouldest not have looked 
on the day of thy brother, in the day that he be- 
came a stranger; neither shouldest thou have 
spoken proudly in the day of distress—as thou 
hast done it shall be done to thee, thy reward shall 
return upon thine own head.” In Isa. li. 17—23.

the prophet, addressing the Jews, says, “ Awake,

The duties incuinbent upon Christians, directing 
us to preach the coming kingdom to the Jews, 
are clearly pointed out in Isaiah lii. 7, <“ How 
beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him 
that bringeth good tidings, that publisheth peace;

thee,” cxxii. 6. It might be well also, as tracts 
and elementary books of instruction are published 
for other persons, to publish some exclusively 
adapted to the Jews, explanatory of the geography, 
customs, &c. of their own Old Testament Scrip- 
tures; for it is remarkable how literally the curse 
in Isaiah iii. has been fulfilled upon them, and 
none of them have ever risen to eminence in any 
of the sciences or arts, which distinguish all other 
nations. 
| Thales.—It is on this account that I should 
speak to a Jew more as to a fallen child of Adam, 
than as toa man well versed in these rites and 
ceremonies, the meaning of which are so clearly 
pointed out in Paul’s epistle tothe Hebrews. There 
are, however, points which might be pressed upon 


142 


the conscience and judgment of a Jew, which 
would have no application to a Gentile: for in- 
stance, we might direct their attention to the pro- 
mise of God to give them the land of Canaan for 
ever; to their having once possessed it, and to 
their ultimate rejection from jit. These facts they 
could not deny. We might then lead them to 
Eden, and observe that the casting out of Canaan 
seems very similar to the casting out of Eden, and 
if so, appeal to them if it is not probable that the 
two rejections arose from one and the same cause, 
namely, sin. Now, for the sin which caused the re- 
jection from Eden, we might shew that there was a 
remedy provided, namely, the seed of the woman, 
and that therefore it is likely that the same remedy 
would serve for the rejection from Canaan. This 
might farther be illustrated by the remedy which 
was provided for the bites of serpents in the Brazen 
Serpent; and by the object and meaning of sacri- 
fices ; all pointing to the blood of the seed of the 
woman; and to substitution, the innocent for the 
guilty, that the guilty might go free; especially so 
in the case of the scape goat. Their own law 
might be pressed upon them, as Paul urges it in 
his second chapter to the Romans; where the 
apostle, having begun by exposing the sins of the 
Gentiles, proceeds until he has extended his state-. 
ment so as to include the Jews also, and bring in the 
whole world guilty before God. The law might 
then be shown to be utterly insufficient to give 
peace to the conscience, and hence the necessity 
of something else, if peace be ever to be attained 
in this world. 


143 


Whilst condemning the Jews, however, let us 
look to ourselves, that we do not rest in the letter 
of the Gospel, as they do in the letter of the law; 
“the letter killeth,” whether it be the letter of 
one dispensation or of another. To-day, it was 
said to them, to-day if ye will hear his voice, 
harden not your hearts; and now amongst us also, 
there is such a thing as hardness of heart, and 
contempt of God’s word and commandment: as a 
road becomes harder the more it is driven over by 
a carriage, so does the heart become more insen- 
sible by the constant rolling over it of the chariot 
of Christ, where the affections are not carried 
along with its wheels. Corruptio optimi pessima. 
Let us take heed that we do not make the ordi- 
nances of religion a God. Making prayer a task 
spoils it; the service of God is perfect freedom. 
But let not the Christian man fear: his last end 
shall be glorious; dust he may become, but ashes 
never: the word “ ashes,” ought not to be used at 
the burial of a believer in the Lord Jesus Christ, 
for he has nothing to do with fire, which denotes 
wrath and hell. The devil has got hold of this 
world, and therefore he reduces us to dust, but 
not to ashes, for he has no power to take us into 
hell-fire. Into Eden again the Christian will 
return; for Eden that was, is but a type or alle- 
gory of. that which is to come.

He seems to be the very embodying of the

in the sermon preached before the London

they are better read in them than a more intimate

Anastasius.—On this account it would be desir-

Old Testament as the ground-work of their ser- 
mons, and make their discourses rather running 
comments upon one whole prophet, or book, at 
once, than essays upon single doetrines. With 
respect to the journals of Wolff, they ought to — 
be all printed exactly as he sent them over; for 
the wonderful dealings of God with this man were 
performed neither for his own private benefit, 
nor to furnish idle gossip, but for the instruction 
and edification of the whole church of Christ, the 
knowledge -of which therefore ought not to be 
withheld from it. The chief scene of the opera- 
tions of Wolff, or of any other missionary to the

-which he to the east of Jerusalem. As soon as 
Wolff arrived at that city, he received directions

Philalethes.—What are the civil disabilities un-

Anastasius.—The Jews, by the laws of England, 
are held to be aliens, and consequently only enjoy 
the rights granted to foreigners at peace with this 
country. ‘‘ Jews are in England by an implied 
license ;” (this license, however, is only supposed 
to exist, for it is no where to be found on the 
Statute book ;) “ but on a proclamation of banish- 
-ment, they are in the same state as alien enemies, 
on a determination of letters of safe conduct,”

Tomlyn’ s Law Dic: in loco. The plain English of 
which is, that they may be banished by the meré 
caprice of the king at any moment. This arises 
from their having ‘been exiled in the nineteenth 
year of the reign of Edward I., in- which state they 
Continued until the time of Cromwell, when they 
were recalled ; but as many of the acts of Crom- 
‘well were disallowed by Charles II. after his 
restoration, the Jews are here only by sufferance, 
which sufferance may be withdrawn at any mo- 
‘ment. A Jewess, wife of a converted Jew, cannot 

- claim dower after his death. 1 Inst.31 b. There 
` are seventeen societies in London established for 
the temporal relief of the Jews amongst each other; 
‘but the total amount of their revenue is very 
‘small, and the poorest members of that commu- 
‘nity are in greater misery than the poor of any 
‘other denomination. As a class, they obtain their 
livelihood with greater difficulty than any other 
people. Saturday is the day on which the majo- 
rity of the labouring population buy the articles 
of their weekly consumption, but on this day the 
Jews’ shops are most rigidly closed. Only a 
limited number are allowed to be brokers by 
many corporations of cities and towns; nor are 
they allowed to take up their freedom, even when 
inclined to do so, unless they can prove that they 
are descended from parents who have been bap- 
‘tized ; a condition which amounts to an absolute 
‘prohibition. A small volume has lately been

. Philalethes.—Surely it is of very great im-

should be warned of their danger in being found

Anastasius -—No doubt it is, arid it was from 
seeing this clearly that Mr. Lewis Way became, 
and still continues, anxious that the. church of 
England should take up their cause. He set the 
noble example himself, and was willing to re- 
nounce his whole property, and to make it over 
to the church of England, in order that it might 
be applied to the promoting of Christianity, and in 
any other mode benefiting that nation. This offer 
was refused; but let England, however, remember 
that she is one of the ten toes of that image which 
_ the stone smites and crumbles into powder, fine as 
the dust of the summer threshing floors, Dan, 
xi. 35.; let her remember that it is no despised 
. reader of the will of God who says this of her, 
but that Machiavel, and Gibbon, writers with 
whom her statesmen are much better acquainted 
than they are with either Daniel or St. John, 
include her in the list of the kingdoms into which 
the various broken members of the Roman Empire 
settled after its destruction by the barbarian na- 
tions ; of which Roman Empire consequently she

once formed an integral part. It is not only ‘in-

Jewish people. He undertook a journey into 
Prussia, Russia, the Crimea, Poland, and, subse- 
quently, into Palestine, on their account. Heat- 
tended also the Congress of Sovereigns and States< 
men at Aix-la-Chapelle, in order that he might 
bring under the consideration of those great men, 
the interests of the only people in Europe who 
were beneath their notice, namely, the chosen na-

destiny of this nation, that the duration of their 
own thrones and dynasties hangs. Mr. Way was 
successful ; and the spirit of God came upon those 
Sovereigns, (1 Sam. xi. 76.) when they heard the

stirred up (Ezra i. 1. Isa. x. 6—15.) to acknow-

warning to the Gentile nations, throughout the 
Old Testament, is the restoration of the Jews ; and 
unless England can shew that her conduct towards 
them has been more lenient than that of other 
people, she has no right from Scripture to con- 
clude that.a milder doom awaits her than betides 
the rest. | .

Anastasius —I would gladly think this, yet I

tis to reason correctly upon the probabilities of the

in the past. The Ninevites are the only people

God’s prophets. The Antediluvians were un- 
mindful of the practical preaching of Noah during 
one hundred years in which he was employed — 
in building the ark, though they no doubt ridi- 
culed him as a madman and enthusiast, wasting 
his property in constructing that wondertul vessel, 
which ultimately proved him to be the only man 
amongst them who was in his right mind. Read 
an excellent Sermon of Mr. M‘Neile’s upon this 
subject. The Egyptians despised the warnings of 
Moses: the Canaanites refused to submit to God, 
though they perceived and acknowledged that he 
was working miracles in the midst of the children 
‘of Israel, so that it was their interest and duty to 
enter into covenant with and obey him. The Jews | 
rejected the warnings of the prophets before the 
Assyrian and Babylonian conquests: and in pro- 
portion as the time for the execution of the judg- 
ment.drew near, so much the more did the people 
rage against those who gave them warning, smit- 
ing and imprisoning Jeremiah. Neither would 
they hear the voice of the Son of God himself 
foretelling the razing of their temple, and their 
own dispersion. The Papists have despised the 
testimony of the Reformers; and as the brute ass 
. rebuked the madness of the false prophet of old, 
so even infidels have rebuked the false prophet of 
Rome for his idolatry and lying miracles; and 
the popish kingdoms have not repented for the

preaching of the Reformers. More lately God 
has made use of the sword; as he did by Sen- 
nacherib of old, so has he by the sword of France 
sent a victorious army into the heart of every 
capital in Europe, yea, even into the seat of the 
Beast, Rome itself, preserving Protestant Eng- 
land alone; yet have the Papists not repented 
to give God glory. It is worthy of remark, too, 
from Sir J. Malcolm’s History of the Rise of 
British Power in the East Indies, if he is to be cre- 
dited, how invariably it has been the policy and de- 
sire both of the Company and of the Government to 
remain at peace, and that they have been in every 
instance driven to war with the native powers 
against their wishes and interest. Thus God has 
made use of the unwilling sword of England to pu- 
nish the followers of Mahomet, Brahma, and Bhudd. 
Saints are in the cabinet, of the King of kings. 


_ Armies and generals execute His orders blindly: 


philosophers search into the properties of matter: 
poets sing the beauties of His works: historians 
narrate the actions of which they cannot trace the 
motives; but the Christian, by his Bible, enters 
into the cabinet, and sees that it is the Lord 
of Hosts, the King of Glory, who ruleth 
amongst the children of men, setting up one 
‘kingdom and pulling down another, and dis- 
„posing of crowns and sceptres severally as he 
will: yet England has not acknowledged the 
mercies of her God, nor made use of the warn- 
ings she has received, as she would have done 
if she had felt them. | |

Anastasius.—Two I think distinctly ; Ist, The

a memorable instance. We must be careful, more- 
over, not to suppose that there is no infidelity 
amongst the higher orders, because it assumes 
amongst them a less offensive character. The 
appetites and sensibilities of St. Giles’s are of a 
coarser texture than those of St. James’s. The 
rank meat, and rough clothing, that can be di- 
gested and worn by the one, would be perfectly 
intolerable to the other, which requires more de- 
licate ragouts and finer linen. In like manner, 
the vulgar ribaldry which is adapted to the 
„mental appetite of the one, must be superseded 
in the ‘other by the’ elegant sneers of such 
‘writers as the author of Waverly. This mas- 
` ter-spirit has succeeded more effectually than 
Paine or Voltaire, in bringing true religion into 
disrepute amongst the higher circles. He has 
imagined characters with qualities, the most 
odious and disgusting, and called them religious. 
He has evidently associated them with the spiri- 


152 


‘tual religion of the present times, and then di- 
rected against them with exquisite point the shafts 
of his keenest wit, and most bitter sarcasms; as 

‘Dr. Chalmers has justly observed in his sermons 

‘preached in St. John’s Church, p. 76. The ha- 

tred, however, to the essential peculiarities of 

‘the religion of Jesus of Nazareth, is not less ap- . 

‘parent in one class of writings than in the other. 
- “I have frequently heard the flippant remark of the 
profligate Charles II., alluded to in conversation, 
that ‘‘puritanism was not a religion for gentle- 
men;” and generally with such additions, as 
“shocking creatures the Methodists! vulgarity 
‘seems an essential part of their constitution !” and 
nothing can be more soothing than this to the 
self-complacency of nominal Christians. It ap- 
peared, at an examination before the Lord 
Mayor, that on Tuesday evening, January 23, 
1827, at a public meeting held statedly in 
Salters’ Hall Chapel, Cannon Street, Taylor, the 
President of a Society, called ‘the Society of 
Universal Benevolence, or Christian Evidence 
Society,’ after a speech in which he deprecated 
the Bible as a book of lies and fables, held up in 
his hand the New Testament, and then, in the pre- 
sence of the assembly, consisting of 300 persons, 
denounced it as a book of falsehood and priest- 
craft, contemptuously threw it on the platform on 
which he was standing, and placing his foot upon 
it, exclaimed, ‘If any man in this assembly can 
prove the contrary, let him come forward and 
take it up.’ The silence that followed for a few 
seconds was succeeded by a burst of ‘applause, 


«153 
‘three times repeated by the assembly, to evince 
‘their approbation at the courage and intrepidity of 
the orator. Few Christians are aware of what is 
‘going on; and we are sometimes told that it is 
injudicious to apprize them of it, as it will only 
excite doubts, which-might otherwise in all pro- 
bability never have arisen. Whatever justice 
there may be in this as respects school-children,

his work, unless, like the bee, he can alight unhurt

honey out of poison, and rendering the conduct 
and writings of infidels, as well as the providences

of observation upon the signs of the times. The

American Jew, Mr. Levy, jealous for the honour

Philalethes.—But a Jew will never believe in 
_ the Trinity ? 
Anastasius.—Every man, whether Jew or Gen- 


154 


-tile, when converted by the Holy Spirit of God, 
must believe in the Trinity. But do not, my dear 
brother, mistake an assent to a creed, or to a 
term, for a practical belief in the offices, or persons 
of Jehovah; nor deny to another a saving faith in 
this essential branch of divine truth, because a 
. tender conscience and a logical mind may see flaws 
in every attempt at definition that has hitherto 
„been made. The facts revealed in Scripture are, 
that the Father is God, that the Son is God, and 
that the Holy Ghost is God; but yet that there is 
but one God. A vain attempt to define the mode 
of subsistence of the Deity is the rock upon which 
all have split; some calling themselves orthodox, 
establishing tritheism ; others, in their zeal for the 
unity of the Godhead, inclining to deny the divi- 
nity of the Lord Jesus Christ, or the personality 
of the Holy Ghost. In preaching to the Jews, 
Jet us confine ourselves rigidly to such terms only 
as we find in the Holy Scriptures, and beware 
of teaching for doctrines of God the inventions 
of man. 
= Philalethes.—Whence would you date the justi- 
fication of a Jew when converted ? — 
.. Anastasius.—Ah, my dear Philalethes, you are 
inclined to enter into intricate branches of deeper 
theology than I have now time to discuss; but I 
will shortly tell you that I can see no difference in 
the question, whether relating to Jew or to Gentile. 
There are three justifications spoken of in Scrip- 
ture; lst, Justification in the Lord ; 2d, Justifica- 
tion by faith; 3d, J ustification by works, These 
three must all be reconciled, and to the world 


155 


they seem contradictory. There are also three 
places, or courts, as it were, in which a sinner 
requires to be justified: Ist, The court of God’s 
holiness; 2d, The court of his own conscience ; 
3d, The court of the world, or before men. These 
harmonize with the three justifications above men- 
tioned, The first may be dated as in Christ from 
before the foundation of the world; the second at 
the time when the sinner receives it into his heart, 
and. believes it; and the third, when it is exhi- 
bited before men. — 

Philalethes.—In what, then, do you make faith 
to consist ? 

Anastasius —The word “faith,” in all lan- 
guages of which I know any thing, denotes two 
distinct operations of the mind; Ist, belief of a 
fact related; 2d, reliance upon, or trust to the 
fact so related. In religion, faith is, first, belief 
in the facts related in the Bible, viz. that we are 
guilty before God; will be cast into hell, unless 
cleansed by the blood of Christ, and renewed by 
the Spirit of God; that the blood of Christ 
cleanseth from all sin; that God will freely par- 
don every one who trusts to that blood; and that 
he will give his Holy Spirit to every one who 
prays to him for it. Second, Faith is reliance 
upon that blood for cleansing, and upon the Holy 
Spirit for renewing, that is, giving new affections 
to the heart. The first alone, without the second, 
is the faith of devils and of nominal Christians: 
the two combined, constitute the faith of real 
Christians: and therefore, ‘Faith is the con- 
fidence of things hoped for, the conviction of 


156 


things not seen,” Heb. xi, 1. The Letters of 
Sandiman on Hervey’s Theron and a are 
very good upon this subject. 

Philalethes.—This seems to throw some light 
upon the multum vexata questio of progressive 
sanctification, 

Anastasius.—Messieurs definissez vos termes, 
said Voltaire to the Doctors of the Sorbonne. 
If by sanctification be meant that essential purity 
of nature, which makes the sinner meet for the 
présence of a holy God, that can only be found 
in his union with Christ, in whom alone is all his 
“ sanctification,’ 1 Cor. i. 30, as well as ‘‘ righ- 
teousness and redemption.” If by progressive 
sanctification be meant, the progressive effects of 
that purity, constraining its possessor to devote 
every faculty of his mind and affections to the 
service of Jesus Christ, and giving him success 
in so doing, there is no doubt that every soul in 
which the spirit of God dwells, does make this 
progress; but to push this doctrine to the reductio 
-ad absurdum, by saying, that such a view only | 
requires time for the sinner to become perfect 
whilst in his present state, is as erroneous as to 
suppose that whilst bound to the present huma- 
nity, influenced by decaying material bodies, 
and connected by the ties of kindred, hunger, 
thirst, cold, and heat, to this sphere, he might 
become immortal. The decree of God is not 
to make light -agree with darkness; to make a 
man who loves sin find the presence of God a 
source of happiness to him; but the decree goes 
‘forth to change the disposition of the sinner, so 


157 


that he may dislike sin, and love the society of. 
Jesus, and of his saints. The happiness to be 
derived from heaven, must consist in loving the 
things which God loves; for the devil might stand 
in the presence of God, but would find it no source 
of pleasure to him. | 

Philalethes—I must candidly confess to you, 
that the views which you have opened to me ap- 
pear to require more consideration, before I can 
acknowledge them to be according to the truth 
of God; whilst at the same time I admit, that 
they appear at present to be undeniable. I con- 
fess farther, that the greater part of the prophetic 
Scriptures has hitherto been a sealed book to 
me; some few texts here and there, which seemed 
to admit of a spiritual and personal application 
to myself, I have marked in my Bible, and often- 
refer to; but the context, both before and fol- 
lowing them, remained as dark as ever. I now 
perceive, that by taking the Scriptures literally, 
without attempting to spiritualize them away, they 
are perfectly intelligible. 

Anastasius.—It is not necessary to deny the 
spiritual application of any passage, because we 
contend for its literal meaning also; so that I 
have no objection, merely on that score, to the 
interpretation of the most fanciful Hutchinsonian 
who ever wrote. The views which I have en- 
deavoured to place before you, appear to me 
to be the revelation of God, and I know no per- 
son who has embraced them without finding his 
comprehension of all other branches of divine truth 
enlarged, and his affections more undividedly set 


158 


upon the kingdom of heaven, and more comi- 
pletely weaned from all objects connected with 
the present dispensation. Your last questions 
have led us away from the subject with which 
we commenced ; -but I do not regret it, since it 
is well to be often reverting to the first prin- 
ciples of the doctrine of Christ, and examining 
the foundations upon which our faith is built. 
Jesus crucified is the only true foundation, the 
only resting place on which Hope can with safety 
plant her foot, whilst she stretches forth her hands 
and eyes towards the glory which shall be revealed. 
That glory indeed, that great glory quickens the 
beatings of her heart, and animates her frame with 
joy ; but as she looks for it, who does not perceive 
that the uplifted eye must waver, if the foot be not 
steadily established? Whilst therefore we rejoice, — 
- yea, and will rejoice, in our bright, and blessed, 
and fully authorized expectations, we do not, (as 
we are slanderously reported, and as some affirm 
that we do,) forget to realize habitually the founda- 
tion on which we stand. Our hopes are risen with 
Him who was dead, and is alive again; and we 
are waiting in what the apostle calls, ‘the pa- 
tience of hope,” for the glorious appearing of our 
risen Lord; but our whole trust and confidence i is 
in Jesus Christ and him CRUCIFIED.

CONVERSATION THE FIFTH. 


The Doctrine of Scripture concerning the Second Coming of the 
Lord Jesus Christ. 


~ Philalethes.—Yovu told me in our first conver- 
sation upon the times of the Gentiles, that you 
would on a future day point out to me the Scrip- 
tures which relate to the throne of David; will 
you now be so kind as to inform me, whether you 
consider that this throne is to be set up at the 
beginning, or at the end of the millenium? — 
Anastasius.—It appears to me, that the ques- 
tion which you have mooted, is one of those on 
which the church is principally in error. No per- 
son can have been in the habit of attending the 
services of the Church of England, without having 
some notion floating in his mind about the second 
coming of our Lord Jesus Christ ; but because peo- 
ple in general look upon this coming as necessarily 
to happen at a period subsequent to their own 
deaths, which impression is founded upon some 
vague ideas about things which they think are to 
take place previously, none of which have, as 
they conclude, yet begun to be in being, no prac- 
tical effect whatever is produced by a truth, 
which is at best only received as an abstract fact, — 
in which they have very little personal interest. 
One of these intervening things is a reign of 
blessedness for a thousand years; whereas, I 


160- 


conceive, it is very evident from Scripture, that 
this reign of blessedness succeeds, and not pre- 
cedes the second coming of our Lord. We have 
already considered two periods spoken of in the 
Holy Scriptures, under the names of the “ times of 
the Gentiles,” and the “ times of the Jews :” there 
is a third period, called the “ dispensation of the 
fulness of the times,” Eph. i. 10, in which it is said, 
“all things are to be gathered together in Christ, 
both which are in heaven, and which are on earth :” 
and this last period is that ‘‘ dispensation” of 
which I am now speaking. Dr. Gill has some. 
excellent observations in his chapter on the Per- 
sonal Reign of Christ, (Body of Divinity, vol. ii. 
b. 4, ch. 8,) which he treats justly as identical 
with the millenium; but he has a chapter at the 
beginning of the same volume, on the Spiritual 
Reign of Christ, which he supposes is to take 
place between the present dispensation, or at least 
after the present state of things, and before the 
coming of our Lord in person. My object not 
being to controvert the opinions of others, but to 
set forth the passages of Scripture, which may 
direct ourselves to a right knowledge of what is 
revealed, I shall only remark upon this chapter 
on the Spiritual Reign, that the texts to which he 
has referred, relate equally to the period which 
ensues, after the restoration of the Jews, and 
subsequent to the fulfilment of the times of the 
= Gentiles, as do the other texts, which he has 
quoted in reference to the Personal Reign: his 
chapter on this latter subject is very well worth 
reading. Our gracious Lord likens himself to a 


161 


nobleman, who goes into a far country to receive 
a kingdom, and £o return, Luke xix. 12, and who 
delivers to his servants certain trusts which he de- 
sires them to make use of for his advantage until 
he come again: having received the kingdom, he 
returns, and rewards the servants according. to the 
way in which they had employed that which had 
been confided to them. We further learn, from 
Acts i. 19, that “the Lord—shall send Jesus: 
Christ—whom the heaven must receive until the 
times of restitution of all things.” In Acts ii. 30, 
the promise of God to David, that he would raise 
up Christ to sit on his throne, is repeated, and 
thus the question is resolved into the nature of 
the kingdom over which Christ is to reign, and 
the place where the throne of David is situated, 
First, then, with respect to the nature of the king- 
dom, it is declared in Dan. ii. 44, that the king- 
dom of Christ is to destroy and supersede all the 
universal monarchies which have gone before it; 
and it follows, that as those kingdoms were on 
this terraqueous globe, so shall the kingdom of 
Christ be likewise. Ifit be objected, that Christ’s 
kingdom is spiritual, and in heaven, that is, a king- 
dom of or over immaterial beings, and in or over an 
immaterial place; I reply, that there is no inti- 
mation of the fifth kingdom being of an essentially 
different nature, or in a different locality, from the 
four which have preceded it: and further, that as 
it is said in Isaiah ii. 2, that when the mountain 
of the Lord’s house is established in the last days, 
all the other mountains on the earth shall flow 
into it, and that ‘‘many people shall go and say, 
: M 


162 


Come ye, let us go up to the mountain of the Lord,’ 
to the house of the God of Jacob; and he will 
teach us of his ways, and we will walk in his | 
paths, for out of Zion shall go forth the law and 
the word of the Lord from Jerusalem ;” it is impos- 
sible that this shall come to pass in any other place 
than that in which it is said it shall come to pass, 
namely, at J erusalem. Again, in Dan. vii. 14 and 
27, the kingdom of Messiah is stated to be a 
kingdom which shall not pass away, but which 
shall be given to the holy people of the Most 
High. Our Lord assures his “ disciples,” Matt. v. 
that “theirs is the kingdom of heaven,” that 
«they shall inherit the earth ;” and the redeemed 
in heaven are represented as singing, “we shall 
reign upon the earth.” Rev. v. 10. We are told 
that the special occasion of our Lord’s speaking 
to his disciples the parable above referred to (Luke 
xix. 11), was, “because they thought that the 
kingdom of God should immediately appear ;” and 
it is clear, that if they had been mistaken in the 
fact of the kingdom appearing, as well as in the 
time of its appearance, he would not have spoken 
a parable in order to correct their views with re- 
spect to the latter point alone, whilst he left the 
former point untouched; as the latter could be 
worthy of consideration only in so far as they 
were right in the other. From John xii. 34, it is 
evident, that the Jews expected a King in the 
person of Messiah, who was to abide for ever in 
Judea, which expectation our Lord did not say 
was erroneous in fact, but only in the period at 
which it was to take place; as also after his re- 


163 


surrection, when they ask him, ‘ Lord wilt thou — 
at this time restore again the kingdom to Israel ?” 
Acts i. 6, he does not tell them, that the kingdom 
is never to be restored to Israel; but that they 
had other business to do than to understand “‘ the 
time and season” when that was to take place; 
inasmuch as they were first of all to be witnesses 
of him “ to the uttermost part of the earth;” for 
“ to every thing there is a season, and a time to 
every purpose under heaven.” Eccles. iii. 1. 
And this was evidently amongst the things which 
were to be closed up and sealed until the time 
of the end. Dan. xii. 9. In Micah iv. 7, it is 
said, ‘ the Lord shall reign over them in Mount 
Zion from henceforth, even for ever;” and this 
brings me to the second point, namely, the place 
in which the throne is to be set up; and for this 
branch also we have Scriptures equally clear. In 
Psalm cxxii. 2. 5, we read, ‘‘ Our feet shall 
stand within thy gates, O Jerusalem—for there are 
set thrones of judgment, the thrones of the house 
of David :” and in Jer. iii. 17, that after the Jews 
are in possession of their land again, “at that time 
they shall call Jerusalem the throne of the Lord.” 
Upon this throne of judgment, the Lord promised 
_his disciples that they should sit with him “ in 
the regeneration,” Matt. xix. 28, ‘‘ the time of the 
restitution of all things,” when the earth will be 
regenerated ; for as men have a water baptism on 
the entrance into the church, but are not one whit 
the more clean, until baptized with the fire of the 
Holy Ghost, so the world, after its baptism by the

flood, in the days of Noah, has continued 4s

- In this judgment all his saints accompany him :

Philalethes.—But why do you suppose that the 
earth, or at least that part of it where our Lord 
will sit as King, will be purified ? 

Anastasius.—It is said, ‘‘ that the Lord shall 
comfort Zion: he will comfort all her waste places; 
and he will make her wilderness like Eden, and 
her desert like the garden of the Lord,” Isa. li. 3. ; 
and that the wolf also shall dwell with the lamb, 
and the leopard shall lie down with the kid; and 
the calf and the young lion and the fatling to- 
gether; and a little child shall lead them: and 
the cow and the bear shall feed; their young ones 
shall lie down together; and the lion shall eat 
straw like the ox; and the sucking child shall 
play on the hole of the asp, and the weaned child 
put his hand on the cockatrice den ; they shall not 
hurt nor destroy in all my holy mountain, for the 
earth shall be full of the knowledge of the Lord, 
as the waters cover the sea.” Isa. xi. 6—9. And 
again, in Isa. lx. that after the glory of the Lord 
is risen upon Zion, ver. i. that ‘ violence shall no 
. more be heard in thy land, wasting nor destruction 
within thy border; but thou shalt call thy walls, 
Salvation, and thy gates, Praise. The sun shall 
be no more thy light by day, neither for bright-

also shall be all righteous; they shall inherit the 
land for ever, the branch of my planting, the work

of my hands, that I may be glorified.” 18—21. 
In Isa. Iv. 13. it is promised, that, ‘“ instead of the

the brier shall come up the myrtle-tree ; and it 
shall be to the Lord for a name, for an everlasting 
sign that shall not be cut off:” and in Zech. xii. 8.
