Philalethes.—Do you think that the Scriptures 
point to a resurrection of the saints as taking piace | 
before the general resurrection ? 

Anastasius.—You will have no difficulty upon 
this subject, if you will refer to Luke xx. 34—38. 
where it is said, ‘‘ they which shall be accounted 
worthy to obtain that world, and the resurrection 
from the dead, cannot die any more, for they are 
equal to the angels, and are the children of God, 
being the children of the resurrection ;” from 
whence it is evident, that the resurrection there 
alluded to is one of which the children of God 
alone are partakers. At this resurrection the chil- 


167 


dren of God are manifested; during the present 
life they compose the invisible church, each in- 
deed having “ʻa new name written, which no man 
knoweth, save him that receiveth it.” ‘‘ For this 
manifestation of the sons of God the earnest ex- 
pectation of the whole creation waiteth,” Rom. 
viii. 19. “in hope that the creation itself also 
shall be delivered from the bondage of corruption 
into the glorious liberty of the children of God: 
for we know that the whole creation groaneth and 
travaileth in pain together until now; and not 
only it, but ourselves also, who have the first fruits 
of the Spirit, even we ourselves groan within our- 
selves, waiting for the adoption, even the redemp- 
tion of the body.” 23. In Eph. i. 13, 14, we are 
told that we receive the Holy Spirit as “the 
earnest of our inheritance, until the redemption of 
the purchased possession.” Thus you perceive 
that the work of redemption, as far as our indi- 
vidual persons are concerned, is not completed 
until the body is redeemed. For this resurrection 
St. Paul, in his Epistle to the Philippians, chap. iii. 
after speaking of his renunciation of his own righ- 
teousness, says how gladly he would give it up, if 
by any means he might attain to the resurrection 
of the dead, ver. 11. As St. Paul was perfectly 
sure that all mankind would rise again some day or 
other, the resurrection of which he there speaks is 
undoubtedly that same resurrection of which our 
Lord speaks, as appertaining only to the children 
of God; and we shall be further convinced of the 
accuracy of this remark, by observing that the 
Apostle makes use of the word cSavacracic instead 


168 


of avacracic merely, and that this is the only place 
in the New Testament in which the word occurs. 
This, then, I conclude, is the resurrection, which 
takes place at the coming of Christ to reign, of 
those who have fallen asleep in Jesus; when the 
bodies of those believers who are alive will be 
changed from being corruptible to being incor- 
ruptible; when the deluge of fire will destroy all 
unbelievers out of Christendom, purifying and 
changing the face and appearance of it; when 
Satan, sin, and sorrow will be cast out, and the 
enemy of mankind be bound during the whole 
thousand years, and not suffered to enter again 
into that Eden of the Second Adam, as he did 
into the garden of the first. It is to this we must 
refer the declaration of our Lord, “I am the re- 
surrection and the life;” for it is Christ’s mystical 
body, head, and members, which alone rises at 
the beginning of the Lord’s day. The Greek 
term to express the first resurrection in Rev. 
xx. 5. is the strongest that the language admits of, 
for it is not 7 TOWwTN avasaoic but 7 avasactc 7 TOWTN, 
the resurrection, the first. This is ‘‘ the resurrec- 
tion to life,” John v. 29. which is by the mild 
voice of the Saviour; the second resurrection is 
“the resurrection of damnation,” and is by the 
stern command of the Judge. 

Philalethes—But do they not both take place 
on the same day ? 

Anastasius —The whole period of Christ’ s reign 
upon earth is mentioned as “the day of the 
Lord.” Throughout the Holy Scriptures, the 
period of a day is generally reckoned from even- 


169 


ing to evening. The invariable custom of the 
ancients was to count the day in this manner, as 
the Italians do to the present time. Aulus Gellius 
says, eos (Athenienses) a sole occaso ad solem 
iterum occidentem omne id medium tempus unum 
diem esse dicere. N. A. 1. 3. c. 2. Cæsar tells us 
that the same custom prevailed amongst the 
Gauls ; spatia omnis temporis non numero dierum, 
sed noctium definiunt, et dies natales et mensium et 
annorum. initia sic observant ut noctem dies se- 
quatur. Hence too are the expressions in use 
amongst ourselves of a “ sen’night,” a “fort’night.” 
Du Cange, in his Latin Glossary, has cited many 
laws, formularies, &c. which prove that several 
nations, amongst whom he specifies Gauls, 
Saxons, English, and Arabians, reckoned time 
by the number of nights, as well as by the 
number of days. The ecclesiastical day in Eng- 
land, to this hour, begins in the evening at the 
vigil: (and hence, by the way, let me corret an 
error into which many well-intentioned, but ill- 
informed people fall respecting the apparently, 
as they suppose, irreligious custom which prevails 

abroad, of opening the theatres, giving dances, &c. — 
on Sunday evening; whereas the Sabbath having 
commenced on Saturday evening at six o'clock, 
the Sabbath is ended to them at six o’clock on 
Sunday evening ; and thus the opera being open 
on the Saturday night, as it is in this country, 
is in their eyes the real infringement of the 
Sabbath ; even down so late as the time of Arch- 
bishop Laud, we read in his life of his going, as 
soon.as it struck six, on Sunday evening, to play 


170 


at bowls with his chaplain, he considering that 
the Sabbath was then terminated: but to return) 
All the great works of God, whether of creation, 
providence, or grace, are represented as carried 
on during night, and when the following day dawns 
they are seen already done and perfect. But, to 
come mere specifically to- your question, you will 
observe that it is clear that the word “day” does not 
necessarily mean a period of twenty-four hours, or 
one revolution of this globe upon its axis before the 
sun, from the circumstance of its being made use 
of three several times previous to the creation of 
that luminary, Gen. i. 5, 8, 13. and from such 
expressions as, ‘‘ to-day if ye will hear his voice,” 
“the day of temptation in the wilderness,” which 
was a period of forty years. Again, “the day” 
that Adam sinned he was to die, which as he did 
not do literally, some extended meaning for the 
term must be found, and by the help of the ex- 
pression in Peter ‘‘ that the day is with the Lord 
as a thousand years, and a thousand years as 
one day;” and of seeing also that though the man 
who lived to the longest term, reached 969 years, 
not attaining to the full extent of 1000 years, 
but dying within the 1000 years, be still died 
within that day, we have some guide to lead us 
to the right understanding of the fulfilment of the 
curse on Adam, and likewise of the extent to 
which an indefinite period, signified by that word, 
may be carried. There are some good remarks in 
Thruston, vol. i. p. 18, to which you will do well 
to attend if you feel that there is any force in the 
observations of Mr. Maitland upon the word. 


171 


“ Day,” when he objects to its being considered 
as signifying any thing’ but 24 hours. Many 
things are mentioned in scripture as to take place 
in “the day of the Lord,” which cannot possibly 
take place in so short a period as 24 hours; but 
for whatever measure of time the expression may 
serve as a notation, it seems to have its regular 
portions of morning and evening, like other days; 
and the day of Christ is always spoken of as 
reckoned from morning to evening, as our ordi- 
nary days are, with no intervening night, which, 
they would have if reckoned by the other method. 
We learn from the parallel passages in Mark - 
xvi. 2. and Luke xxiv. 1. that “ very early in 
the morning,” of the resurrection of our Lord he 
had already risen. ‘‘ The morning” of Christ’s 
“ day,” is synonimous with the first resurrection 
in Ps. xxx. 5. (see Horsley in loco) and xlix. 14. 
lix. 16. exlii. 8. and in 2 Sam. xxiii. 4, Christ, as 
the ruler over men, is celebrated as coming in the 
morning; and it is in reference only to his second 
coming that Christ is called “the Sun of Righ- 
teousness rising’ in Mal. iv. 2. A promise is 
given to him that overcometh and keepeth Christ’s 
works unto the end, that Christ will give him 
power over the nations, and he shall rule them 
with a rod of iron, and that he will give him ‘“ the 
morning star,” or, in other words, that he shall 
see the beginning of the day of the Lord. Rev. 
ii. 28. As Christ also rose “the first-fruits of 
them that slept,” so his people rise the first-fruits 
of the whole regenerated creation of God. 


172 


« As in Adam all die, even so in Christ shall ail 
be made alive; but every man in his own order; 
Christ the first fruits; afterward they that are 
Christ's, at his coming; then cometh the end, 
when he shall have delivered up the kingdom to 
God, even the Father, when he shall have put 
down all rule and all authority and power; for he 
must reign till he hath put all enemies under his 
feet; the last enemy that shall be destroyed is 
death.” 1 Cor. xv. 22—25. Here the resurrection 
. of all mankind is plainly declared, and that it does 
not take place at the same instant of time, but in _ 
a certain order: first, Christ himself; secondly, 
they that are Christs, who are to rise at his 
coming: between this first and second period, 
therefore, 1800 years have already elapsed, and 
there is no reason for supposing that an. equally 
long period, and probably a longer, may not 
elapse between the second and third: thirdly, 
then cometh the end, which is not until the end 
of Christ’s reign, after he shall have put all ene- 
mies under his feet, even death itself; but as 
during Christ’s reign on the earth, children are 
to be born and men are to die; and as we learn 
that death is not to be destroyed until the end of 
the 1000 years, Rev. xx. 7. 14., it follows, that 
tliey who are not Christ’s, do not rise until the 
end of the millenium. 

Crito.—Christ ‘“ which is, and which was, and 
which is to come,” Rev. i. 4. is the great subject 
of prophecy, and not less at one period than at 
another; the coming of the Lord is mentioned as 


173 


the last event which takes place under both the 
seventh seal and the seventh trumpet. It is im- 
portant to remeniber, in order to attain a right 
understanding of the Revelations given to St. 
John, that the whole book is symbolical; and as 
the circumstance which distinguished the Jews 
was, that “ chiefly unto them were committed the 
oracles of God ;” and as when they were cast out 
the same charge was committed to the Gentiles, 
the Christian church stands now in the same place 
in God’s government of the world, that the Jewish 
church did of old. Hence the 144,000 Jews who © 
are said to be sealed in chap. vii. are probably 
symbolical of some Christian nation which stands 
in the place of the former nation. 

Aristo.—The whole mystery of the coming of 
Jesus Christ from the bosom of the Father down 
to this lower earth, began in Eden at Gen. iii. 15. 
and finishes at the opening of the seventh seal. 
The mystery to be exhibited is that of a God-man, 
and the revelation of this God-man is to take 
place at his second coming. The first coming is 
the darkest page in the revelations of God, and 
will always be foolishness to the world; and all 
that pteceded it is only the making of the 
mystery of which that’ was the dignus vindice 
nodus. The Apocalypse is the unfolding of the 
mystery of Jesus Christ, which ends in his second 
coming; and hence, no doubt, the distinguished 
blessing promised at the out-set to all who will 
study it: “ Blessed is he that readeth and they 
that hear the words of this prophecy, and keep 


174 


those things which are written therein, for the 
time.is at hand.” Rev. i. 3. This opens his per- 
sonal appearance; for if he. is not to appear the 
second time as a person, the types of persons 
who prefigure him, and his own incarnation in the 
womb of the Virgin, seem to have no adequate 
counterpart. For, besides the annus magnus of 
a kingdom, of times, &c. &c. there is a person 
always exhibited also, of whom he is to be the 
antitype, reigning in glory; and it is distinctly 
said, in Heb. ix. 24—28., that he is to come and 
appear the second time, as completely and per- 
sonally as he came and appeared the first time, 
where all the terms which are made use of have 
- reference to the organ of sight. As to the time 
of his coming, I ground this upon the fact that is 
recorded in Acts iii. 12—26. that the heavens 
have only received him until the time of the 
restitution of all things which has been spoken 
of by all the prophets since the world began: 
thus we find in every prophet a time of destruction 
and trouble, and then a time of restoration and 
refreshing. In Acts ii. 22. we are told that 
Christ is still to sit on David’s throne, and as 
David's throne is on earth, and not in heaven, 
it follows that if ever Christ is to sit upon Da- 
vid’s throne, he must come again to the earth 
for that purpose: so that these two passages in 
the Acts, taken together, are irrefragable proofs 
both of the second coming of Christ, and of his 
coming, not only by his providence, but in person. 

Philalethes—Although it appears that the time 


1795 


of Christ's return is also the time of the restitu- 
tion of all things, yet I do not see how you can 
ascertain the time more correctly without refer- 
ence to chronology. 

Anastasius.— Your remark is just ; yet the figure 
which is most frequently made use of by the 
prophets, and by our Lord himself, in reference 
to this very subject, to denote the time of his re- 
turn, is very striking, namely, that of a woman 
with child: we know that a pregnant woman will 
be delivered at the expiration of nine months, 
nevertheless, the birth may be deferred to ten 
months, or be hastened to seven. It is, therefore, 
a time which is both certain and uncertain: for as 
a virgin being with child, was the sign of the first 
advent, so is the woman with child every where 
the figure for the second. In John xvi. our Lord 
tells his disciples, that he is going to leave them 
for a little while, but that afterwards he will re- 
turn to them again, and that the sorrow they were 
then feeling should be turned into joy. “A 
woman when she is in travail hath sorrow, þe- 
cause her hour is come; but as soon as she is deli- 
vered of the child, she remembereth no more the 
anguish for joy, that a man is born into the world ; 
and ye now, therefore, have sorrow; but I will 
see you again, and your heart shall rejoice—and 
in that day, ye shall ask me nothing,” &c. &c. 
21—23. “Bein pain, and labour to bring forth, 
O daughter of Zion, like a woman in travail ; for 
now shalt thou go forth one of the city, and thou 
shalt dwell in the field, and thou shalt go even to 
Babylon; there shalt thou be delivered; there 


176 


the Lord shall redeem thee from the hand of thine 
enemies,” (Mic. iv. 10,) where the context shows 
that it is the last deliverance from the mystical 
Babylon, or Christendom, which is intended. We 
must bear in mind that the things spoken of as 
belonging to the day of Christs coming, may be 
a series of acts, rather than one great and indi- 
visible operation. 

Aristo.—The chronological indications are of 
great importance with a certain class of arguers; 
but there are many signs mentioned in the things 
which are to be restored, such as the new heavens, 
2 Pet. iii. 13, and the fifth kingdom, Dan. ii. 44. 
The Apocalypse is a book which ought to be very 
much studied; I gained great insight into the 
duties and office of the Christian pastor, by attend- 
ing closely to what is contained in the epistles 
to the seven churches. The trumpets announce 

various great comings of our Lord, and it is at 
the sounding of the seventh, that the mystical 
Jericho falls. The manifestation of all things is at 
the second coming of Christ, till which time the 
things of the mystery are only to be perceived by 
faith. Much obscurity seems to hang over the 
manner in which this manifestation will be made. 
Satan, who made such fierce struggles at all the 
periods indicated by the trumpets, will make 
another much more resolute at the sounding of 
the seventh, and he will never give up his réign 
by any preaching of the Gospel, nor by any other 
means than by the manifestation of Christ in 
person, on purpose to cast him out of this earth. 
A very large portion of the Scriptures relate to 


177 


the details of the mode in which the Jews are to 
be restored. The Jews likewise are not only a 
type of the New Jerusalem, but they are the New 
Jerusalem; yet, nevertheless, the whole Apoca- 
lypse of St. John belongs to the Christian, and 
not to the Jewish Church. 

Theophilus.—The coming of the seed of the | 
woman to restore man to his original state of 
blessedness, has been the great object of the 
desire of the saints ever since the fall. His first 
coming is spoken of in the Holy Scriptures in 
various ways, such as personal, providential, and 
by his Spirit. He appeared personally to Abraham, 
the Patriarchs, Moses, Joshua, &c. &c. which ap- 
pearances were chiefly for temporal purposes. 
His advent was also personal, and in the flesh, 
‘when he came to do those acts which no other 
being could do, such as fulfilling the law, and 
offering a sacrifice in our nature, &c. His advent 
is spoken of when he visits by his providences, as 
in the destruction of Jerusalem. His advent by 
his Spirit is also spoken of in John xiv. 23, as his 
coming. It is necessary to keep these comings 
distinct, in order that we may know what we have 
to expect at this time, as some things may be done 
by his providence, some by his spirit, whilst there 
are some which can only be done by himself in 
person. The church has never lost sight of his 
coming, except when it has been in a dead state. 
The chronology of this event was veiled, that the 
expectation may be always kept alive. The dis- 
course of Peter, Acts iii. proves this. It would 
not have answered the purpose of St. Peter to say, 

N 


178 


“repent until 1260 days are accomplished;” he 
would have said, “repent, for you are dying men,” 
as we do now; yet the context shows, that he calls 
them to repentance, by the coming of Christ in 
judgment, and not by the remembrance of their 
death. The early Christians were always expect- 
ing him; the Reformers were expecting him ; and 
the Church of England to this day, in her Com- 
mination Service, holds up the same expectation, 
by reciting the following passages, ‘‘ For lo, the 
Lord is come out of his place to visit the wicked- 
ness of such as dwell upon the earth; but who 
may abide the day of his coming? who shall be 
able to. endure when he appeareth?—the day of 
the Lord cometh as a thief in the night; and when 
men shall say, Peace, and all things are safe, then 
shall sudden destruction come upon them, as sor- 
row cometh upon a woman travailing with child, 
and they shall not escape. Then shall appear 
the wrath of God in the day of vengeance,” &c. &e. 
Some things, which it is declared our blessed Lord 
shall come to do, must be done by a personal advent, 
such as to raise his dead saints—to change the 
bodies of his living saints—to be made visible to 
his people, who are to see him as he is, as we 
learn by these phrases, ‘‘see the King in his 
beauty,” ‘‘ when every eye shall see him,” &c.— 
to judge the quick and the dead—to make new 
heavens and new earth. Other things may be 
done by his providences, such as delivering his peo- 
ple—taking vengeance of his enemies ; other things 
may be done by his-Spiri¢, such as converting the 
world. There are passages of Scripture which 


179 


may be applied to either of these; it is, therefore, 
necessary to establish the fact, rather than the 
mode of his coming, for the church is in a state 
of practical unbelief upon the subject; and, if it 
were not for the advent collects, it would have 
lost sight of it altogether. Our Lord tells us 
to be looking always for signs; saying, when ye 
see certain signs, then lift up your heads, for the 
time of your redemption draweth nigh. Amongst 
the signs given by the Prophet Daniel, which im- 
mediately precede the second advent, are, Ist, an 
oppressive King or power, xi. 36 ;—2, an increase 
of divine knowledge, xii. 4 ;—3, persecution of the. 
saints, xu. 4; it appears that Daniel did not under- 
stand the prophecy from the chronology in ch. vii., 
and asked, therefore, for more signs, which were 
given at ver. 7 ;—4, the wicked shali do wickedly, 
ver. 10;—5, the Jews shall be delivered, xii. I. 
The signs mentioned by our Lord are divided 
into the time of the last tribulation, and the time 
of gathering in his elect. The signs mentioned in 
the Apocalypse are gathering in the elect, occa- 
sioned by a commission gone cut to preach the 
everlasting Gospel, and to urge its acceptance by 
the awfulness of the coming judgments. This stirs 
up persecution, and hence the necessity of the pa- 
tience of the saints. The present is the time of the 
Lord’s waiting to be gracious; the sixth vial is not 
yet run out. It is in this parenthesis, as it were, 
this pause, that we are now endeavouring to 
awaken the church, and may the Lord bless our 
endeavours. 

Anastasius.—In confirmation of the remark of

our brother Theophilus, on the argument of St.

observes, ‘‘ that in the Scriptures we ‘are never 
exhorted to prepare for death, but always for the 
coming of the Lord.” —p. 7. 

Philalethes—I inquired of our dear brother 
Sophron, during our first conversation, whether 
he grounded very strong opinions upon the chrono- 
logical notices contained in the Scriptures; will 
he now have the kindness to tell me whether he 
thinks the decree of Justinian to be clearly the 
commencement of the period of 1260 days, so 
repeatedly mentioned ? 

_ Sophron.—Daniel says that the saints of the 
Most High shall be given into the hands of the 
Papal little horn for that period, vii. 25; and I 
know of no event in history, by which the saints: 
were delivered into the hands of the Pope, so 
clear as the edict of Justinian, appointing him sole 
judge in matters of faith. No one could deliver 
them but the lawful civil power, the Emperor, who 
had them, and this opinion is farther strength- 
ened by the period expiring at the commence- 
ment of the French Revolution. But the expira- 
tion of the 1260 days at the French Revolution 
does not rest solely upon its commencement by 
the decree of Justinian, but likewise from the 
matter of fact, acknowledged by Messrs. Burke, 
Pitt, Fox, and all other eminent statesmen, that a 
total change took place in all the churches and 
states in Europe from that epoch, . and that a new 
era commenced. 

Anastasius.—If farther proof be wanting, that | 


181 


a new era commenced at that time, I refer you 
to a note in the * Reflections on the French 
Revolution,” (Burke, p. 303,) which contains an 
extract from a speech of the President of the 
Assembly, M. Rabaud de St. Etienne, in which 
he says, “Tous les etablissemens en France 
couronnent le malheur du peuple; pour le rendre 
heureux il faut le rénouveler; changer ses idées ; 
changer ses lois ; changer ses mæurs —changer les 
hommes ; changer les choses ; changer les mots— 
. tout detruire; oui, tout detruire; puisque tout est 
arécreer.” It is to be feared that the church is 
not.so well read in the atrocities of the French 
Revolution as it ought to be: an event, on many 
accounts, entirely without a parallel. This pas- 
sage furnishes an epitome of the principles which 
actuated the chief movers in it, and of the visionary 
objects which they expected to attain. Change 
was the first point aimed at; and nothing, however 
wicked, unjust, or cruel, was objected to, pro- 
vided it was new. A contempt for authority, ex- 
perience, and example, united to an immoderate 
admiration of themselves, prevailed over every 
other consideration. 

Sophron.—With regard likewise to the changes 
in the charches, it is remarkable that the Baptist 
Missionary Society, which constituted the first of 
the great movements that have been made by the 
Church in England during these latter days, and 
which Missionary Society has done more in thirty 
years in the work of translating the word of God 
_ into various languages than all that had been done 
previously since the apostolic ages, originated in 


182 


a provincial town, Kettering, by the subscrip- 
tion of a few poor ministers, amounting only to 
£13. 2s. 6d., in that very same year, 1792. Some 
writers, free from all bias, and having no system to 
support, have adopted theoretically the termination 
of the 1260 days as synchronical with the seventh 
Apocalyptic trumpet. Justinian’s edict is not only 
important because it delivered the saints into the 
hands of the Pope, but also because it set up the 
worship of the Virgin Mary. The church of God 
of old were mistaken in the actual day of our 
Lord’s first coming, although they were expecting 
it, and looking out for it, at the right time, as we 
see by the examples of Anna and Simeon. As 
soon as the Levitical priesthood was compelled to 
discontinue its appointed offices through the cap- 
tivity, a system of chronology was revealed to the 
church, as her guide for the future. When the 
spirit of prophecy ceased in Malachi, the church 
was not left without a compass: this is testified 
even by. the heathen, who were expecting some 
great event, as Virgil’s Pollio abundantly proves. 
As we live under a better dispensation, it is not to 
be supposed that the church should now be left 
without a pole-star to guide her through the 
waters of anti-christian troubles. Elizabeth, Anna, 
and Simeon, were the first persons upon whom 
the prophetic spirit was poured out immediately 
before the first advent. 1 once thought that Mes- 
siah’s second advent did not take place till the end 
of the millenium, but as soon as that opinion was 
brought to the test of Scripture, I found it per- 
fectly untenable.

-` Philalethes.—Why is the second coming of our 
Lord doubted? | 

Sophron.—I do not believe that any one who 
reads the Bible does doubt it, for it is the grand 
doctrine of the New Testament, but it is practi- 
cally lost sight of. It is predicted in Dan. vii. 
13, 14, that this coming will take place at the de- 
struction of the Roman beast, which is the fourth 
monarchy, and will introduce a new kingdom 
upon the earth. Whatever may be the nature. of 
his coming, I conclude that all the passages which 
speak of his coming in similar terms, describe one 
and the same coming. Thus must the passage in 
Zech. xii. 10. and Rev. 1. 7. allude to the same 
event, as the words in the Greek, in both places, 
are the same. Also every passage which men- 
tions his coming ‘‘ with clouds,” must denote one 
and the same act. The term zapso.a, occurring in 
Matt. xxiv. 3. 27. 37. 39. and many other places, 
and also em@avaa, denote personal appearance: 
the former word is never used but with reference 
toa person visible to the eyes: and unless such 
passages mean a personal appearance, there is no 
personal appearance revealed in Scripture, and 
the whole church has been in error from the days 
of St. Paul concerning it. As to the appearances 
to Abraham, Moses, &c. in the wilderness, being 
not a personal, but a spiritual appearance, the 
assertion is a mere play upon terms, for it was 
a visible appearance; and when we speak of 
spirit, we mean something that is invisible to our 
material senses: it may be admitted, however, that 
these appearances were not in the body, for he was 


184 


not yet made flesh: but the truth is, that there is 
not much force, either in the objection, or in such 
answers to it. The Lord was really and person- 
ally with his church, both in the wilderness and 
in the temple; spiritually indeed, but visibly also. 
The dedication of Solomon's temple was a type of 
the great High Priest’s inauguration in his temple, 
that is, of the Lord’s presence in his millenial 
church. It is not likely that Christ will converse 
with men in the flesh in the millenial state, but 
possibly the idea of his communication with the 
church then on earth will be such as is conveyed 
by Jacob’s ladder, on which, as Nathaniel was 
told by him, he should see the angels ascending 
and descending on the Son of Man. The signs of 
our Lord’s coming, at the present day, seem to 
be so clear, that he who runs may read; for 
we have the shaking of all the political rulers, 
powers, or heavens; the Gospel preached for a 
witness to all nations; and the hour of affliction to 
the church is the only thing that 1s now wanting.

“Out of Zion, God, the perfection of beauty, hath

Thus we read in Eccles. viii. 11, ‘* because sen-

which are as a shadow, because he feareth not 
‘before God:” and in Isa. lvii. 11. God asks,

me, those that made a covenant with me by sacri-

that he, whom they had once rejected, and used

so despitefully, is nevertheless their God. ‘‘ I am

that he is.not come to rebuke them for having dis-

evident that it must be the professing Gentiles 
who are described by this term, because he re-

that is, promulgated his written word, and taken 
his covenant in their mouths, notwithstanding 
which they practised things forbidden by those 
very statutes: these charges, it is obvious, cannot 
be matters of complaint against the heathen, but 
only against the inhabitants of Christendom. He 
concludes by declaring, that although they had 
thought he never would come to punish them for 
‘such things, and that he was such an one as them- 
selves, who tuought sin a trifle, nevertheless he was 
now come to reprove them, and to set all things in 
order before their eyes. Inthe two last verses the 
Psalmist invites attention to this subject, as a 
warning to all who forget God. 

Basilicus.—It is impossible to use language to 
describe the coming of the Lord plainer than that 
which is used in the Scriptures; and they who 
will not understand from the mouths of the 
Prophets and Apostles, are little likely to com- 
_prehend it better from us. It is clear from them, 
that the coming of our Lord, though preceded by 
a variety of manifestations to which that term 


188 


may be applied, will eventually be personal, visible, 
local, terrible, and glorious; that as he came to 
suffer, he will come to reign; that as he was 
rejected, he will be received as a King; that as 
he came in suffering flesh to redeem the soul, he 
will come in his spiritual and glorified humanity 
for the redemption of the body ; thus fulfilling his 
title and character as the Second Adam, the Lord 
From heaven, the Son of Man in the power and 
glory of his Father, and of his holy angels, and of 
all his saints.

Anastasius—The term “‘ Everlasting Father,”

age to come.” Much light is thrown upon the 
meaning of the expression, Father of the age to 
come, by comparing the passages which speak of 
him in that character, with those which speak of 
the Father of the present age; the former being 
that which the Apostle calls, Heb. ii. 5., “the 
world to come whereof we speak.” We read, in 
Eph. ii. 2., that the course of the present dis- 
pensation is according to the prince of the power 
of the air; and Satan, shewing to our Lord all the

walketh about, seeking whom he may devour ;”

Philalethes—You mentioned, whilst relating 
some of the circumstances in which our blessed 
Lord is said to be the antitype of Adam, that the . 
paradisaical state would be restored; I wish you 
would point out any other resemblance which 
occurs to you. 

Basilicus.—Since you wish to revert to that 
subject, I beg you to remark, that it was then 
said, that at the creation God endued Adam with 
the sovereignty of the world, as Theophilus justly - 
observed ; made him his vicegerent upon earth; 
and put the world, and all that it contained, under 
his feet. Adam exemplified in his own person 
the three creatures,—material, animal, and intel- 
lectual :—his body was material, made of the dust 
of the ground; 72187 1», Gen. ii. 7; his blood was 
the seat of animal life, Gen. 1x. 4; and his soul of 
intellect, (the breath of life, cn mow) breathed 
into him by the Almighty, constituted him a spi- 
ritual being. His dominion endured so long as his 
will coincided with the will of God; but when it 
came to be separated from Him, and in agreement 
with the will of the devil, he fell, not only from 
his state of purity, and thus lost the kingdom 
within, but also from his kingly authority over . 
creation, which was henceforth usurped by Satan. 
I have just observed to you, with reference to an- 
other subject, namely, the supplanting of Satan’s 
government over this dispensation, that the great 
end of Christ’s work is to restore what man has 
lost, by destroying the works of the devil. This 


192 


great object he has in part accomplished. By 
his first coming ‘he delivered the souls of his 
people from the dominion of Satan, and esta- 
blished his kingdom within, (which is righteous- 
ness, peace, and joy in the Holy Ghost,) in their 
hearts. It remains that he should redeem their 
bodies, and wrest from’ Satan the dominion he 
usurped over the material world, or the kingdom 
without, which he will do, at the setting up of 
his kingdom upon earth. That the kingdoms of 
this world, and the power and the glory of them, 
are for a season left in the hand of the enemy, to 
be disposed of by him according as he will, we 
know from the history of our Lord’s temptation, 
and other passages to which I have just referred 
you; and it is for the restoration of this usurped 
authority that the church is instructed to pray, 
“ Thy kingdom come, thy will be done on earth 
as it is in heaven,” and to ascribe the kingdom, 
and the power, and the glory unto God, to whom 
of right they belong, and to whom, in the person 
of his Son, they shall be restored, when “the 
kingdom and dominion, and the greatness of the 
kingdom under the whole heaven shall be given 
unto him.” Thus shall the Lord from heaven 
prove himself to be the second Adam, by restor- 
. ing to man “that which he took not away,” even 
all that the first Adam lost; namely, the image of 
Ommipotence, in which the first man was created, 
and which, by the new creation, is again im- 
pressed upon the soul of every believer; the 
glorious body of light, which, for the consolation 
of the church, was exhibited to the three disciples 


193 


upon the mount of transfiguration ; the. dominion 
over the animal creation exercised by our Lord in 
the days of his humiliation, in the two instances 
of controlling the fish, and taming the unbroken 
ass; and the sovereignty of the elements, exem- 
plified also by him when he stilled the tempest by 
his word, when he gave power to Peter to walk 
upon the sea, (for in him resided the power, so 
that while Peter looked on him he was able to 
walk, and as soon as he looked at the waves he 
began to sink); and when he transported Philip 
from the desert to Azotus: the restored sove- 
reignty, of which these several acts are so many 
pledges, shall be given to man: to Christ, the 
Son of Man, they will be given, having been 
originally given to Adam, as son of God. The na- 
tural man. takes no thought for the soul; it is 
with him, what shall we eat, what shall we drink, 
wherewithal shall we be clothed? questions which 
concern the body only, The believer, perceiving 
the worth of his soul, often thinks of the future 
state of that, to the utter exclusion of all ideas 


respecting the future state of his body. Redemp- | . 


tion has to do with both. We that have the first 
fruits of the Spirit in the soul wait for the adop- 
tion, to wit, the redemption of the body. The soul 
must have a dwelling-place. These bodies are 
incapable of sustaining the weight of glory that 
awaits us; they must be changed, and on this 
point we have an explicit promise from Him 
‘‘ who shall change our bodies of humiliation, that 
they may be fashioned like unto his glorious body, . 
according to the working whereby he. is able to 
i © Q 


194 


subdue all things unto himself:” and it is this 
change for which we hope: our soul's salvation is 
not the proper subject of hope, but of faith: we 
are saved Now, or we never shall be; we must 
have eternal life abiding in us now, or we shall 
never taste it hereafter. Faith respects grace; 
hope respects glory. Christ in us is the hope of 
glory. When he shall appear we shall be like 
him, for we shall see him as he is, (not the Father, 
whom we may never see as he is); and ‘‘ every 
man that hath ¢his hope in him, purifieth himself, 
even as he is pure.” As the second Adam is in 
all respects the antitype of the first Adam, so is 
the church, his spouse, the antitype of Eve— 
taken from Adam, and then presented to Adam 
on the Sabbath morn, she typifies the spiritual 
bride chosen in and taken out of Christ, and hav- 
ing been foreordained of God to be redeemed and 
saved by him, and finally to be presented to him 
on the great Sabbath morning, the beginning of 
the day of Christ, as his bride, indissolubly united 
to him, as one spirit in one body. The union of 
Adam to God was a creation union in time; that 
of the second Adam to God, and of the church in 
him, is a personal union, and is eternal; and this 
is what we gain by the fall: a closer relation to 
God in Christ, who came to earth, not only that 
we might have life, but that we might have it 
more abundantly. John x. 10. Christ comes the 
second time to gather his elect unto himself; and 
St. Paul mentions his coming, and the gathering 
. of his saints unto him, as simultaneous events, at 
the second advent in glory. His marriage union 


195 


was not only shadowed forth by that of Eve, but 
also by that of Solomon with Pharaoh’s daughter, 
the Egyptian, who was black, but he made her 
comely. The saints are hence to learn, that the 
_ deepest view of their own blackness is not to dis- 
courage them, because Christ makes them, and 
will make them, all fair in the comeliness which he 
puts upon them. But he cometh! suddenly; in 
a moment he cometh! The five first vials have 
been poured out; the sixth is over our heads; 
the seventh is near at hand: six trumpets have 
sounded ; the seventh, the last, 1 Cor. xv. 12, is 
preparing to sound ; and then the mystery of God 
shall be accomplished. Let us be ready; for 
when that moment arrives, there will be no more 
time to fill the lamp with oil, but the foolish 
virgins shall be for ever excluded from the mar- 
riage supper of the Lamb. 

Philalethes—What proofs have you of the 
doctrine of the second advent being preached by 
the primitive church, except what you collect 
from the Epistles in the New Testament ? 

Philemon.—The proofs of this are derived from 
the most unexceptionable sources, namely, from 
Gibbon, vol. ii. p. 301. who cites Justin Martyr 
and Irenæus. Mr. Maitland has lately reprinted 
the testimonies to this doctrine being that of the 
early church; and with them an Extract from 
Burnet’s Theory of the Earth to the same point. 
The personal coming is always mentioned in such 
a manner as to distinguish it from the coming by 
the Spirit. Among the objects revealed as to be 
accomplished by Christs personal coming. are, 

02 


196 


the destruction of Antichrist; the conversion of 
the Jews ;-to raise the dead saints, and to change 
the living saints; to bind Satan; to convert the 
world; to take possession of his kingdom ; and to 
enthrone his saints. There is a great difficulty con- 
cerning the new heavens and new earth, whether 
or not they are to take place at the beginning of 
the millenium; and therefore it is probable that 
there is a difference between the new Jerusalem 
on earth; and that which comes down from above. 
In the one we are told, that the saints will reign 
for 1000 years ; that the ships of Tarshish will be 
converted ; that there will be rain; that the sea 
shall give up her dead; and that there will be 
death and generation. In the other it is said, the 
reign is to be for ages of ages; that there is to be 
no sea; no rain; no death. The same kind of 
observation applies to the Gog of Ezekiel and the 
Gog of the Revelations. In the first he is said to 
be led by the prince of Meshech; to come up 
from the north; against the land after the Jews 
are in peace and safety ; is destroyed by enemies ; 
the bones of his army buried. In the second he 
is said to be led by the devil; to come up from > 
the four corners of the earth; comes against the 
camp of the saints; destroyed by fire from 
heaven ; bones consumed, and therefore nothing 
to bury. | : ; 
Philalethes.—i read in a Commentator on the 
Revelations xxi. 2, that the descent of the new 
Jerusalem from above; “ figuratively describes the 
ascent of the souls of the righteous,” and that, 
“with this explanation, the remainder of the 


197 


description becomes perfectly clear.” Holmes, 
vol. ii. p. 471. : 

Anastasius.—Very figurative indeed ! and with 
such an explanation any passage may be made to 
say any thing: when this is the mode of inter- 
pretation, it can be no matter of surprise that the 
ideas of the church should be exceedingly con- 
fused upon every point connected with the glorious 
epiphany of Immanuel. 

Polydorus.—The appearance of the body of 
Christ was not so uncommon a circumstance as 
the church seems in these days to suppose. He 
shewed himself to Thomas, to-all the Apostles, 
to large assemblies, to St. Paul at his conversion, 
and to St. John at Patmos. His next personal 
appearance will be to destroy his enemies, and 
perhaps also to lead the Jews into their own. 
land. It is possible that his reign with his saints 
over the unconverted world, may be carried on in 
an invisible manner, in the same way as Satan 
carries on his reign at present with his subordi- . 
nate agents. Our Lord has many risen saints 
with him already; Enoch, Elijah, Moses, and 
many who rose at the same time when he himself 
rose, we know of; and it is not improbable there- 
fore, that he often raises up his people to sit and 
reign with him. When he returns to earth, a 
change will be effected on the material world, 
similar to that which has taken place on their 
bodies. 

fsocrates.—It is worthy of remark, that whilst 
all the rewards promised to six out of seven of the 
churches in the Apocalypse-are passive and quiet, 


198 


the reward promised to Thyatira, whose. works 
and activity are chiefly commended, is to be ac- 
tively employed in ruling the nations. 

- Anastasius.—Such. an expression as “‘ the feet. 
of the Lord standing upon mount Olivet” would. 
be of itself sufficient to establish that the next 
appearance of our Lord is to be in his body. But. 
it seems possible, and probable, that his manifesta- . 
tion to the world, to the Jewish nation, and to his 
church will. be different, and that that difference 
will continue throughout the millenial state. We 
do not know that our Lord may not be in his. 
body in this room, in which we are now assem- 
bled; and Moses and Elias with him; and al- 
though invisible to us, they are doubtless visible . 
to each other. Thus he may reign with his saints. 
in this regenerated world, he and they visible to 
each other, whilst to his restored Jews he may 
be only manifest in his rebuilt temple at Jeru- 
salem, whither the Gentile nations shall come up. 
continually to worship the King of the whole 
earth ; according as it is written, ‘‘ and it shall 
come to pass that every one that is left of all the 
nations which came against Jerusalem, shall even. 
go up from year to year to worship the King, the. 
Lord of Hosts, and to keep the feast of taber-. 
nacles; and it shall be, that whoso will not come. 
up of all the families of the earth unto Jerusalem. 
to worship the King, the Lord of Hosts, even 
upon them shall be no rain.” Zech. xiv. 16. In. 
his temple he may be manifested as by the- 
Shechinah, or Urim and Thummim of old, or. 
through the Prince of the temple ruling in his 


199 


power and name: ‘‘the Prince, he shall sit in it 
(the gate) to eat bread befure the Lord.” Ezek. 
xliv. 3. In like manner, at his next coming, he 
may bring the judgment on the nations of Chris- 
tendom by his providences, as he brought the 
armies of Sennacherib, Nebuchadnezzar, and Titus 
upon the Jews. But to the Jews he may appear 
as Joshua, the captain of their host; whilst to his 
elect bride be shews himself as Solomon in all 
his glory; in his glorified body, and ruler over 
the purified habitable globe. It does not appear 
that any signs of his coming are given to the 
ungodly and unconverted world; for those indi- 
viduals to whom it is given to perceive the signs, 
become convinced, and converted, and saved, by. 
turning to Christ for refuge ; to the world he ever 
will come as a thief, and in clouds, and his peo- 
ple are warned to be looking out for the signs of 
his coming, that he may not come in those charac- 
ters of obscurity to them. The great present sign 
to the church of the end of all things being at 
hand, is the increase of religious knowledge which _ 
has taken place within the last thirty years, and 
the revived attention which is now paid to the 
expectation of the coming Lord from heaven. 
“ Behold, a whirlwind of the Lord is gone forth 
in fury, even a grievous whirlwind; it shall fall 
grievously on the head of the wicked; the anger 
‘of the Lord shall not return until he have exe- 
cuted, and till he have performed the thoughts of 
his heart; in the latter days ye shall consider it 
perfectly.” Jer. xxiii. 19, 20. Again in chap. xxx. 
18—24,it is said, ‘‘ Thus saith the Lord, behold, 


200 


I will bring again the captivity of Jacob’s tents, 
their children shall be as aforetime—and their 
Governor shall proceed from the midst of them— 
and ye shall be my people, and I will be your 
God. Behold the whirlwind of the Lord goeth 
‘forth with fury—in the latter days ye shall consider 
it.” Daniel is told to ‘shut up the vision,” 
vili. 26, ‘ for it shall be for many days;” and 
in x. 14, he is told that another vision relates to 
“ what shall befal his people (the Jews) in the 
latter days; for yet the vision is for many days.” 
Again, at xii. 4, he is desired to ‘‘ shut up the 
words and seal the book (of the visions and inter- 
pretations which he had received), even to the 
time of the end; and it is immediately added, 
‘ many shall run to and fro, and knowledge shall 
be increased ;” at ¥€. 10. he is told, that ‘“ the words 
are closed up and sealed ¢ill the time of the end ;” 
and again it is added, in the following verse, ‘ the 
wise shall understand.” Whence it clearly follows, 
that considering and understanding the things 
belonging to the time of the end is a proof that the 
time of the end itself is that in which they are 
considered and understood. __ 

Philalethes.—A difficulty probably arises in the 
minds of some persons respecting the materiality 
of the bodies of the risen saints, whom you con- 
sider as to dwell on earth, from its being said that 
“flesh and blood cannot inherit the kingdom of 
heaven.” 

Anastasius. —Our Lord, after his resurrection, 
proves his identity to his disciples, when ‘“ they 
supposed that they had seen a spirit,” Luke 


201 


xxiv. 87, by saying, ‘‘ behold my hands and my feet 
that it is I myself; handle me and see; for a 
spirit hath not flesh and bones as ye see me have.” 
The figure which the Apostle uses in 1 Cor. xv. 
the very place from which the objection you sup- 
pose is taken, seems not only well adapted to 
illustrate the nature of the resurrection of the 
body, but also the most proper to convey the 
information intended to every class of Christians, 
because it was a fact with which they were all 
familiar, namely, the change produced upon a 
vegetable seed that perishes in the ground, and 
by its apparent death gives rise to fresh repro- 
duction. The Greeks represented this by many 
emblems, and amongst others by the insect be- 
coming a crysalis, and subsequently rising as a 
butterfly; this reproduction out of apparent de- 
struction, was the great fact inculcated by the 
Eleusinian mysteries, and by the Egyptian and 
Chinese ceremonies of religion, which you may see 
well treated of in a beautiful work by Mr. Christie, 
on ancient vases. Modern science has shewn 
that there 1s no such thing as annihilation; all 
created matter is indeed perpetually resolving 
itself into the ultimate elements of which it is 
composed; but these are again reuniting them- 
selves, and thereby forming other substances. 
Nothing can appear more incredible to a person 
ignorant of the operations of chemistry than that 
a beautiful metal should be produced out of the 
ashes of a burnt tree; or that still more beauti- 
ful product Iodine, from sea-weeds. The most 


“ 


202 


powerful substances with which we are acquainted 
are invisible and imponderable, such as heat, 
electricity, light, &c. The experiments of Ber- 
zelius, and of Mr. Dalton, have shewn divers 
substances, apparently the most dissimilar to each 
other, to be composed of the same materials, but 
combined in different proportions. The body of 
every creature is but, as it were, the machinery 
by which certain functions are performed, which 
functions correspond with the kind of existence 
that it is intended should be maintained; this 
machine may, and must, vary in each succeeding 
state into which the individual is brought, accord- 
ing as its means of support in those states differ 
from each other. The immaterial part, the soul, 
is that alone which does not vary, and hence, 
unless the soul is in this present life created after 
the image of God, it never can enjoy his holy 
presence in the life which is to come. Facts 
similar to this may be observed in those animals 
whose changes we daily witness; the caterpillar 
has certain functions to perform, and has a body 
provided for these purposes; it has certain other 
functions to perform as a butterfly, and has a 
body provided accordingly ; but there is no reason 
to suppose that its consciousness of enjoyment 
is greater in one state than the other. Not so 
with the heaven-born soul whilst imprisoned in 
this its earthly tabernacle; it has a strong desire 
to love, and serve, and enjoy its God, in a way 
which it finds its body belonging to this dispensa- 
tion perpetually prevents its doing; and hence 


203 


it longs for the redemption of this body, that it: 
may be clothed upon with another, adapted. to 
another state of existence, in which it shall be able. 
to. perform all that it now can do little more than. 
pant after. It is certainly no great demand upon. 
the faith of any one, to be required to believe that. 
He who first composed our present bodies of certain ; 
ultimate elements, and who has decomposed them 
into those elements again, can recompose them 
in a different proportion from that in which they 
existed before. You will find many analogies of 
this kind in Shepherd’s Thoughts on Devotion, 
a very excellent work, in which several interesting 
facts in natural philosophy are brought forward to 
illustrate various parts of the great scheme of 
redemption. It is delightful to see science really 
made the handmaid of religion by this writer, as 
metaphysics have been by Riccaltoun. An objec- 
tion, similar in kind to that which you have men- 
tioned respecting the nature of the bodies of the 
risen saints, has been also raised concerning the 
materials of the New Jerusalem, which are de- 
scribed as composed of precious stones, in Isaiah 
and the Apocalypse. But since we know that the 
most brilliant gems are inferior in lustre and mag- 
nificence to the tints which often accompany the 
setting sun; and as we know further, that that 
splendid phenomenon, like the rainbow, is pro- 
duced solely by the arrangement of a few particles 
of light and water, the description falls short of 
that which we constantly see, and therefore can 
scarcely be considered as either figurative or 


204 


hyperbolical. It would not be difficult to shew 
that the belief of a deist, in all the ordinary oc- 
currences of life, has less ground to stand upon, 
than that of the Christian, even for the most ex- 
alted mysteries. But arguments derived from 
such sources never can be of the smallest service 
towards converting infidels.
