CONVERSATION THE SIXTH. 


SEE 


Practical Duties arising to the Church, from the Second Coming 
of the Lord Jesus Christ. 


Philalethes.—It is easy to perceive, that the 
doctrine of the Second Coming will be highly 
influential on the practice of those who believe 
in it, because it is chiefly with reference to this 
that you have considered the practical application 
of the doctrines of the times of the Gentiles, and 
of the times of the Jews. | 

Anastastus.— Where events are spoken of either 
as synchronous, or as to take place in very close 
and rapid succession to each other, it is obviously 
difficult to separate distinctly all which belongs 
to one, from some things which belong to another. 
But whilst the restoration of the Jews is the prin- 
cipal ground of warning to the Gentiles in the 
Old Testament, the coming of Christ is the chief 
ground of exhortation to the church in the New 
Testament. 

Theophilus:—It is of the highest importance 
that we apply these exhortations personally to 
ourselves, for if we are assured that it is our 
Father’s good pleasure to give us the kingdom, 
Luke xii. 31—59, we shall have our hearts there 
where our treasure is. As with every other doc- 
trine, so with this, many shall say in that day, 
<‘ have. we not prophesied in thy name?” to 


206 
whom our Lord will answer, ‘‘ Depart from me, 
I never knew you.” There never was an error 
in the church which the devil did not find some 
holy man to promulgate. Monkery, the worship 
of the virgin and the saints, &c. &c. all no doubt 
began in what was considered to be a good motive. 
It is chiefly with reference to the second advent 
that the exhortations to holy practice are founded. 
Peter says, 2d Ep. i. 10. “‘ The day of the Lord 
will come as a thief in the night, in the which the 
heavens shall pass away with a great noise, and 
the elements shall melt with fervent heat; the 
earth also, and the works that are therein, shall 
be burned up. Seeing then that all these things 
shall be dissolved, what manner of persons ought 
ye to be, in all holy conversation and godliness, 
looking for and hasting unto the coming of the 
day of God.” The Thessalonian church, which 
seems more than any other to have been living 
under the expectation of the coming of our Lord, 
was most marked for this ‘holy conversation.” 
‘We should consider what are the most prevalent 
errors in the church, and endeavour especially to 
adapt our conduct to the correction of these errors. 
It is needful for us not only to come out of the 
= world, but also out of the professing religious 
world, and particularly in a more strict observance 
of the Sabbath, concerning the sanctity of which 
very lax notions are entertained: yet the reward 
promised to its observance is strongly connected 
with the subject in hand. “If thou turn away 
thy foot from the Sabbath from doing thy plea- 
sure on my holy day, and call the Sabbath a 


207 


delight, the holy of the Lord, honourable, and 
shalt honour him, not doing thine own ways, nor 
finding thine own pleasure, nor speaking thine 
own words; then shalt thou delight thyself in the 
Lord, and I will cause thee to ride upon the high 
places of the earth, and feed thee with the heri- 
tage of Jacob thy father; for the mouth of the 
Lord hath spoken it.” Isa. lviii. 13. This is par- 
ticularly necessary, on account of our connection 
with the Continent, from which so many of .our 
countrymen have returned worse for what they 
have seen. It is said of St. John, that he was 
“in the spirit on the Lord’s day,” and we should 
be endeavouring to be mounting, as it were, up to 
heaven, and keeping our minds as free as possible 
from all secular employments, such as reading, 
and still more answering, letters, - newspapers, 
&c. &c. Laymen may be of great assistance to 
ministers in resisting the luxuriousness of the age, 
and it is particularly incumbent upon them to 
avoid throwing any temptation in the way of poor 
clergymen: ‘Take heed least at any time ye are 
overcome by surfeiting and drunkenness, so that 
that day come upon you unawares ;” and much of 
our Lord’s conversation was directed to the same 
end. The improvement of the present moment is 
our great business; for though the Lord is sitting 
still now, Isa. xviii. 4, yet is his eye fixed upon 
his prepared habitation, and the time of his com- 
ing draweth near. One of. the principal modes of 
effecting this is by being more diligent in obtain- 
ing a fuller acquaintance with. the subject, and by 
endeavouring to make the best of our knowledge, 


208 


and in using it only where it can produce the 
greatest effect, like soldiers who keep their fire 
till they come close to the enemy: we should 
shew that the doctrine itself is founded on the 
plain declarations of Scripture, supported by pro- 
phetic signs, by chronology, by scripture types, 
and by analogy. Our Lord’s discourse to his dis- 
ciples, Matt. xxiv. 4, may be of great use as a 
model on which to frame our own discourses. He 
begins by warning them against deceptions, ‘take 
heed that no man deceive you;” he next adduces 
scripture, and then refers to types and analogies. 
Much wisdom and judgment is needed, that we 
may not press analogies too far; and there is more | 
danger of this, from their power tc captivate the 
mind. Another mode of improvement of the time 
is, to bring others acquainted with the subject by 
preaching, by the press, by correspondence, con- 
versation, &c. Another mode is to ve fervent in 
prayer for the outpouring of the Holy Spirit; for 
until that is done, the blessing of the reign of 
Christ will not be perfected, Isa. xxxii. 13—16; 
and especially in promoting the establishment of 
prayer-meetings in every church and chapel for 
-= the minister of it. Another mode is to unite with 
all others in their efforts to spread the Gospel. 
Our religious societies, indeed, are far from being 
as perfect as they might be; but nevertheless, 
the attendance given to them is a tolerably fair 
criterion of the state of religion in any county or 
other place. There is ground to hope, also, that 
they are much improved of late; and there is a 
growing conviction amongst their members, that 


é 


209 


worldly patronage is not to be sought. Perhaps 
the worldly spirit which has crept in upon the 
Bible Society has been owing to its not com- 
mencing all its operations with prayer ; God has 
left it to itself, to mark that it is not by worldly 
patronage and money that this work is to be 
carried on. In private we cannot do better than 
meditate on the patient waiting and looking for 
the kingdom of our Lord, and enjoy the rich sub- 
ject for contemplation which it affords. 
Anastasius.—I must draw your attention par- 
ticularly to one remark of our valued brother, 
because it appears to me to be very important; 
I mean that wherein he says, ‘‘ we ought to come 
out, not only from the world, but hkewise from 
the religious world.” It is truly painful to reflect 
how many persons there are, who, in what they 
call renouncing the world, have only changed one 
world, in which they could make no figure, for 
another, in which they more easily obtain dis- 
tinction. Several are more in fear of this religious 
world, than ever they were of that world which 
they profess to have quitted. This fear is gene- 
rally masked under the specious pretext of ‘‘ pru- 
dence ;” but I never heard this word made use of 
at any of the meetings, nor in the Committees, but 
to express one of two ideas; either the withhold- 
ing of some portion of divine truth, or the turning 
the profession of it into a source of profit. It is 
owing to this fear that, whilst we constantly find 
in private circles the evils which are attendant on 
the public societies loudly condemned, the parties 
complaining never state their opinions to the Com- 
P 


210 


mittees, lest they should be offended ; whence it is 
clear that the fear of man is much more prevalent 
than love of the truth, or of the souls of their fellow- 
creatures. One of the most serious errors incul- 
cated by the Committees of Missionary Socteties 
is, that its members are the persons in the Society 
by whom the purposed work is carried on, and 
that the missionaries and agents who are sent out 
by them are the inferior members: the conse- 
quence of this opinion is a spirit of popery, and 
dogmatism exercised over the agents, and an 
overweening vanity engendered in the committee- 
rooms. The truth lays exactly in the reverse: 
the agents and missionaries, who go into foreign 
lands, or who travel about in their own country,’ 
daily preaching, or distributing bibles and tracts, 
in churches, chapels, houses, or in the open air, 
are the persons alone by whom any religious good 
is effected, and are the really efficient members. 
The Presidents, Vice-Presidents, Committee-men, 
&c. &c. are very useful in their subordinate and 
lower capacities of drawing up reports, making 
speeches, collecting money, &c.; but labour of 
this kind does not cost the abandonment of one 
luxury, nor scarcely the postponement of a single 
meal. But what is worse than all is, that the 
Committees take upon themselves to garble and 
suppress the reports which their agents send 
home; and instead of feeling themselves the con- 
scientious servants of their superiors, the agents, 
whose orders they ought to obey, and be merely 
the tools and channels of communication with the 
body of the subscribers, they erect themselves 


211 


‘into a lordship, both over the agents and over the 
subscribers, and publish, or hold back, and even 
fabricate (see publication on Strasburg Preface) 
just what they please. The agents employed by 
four of the largest societies in London have com- 
plamed to me of this; and I have caused repre- 
sentations to be made to three out of the four, 
being those only of which I was a member. This 
perversion of right judgment upon the matter, 
arises from the habit of estimating every thing 
by money. Because the home officers collect 
and distribute the money, they consider them- 
selves the most efficient members of the Society. 
In consequence of this, they pass resolutions, and 
issue orders to the missionaries and agents which 
they would not think of doing towards a fellow- 
miember of the Committee. Most acts of bene- 
volence and christian love are compounded for in 
this country for money: but, from what I have 
witnessed in foreign countries, I am much in- 
chned to believe that, with less money, and less 
show and ostentation, more personal kindliness is 
exercised in France and Italy than in England. 
The Tour to Alêt, and other accounts published 
by Mrs. Schimmelpenninck of the practice of Pa- 
pists, may well make many Protestants blush. The 
moment a portion of divine truth is kept back, in 
all probability more money flows in to a Society ; 
the Committee then begins to do one of two evils ; 
either lay up treasures on the earth, and by so 
much diminish their dependance for their daily 
supply on the Lord; or else they entice a less bold 
and zealous person to undertake the office of mis- 
P 2 eo 


212 


sionary, who would not otherwise have been em- 
ployed: whereas, if they would never compromise 
one iota of divine truth, nor employ any but those 
who had the zeal of the early Christians, God 
would always raise up agents and money, in exact 
proportion to the work which he has ordained 
should be done, and much more real good would 
be effected, with less deceitful show. The office 
and character of the missionary is grossly under- 
rated by our modern societies, which conduct 
their operations upon purely commercial, shop- 
keeping principles. Hear Bishop Hurd upon this 
subject. ‘The difficulties, the dangers, the dis- 
tresses of all sorts, which must be encountered by 
the Christian missionary, require a more than 
ordinary degree of that virtue, (charity,) and will 
only be sustained by him, whom a fervent love of 
Christ and the quickening graces of his Spirit 
have anointed, as it were, and consecrated to this . 
arduous service.—We have seen him forsake ease 
and affluence, a competency at least, and the ordi- 
nary comforts of society, and with the Gospel in 
his hand, and his Saviour in his heart, make his 
way through burning deserts, and the howling 
wilderness: braving the rage of climates, and 
all the inconveniences of long and perilous voy- 
ages.—I confess, when I reflect on all these 
things, I humble myself before such heroic vir- 
tue, or rather I adore the grace of God in Christ 
Jesus, which is able to produce such examples 
of it in our degenerate world.—When the duty is 
clearly enjoined by the Redeemer himself; when 
no weapon is employed by the enterprizing adven- 


213 


turer but that of the Spirit; when the friendliest 
affections prompt his zeal, and the object in view 
is eternal life; when, I say, the authority is un- 
questionable, and the means blameless; the mo- 
tive so pure, and the end so glorious—O ! let not 
the hard heart of infidelity profane such a virtue 
as. this with the disgraceful name of fanaticism or 
superstition.—Nay, candour, methinks, should be 
ready to make allowance for some real defects or 
miscarriages, which will ever attend the best per- 
formances of mortal men. What though some 
error in judgment, some impropriety of conduct, 
some infirmity of temper, I had almost said, some 
imbecility of understanding, be discernible in the 
zealous missionary? Something, nay much, may 
be overlooked, where so much is endured for 
Christ’s sake. It is enough that the word of the 
cross is preached in simplicity, and godly sin- 
cerity. He whose strength is made perfect in 
weakness, will provide that even the frailties of 
his servants contribute, in the end, to the success 
of so good a cause, and the display of his own 
glory.” Vol. viii. p. 29. Unless the managers of 
Missionary Societies think of their agents as men 
far superior to themselves in every necessary 
qualification for carrying on such works, looking 
up to them for advice, and obeying their direc- 
tions, the business must be conducted upon low, 
grovelling, money-making principles, derogatory 
to God, and useless to man. 

Philalethes.—Would it not be very imprudent 
for you to hold this language respecting the va- 
rious Committees ? 

Anastasius.— Bishop Latimer said, in his sermon 


214 


before the King, “ It rejoiceth me when my friends 
tell me that people find fault with my discretion ; 
for by likelihood, think I, the doctrine is true; 
for if they could find fault with the doctrine, they 
would not charge me with the lack of discretion.” 
If the question were merely one of opinion about 
the mode in which societies ought to consider 
their agents, I am quite willing that mine should 
be considered of less value than theirs. But if 
the managers of these bodies desire to be made 
useful in testifying of God’s truth, and in rescuing 
souls from the destruction which is coming down 
upon Babylon, they must adopt more scriptural 
views both of themselves and of their work. Surely 
the Committee-men must acknowledge themselves 
to have less zeal than the agents; less privation 
to endure ; and to evince, as far as outward acts 
ean evince such things, less faith, and less love to 
Christ’s salvation, and to the souls of men. Is it 
not then both their duty, and their best interest to 
sit at the feet of those who are their superiors in 
spiritual attainments? I am of course speaking 
only of those who are worthy to be missionaries ; 
not of those who have been tempted into that 
office by secular motives, of which latter kind there 
is a fearful number in every Society. The Com- 
mittee-men are richer than the missionaries ; have 
more secular pursuits; and more cares of this 
world; does it not therefore follow, that the seed 
of grace in their hearts is more choked than in the 
hearts of their agents? If they are Christians, if 
they know any thing practically of the growth of 
spiritual life in their own souls, they must also 
know, that in proportion as they are cumbered 


2195 


with the things belonging to this present dispen- 
sation, so, ceteris paribus, they are inferior in 
holiness (in all the relative senses of this word) to 
those who possess less. Instead of doing this, 
they look down upon their agents on the prin- 
ciples of the world, because they are poorer. 
‘‘Hearken, my beloved brethren, hath not God 
chosen the poor of this world rich in faith and 
heirs of that kingdom which he hath promised to 
them that love him? but ye have despised the 
poor.” There is scarce a single Society that has 
not incurred this censure of the Apostle, and 
which its most valuable agent has: not been 
obliged to quit in consequence. Instead of con- 
ducting the business of Missionary Societies as 
they would that of a merchant’s counting-house, 
and looking upon the agents as if they were the 
riders for orders, the Directors ought to take the 
example of our Lord when he sent out his dis- 
ciples, and that of the apostles when they began 
to preach the Gospel. They must crucify the 
maxims of the world, and act in all religious 
matters in direct opposition to them. If their 
agents get into prison, and undergo stripes, in- 
stead of being censured for lack of prudence, they 
ought to be praised, and encouraged for their 
zeal. Acts iv. 13—33. Instead of asking for direc- 
tion from Committees, they ought to be directed 
to follow the example of him who, as soon as 
Christ was revealed in him, did not go to consult 
even the apostles, and conferred not with flesh 
and blood, but went into Arabia, and was unknown 
by face to the churches. Gal.i. Even the Pope 


216 


of Rome sends out his missionaries cum omnibus 
facultatibus apostolicis, a liberality for which we 
should in vain seek a parallel in our London 
Societies. Let us beseech them, by the coming 
of the Lord, to examine themselves well in this 
matter; and may He give them a right under- 
standing in all things. 

Sophron. — Our blessed Lord says, ‘‘ Be ye 
ready, for in such an hour as ye think not, the 
Son of man cometh—blessed is that servant whom 
his Lord when he cometh shall find so doing,” 
Matt. xxiv. 44; the question then is, what is 
the special occupation in which we should wish 
to be found when the Lord comes? Certainly we 
shall answer, In preaching the cross of Christ; 
for, however necessary it may be to become 
acquainted with the second advent, so as to give 
meat to strong men, it is undoubtedly more ne- 
cessary to give milk to babes. The doctrine of 
the kingdom is of a peculiarly encouraging nature, 
and vouchsafed to few; the subject partakes of 
the nature of that hidden manna which the Lord 
gives to those who “ follow on to know” him, that 
it may support their fainting hearts, and above all 
things it is necessary, both for the clergy and for the 
laity, to preach the Gospel to the poor. So that 
there may be some situations in which it would be 
wrong for a clergyman to give himself up to the 
exclusive study of this subject. For example, if 
a clergyman in a poor wild district of Ireland, 
were to give up his time to study this doctrine for 
his own personal and intellectual profit, and, on 
that account, neglect the preaching of the Gospel 


217 


to the ignorant, he would be neglecting the most 
important duty of feeding the lambs of Christ ; for 
this doctrine is peculiarly seductive, and therefore 
more likely to be pursued with a morbid, because 
disproportionate, affection than any other. 

Anastasius.—It is on this account, perhaps, that 
the Lord has so often chosen his prophets, not 
from among the priests, but from among the laity. 
When Mr. Lewis Way first went into the ministry, 
having no prospect of being appointed to a paro- 
chial charge, he consulted a learned and pious 
clergyman in what way he could most benefit the 
church of Christ: his friend recommended him to 
study the prophetic books, assigning as a reason, 
that they were but ill understood, and that he, 
being Rector of a populous parish in a large city, 
had no time to devote to the subject, without 
neglecting more important, or at least more press- 
ing, duties. Mr. Way followed his advice, and 
God has been pleased to bless his labours, by 
honouring him to be the first person to revive the 
attention of the church to the long-forgotten sub- 
ject of the glorious Epiphany of the Lord, which 
he did, first, by private conversation, and, subse- 
quently, by letters published in the Jewish Expo- 
sitor. They have been reprinted in the form of a 
pamphlet, and.this now goes round the land, like 
Benaiah of old, slaying Adonijah, and all the 
enemies of Solomon, in every quarter. King, in 
his Signs of the Times, first sounded the alarm, in 
1787, but did not succeed in awakening the 
church as Mr. Way did. 

Polydorus.—In the passage to which our dear 


218 


brother Theophilus has referred us, Matt, xxiv. 48, 
our Lord represents the neglect of the doctrine 
of his coming as the cause of the seryant’s sin in 
smiting his fellow. Again, men are represented, 
in 2 Pet. iii. 3, as walking after their own lusts, 
and scoffing at the promise of his coming; while, 
on the other hand, a belief in it is shewn, in Phil. 
iii, 12—14, to be connected with a high tone of 
watchfulness and sobriety. The doctrine is al- 
ways associated in Scripture with joy, and held 
out as a source of comfort, rather than of threaten- 
ing, to the church. We should be careful to look 
at it, especially as for Christ’s glory, rather than 
our own personal interest in it. 

Philemon.—The Gospel is first called everlast- 
ing, or the Gospel of the age, awey, in the Reve- 
lations; and therefore, in this connexion, the 
charge of the Apostle Paul to Timothy, 2d Ep. 
iv. 1, 2, to preach the Gospel, and tọ which he 
urges him by the coming of Christ, is the more 
striking. In 2 Cor. y. 10, the doctrine is made 
use of to awaken sinners; and in 1 Thess. iy. 18, 
to promote the spirituality, love, and comfort of 
the church; and if we find it comfort our own 
souls, we shall no doubt make use of it for the 
same purpose to the souls of others. 2 Cor, i. 4, 
This doctrine is very likely to be useful in calling 
out Papists from Babylon, and to be the means of 
saving some of them: and in stirring up our own 
church to increased activity: particularly by shew- 
ing that this is the proper place, in the Christian 
scheme, in which a reward according to works 
will find its just position, and which fully explains 


219 


all the passages which speak of degrees of glory. 
It is eminently calculated also to lead to patience 
under trials, Prov. ii. 20—22, Ps. xxxvii. 28, and 
to stimulate to self-examination. It is also pecu- 
- larly a subject for Sabbath meditation, oaBBariopoc, 
Heb. iv. 9; and although the Committee of the 
Jews’ Society maintain, as a Committee, a perfect 
neutrality upon the question, I am happy to say 
that the majority of them are believers in it. 
Anastasuis.—Toplady, in a speech delivered at 
the Queen’s Arms, Newgate Street, says, “ I am 
of opinion, that good works, extensive usefulness, 
and eminent sufferings for Christ, will, in one 
respect, be followed by a proportionate degree of 
glory, and in another, not. I am one of those 
old-fashioned people who believe in the doctrine 
of the millenium, and that there will be two dis- 
tinct resurrections of the dead: 1st, of the just; 
and, 2dly, of the unjust; which last resurrection 
of the reprobate will not commence till 1000 years 
after the resurrection of the elect. In this glorious 
interval of 1000 years, Christ, I apprehend, will 
reign in person over the kingdom of the just, and 
that during this dispensation different degrees of 
glory will obtain.” Vol. iii. p. 474. To the same 
effect Dr. Gill says, ‘‘ Indeed, good men also will 
be judged according to their works, but not ad- 
judged to eternal life according to them; for there 
is no proportion between the best works of men 
and eternal life; eternal life is the free gift of 
God through Christ; but upon the judgment of 
them the distribution of rewards, or of peculiar 
and distinguished favours, more or less, in the 


920: 


kingdom state, will be according to every man’s 
works.” -Body of Div. vol. ii. p. 1062. The church 
is invisible, and so must the works be which will 
obtain reward. Jehovah has revealed himself as 
the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the 
God of Jacob. Abraham is the father of the faith- 
ful: of Isaac it is recorded, that he went out to me- 
ditate at eventide: and of Jacob, that he wrestled 
with God in prayer, and prevailed. It is only in 
faith, in meditation, and in prayer, that Jehovah is 
to be met with; and they who have found most 
delight in drawing near to him on earth, will have 
the place nearest to him in heaven. These are 
the works in the which there is great reward now, 
and will be greater hereafter ; and they alone can 
produce that abstraction from the world which 
causes the things belonging to it to sit loose upon 
us, and which makes us ready to part with them 
all, and to impart our own selves also, if we can 
glorify God by so doing, and communicate the 
knowledge of his salvation to our fellow-creatures: . 
these will be recognized as the fruit of the true 
vine (Hos. xiv. 8. John xv. 5.) at his coming, 
whilst all other works can be equally well per- 
formed by mere moralists. 

Aristo.— Whatever darkness there may be in. 
the minds of others upon the subjects which we 
have discussed in these conversations, we, who 
have partaken in them, can plead no such excuse 
for the neglect of any duty that ought to arise from 
a knowledge of these doctrines. We have had a 
good seed sown in our minds upon this point, and 
we shall incur an awful responsibility if we neglect 


221 


the right use of a grace so liberally conferred. We 
have laid our hearts open to one another in this 
matter, and there has not been more diversity 
of opinion manifested than existed amongst the 
apostles, nor than must always exist from variety 
of natural character, and where men are speaking 
upon subjects of deep interest, and without any 
spirit of flattery and mutual compromise: and 
rather than regret a manifestation of such variety, 
I would rejoice that, whilst all uniting in the ge- 
neral outline and important foundations, the dif- 
ferent gifts possessed by each may be useful to 
one another, and to the church at large, one sup- 
plying in his own way that which is lacking i in 
another. 1 Cor. xii. &c. 

The subject proposed by Philalethes may be con- 
sidered as influential upon us, in its, 1. personal, 
2. official, 3. ecclesiastical, and 4. political aspect. 
Wisdom is the grace which is most requisite in 
these latter times : we all agree that Dan. xii. 3. 
applies to these times. I think, too, that Mr. 
Stewart is correct in saying, in his discourses, 
that the knowledge there spoken of is spiritual 
knowledge, and not exactly as it is stated by 
Mr. Irving in his book; for no other but spiritual 
knowledge would receive this appellation from the 
Holy Spirit; and the expression refers to under- 
standing that which is sealed up, which none of 
the wicked shall understand. What was it that 
induced St. Paul to found the Gentile church, but 
his knowledge of the mystery of the Gentiles, as he 
plainly declares in Eph. iii.? This mystery of the 
coming of the Lord would not have been hid also, 


222 


unless, when revealed, it was to meet some mighty 
end. This end is to stem the tide of infidelity, 
which is more destructive than even superstition. 
This tide has set in with undiminished force since 
the French Revolution: and whereas superstition - 
is like a mist which obscures the view, and which 
does not require so powerful an influence to 
disperse it ; this is ike a deluge, which threatens 
to carry every thing before it, and which the 
fulfilment of prophecy is the only evidence to 
withstand. Miracles themselves would be insuf- 
ficient, and indeed their great efficacy for convic- 
tion lay in that they were foretold; and mere 
external evidence has made a thousand infidels for 
one believer. Thus the opening of this subject 
now, and the consequences to arise from it, is to 
serve a purpose analogous to that for which the 
_ former mystery, which had been hid, was m those 
days revealed to St. Paul. Observe the effects of 
this subject; all who have studied it confess that 
it has led them to examine the structure of the 
` Holy Scriptures more accurately than they ever 
did before, and to compare the word with the 
providence of God. They find that invariably 
“ before weird there’s word;” and as the revela- 
tion of the mystery of the Gentiles was to St. Paul, | 
so was the revelation of justification by faith to 
.the reformers, and so is the revelation of this truth 
to us now. It looks a small thing to us now that 
we should be believers in Christ, and the Jews 
hot; ft was a grain of mustard seed at the first. 
When Luther began to struggle for the doctrine of 
justification, it was not imagined how great a deli- 


223 


verance by it would be effected for the church; 
this doctrine of the second coming has already 
been more powerful in redeeming several young 
men in London from unbelief, than all other argu- 
ments together. This doctrine is the mean of 
revealing the standing and bearing of all other 
doctrines. It is indeed the wisdom mentioned 
by Daniel, and has thrown light upon the whole 
Gospel. I did not understand the incarnation, 
neither did I understand the doctrine of election, 
until I saw it in connection with this subject: 
I saw the latter indeed in the decree of God, and 
I saw the manifestation of it in a separation from 
the world. I saw also in the history of the church, 
that in proportion as she became Arminian she 
relapsed into the world, and that in proportion as 
she became Calvinistic she came up out of the 
world: but now I perceive that its ultimate end 
is to prepare a bride for the Lamb; inhabitants 
for the new Jerusalem ; prime ministers to Christ 
in his kingdom, and governors of the world. It is 
necessary to set forth foreordination as the only 
security for final safety, and therefore as the 
grand inducement to embrace salvation. For 
how could you be prevailed on to go into a ship 
‘built in the Eclipse,” half scuttled, doubtful 
whether it would reach its destined port or no? 
but you would have no hesitation when you saw 
the tightness and security of its planks, the abso- 
lute certainty of its safety, and Jesus Christ him- 
self standing at the helm. The wisdom of the 
wise virgins may be supposed to consist in this, 
as well as that of James i. 5, and 11.7. Next 


224 


after, and in consequence of, this wisdom is turn- 
ing many to righteousness; and we learn the 
importance of understanding and knowledge from 
Eph. i. 18, and Col. i. 9. If any man is using 
his knowledge of God amiss, it 1s because his 
knowledge is as yet incomplete; instead, there- 
fore, of opposing his views, the only way by which 
you can proceed to recover him is, to shew him 
more of the word of God, telling him at the same 
time that he cannot understand it but by the 
Spirit of God. For the word came before the 
Spirit, and the Spirit never causes us to make 
progress but in prayer. Prayer is the form of 
our submission to the Father, as faith is the form 
of our submission to the Son: and it is not by cau- 
tions, and limitations, and Arminian expedients, 
but by declaring all the counsel of God, by giving 
a full revelation of the awful sovereignty of the 
Father, and of the fulness of Christ, that the Holy | 
Spirit makes progress in the soul. Ministers 
have a direct calling to this end; but it may be 
asked, how are laymen to do this? I answer, 
by letting wisdom drop at every opening of the 
mouth, and by seeking the Holy Spirit to abide 
ever in the heart, in all situations into which his 
duty calls him. The Spirit is not a set of-rules, 
but a principle: Christ is the embodying of rules, 
to whom we must be conformed; so let every | 
man pray for the Spirit, and let him look always 
unto Jesus, so that the word he speaks may be 
Christs word, and the character he exhibits be 
Christ’s character; and thus let him not only pre- 
sent truth, but the True One also. 


225 


2. With respect to our official ‘character, as mi- 
nisters, and particularly in preaching, we must 
remark, that the work of God was wrought by the 
preaching of the apostles; and if we preached as 
faithfully as they did, our preaching would be 
attended with the same effects. If preaching be 
weighed against all other occupations of a Chris- 
tian minister, it is as 99 out of 100. The per- 
fection of a Christian would consist in having all 
knowledge; but all human science must be but 
the rearward to back the doctrine, whilst the 
pourtraying of Christ crucified, buried, risen, 
glorified, and coming, must be the van. We must 
preach a whole Christ; declare him in all his 
offices: the dispersion of a minister’s congrega- 
tion may be the effect of his fidelity, as Paul lost - 
all in Asia, and the fulness of his chapel may be 
the effect of his compromising unfaithfulness ; and 
in this the trial of a minister consists. The 
greatest truths are liable to the greatest abuse ; 
and thus it has been with this doctrine; but ex- 
perience has shewn its great profit to many souls : 
it has proved particularly humbling to ambitious, | 
learned, and titled men.. The contemplation of any 
particular doctrine indents the seal of. that doc- 
trine on the soul, and it is only by Christ being 
preached in his perfection, that men can receive 
the indentation of the whole character of Christ. 
The Spirit can take any part of truth and impress 
it; and therefore all doctrines must be preached, 
because we cannot tell where the Spirit will be- 
gin: he may make use of the glory, as well as of 
the humiliation of Christ, in first impressing a soul; 

Q 


226 


and I believe that he takes a part according to 
the spirit of the times. Satan has always been 
setting up a spurious Christ, a mimic of Christ; 
and he is now setting up a spurious, a mimic 
kingdom of peace, blessedness, and perfect world, 
by knowledge, societies, &c.; and the preaching 
of the Gospel, the glad tidings of the true king- 
dom, will be the appointed means of defeating his 
designs; ‘as it is only by Christ, the redeemer of 
the world, being about to come and take pos- 
session of it, that Satan, the prince of this world, 
can be cast out: just as the setting forth of the 
true doctrine of justification by the reformers was 
the means by which the false doctrine of it set up 
by the Papists was cast out. I have stood upon 
platforms in London till I have trembled in seeing 
the tide of infidelity which had set in upon the 
Committees who conducted them. I have heard 
clergymen called upon, and urged repeatedly to 
withhold their views of Scripture, lest certain 
men who were present should be offended, and 
withdraw their subscriptions. I was myself di- 
rected by a clergyman, at the anniversary of the 
Hibernian Society, to abstain from alluding to 
Popery, because a compact had been entered 
into with some popish orators, that nothing dis- 
pleasing to them should be said. This is a proof, 
amongst many more, that money, which is the 
least part, is become in the Societies the cines 
part of all they aim at. 

3. With respect to the ecclesiastical aspect in 
‘which this doctrine becomes practical, our -first 
duty is to the respective churches to which we 


227 


severally belong, under Christ, the head of those 
churches. It was with this view that Mr. Irving 
tells us he had his work on prophecy first printed 
and published in Scotland; and although it ap- 
peared unwise, in a pecuniary point of view, 
inasmuch, as he is an outcast, and scorned in his 
own land, yet it is now selling largely. Let every 
ordinance -in our churches be highly honoured, 
and. especially because the Christian Church is 
the grand type of the kingdom of Christ; and as. 
the first fruits of the spirit are the first fruits of 
the knowledge of Christ, so is the chureh here 
the first fruits of the church hereafter; for the 
ordinances. of the church, and differences of de- 
grees in the church, are types of the ordinances. 
and-ranks in the church above; and it is from this 
view alone that the unity of the true church will 
be firmly held, and that we shall have no EASY 
amongst sects, but real brotherly love. 

4. As to the political aspect of this doctrine, 
the mystery of subjection to the powers that be, 
cannot be understood but by reference to the 
coming ef the Lord. Unless we know that power 
is unredeemed, and that the kingdoms of this 
world are the kingdoms of Satan, we shall be 
always wishing and aiming at setting up a better 
kingdom. Instead of acknowledging themselves 
merely the vicegerents of Christ, and ruling as 
kings in‘early Christian times confessed they did, 
only by the grace or favour of God, the present 
sovereigns are defending their titles to their seve~ 
ral. thrones by a reference to a “ Monarchieal 
Principle,” and the “ Doctrine of Legitimacy.” 

Q2 


228 


The people, in their turn, reject this doctrine, 
and broach another equally, though oppositely, 
removed from the truth, crying out, “the People, 
the source of legitimate Power.” „Whilst the true 
source of legitimate power is neither in an ab- 
stract principle, nor in the people, but in the 
blessed and only Potentate, the King of kings, 
. and sole Ruler of princes and of people. Satan’s 
great art is to put the first last, and the last first, 
and to take part in purifying the present kingdom. 
Thus, as the religious societies are trying to bring 
about a millenium without a personal coming of 
Christ, so are philosophers and radicals trying to 
bring about a perfect state of rule and government 
before Christ come to set up his kingdom. In 
the same way the whole doctrine of toleration, 
as now generally broached, is off its hinges, and 
is nothing but a fruitless attempt to make Satan 
harmless, and to bind him before his time. We 
did, indeed, bind him at the Reformation, but not 
by the constitution, nor by the exertions of states- 
men and warriors, but by. the blood and martyr- 
dom of our reformers. The British Constitution 
has become liké the brazen serpent: as it was 
Christ in the serpent which healed the bitten 
Israelites, so it was Christ in the British Consti- 
tution, who was preached at the Reformation, 
which preserved the English people. But now 
Christ is left out and forgotten, and the Consti- 
tution without him is become the object of idola- 
try from Panamá to Kamschatka, and which the 
Lord will break and bruise to powder. Civil 
government is doubtless the greatest blessing 


229 


which God can confer upon a people, but the way 
to preserve it is to look to the church as to the 
kernel within the shell. Perfection in all things 
turns upon two points, the humiliation and the 
glory of Christ. 

Anastasius. —This view is doubtless correct, and 
hence all the schemes of setting up constitutions 
have failed. Sicily, Naples, France, Prussia, 
Spain, Portugal, are proofs of this: their object 
was, not the purity of the religion of Christ, nor 
zeal for his word and a determination to possess 
it, but a mere struggle between contending fac- 
tions for worldly power and aggrandizement. 
With respect to the remark, that the contempla- 
tion of any particular doctrine impresses the cha- 
racter of that doctrine on the soul, many striking 
_ confirmations of it may be daily observed. I have 
met with men, who have so long meditated on, 
and preached about the sovereignty and unchange- 
ableness of the decrees of Jehovah, that his other 
attributes, such as tender pity, loving-kindness, 
and mercy, were nearly overlooked; and thus a 
harsh, hard, and forbidding temper was engen- 
dered in their minds. I have met again with 
others who have entirely overlooked the awfulness 
and severity of a sin-hating God, and dwelt ex- 
clusively upon his love, till their spiritual views © 
` had assumed a kind of sickly sentimentality and 

morbidness, wholly removed from that manly 
energy (virtus apernc, 2 Pet. i. 5,) which the Chris- 
tian is exhorted to add to his faith. The first is 
the error into which some really pious men have 
fallen, and which has been the cause of their 


230 


being. unjustly- accused of Antinomianism : . the 
second is the error into which the Quietists fell, 
and which preyented their being so useful in their 
generation as they otherwise would have heen. 
These two extremes shew us how important it is 
to take our views of the character of Jehovah pre- 
cisely as he has been pleased to reveal himself, 
lest we be found at last to have been worshipping 
a creation of our own fancies, and not the. God 
and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ; and hence 
also, if we desire to embody Christ in all we say 
and do, we must study his whole character, and 
not a fragment of it merely: we must study him, 
“ thanking God for hiding” his coming judgments 
‘‘from the wise and prudent,” as well as when he 
weeps over the devoted city. In this have con- 
sisted the errors both of Calvinists and of Armi- 
nians, that each has taken partial views of the 
Scriptures, and neither have been so wrong ia 
what they have affirmed, as in what they have 
denied. Perhaps no writer is more fair than 
Calvin himself, a man whom many. abuse without 
baving read. Lord Bacon's Christian Paradoxes 
might be useful studies to both parties. 
Isocrates.—Although it is alleged that the. king- 
dom of Christ ought not to be made the subject 
of our preaching, it is remarkable that St, Paul 
thought otherwise; for we find that he proclaimed 
it at Thessalonica, Acts xvii. 7, notwithstanding 
he remained there only three weeks, ver. 2. If 
a great extension of real Christianity is to take 
place before the coming of Christ, the Christians 
will compose the majority of mankind:. but the 


231 


.Bible eyery where represents the people of God 
as exceedingly few in comparison with the rest of 
the world, and that Christ’s is “a little flock,” 
even to the end of this dispensation: consequently 
another Bible would be necessary, for the record 
which we now have, would no longer describe the 
state of the world as it would then be: this ap- 
pears to me to be an unanswerable argument to 
those who maintain that our societies are to intro- 
duce an increased diffusion of religious ADONAN E

: Philalethes—Is there not danger in building 
too much upon analogies and types drawn from 
the Old Testament Scriptures ? 

_ Anastasius—lIt is hard to say that it is not 
possible to build too much upon analogies; and the 
propriety of that which is so built. must be left. to 
the judgment of each individual. But it is much 
more obvious that too little may be built upon 
typical persons, things, and acts, since we are 
told that Moses wrote of Christ; and consequently, 
unless we find Christ in the writings of Moses, we 
miss the subject-matter of Moses’ writings. The 
greater number of the things, for which we have 
the direction of Christ and his apostles to consider 
as typical, are the very things which, without such 
authority, we should be accused of being most 
fanciful in so understanding: amongst these we 
may only refer to the pillar, cloud, rock, manna, 
brazen serpent, Hagar and Ishmael, Jonah, Abra- 
ham. Bishops Horne and Horsley have laid it 
down as an axiom, that every Psalm which con- 
tains a passage which can be applied literally 


232 


alone to Christ, properly belongs to him exclu- 
sively. If we do not find Christ in typical per- 
sons and things, how shall we find him in the 
book of Genesis at all? We might as well admit 
at once the absurdity that neither a future state 
nor a resurrection of the dead are revealed in the 
Old Testament. The types and figures are in- 
timately connected with the names of God and 
of Christ; a right understanding of the meaning 
of which is of more importance than many persons > 
seem to imagine. The names of things are the 
powers of those things. When God brought the 
animals to Adam he named them; ‘‘ and whatever 
Adam called every living creature, that was the 
name thereof.” Gen. xi: 19. No doubt the name 
which Adam gave-to each expressed its character 
and essential property. The names of God are not 
arbitrary sounds; and the only real imperfection 
in the English authorised translation of the Bible, 
is the slovenly manner in which all the names by 
which Jehovah has been pleased to make himself 
known to his church, have been rendered by the 
two words “God” and “Lord.” Tilloch has an ex- 
cellent dissertation on this subject, which I strongly 
recommend to your attention. The greatest con- 
fusion is constantly made in sermons by using the 
names of Christ as if they were arbitrary sounds: 
a confusion analogous to that which arises from 
blending figures, two remarkable instances of 
which occur in the ordinary expressions of the 
Papists to designate the Virgin Mary, namely, 
“the mother of God;” and also in one equally 
common amongst the Moravians, who use the 


233 


phrase ‘“‘Epoux sanglant” as synonimous with 
Jesus Christ. With respect to the first, in so 
much as Mary was his mother, he was not God ; 

and vice versd, in so much as he was God, Mary 
was not his mother. So likewise with respect to 
the other: in so much as he is the spouse of the 
church, he is not bloody ; and in so much as he is 
the bloody victim, he is not the spouse. The word 
Redeemer is perpetually used in sermons, where 
it confuses the argument, and by writers who 
would be horrified at the doctrine contained in 
the word, if they comprehended it. Rightly un- 
derstood, the types and figures are but amplifica- 
tions, as it were, of the various names, properties, 
powers, and relationships of Immanuel to his 
church; so that the objection savours of the in- 
fidelity of the day, which has not only overrun 
the world, but pervaded every part of the church. 
Believers should be much on their guard against 
it, for few are aware how much it creeps in and 
infuses itself into every article of their faith. This 
is the cause of our faith not producing, in these 
days, the effect which it produced in the days of. 
the apostles, and is a point which we ought to 
make the subject of earnest prayer. Christians 
properly divide the inhabitants of Christendom 
into only two classes, believers and unbelievers ; 

and although this is a correct division, yet never- 
theless there is, in the faith even of the most 
pious, a leaven of the prevailing infidelity, which 
carries us away when we are least aware of it. 

Laymen are particularly exposed to this, and 
especially those whose secular duties call them 


to act as legislators, magistrates, &c. &c. They 
on principle endeavour to check evil, and whilst 
they lop, sometimes successfully, the most luxu- 
riant branches of sin, become decoyed into the sup- 
position that mankind is getting essentially better. 
Basilicus.—The great practical use to be de- 
rived from the doctrine of the Second Coming of 
Christ, is to lead us ever to look for such his 
glorious Epiphany, hastening towards, and loving 
it, as the fulfilment of all promise, the scope of 
all prophecy, the consummation of all dispensa- 
tions, and the restitution of all things, in and 
through him. Such a view of the subject can 
alone emancipate the soul from bondage under 
the elements of the present world, deliver it from 
the fear of death, and the power of the Prince of 
darkness, and does actually, to those who possess 
it, (not as a speculation, but) as a principle, effect 
this, and many other good purposes, by the com- 
prehensive scope which it takes of the scheme of 
revelation, and the foretaste which it yields, even 
now, of a tangible and attainable felicity on the 
theatre of suffering humanity: when the great 
catastrophe of all intermediate conditions shall 
issue in the establishment of the paradise of God, 
co-extensive at least with the whole of Imma- 
NUEL’s LAND, then made glorious, and fitted for, 
the habitation of the righteous; the joint heirs 
with Christ, and Abraham, their common father of 
a renovated world, and inhabitants of that New 
Jerusalem, which is the mother of us all. 
Philalethes.— Without referring to chronology, 
a subject upon which I purpose asking for in-

_Anastastus.—The present dispensation is uni- 
versally called the militant state of the church, 
not only from the comparison drawn by the Apostle 
between a Christian and a soldier, but also in re- 
ference to the life or reign of David, which pre- 
ceded that of peace under Solomon. At the close 
of the period of David’s reign, but before it was 
concluded, an attempt was made to set up Ado- 
nijah, through whom it was expected that a period 
of blessedness might be enjoyed, which was how- 
ever exclusively promised under Solomon. I have 
already referred you to the present attempt ac- 
tually making to convert. the world by means of 
societies, instead of by the personal coming of 
Christ as the antitype of this part of the history. 
The attempt to set up Adonijah called up the friends 
of Solomon to cause him to be proclaimed as the 
fulfiller of the purposes of Jehovah, although 
David’s reign was not concluded. This, then, is 
the date in the antitype of the present period. 
The militant state of the church is not concluded’; 
but nevertheless some of us are proclaiming that 
there is to be no reign of blessedness, no exten- 
sion of real religion, whatever extension of pro- 
fession there may be, until Christ the king’s reign 
is begun. When he does actually mount his 
throne, will also be the day of his taking his elect 
to himself, as Solomon was crowned on the day of 
his espousals. Cant. iii. 11. In the mean time the 
proclamation is making. Some interesting re- 


i 236 
marks upon this subject have been made at dif- 
ferent times in the Jewish Expositor. As the 
histories of individuals are given for ensamples to 
us in our private capacities, so are the histories of 
public events intended as ensamples or warnings 
to us in our national or collective characters. 
Philalethes.—But Elias is to come before the 
great and dreadful day of the Lord, and therefore 
the world will have full warning of it. 
Anastasius.—Doubtless Elias will come, and 
come immediately before the great and dreadful 
day of the Lord, and those who believe him when 
he is come will be warned; but the world will 
deny him even whilst he talks to them, as they 
denied him when he came in John the Baptist, 
before the first coming of Christ. | | 
Philalethes— When do you think we may ex- 
pect him ? | | | 
Anastasius.—Let us examine this point fully. 
Malachi is the last of the prophets; that is, the 
spirit of prophecy ceased with Malachi, or rather 
was in abeyance from the time at which he died, 
being 400 years before Christ, until the time 
when it was revived in the person of John the 
Baptist. In his last chapter he begins with in- 
Viting attention to “ the day that shall burn as an 
oven; and all the proud, and all that do wickedly, 
shall be stubble, and the day that cometh shall 
burn them; but unto you that hear my name shall 
the Sun of Righteousness arise; and ye shall 
tread down the wicked: remember the law of 
Moses my servant. Behold, I will send you 
Elijah the prophet, before the coming of the great 


237 


and dreadful day of the Lord: and he shall turn 
the heart of the fathers to the children, and the 
heart of the children to their fathers, lest I come 
and smite the earth with a curse.” Moses and 
Elijah are the types of the law and the prophets: 
and if there be a passage in Scripture which 
would seem to require to be literally fulfilled, 
even by the person of Elias again appearing, 
surely this is that passage: so the Jews under- 
stood it; for “his disciples asked him, saying, 

Why then say the scribes that Elias must first 
come ?” and Jesus ‘“‘ answered and said unto them, 
Elias truly shall first come and restore all things; 
but I say unto you, that Elias is come already, 
and they knew him not, but have done unto him 
whatsoever they listed. Then the disciples un- 
derstood that he spake unto them of John the 
Baptist.” Matt. xvii. 10. When the angel was 
sent to Zacharias to announce the birth of John, 
he was commissioned to inform him, that “ he 
should go in the spirit and power of Elias, to 
turn the hearts of the fathers to the children,” 
Luke i. 17, evidently quoting the very words of 
Malachi. It follows, therefore, that notwithstand- 
ing the expectation of a literal personal appear- 
ance of Elias, we have the authority first of the 
angel, and secondly of our Lord, that it was not 
the person of Elias, but the spirit of Elias in the 
person of another, which was the subject of that 
prophecy, adding further, ‘‘ and if ye will receive 
it, this is Elias which was for to come.” Matt. 
xi. 14, Our Lord also says, that ‘‘among those 


238 


-= which are born. of woman there is. not. a greater - 
prophet than John ;” and also, that he is ‘‘much 
more than a prophet,” Luke vii. 28. 26. The- 
prophets before John had only seen Christ in 
vision, but John saw him in his human body. 
walking upon the earth, and pointed to him with 
his finger: ‘‘ but he who is least in the kingdom 
of God is greater than” John, for he will see him 
glorified. If, then, it was the spirit of Elias, and 
not the person of Elias, which was foretold as to- 
precede the first coming, and that is undeniable, 
it follows, by parity of reasoning, that it-1s the 
spirit of Elias, and not the person of Elias, which 
is to precede the second coming. What then was 
the spirit of Elias, the spirit of prophecy, to do? | 
‘and why was it revived immediately before the 
coming of Christ? Doubtless in order to testify of 
him, and to give notice to the world that he was 
in it. All attention to the prophetic Scriptures - 
in reference to the second coming of Christ had 
ceased ; the spirit of prophecy has been in abey- 
ance, as it were, ever since the third century, a 
period of above 1500 years, until now, when it 
‘has pleased God to awaken the attention of his 
church again to the subject, and to excite many 
persons in every part of England, at one and the 
same time, to cry, “ Fear God, and give glory to 
‘him, for the hour of his judgments is come.” The 
virgins are gone out to meet the bridegroom; the 
ery is making, “ Behold the Bridegroom cometh, 
‘go ye out to meet him.” This, then, is the spirit 
‘of Elias testifying in us of the second coming. of

Christ in his glorified body, as it did in John of 
the first coming of Christ in his body of humi- 
liation, Zwua rne raravwoewc. Phil. iii. 21. 

Philalethes.—| think you have made out this 
case very clearly; but what do you say to the 
other purport of his coming, namely, to turn the 
hearts of the fathers to the children, and the hearts 
of the children to the fathers? 
. Anastasius.—Let us ascertain who are called in 
the Holy Scripture the “fathers,” and then we 
shall have little difficulty in ascertaining who are 
“the children ;” and also whether the same Spirit 
has-turned the hearts of one to the other which 
has borne witness to the second coming of our 
Lord from heaven. St. Paul tells us, that he was 
“taught according to’ the perfect manner of the 
law of the fathers.” Acts xxii. 3. In Heb. i. 1, 
it is said, that ‘‘God spake unto the fathers by 
the prophets ;” and again, when describing the 
Israelites, in Rom. ix. 5, the same Apostle says of 
them, “ whose are the fathers :” and in xi. 28, he 
speaks of them ‘‘as beloved for the fathers’ sake :” 
and in 1 Cor. x. l, he says, that “all our fathers 
were under the cloud,” &c. From which passages 
it js clear, that by the expression “ fathers” is 
intended the Jewish nation, and consequently by 
that of “children” is meant the Gentiles. The 
first person, who in these “last days” called the 
attention of the church to the nearness of the 
second coming of our Lord, was, as I before ob- 
served to you, Mr. Lewis Way: it is he also who 
has given the most undeniable proofs of his anxiety 
to turn the hearts: of the Gentile children to act 


240 


more leniently towards their Jewish fathers; and 
he succeeded in procuring the signature of all the 
Allied Sovereigns of Europe at Aix-la-Chapelle to 
a Protocol in their favour. They who are most 
earnest in their endeavours to promote this, espe- 
cially those whose earnestness is founded upon 
the examination of the Scriptures, are likewise 
the persons who are most alive to the approach of 
the glorious kingdom of the Lord. So that we 
have proof, from matters of fact, that the same per- 
sons who are influenced by one of those great 
subjects are equally influenced by the other also. 
This therefore, furnishes addititional evidence that 
the same Spirit, which induces the testimony 
analogous to that of Elias before the first advent, 
induces also the turning of the hearts of the 
fathers to the children, and of the children to the 
fathers, thereby fulfilling all the points which ‘it 
was foretold that Elias should come to perform. 

Philalethes.—Although you do not expect that 
Christendom in general will take warning from 
the preaching of these truths, what do you think 
will be the effect produced upon professors of 
religion ? 

' Anastasius.—Our Lord likens the whole mass 
of the professing world who are on the look out 
for his coming to ten virgins, half of whom are 
proved falsely pretending to be his disciples. 
Some of the most accurate observers of the de- 
ceitfulness of the human heart have drawn similar 
conclusions. If you will peruse Mead’s “ Almost 
a Christian,” and some of Mr. Bickersteth’s works 
on the religion of the heart, you will find this — 


241 


idea enlarged upon more in detail. The Bishop of 
Llandaff has preached an admirable sermon lately 
before the Society for Promoting Christian Know- 
ledge, in which he has dwelt much upon the 
important fact that ‘profession is not religion.” 
There are many persons of comparative wealth 
and influence now in the world, who, under the. 
excitement arising from sickness, or domestic 
calamity, have met with some one who has ex- 
plained to them the scheme of salvation as it is in 
Christ Jesus. Having bestowed sufficient pains 
upon the subject to apprehend it by their intel- 
lect, they are pleased with the attainment of a 
new point of knowledge, and long for an oppor- 
tunity of displaying it; as mineralogists, che- 
mists, naturalists, &c. do the various sciences 
with which they are conversant. Thus they are 
brought to subscribe to, and perhaps attend 
the meetings of, various religious societies; their 
names are associated with them: their reputa- 
tion for consistency is implicated in continuing 
to support them; their fame is connected with 
the extension of religion; and they really feel 
an interest in its success. All this may take 
place in a very considerable degree, without being 
united to Jesus Christ, by partaking of the same 
spirit which was in him; without being graffed 
into that living vine; without, in short, being 
born of the Holy Ghost. This ought not to sur- 
prise us. The case of Ananias. and Sapphira 
shews that in the days when the profession of the 
religion of Jesus Christ was attended with peril 
of life, yet there were motives sufficiently strong 
R 


242 


to urge persons to profess it, and even to itiduce 
avaricious men to part with their lands in order- 


to evidence the sincerity of their attachment to 
the rising sect. Lots wife affords a similar in- 
stance: she no doubt willingly coincided with 
her husband in protesting against the wickedness 
of her neighbours, and joined him im the ob- 
servances of private and domestic devotion. Had 
any one questioned her sincerity, she would have 
complained of their hard thoughts of her, and de- 
manded the grounds of their uncharitable opinion. 
She left her home at the command of God, be- 
lieving the speedy fulfilment of his threatened 
judgments: yet notwithstanding her seeming zeal 
and pious obedience, she is selected by our Lord 
as the subject of a warning upon this very point. 
She could do great things, but her heart was still 
not right with God. Now the profession of belief 
in the Gospel is with many the road to distinc- 


tion, and with some the road to emolument; rarely 


is it attended in England with persecution, or even 
privation. And if persecution were now to arise, 
there would be but little trial in it, for the number 
who would sympathize with the sufferer is so 
great as to deprive the endurance of its greatest 
sting. Popularity takes away half the terrors of 
martyrdom. Our Lord was alone: many of His 
earliest followers nearly so; and also the re- 
formers, (such as Lord Cobham;) but the non- 
conformists, Whitefield, &c. have had the applause 
of multitudes, and therefore little or no trial. The 
Swiss who have been persecuted lately at Geneva 
and Lausanne have been few, and suffered much;

` and the Christian public in England has not sym- 
pathized with them as it ought. The church is 
rich, and at ease; the very situation which our 
Lord deseribes as the most dangerous to indi- 
viduals, and consequently to communities: and if 
communities as well as individuals are not daily 
growing in Christ, depend upon it they are grow- 
ing owt of him. The subject is full of alarm in 
the highest degree: let it be our unceasing prayer 
that- our lamps of profession may be filled with the 
oil of the Holy Ghost, that we may be always 
ready : let us recollect also, that whilst the foolish 
haif is represented as asleep, even the wise half is 
described as slumbering; so that we have addi- 
tional reason to struggle against the drowsiness 
predicted as belonging to this subiect. It is one 
thing to be ignorant of any truth, and it is another 
to reject it when laid before us. I would ear- 
nestly conjure every one not only to use caution 
in adopting an opinion, but much more caution in 
refusing to examine one, however new it may 
appear, in support of which so large a portion of 
God’s word has been brought forward. It may 
happen that some to whom this statement is made, 
by: rejecting it, not because the testimony is in- 
sufficient, but because the matter interferes with 
their preconceived opinions, may be struck, like 
Zacharias of old, with a judicial dumbness upon 
this glorious subject, until the time of its accom- 
plishment; and be debarred from the possession of

themselves shine as stars in the firmament of that-
