- Philalethes.—Do you doubt that there- exists 
much real religion amongst those persons who 
compose what is called the religious world ? 

Anastasius.—I by no means doubt this; nor 
that notwithstanding the worldly spirit which has 
crept into them, the societies now existing in 
England for religious purposes, form the most 
splendid monument which ever was reared by 
Christian philanthropy. The clergy, called the 
evangelical, whether in the church of England or 
in dissenting churches, are alone the salt which 
has seasoned the whole land of Britain. This. 
term evangelical is the term of reproach with 
which they have been reproached for the name. of 
Christ. The term Christian was originally one 
of reproach, till faithless men assumed it, and it 
then ceased to be so: the terms Waldenses, 
Hussites,. Lollards, (which means Psalm-singers) 
Wickliffites, Protestants, Puritans, Methodists, 
and in these days Evangelicals and Calvinists, 
are all terms of reproach which the followers of 
Christ have had to bear in different succeeding 
ages of the church; happy are they who have 
borne it justly, for the spirit of grace and of glory 
has rested upon them. But as I have just ob- 
served to you that the church is in the most 
perilous state in which a church can be, namely, 
that of ease, and of being increased with goods, 
and all men speaking well of her; therefore it is 
the part of those who really love her, not to 
flatter, but to arouse her; to warn her to be sure 


245 


that her gold is that of the sanctuary; her riches 
those with which her Lord has endowed her; 
her garments of his putting on; their cleanliness 
given by being washed in his blood; her discern- 
ment by having her eyes anointed with his eye- 
salve, so that she may be thoroughly prepared 
to’ open the moment he knocks, and go in with 
him to the marriage feast. What I say unto you, 
I say unto all, watch; and so: much the more as 
ye see the day approaching. Mankind are prone 
to priestcraft; and the priests of all creeds 
have arrogated to themselves the office of being 
depositories of the knowledge of the road to 
heaven. The grand sign to the world that the 
evangelical clergy, and all who have professed 
the sentiments they profess, under whatever name 
they may have gone, are the true ministers. of 
God, consists in their having renounced all pre- 
tence to a monopoly of this knowledge. They 
have freely declared that they know nothing but 
what is contained in a book, which the people 
might read as well as themselves; and they have 
been ever willing to let the doctrines they incul- 
cated be tried by that unerring standard ; herein 
following the example of the Apostles, who re- 
ferred always to the scriptures in corroboration of 
what they advanced ; declaring that they would 
not have aes over the faith of the people, 
2 Cor. i. 25, nor lord it over God’s heritage. This 
test is sufficient to condemn the church of Rome, 
if every other were wanting ; and not only popish 
priests, but all clergy of every creed, who do 
not make the Word of God paramount to every 


246 


ether consideration, mark thereby their conscious- 
ness that their preaching will not stand that trial. 
It was this conviction that raised up all the 
worldly clergy of England to oppose the Bible 
Society, and to pretend a zeal for the church 
against the book cn which that church is built. 
But in speaking of the religious world we must 
judge of it at large by the rules which our Lord 
has laid down, the first of which is love of the 
brethren; and if this rule be the test now, it will 
be found that there never was a period in the 
history of the church when there was so little 
of it. Not only in the time of the. reformation, 
but long suosequently, when popery reared its 
self-justifying front once more under the mask 
of Arminianism, the Christians of England 
warmly sympathized with their brethren in Hol- 
land and France; they did so likewise during 
the persecution of the saints under Louis XIV. 
But at the present day the evangeltcal clergy, 
who have been driven from their homes by the 
infidels of Switzerland and Germany, have drawn 
forth no sympathy from the religious. world in 
England; nay,-what is worse, it has taken part 
with their persecutors. This is a strong charge, 
but unfortunately too well proved in the publica- 
tions issued by the Committee of the Bible So- 
ciety, relative to the Strasburg Preface; where 
it appears that the officers and committee testify 
the greatest tenderness and delicacy towards an 
infidel, who had, in the energetic language of 
Dr. Thompson, ‘‘after breaking his faith, which 
had been solemnly pledged, persisted for two years 


247 


in a system of fraud and mendacity;” whilst 
they refer to the poor, honest, and conscientious 
disciple of Jesus Christ, who detected, and single 
handed attacked and exposed him, as a slan- 
derer!!! The usual excuse for such conduct is, 
that the faithful Christian is imprudent. Such a 
defence is almost worse than the original offence ; 
“by faith Moses, when he was come to years, 
refused to be called the son of Pharaoh’s daughter ; 
choosing rather to suffer affliction with the people 
of God, than to enjoy the pleasures of sin for a 
season; esteeming the reproach of Christ greater 
riches than the treasures of Egypt.” Yet it is 
probable Moses might have found some examples 
of imprudence amongst the Jewish brickmakers, 
without having so keen an anxiety to discover it 
as is shewn by the religious world in these days, 
We must not let go the facts which evince love 
to the brethren of Christ, as evidences of the truth 
of religious profession, for if we do, there is not 
another which can supply its place: as it is the 
only one which our Lord has left us for that pur- 
pose. If talking about charity be charity, we 
have abundant; if taking the part of persecuted 
Christians be charity, we have little or none. 
There is no means of ascertaining the opinions 
of the religious part of the public but through the 
publication of such a society as this, and, there- 
fore, I am compelled to refer to it. Of all in ex- 
istence this is the one which has talked most 
about prudence, and brotherly love. It would 
have none of God’s ways, and he has left it to its: 
prudence; and see what a lamentable spectacle 
it is now. Oh, my dear friends, let this teach us 


248 


to look into ourselves, The religious is the most 
righteous part of the world, “and if the righteous 
scarcely shall be saved, where shall the ungodly 
and the sinners appear,” when the Lord shall be 
revealed from heaven in fire, taking vengeance 
upon those who, not having succoured his brethren, 
will be proved not to have loved him? ‘‘ But many 
walk of whom I have told you often, and now tell 
you even weeping, that they are the enemies of 
the cross of Christ,” for, ‘“‘ whoso shall scandalize 
one of these little ones that believe in me” (call 
him a slanderer, because he exposes the insidious 
attacks of an infidel upon the inspiration of God’s 
Holy Word, thereby denying the work and office 
of the Holy Ghost) “it were better for him that 
a mill-stone were hanged about his neck, and that 
he were drowned in the depth of the sea;” take 
heed that ye despise not one of these little ones. 
“ Oh that my head were water and mine eyes ‘a 
fountain of tears, that I might weep day and 
night for the slain of the daughter of my people 
—they are not valiant for the truth upon the earth, 
saith the Lord.” And “I will very gladly spend, 
and be spent for them, though the more abun- 
dantly I love,” and evince it by warning them 
of these things, “the less I be loved.” 2 Cor. 
xii. 15. Homo sum, et nihil humanum a me alie- 
num puto. This fine heathen sentiment is, when 
construed by a Christian, ‘“‘ I am a man, and there 
is no sin which ever was conceived by another, 
that I also am not liable to commit.” With this 
abiding feeling, it cannot be in a self-righteous 
spirit that these remarks are made. It is no sub- 
ject of argument on which I seek for victory; it is 


249 

‘no set of opinions to which I desire proselytes ; it 
is to a more self-devoted crucifixion of the maxims 
of the world in religious practices that I would 
exhort, not others to the exclusion of myself, but 
myself first, that any others whom I could influ- 
ence may be induced to follow after. The Chris- 
tian soldier should say oftener “come” than “go :” 
but as there must first be the willing mind, it is 
only by laying these things before you that I can 
hope to gain one step, which is your judgment. 
‘If the real Christian says video meliora proboque, 
he knows where he can get strength which will 
prevent his being constrained often to add, dete- 
riora sequor. Not that the affections of some 
men are as easily swayed as their judgments, 
or that any live up to that full measure of light, 
however small it may be, which the Holy Ghost 
has implanted in their souls. No real Christian is 
offended at observations which make him look 
within; and though I should make such sorry, 
yet I do not repent; nay, I rather rejoice, not 
that they be made sorry, but that they may sor- 
row for a season unto repentance after a godly 
manner ; for sorrow as before God is not as sorrow 
before the world ; for godly sorrow produces care- 
fulness; clearing of ourselves; indignation against 
what we have done amiss; fear of offending in 
like manner again; vehement desire to amend; 
zeal for God’s truth, and for God’s truth alone; 
and revenge against the delusion by which we 
have been deceived to betray it. 

-= Philalethes.—You constrain me to cry, “ Lord, 
is it 1?” for I am quite convinced that the reli- 


290 


gious world is under a fearful delusion respecting 
the amount of its spirituality; but tell me besides, 
what are the chief national sins which seem to 
provoke God’s judgments upon us as a nation, 
in distinction from those of which -we are guilty in 
the church and as individuals? | 
Anastasius.—National sins are so necessarily 
combined with political questions, that it is very 
difficult to consider them in a proper point of 
view. Hence they have proved, in every age, 
a fertile source of delusion to all who have at- 
tempted to build much upon them. To establish 
fairly that any sin is a national sin, it must be 
shewn that it is one which affects every part of 
the community; for it is well observed by the 
most eloquent divine of the present day, that 
“the system which teaches us to consider a 
people as acting through the medium of its 
prince, or legislature, is too technical, too artifi- 
cial, too much of a compromise with the imper- 
fection essential to human affairs, to enter into the 
views, or regulate the conduct of the Supreme 
Being. He sees things as they are; and as the 
greatest part of the crimes committed in every 
country are perpetrated by its inhabitants, in 
their individual character, it is these, though not 
to the exclusion of others, which chiefly provoke 
the divine judgments.”—R. Hall's Sermon on Fast, 
1803. The mode which appears most likely to 
prevent aur being carried away by political pre- 
possessions, is to enquire what the sins are which 
are mentioned as chiefly provoking God's indigna- 
tion, and then to examine whether these sips can 


251 


be seen clearly and unequivocally established - 
amongst us. I have already mentioned infidelity, 
and therefore will not again dwell upon it; but 
cannot refrain from mentioning two instances of 
its increase which I omitted before: one is, that 
in Salter’s Hall Chapel service is regularly per- 
formed every Sunday upon principles of pure 
deism, according to a liturgy composed expressly 
for the purpose: the second is, that a weekly 
journal, the Examiner, of considerable circulation, 
as avowedly conducted upon the same principles, 
and systematically advances the cause of deism, 
and opposes that of revelation. If the state 
should unite itself to the papal mother of harlots 
and abominations, the cause of infidelity will be 
still further advanced: which will be evident 
when we consider, that if the worship of saints be 
analysed and traced to its source, it will be found 
to be the worship of Satan. No saint would take 
to hiniself the honour that is due to Christ alone; 
the Virgin Mary would not; an angel would not; 
nothing but the devil himself would. We are told 
that the Gentiles worship devils and not God, 
i Cor. x. 20; that the abstaining from meat and 
marrying are the doctrines of devils, 1 Tim. iv. 1; 
and Babylon is said to be the habitation of devils, 
Rev. xviii. 2. If the government of England ac- 
knowledges the Church of Rome to be any thing 
but THE ANTI-CHRISTIAN AposTaAcy, it is bound in 
consistency to put down the Church of England. 
If the Church of Rome be not THE AposTacy, 
the Church of England, as well as every other 
Protestant Church, is a schismatic church, which 


252 
has rent the body of Christ, and it is a sin to be 
a member of it. And if these points do not com- 
pose the very essence of the question, there 
is no such thing as a Church of Christ, nor an 
Anti-christian Apostacy, revealed in Scripture; 
and the infidels who look upon all church go- 
vernment’ as nothing but an expensive engine 
of state, are correct, and more consistent than 
ourselves. If England, therefore, wed that 
church again, after having separated from her, it 
justly deserves to be partaker of her plagues. 
But I pass on to the two vices most frequently 
denounced in Scripture, which are avarice and 
oppression. The Apostle calls ‘the love of 
money the root of all evil,” and says, that 
‘*covetousness is idolatry:”. a truth so- obvious, 
that even heathen moralists and satirists have 
described avarice as the most debasing of all 
passions. In England avarice has engendered 
oppression in two striking instances: Ist, in the 
Corn Laws; and, 2d, in Slavery. By the first a 
direct tax is levied upon the poorest class of the 
community, the produce of which goes imme- 
diately into the pockets of the rich. The majority 
of the legislature consists of landholders, and they 
impose a burden which doubles the price of the 
poor man’s loaf, in order to increase the value of 
their own possessions : and it has been stated in a 
public journal, ‘‘ that the problem to be solved by 
the legislature does not seem to be ‘ how can the 
people be fed best and cheapest ; but, ‘how much 
starvation will they contentedly bear.’” Surely 
St. James must be alluding to such things as 


253 


these, when he says, “Go to now, ye rich men, 
weep and howl for your miseries that shall come 
upon you; your riches are corrupted, and your 
garments are moth-eaten ; your gold and silver is 
cankered, and the rust of them shall be a witness 
against you, and shall eat your flesh, as it were 
fire: ye have heaped treasure together for the last 
days: behold the hire of the labourers who have 
reaped down your fields, which is of you kept back 
by fraud, crieth; and the cries of them which 
have reaped are entered into the ears of the Lord 
of Sabaoth. Ye have lived in pleasure on the 
earth, and been wanton; ye have nourished your 
hearts, as in a day of slaughter. Ye have con- 
demned and killed the just, and he doth not resist 
you. Be patient therefore, brethren, unto the 
coming of the Lord. Behold the husbandman 
waiteth for the precious fruit of the earth, and 
hath long patience for it, until he receive the early 
and latter rain: be ye also patient, for the coming 
of the Lord draweth nigh.” James v. 1—8. ‘ Hear 
this, O- ye that swallow up the needy, even to 
make the poor of the land to fail.—Shall not the 
land tremble for this, and every one mourn that 
dwelleth therein ?” Amos viii. 4. 8. 

The other sin of oppression, proceeding from 
avarice, 1s more heinous even than this: it consists 
in men buying and selling their fellow creatures ; 
treating them exactly like the beasts of the field ; 
driving them to labour for their master’s benefit by 
a cart-whip ; and depriving them of all legal rights, 
so that they can procure no redress, let them be 
ever so much ill-used. The laws actually existing 


254 


upon this subject, are treated in a very masterly 
manner by Mr. Stephens, in his “ Delineation of 
the Slavery of the British West India Colonies, 
compared with the Slavery of other countries, 
ancient and modern.” This horrid system, which 
is without a parallel in the annals of heathenism, 
is kept up at the sole instigation of avarice, by 
men who call themselves followers. of that Creed, 
the first principle of which, as far as relates to 
man, is to do unto others as we would they shoutd 
do unto us. The Government of England not 
only refuses to extinguish it, but even imposes 
heavy burdens upon the people in order to main- 
tain it. : :

A remarkable change has taken place in the 
public feeling of late years. Within a very short 
time the general reliance was upon the navy 
of England, which was called her ‘‘ wooden 
walls,” and was the theme of every patriot’s 
eulogy, and of every poet’s song. But now, com- 
mercial and financial prosperity is the idol that 
has been worshipped. Political economy, the 
science that teaches the best means of promoting 
public wealth, is the chief object of study. ‘ The 
nation has forsaken the Lord God of Hosts, and 
gone after idols. Covetousness is idolatry, and 
covetousness is the ruling sin of England.——The 
great idol of this country has been shaken upon 
his pedestal in the sight of all its worshippers. 
The king from his throne, and the prime minister 
from his place, did homage to it, proclaimmg our 
commercial. prosperity, and the permanency of 
our monied: system; but God smote it, and in 
six months from the day when the proud boast 
was uttered, we were within three days of a na- 
tional bankruptcy.” — M‘ Neile’s Assize Sermon, 
p. 48. The judgments of God are always sudden, 
and come from quarters the least suspected by 
political sagacity. I forbear to dwell longer upon 
circumstances which savour so much of pure 
politics; but the signs of the times must be read 
in public transactions, in the consideration of 
which, we must be on our guard not to be carried 
away by them, but be above them; using them, 
but not abusing them. I cannot resist. this op- 
portunity of shewing you an extract of a sermon 
of Mr, Irving’s, which I cut out of the Morning 


256 


Chronicle ; and, although it is doubtless. very 
defectively reported, yet it contains some excellent 
observations upon our present subject. 

“The text was from the 2lst chapter of St. 
Luke’s Gospel, verses 25 and 26.—‘And there 
shall be signs in the sun, and in the moon, and in 
the stars, and upon the earth distress of nations, 
with perplexity, the sea and the waves roaring, 
men's hearts failing them for fear, and for looking 
after those things which are coming upon the 
earth, for the powers of heaven shall be shaken.’ . 

«The Reverend Preacher observed, that these 
awful words related to the signs of the coming of 
Christ—an event in the history of the church, 
the most glorious, being the termination of her 
warfare, and the commencement of her power and 
glory on earth—an event also, which, on the 
world not less than on the church, sheds the 
beams of its brightness through a. cloudy sky, . 
.over seas of peril and of blood. 

“I regard (said the Reverend Preacher) that 
yawning chasm which opened under our feet in 
the solid ground of national prosperity, wherein we 
trusted that our nation was standing strong ;—this 
shock which our country hath sustained, in the 
highest state of British grandeur, when all men 
were offering incense to the idol !—I regard it as 
God's sign sent to teach this land how. super- 
ficial are the views of the wisest—how short- 
sighted the calculations of the most politic— 
and that though the vessel of the state may be 
surely sound in all her timbers, with pilots most 
wakeful, and all hands on board most staid and 


257 


steady, yet the Lord hath in his stores traversing 
winds, and his storms ready at his will, against 
which neither the nobleness of our constitution, 
nor the wisdom of our governors, nor the steady 
perseverance of our people, can avail. 

“As I do not expect that the other nations 
whom the Lord hath terribly shaken in his anger, 
during the last thirty years, will ever be restored 
to the state in which all things were before these 
years of omen; as I do not expect they will come 
through the storm, but will sink and utterly go 
down, in that terrible night which is at hand; 
I far less expect that we shall have any second 
warning after this is past, but one continued 
tumult of judgments and crushing of nations. But 
it may be well for us to experience such destruc- 
tion of credit as will wound us in the part we 
deem most invulnerable. Be it remembered, that 
though our land separated from the anti-christian 
combination of kingdoms, against whom the Lord 
has had his controversy, we have been for the last 
century a Mammon-worshipping people; idolators 
of political wisdom, national wealth, and commercial . 
prosperity. 

“ For a thousand other misdeeds of this people, 
the Lord certainly is very wrath with us also, and 
we shall not escape the terrible day of his judg- 
ment with which he is about to visit the nations. 
Of which coming visitations he hath given us some 
visible signs in that shock we have received here 
to the vitals of our existence. It will be seen, as 
one of the portents of which these days are full, 
that this nation, having recovered from her trials, 

$ 


258 


and. borne her heavy burthens with magnanimity, 
astonishing even to herself, had like to have fallen 
asleep in the arms of national prosperity and 
glory, never dreaming of: ‘judgment near at hand. 
And it is true, that when those skilled in pro- 
phetic Scriptures, pointed at these judgments, 
they were set at nought, as men born out of time, 
who might have prophesied of these things thirty 
years ago, but were deemed as madmen to the 
present prosperous generation. It is amazing 
how calamity arrests their attentions, and makes 
them willing to be taught. The boldest look with 
dismay in the face of one another, and say— What 
meaneth this? What causeth it? Where is it to 
end? It is our belief, as well as the belief of the 
best interpreters of prophecy, that the long period 
in which the Gentiles trod the Holy City under 
. foot, was the period of popish apostacy, which 
terminated twenty-three years ago. The Maho- 
metan captivity of the church is about to be 
broken, and the abomination of Asia to be anni- 
hilated in the same act of. judgment in which in- 
fidelity will also fall. In these twenty-three years, 
all the signs have been accomplished foreshown by 
our Lord as about to happen before the great and 
terrible day. We have had several times, from the 
beginning of these years of omen, admonitions, 
and symptoms, from which we might discern the 
seeds of the disease in our constitution calling for 
immediate remedy. The warning has been three- 
fold—the dissemination of infidel principles—the 
propagation of revolutionary doctriries—and the 
shaking of national credit. The Lord having great 


259 


purposes to accomplish by means of this nation, 
which is, as it were, the political sanctuary of 
Christendom, did not allow her to sink under the 
billows, but to rise more terrible ‘from every 
struggle, because by our means the overthrow of 
that great infidel Prince was to be accomplished, 
whom God raised up to scourge these kingdoms, 
until his measure of iniquity was filled up. And 
as long as that mighty one laboured: in the work 
to which he was appointed, he pushed beyond all 
example since the days of Charlemagne, who was 
God’s instrument for building up that power which 
the latter was raised to overthrow. But when 
this infidel prince began to provide for his own 
perpetuity—to found a dynasty—to intermarry 
with the head of the Papal Empire—when he 
struck an agreement with a Prince of the Anti- 
‘christian Government—when he established the 
Gallican Church again, having forsaken the pur- 
poses for which he was raised up, then the Lord 
‘withdrew his hand, and consigned him to a swifter 
destruction than had previously been his rise to 
imperial power. Then the Lord called for this 
nation, which he had so long preserved from those 
direful convulsions which shook the kingdoms of 
the Continent, to overthrow the arrogance of their 
power when his work was accomplished ; and to 
‘punish that Peninsular Nation which had most 
vehemently stirred against his church,.he made 
their kingdom the seat of a seven years’ war, and 
‘there he trained our captains and horsemen, and 
‘prepared them for that direful overthrow with 
“which he cast down the mighty one. From that 
time this nation, being filled with naval and mili- 


ie | 


260 


tary glory, hath addressed the chief part of her 
energy to the establishment of internal resources. 
—A thousand heads were busy in planning, and 
a thousand pens busily employed in setting forth 
the best means of diffusing wealth, while every 
tongue was full of the great glory and dignity of our 
beloved land. Even the foes of princes were con- 
strained to applaud the wisdom of our rulers, and 
every heart rejoiced in the long year of blessed- 
ness which was deemed to have opened on our 
nation. But the Lord, by our late catastrophe, 
gave us the just and proper sign, by shaking that — 
Power which held the church and the nation in 
fatal slumbers. We had brought the struggle of 
thirty years to such a glorious termination, that all 
the nations of Europe looked up to England, and 
said the strength of her arm lay in her commercial 
‘prosperity. But behold! this staff and stay of our 
right hand, the Almighty shook in a day, and it 
bent like a reed shaken with the wind. Between 
neighbour and neighbour, townsmen and towns- 
‘men, brother and brother, confidence was de- 
stroyed. I doubt not, but we shall yet recover 
the shock, and rise greater and greater, for Eng- 
land will be the last of the nations to be swal- 
lowed up in that whirlpool of judgments; but this 
may teach us that the Lord hath the arrow in his 
quiver, which may lay the noblest head in the 
forest low. I can see a disease generating in the 
bowels of the land, which of itself will strike us 
down, if not remedied; I mean the growth of in- 
fidelity in religion, and insubordination in social 
life; I perceive now, as at the beginning of these 
years of omen, a regular party of philosophers, 


. 261 


engaged in the service of infidelity. I perceive 
infidelity couching among the flowers of poetry. 
Infidelity appears in the first works of sentiment— 
it is built up into a system of morals, and advo- 
cated in the daily and weekly Journals. Public 
opinion is become the voice of God among men 
of liberal opinions, and it is no longer the old 
question of Whig and Tory which rends public de- 
bates, but you shall find this is the question—Shall 
the nation rest on irreligion, as it steals slowly its 
way to ruin; or shall it stand like a rock of ada- 
mant by the fear of God? These things proceed 
by slow marches, and very specious names, but 
aman of penetration can discern them, for there 
is a something which may strike the stupidest. 
-And O! ye people of this ancient city—the Jeru- 
salem of the whole earth, when ye consider the 
Prophets which have been sent, and the warnings 
ye have despised, well may ye tremble at the 
prospect of coming judgment. The people of this 
city, have become out-spoken, rash, and furious 
infidels; and what is the consequence? The 
ancient relation of master and servant is changed 
to an aversion, and we are mocked and maltreated 
of men who eat our bread. This amazing revolu- 
tion in civil life is the main fruit of our new system 
of education, whose tendency is to foster, and 
_cherish, and increase that power which will even- 
tually overpower us. The visible hath got the 
victory over the invisible—the sensual hath verily 
triumphed over the spiritual—and this land is 
_ Tipe for the judgment. The profitable is almost 
every thing, and the moral is all but nothing ; and 
- they call the age Religious! O, that nations 


262 


would learn a lesson of their instability, by that — 
reverse which hath befallen us within the space 
of one short month, which short period has seen 
the most splendid fortunes overthrown, the most 
ancient and revered names dishonoured, the 
surest establishments laid prostrate, credit be- 
tween man and man suspended, and the pecu- 
niary bulwarks of the nation subverted, the nation 
itself reeling and staggering like a drunken man. 
Who looked for the outward distress—the de- 
struction of the idolatry of commerce, the stag- 
nation of trade? But the very contrary of all 
this, peace abroad, prosperity at home, flourish- 
ing husbandry, internal resources, and enlightened 
principles of trade. There was never a period so 
favourable for new experiments. The New World 
was laid open to us; the obstructions which had 
So long stood between it and the Old were com- 
pletely removed, when behold, in one week, all 
is dismay, confusion, and blank astonishment, and 
the man who thought himself rich when he rose 
in the morning, went to bed in poverty; and he 
who had wealth in abundance, was fain to shut 
his house from his inability to satisfy the demands 
of his creditors. | 

‘< There is no greater aggravation of the misery 
of those who trust in this world’s riches, ‘than 
that when they take to themselves wings and flee 


away, you dare not utter one word of your loss, 
even to your wives and children, for fear of 


shaking your credit ; and this suppression of 
misery is worse than the misery itself; it gnaws 
the heart like a vulture, and makes miserable men 
take arms against their own lives. Though this

event hath not robbed some of you, it hath robbed 
those whom you love; and if you have ‘hot goné 
down amidst the tempest, many a stouter and 
statelier ship hath gone down by your side. 
Even now no man knows what he is about, or can 
tell what may arrive by to-morrow's post. Why, 
hardly any battle slayeth all—hardly any pes- 
tilence destroyeth all—no famine: killeth all by 
the keen pangs of hunger. The wolves of the 
mountains, and the tigers of the desert, devour not 
all that journey through these fearful solitudes, 
with their blood-thirsty jaws. Yet the sword, the 
pestilence, the famine, and the beasts of the earth, 
dre the four great instruments of human destruc- 
tion, in the hand of the Lord, but we have added 
a fifth. Thé violent fluctuations in the medium of 
Exchange’ are to the merchant what the storms in 
‘the ‘atmosphere are to the air we breathe.”: 

© Philalethes—This passage certainly contains 
serious matter. for considération, and very powet- 
fully stated: The more I compare the views you

Anastasius.—I have already mentioned that 
there is a leaven of infidelity working in every 
part of the church, aswell as in the world. No 
ma is or can be free from it: it is the form that 
Satan is assuming at the present day, and which 
we must be earnest in our prayers to the Captain 
of our Salvation to assist us in withstanding. In- 


264 


deed, the church is so full of prejudice upon the 
subject of the second coming of our Lord, that it 
would be more easy to convince a bench of candid 
infidels, than a bench of modern divines, that 
such was the doctrine contained in the Bible. 
But I am perfectly assured, that to preach the ful- 
filment of prophecy, and the speedy coming of 
Christ in glory to set up his kingdom in the earth, 
is the ouly subject which can arrest the tide of in- 
fidelity, or call in men of the present day to the 
fold of Christ. In the Acts of the Apostles we 
find the prophecies made the constant subject 
of their preaching, of their prayers, of their re- 
joicing: thus it was at the beginning of this dis- 
pensation, and thus it must be at its close. In 
this subject the holy catholic church must be built 
up. No single sect, nor congregation, nor church, 
can rise but as the whole rises, for the church is 
one. A single limb of the body cannot grow 
unless the whole body grows; neither will one 
member or fragment of the body of Christ grow, 
unless the whole church, which is his body, grows 
together. Let us pray for the whole catholic 
_church, the whole mystical body; that it may all 
_ grow in grace, and in the knowledge of its coming 
Lord and Saviour. Besides praying, we must 
bring the subject forward, in season and out of 
season, teaching and warning every man. “If 
thou warn the wicked, and he turn not from his 
wickedness, nor from his wicked way, he shall 
die in his iniquity, but thou hast delivered thy 
soul.” Ezek. iii. 19. | 
LIBERAVI ANIMAM MEAM.

CONVERSATION THE SEVENTH. 


On the Systematic Arrangement and Chronology of the 
Prophetic Scriptures. 


Philalethes.—In reflecting upon the passages 
of Holy Scripture, brought forward in support of 
the opinions which you have laid before me, I 
observe that you have seldom alluded to the 
books of Daniel and the Apocalypse; yet these 
two seem to have attracted the largest share of 
the attention of writers upon the Prophecies; did 
you avoid referring to them purposely, or was the 
omission unintentional ? 

Anastasius.—The book of Daniel and the Apo- 
calypse of St. John are distinguished from all 
‘other parts of scripture by two circumstances; 
Ist, because they contain a Chronology, and are 
therefore what Mede properly calls them, THE 
SACRED KALENDAR AND GREAT ALMANACK OF 
Propuecy, p. 654; and, 2dly, because their 
meaning is veiled in symbols. Although some other 
prophecies contain certain dates, yet they are 
discursive, less precise, and more easily, because 
they can be literally, understood. I therefore 
thought it better to bring forward first only such 
passages as would admit of no doubt respecting 
their interpretation. 


266 


Philalethes.—Is the book of Revelations really 
much more difficult to understand than any other 
part of the Bible ? 

Anastasius.—I will reply to you in the words 
of Dr. Cressener, who, speaking of the various 
commentators on this book, says, ‘‘ they do almost 
as generally agree about the first grounds of the 
intepretation of these visions, as about other 
books of scripture."— Dem. of Apoc. Introd. 

Philalethes.—Do you think it of very great im- 
portance to study the Apocalypse ? 

Anastasius—I know not how to express in 
terms sufficiently strong the importance of study- 
ing this book: it is entirely owing to the neglect 
of it that neither the Church of England, nor the 
Dissenters, know how to take their true stand 
against the encroachments of Popery. Hear the 
words of an accurate Dissenter on this matter. 
«Since the denunciation against the Church of 
Rome, included under the figure of Babylon, as 
_anti-christian and idolatrous, is one of the clearest 
and most prominent parts of this sacred prophecy ; 
and since it expressly commands men to’ ‘separate 
from her, it encouraged the ‘authors of the 
Protestant Reformation to commence and to pro- 
_secute those changes, which they might other- 
wise perhaps have been intimidated from at- 
tempting, or might have attempted without suc- 
cess. Not to mention the virulence of that ‘ex- 
ternal opposition under the weight of which they 
were sometimes on the point of being crushed, a 
separation from the visible head of the church, 
under the dreaded appellation _ of SCHISM, was 


267 


represented as the most inexpiable of crimes; 
and such were the prejudices of the times, that 
it was almost universally admitted to be criminal, 
and the most undaunted of the reformérs, even 
Luther bimself, shrunk from the charge. (See 
Bishop Hurd, vol. ii. p. 46—51.) But an exami- 
nation of the Apocalypse enabled them to repel 
it, and furnished them with the ‘most: powerful 
weapons for conducting an attack against the See 
of Rome with vigour and success. On every side, 
indeed, they loudly appealed to the predictions of 
the Christian Prophet, as authorizing their con- 
‘duct; ‘multitudes acknowledged the validity of 
their appeal; and Bishop Hurd -hesitates not to 
‘assert, that ‘on this popular ground chiefly, the 
Protestant cause in those early ‘times was upheld.’ 
If then the Protestant Reformation was chiefly, or 
‘only in a great degree, supported by arguments 
drawn from the Apocalypse, (which at that period 
became an object of general investigation,) in how 
high a degree are we indebted to that prophetic 
part of the New Testament, for to this revolution, 
‘imperfect as it was, we unquestionably owe, not 
only the promotion of civil as well as religious 
liberty, not only the knowledge of Scripture and 
of. genuine Christianity, not only the spread of 
light and science, but to this great event is justly 
to be attributed a considerable -portion of the 
virtue, the liberality, and the civilization which 
prevail in the European world.”— MMlustrations of 
Prophecy, p. 33. 

‘Philalethes.—-How is the meaning .of these 


268 


books to be ascertained, if they are not, to be 
understood literally ? | 

Anastasius.—Mede laid down these principles ; 
first, that “the Apocalypse, considered only ac- 
cording to the naked letter, as if it were a history 
and no prophecy, hath marks and signs sufficient 
inserted by the Holy Spirit, whereby the Order, 
Synchronism, and Sequel of all the visions therein 
‘contained, may be found out and demonstrated, 
without supposal of any interpretation whatso- 
ever.” Secondly, “This Order and Synchronism, 
thus found and demonstrated as it were by argu- 
menta intrinseca, is the first thing to be done, and 
forelaid as a foundation ground, and only safe 
rule of interpretation.” p. 581. The propriety of 
these principles have been generally admitted, 
: but not much acted upon. Having then first as- 
certained its Structure, the next step 1s to discover 
the meaning of the Symbols in which its truths are 
conveyed, and this can only be done by comparing 
those Symbols, which we find here, with similar 
terms in other parts of scripture. The third step 
is to fix the Dates, assigning to each its corre- 
sponding historical event. 

Philalethes.—Why do you consider the Struc- 
ture of the Apocalypse as of so much impor- 
tance ? | 

Anastasius.—The wisdom which is charac- 
teristic of those who are to understand the things 
that were closed to Daniel, must be that which 
makes them conversant not with detached shreds 
. and partial applications, but with the whole plan 


269 


of God’s dealings with his church, and with the 
world. We shall never arrive at any satisfactory 
understanding of one numerical period, nor at the 
meaning of one solitary text, or insulated symbol, 
but by a scheme in which the interpretation of 
each single passage shall throw some collateral 
light on other parts of these books. Hence the 
scattered notices which are to be found up and 
down the writings of almost every divine of emi- 
nence are not worth considering, except in so far 
as they may be useful in illustrating a connected 
system which shall be laid down by some other. 
I do not think it probable, therefore, that a sound 
explanation can be given of any one numerical 
period, symbol, or passage, taken per se, and 
that it is only by the concentrated light of many 
independent testimonies, and bearing upon one 
uniform and consistent arrangement, that any 
mind can arrive at a satisfactory conclusion from 
the study of the Chronological and Symbolical 
prophecies. |

Crito.—Before you examine the Apocalypse it 
. will be advisable to take notice of a peculiarity 
in the Structure of the symbolical prophecies of 
Daniel, with which this is intimately connected ; 
the peculiarity to which I allude consists in each 
' prophecy being composed of two parts ; first, the 
vision, and, secondly, the interpretation of the 
vision, with a break intervening to mark the se- 
paration. The first prophecy is in chap. ii.; the 
vision is contained in ver. 29 to 35 inclusive; ver, 


+ 


271 


36 is the break; the interpretation of the vision 
is given in ver. 37 to 45 inclusive. A simitar 
division will be found in chap. iv., but passing by 
that, we come to chap. vii., in which is a vision 
contained between ver. 2 and 14; the break con- 
sists of ver. 15 and 16; the interpretation 17 to 27. 
In chap. viii.‘ is another vision between ver. 2 
and 14; the break in ver. 15 to 19; the: inter- 
pretation 20 to 25. In chap. ix. at ver. 2, Daniel 
was studying the prophetic numbers given by 
Jeremiah; he prays to be enabled to understand 
the meaning of them, as we learn by ver. 22, 23, 
and that that prophecy may be fulfilled; this 
prayer is contained in ver. 4—20; the interpre- 
tation in answer to his prayer is given from 
24 to 27. Chap. xi. contains a’ prophecy which is 
not given in symbols, for as it is the history of a 
succession of individuals, it would require a suc- 
cession of symbols which would render the inter- 
pretation so difficult as to be useless ; the narrative 
closes at chap. xii. 4, then follows a break in ver. 
5,6; a declaration of the date, the only thing 
which was left untouched in the history, at ver. 7; 
another break at 8 and 9; and a farther expla- 
nation in ver. 10—12. 

Anastasius.—This observation of Crito is. of 
great importance, and has been remarked by Sir 
I. Newton. It seems to have been a frequent 
practice with symbolical prophecies, that the sym- 
bol should be shewn at one time, and the explana- 
tion given at another, as may be seen in Jer. xiii. 
xviii.; Hos. i. iii. and other places. There is ano-

_Aristo.—As at every severe trial in the 
Church’s History ; under Pharaoh; under Ahab ; 
on the eve of the Assyrian, and of the Babylonian 
captivities; in the duration of the same; in the 
hard times which followed them; and finally, for 
the 70 years which preceded the destruction and 
downfall of Jerusalem, there were raised up pro- 
phets, who should direct the eye of the church 
to her great redemption and restoration at the 
coming of Messiah; by one of whom was given 
also the times and seasons thereof, exactly num- 
bered and set off: so analogy will lead us to be- 
lieve that the Lord would do the same by the Chris- 
tian Church, in that book which was added for the 
interpretation of ail other books of revelation, and 
for the express manifestation of that coming king- 
dom, whereof the expectation of the church had 
been defeated at the time of the former advent. If 
such a book was to be given, to conclude the 
' canon, and serve the part of a prophecy to the 
Gentile church, leading in, and pointing directly, 
like all prophecy, to the grand restoration at the 
coming of the Lord ; it is according to the analogy 

T 


274 


of all God’s dealings, that it should be so disposed 
as to open now one part and now another, accord- 
ing to the successive judgments of his providence. 
And if this idea be true, we have the best reason 
to assign why there should be so much disagree- 
ment amongst the interpreters of this book. For, 
as it would have been bad husbandry to have 
given to Moses what fitted the times of Isaiah, or 
to Isaiah what suited the times of Daniel, or to 
Daniel what suited the times of the Baptist; so 
would it have been equally bad husbandry to have 
opened.the prophecy, which respects the downfal 
of the Papacy to the church whilst suffering under 
Paganism ; or that which respecteth the downfall 
of Infidelity to the church whilst suffering under 
the oppression of the Papacy; but rather to open 
unto each that which concerned its own trial, and 
_in mercy to hide the various trials which were to 
follow, lest haply she might think them intermi- 
nable, and lose heart altogether. Yet, as in the 
former prophets of the old dispensation, there is an 
infantine discernment, and as it were an embryo 
revelation of all the. prophecy which was to follow, 
SO. ought there to be asystem, and combination, and 
evenness of progress and end in this book, which 
was thus gradually to be opened as the church 
should stand in need of its consolation. And as, 
upon the eve of Messiah’s former coming, there 
= was the dispensation of a forerunner and a. pre- 
paration; so upon the eve of his latter coming, 
there will be a dispensation of special preparation, 
and of a forerunner, which I think is signified in 
that prophetic voice announced under the sixth 


279 


vial, “ Behold I come,” &c. It is enough for the 
confirmation of these views, to find that each of 
the three great oppressions of the church, the 
Pagan, the Papal, and the Infidel, was attended 
with a.corresponding opening and understanding 
of that part of this hook which severalHy concerns 
each, and of. the corresponding Scriptures which 
breathe the promise of the like deliverances. And 
this I assert has been the case: during the first 
three centuries the Apocalypse was known and 
cherished in the church as the great assurance of 
the downfall of Paganism ; during the sufferings of 
the Waldenses and Protestants, it was cherished 
as the ground of belief, that the Pope and Papal 
empire was the beast; and now it is beginning to 
be .known from the same, that the Infidel anti- 
christ, who is to take the other out of the way, is 
the great sign of, and shall himself be destroyed at, 

the coming of the Lord.

Anastasius.—In the first chapter of the Apoca- 
lypse we are distinctly told the subject of the 
book: John is desired to write down, first, ‘the

person of our blessed Lord himself : secondly, 

T2

“the things which are ;” namely, those relating to

“ the things which shall be hereafter.” It follows, 
therefore, that John was told nothing about what 
had been, and was past; and, indeed, a prophecy 
about what is past is absurd, for it ceases to be 
prophecy after the event has taken place, and 
becomes a history, which is a sufficient objection 
to the theory of the estimable author of. “ The 
Even-tide,” to which I suppose you allude. 
Philalethes.—-On what grounds do you consider 
that the Apocalypse applies almost exclusively to 
the Gentile, and not to the Jewish church ? | 
Anastasius.—First, because the only subject 
with which the Jewish church can have any 
concern, namely, her restoration, is fully disclosed 
in the Jewish Scriptures, without there being any 
hiatus to be supplied by this book. Secondly, 
because there could be no use in giving promises 
and encouragements to that church in a book 
which she would never receive as a revelation 
from God. : 
Aristo.—The Epistles to the seven churches of 
Asia, to whom the whole book of the Apocalypse 
is addressed, do shew that it was intended for the 
instruction and consolation of the Gentile, and not

on which I conclude that Isaiah was inspired to 
be a witness to the Jewish church and nation, and

of them, I conclude that the Apocalypse belongs:

reason, because to them it is addressed.. Will 
any one say that what was written to the Thessa- 
lonians was not intended for the Thessalonians ?. 
and will any one then say, that what was written to 
Gentile churches was not intended for them? To

through them, to all the churches of the Gentiles, 
not of that generation only, but to all that should

conclude, as well as from the emblematical charac- 
ter of the whole book, that if Jewish names occur: 
in it, as in the sealing of the Tribes, and in the 
new Jerusalem which comes down from heaven, 
they ought not to be understood literally, but em- 
blematically, as Egypt, and Sodom, and Babylon 
are.

former commentators which did: not favour one

his own arrangement exclusively. The only two 
schemes worth noticing, are, Ist, that which pre- 
sumes that within the period ef the last seal, all 
the events symbolized by the seven trumpets must 
be included; and, likewise, that within the period 
of the last trumpet must be contained all the 
events symbolized by the seven vials. This is the 
scheme for the structure arising from internal 
evidence, which, with trifling variations, has been 
adopted by the majority of commentators. The 
2d is that of Mr. Frere, which presumes that 
the visions contained im the Apocalypse are, 
like those of Daniel, parallel in respect to time ; 
and relating in one line to one division of the 
last empire, in a second line to another division 
of that empire, and in a third line to the elect 
church of Christ; the two former deseriptive of 
events which terminate in the destruction of all 
the kingdoms of this Roman earth, and the esta- 
blishment of Christ’s kingdom upon their ruins ; 
the third line terminating synchronically with the

to her Lord. | P 
Sophron.—The chief ebjection to this latter 
scheme, as stated thus far, is, that it militates 
against what we must lay down as an Apocalyptic 
axiom, or first principle in the interpretation of 
the Apocalypse, namely, that the book with seven 
seals is a complete prophecy, needing no appendix 
er supplement, and that it necessarily includes 


281 


within itself the whole of the Apocalyptic visions, 
from chap. vi. to the end. 

Anastasius.—If the above axiom is to be con- 
sidered as undoubted, I know not how it is to be 
got over.. I, however, am inclined to doubt its ac- 
curacy, for it appears to me that there is no more 
reason to maintain that the vision of the vials ne- 
cessarily succeeds, in the time of its fulfilment, the 
vision of the trumpets; and this latter, again, the 
vision of the seals; than to say that in Daniel the 
vision of the ram and goat must necessarily succeed 
_ the vision of the four beasts; and this in like man- 
ner the vision of the image: nor will a supposed 
retrocession of the last seal get rid of the difficulty. 
I should, on the contrary, expect directly the re- 
verse; and that as the affairs of the eastern and 
western branches of the last empire had been made 
the subject of distinct visions and series of sym- 
bols in Daniel, they should be likewise subjects 
of different symbols in the Apocalypse. For as 
Lancaster says, p. 12, ‘‘ when the matters require 
that they should be considered under another 
aspect, the strain of the symbols must change, and 
the scene of the vision alters; so that many sym- 
bols may be used to denote the same thing in 
different respects. Now there are placed such 
inward marks which belong to every part of a 
vision, that we may thereby discover how the 
matters of that vision are related to the rest; thus 
we find what is antecedent and consequent, or 
what is only collateral; and so it appears what 
visions and their parts synchronize, and what do 
not. By this method, what was before treated 


282. 


of succinctly, is enlarged upon, and more. fully de- 
monstrated. So that the Revelation is not wrote 
in. the way of annalists, who, being content to 
reduce all matters to a chronological series, only 
relate chiefly what happens every year,. without 
enlarging. upon the intrigues or causes of the 
events, and omitting for the most part the con- 
sequences ; but in the way of the more judicious 
historians, who endeavour to give a full account 
of every matter as they take it in hand, in order 
to make a complete system of the whole; inter- 
posing digressions, and then returning to the 
principal matter, by giving such hints and transi- 
tions as suffice to let us understand to what they 
belong, and how as to point of time they come in 
or end with the rest. Upon this account there 
are frequent transitions, wherein the prophet 
seems to cast his inspired sight upon different 
objects, which being thus seen one after another, 
the particles expressive thereof may seem to 
imply a posteriority in a matter which may be 
synchronical, and even may have its origin higher 
than that whose description preceded it in the 
account. And this is the method, not only of the 
most exact histories and discourses, but in a 
special manner, that of all the inspired writers, 
in whom the conjunctive particles do rather im- 
port that one passage comes to be related after 
another, than that it was really transacted after 
it. (Vide Bishop of Sarum’s Exp. 6th Art. 
Church of Eng.)” Mr. Croly also, p. 41, con- 
siders the Seals, Trumpets, and Vials as parallel ; 
and Dr. Tilloch denies the necessity of the 


283 


trumpets being a division of the last seal. Un- 
der the first scheme, the commentators have 
been obliged to assign very different portions 
of time to each seal, greatly extending the 
term of the last in order to make room for 
the trumpets; thus, according to Bishop Newton, 
the first seal occupies the space of only about 
28 years; and all who follow him give only 
about 325 years for the first six seals, whilst they 
make the seventh run from that time (death of 
Theodosius), to the end of the world ; whereas, as 
Vitringa well observes, we should expect some 
sort of proportion with respect to the length of 
the visions ; oportet enim sit cupperpia et proportio 
aliqua inter visa Sigelli Septimi, et praecedentium 
Sigillorum. But these able commentators, Vi- 
tringa, Daubuz, Coceius, and many others, are of 
opinion that the seven trumpets are by no means 
to be included under the seventh seal, but con- 
stitute a new series of distinct visions. Vit. p. 319. 
Largitur nobis vir cl, Tubicinia septem describere 
casus illustres et fata rerum futurarum non subor- 
dinata fatis sigillorum sed coordinata, quod dicunt, 
et magnam partem rapañànàa atque contemporanea, 
ac proinde Tubicinia novam inchoare rerum se-

Crito—With great pleasure. But first let me 
remark, that the opening of the seven seals opened 
seven divisions of a period which reaches to the 
end of all things; the events of which periods are 


284 


detailed in three parallel histories, which may, 
by various evidences, be shewn to form but one 
_ prophecy. The book sealed with seven seals, 
refers then indiscriminately to all these three 
histories, and forms a general introduction to 
them, because it embraces the whole of their 
periods: nor can any one portion of prophecy 
be said to contain any other, except in reference to 
its including its time. Thus, when it is said that 
the seventh seal (or the last cherubic voice of Rev. 
xv. 7, xvi. l,) contains the seven vials, and that 
the seventh trumpet contains the seven thunders, 
the meaning is, that they respectively introduce 
the periods of these latter, which are subdivisions 
of their period. But the supposition that, me- 
chanically and irrespectively of time, the seventh 
seal contains the seven trumpets, or the little 
opened book, while both these latter refer back 
to the times of the first seals, appears only to 
introduce intricacy and defect into the structure 
of the Apocalypse. The supposition entertained 
by some, that the book sealed with seven seals, 
consisted of seven distinct rolls, of which one 
was unrolled at the opening of each seal, and 
that the little opened book was the last of these 
rolls, is in every part of it, without any authority 
from the text itself, and tends to divert the mind 
from the simple meaning of the act of Christ’s 
opening the seven seals, which seems to be only 
indicative of his having power as the prophet of 
his church to open to them, for their edification 
and support, the events of all future time, the 
number seven, implying totality or perfection. 


285 


_ Difficulties must naturally be produced by 
partial interpretations of the Apocalypse, and any 
confusion of ideas which may have arisen relative 
to the connexion that exists between the seals and 
trumpets may probably have been caused by com- 
mentators not having first ascertained the univer- 
sal symbolical meaning of the voice of the trumpet, 
and the voice of thunder; nor that of the che- 
rubim themselves, whose voices are heard as thun- 
der, and of the seven angels who sound the trum- 
pets. Whence they have considered the act of 
sounding the trumpets as analogous to that of the 
opening of the seals, instead of understanding it 
to be, what it really is, the act of communicating 
by the church in heaven, to the Apostle John, one 
of the three parallel histories which had been re- 
vealed by Christ in the opening of the seals, and 
as analogous therefore to the similar communica- 
tion made to St. John by the voices of the cheru- 
bim, which were heard as thunder. 

The history of the Western Empire commences 
thus, (chap. vi. 1,) “ And I saw when the Lamb 
opened one of the seals, and I heard as it were the 
= noise of thunder, one of the four living creatures 
(or cherubim) saying come and see!” The same 
introduction of the opening of the seals, is to 
be considered as repeated in the history of the 
Eastern Empire, (chap. viii. 2. 7.) Thus, “ And 
I saw when the Lamb opened one of the seals” — 
and ‘‘ the first angel,” being one of those “ seven 
which stood before God,” which is another de- 
finition of the cherubim or church in heaven) 
“sounded.” Only the opening of each seal before 


286 


the voices of the cherubim are heard having been 
already stated in giving the events of these seven 
periods in the history of the Western Empire, there 
is no occasion that the same should be actually 
repeated, though they must equally be understood 
as taking place before each of the voices of the 
trumpet angels are heard, which relate to the same 
seven periods in the history of the Eastern Empire ; 
in the same manner as the supplementary history 
of chap. xv. xvi. must be understood, in an exact 
view of the Apocalypse, as being three times re- 
peated, to complete each of its three parallel 
histories, though it is found but once actually 
introduced into its text. | 

The Church history is in like manner revealed 
by the opening of the seven seals, its whole time 
being the same; but not being divided into. seven 
periods, as the history of the Empire is, it is com- 
municated to St. John in a different form, viz. as 
a little opened book, which he, like the prophet 
Ezekiel, is commanded to eat, that he might bear 
witness of its contents “before many people and 
nations and tongues and kings.” 

The difficulties supposed to exist in the arrange- 
ment of the Apocalypse, considered as consisting 
of three parallel histories, appear, indeed, to 
arise from the application to this theory of a 
hypercriticism, which has no foundation in the 
prophecy itself, and which, instead of elucidating 
it, has a tendency to produce obscurity, by 
destroying its divine simplicity. I have here an 
abstract of Mr. Frere’s arrangement, beginning 
at the fourth chapter, which, together with the 


287 


fifth, is to be considered as prefatory to the whole, 
but principally to the vision of the seals. r 3

at chap. iv. begin “ the things which shall- be

The commencement of these several distinct 
revelations, or contemporary histories of the Apo- 
calypse, are sufficiently marked by their respec- 
tive prefaces, and their several terminations are 
also shewn by certain notices in the text; these 
notices being the means by which the several 
parts are to be arranged, before any interpretation 
of it is attempted. The contemporary termi- 
nations ofthe first of the three main histories, and 


288 


the three explanatory histories (xvii. to xix. 10,— 
xix. 11, to xxi. 8, and xxi. 9, to end) are marked 
by three pairs of texts; each of which, coupling 
together two of them, demonstrates the synchroni- 
cal termination of all these four; or rather, as the 
second and third main histories are also otherwise 
shewn to terminate synchronically with the first 
main history, they prove the synchronical termina- 
tion of all the six lines of history, of which the 
Apocalypse principally consists. And as there are 
no other such pairs of texts, (except one which 
belongs to the supplementary history, coupling in 
like manner its termination with the rest,) it is 
evident that their occurrence is not a circum- 
stance without meaning. The corresponding texts, 
which mark the synchronical termination of the 
first explanatory history, and the third explana- 
tory history, are as follows: 


XIX. 10. xxii. 8, 9.

So that, in the close of the Apocalypse, where the 
trumpet history is that chiefly referred to, as well 
as in the opening of it, where the whole revelation 
is addressed to the seven churches of Asia, a pre- 
ference appears to be given to the Eastern branch 
of the Empire, which is, doubtless, owing to its 
containing, within its limits, the holy city Jerusa- 
lem, the future chief seat of the church, and me- 
tropolis of the millennial kingdom.

The method by which the main history of the 
Eastern Empire, and the main history of the 
church, are shewn to terminate synchronically 
with the main history of the Western Empire, is 
not by the occurrence of the same exact passages 
at the close of each, but the principle is still so far 
the same, that these synchronisms, like the former, 
are capable of being ascertained from the text 
alone, irrespectively of any interpretation of the 
book. Thus the main history of the Eastern 
Empire, communicated by the seven trumpets, is 
shewn to terminate synchronically with the main 
history of the Western Empire, by the same earth- 
quake which immediately precedes the seventh trum- 
pet (xi. 13,) being mentioned in the period opened 
by the sixth seal (vi. 12); and again, by the day of 
wrath, during which the vials of wrath are poured 
out upon the earth (xi. 15—19; xv. xvi.), being 
also similarly described (vii. 1—3), as that during 
which the earth is hurt by the four winds let loose 
to destroy; and there is this remarkable and 


291 


appropriate variation to be-observed in the mode 
of the announcement of the day of wrath, namely, 
that in the history of thé kingdoms of this world 
(vi. 15—17), it is put into the mouth of ‘kings of 
the earth,” and those subject to them; while in 
the church history (xi. 15—18), it is heard from 
the four and twenty elders, or the church in 
heaven. 

The main prophecy anood by the trumpets, 
is also shewn to be brought to a termination at 
x. 7, by its analogy with the last prophecy of 
Daniel; for as this opens (Dan. x. 4—9,) with a 
manifestation of Christ to the eyes of the prophet ; 
after which the prophecy itself, relating to the 
infidel antichrist, is communicated by the ministry 
of the angel Gabriel, and then, at its close, (Dan. 
xii. 5—7,) a second manifestation of Christ is 
made as entering into solemn covenant by oath, 
that the afflictions both of the Jewish and Gentile 
churches should not extend beyond certain 
periods; so the trumpet prophecy opens with a 
vision of the Lord Jesus Christ, as walking in the 
midst of the seven golden-lampstands, (i. 10—18,) 
which vision more especially belongs to the 
Eastern Empire, being addressed, with the rest of 
the Apocalypse, to the seven churches in Asia. 
The main trumpet propheey is subsequently 
communicated by the ministry of an angel, and 
closes (x. 5—7,) with a second revelation of Christ, 
“swearing by himself, because he could swear 
by no greater,” that the permitted time of the op- 
pression of his church under that apostate power, 
whose forehead is represented as inscribed with 

U 2 


292 


the name “Mystery, Babylon the Great,” should, 
continue no longer than to the sounding of the 
seventh trumpet. The mark, therefore, of the 
terminations of the main prophecies, is the glo- 
rious appearance of Christ the second time to the 
eyes of both the prophets, (Dan. xii. 5—7; Rev. 
x, 5—7,) assuring his church, by the solemnity of 
an oath, that he regards her afflictions, and sets 
bounds to their duration; and the analogy of the 
two prophecies is then completed by the respec- 
tive explanations given by tbe interpreting angels, 
which, in that of Daniel,.(x.—xu.) consists of 
chap. xii. 8,.to the end, and in the trumpet pro- 
phecy, follows in its proper place at Rev. xix. 11. 

The synchronism of the last period of the 
church history in brief, (which is part of the little 
opened book,) with the period of the seventh 
trumpet, is shewn, in a very remarkable manner, 
` viz. by the sounding of this trumpet being an- 
nounced in the church history (xi. 15), solely for 
the purpose of marking the synchronism; the 
artificial divisions of seals and trumpets being 
otherwise. totally foreign to its character. And 
it may be further remarked, that the reason why, 
at this period of the church history, the seventh 
trumpet is said to sound, rather than the seventh 
seal to have been opened, which would equally 
have marked the agreement of time between 
the last seal, the last trumpet, and the last period 
in the little opened book, is derived from the 
connexion, which, throughout the Apocalypse, is 
closely maintained between the history of the 
Eastern Empire, and that of the church, in con- 


293 


sequence of the holy city Jerusalem being situ- 
ated in the Eastern Empire. It is evident also, 
that this synchronism could not be more appro- 
priately notified in the church history, than by the 
voice of the church in heaven, which is every 
where the symbolical meaning of the voice of the 
Apocalyptic trumpets. After this announcement 
the synchronism is further confirmed by the men- 
tion of ‘the voices, and thunderings, and the 
earthquake,” of the seventh vial, as PECTS in 
x1. 19, and xvi. 17—21. 

- That the church history i m detail, commencing 
at xii. is brought to a close with xiv. will appear 
by a comparison of the last verse of that chapter, 
which contains a description of the treading of 
the winepress of the wrath of God, with xix. 15, 
where we find the treading of the winepress to 
be the last event introductory to the millennium. 

Thus the arrangement of. the subjects of the 
Apocalypse has been established on the ground of 
its analogy with the prophecy of Daniel, and on 
that of the synchronical termination of its several 
histories, according to the terms of the proposition 
introduced by Mede, as derived from the evidence 
of the text alone, irrespectively of any inter- 
pretation of the book. This diagram shews the 
structure which I have endeavoured to explain.

' Valerius.—The correctness of this structure 
may be strengthened by other internal evidence, 
drawn from a totally different source than mere 
parallelisms of texts, and that is by reference to 
the various allusions which are made in many parts 
of the book to the Jewish times and seasons. To 
illustrate this, I beg you to refer to chap. xiv. and 
you will find the subjects on which it treats ar- 
ranged in the natural order of the seasons, from 
which the figures are borrowed. The chapter 
opens with an account of 144,000 redeemed, who 
are, in ver. 4, called the first fruits ; certain events 
are then described as taking place; after which, 
ver. 15, the harvest is announced, and imme- 
diately afterwards, at ver. 20, is the vintage; this 
vintage being mentioned again at xix. 15, tieş 
these two passages together as equally descriptive 
of events, which are either identical, or to take 
place simultaneously. Again, in chap. xv. imme- 
diately after the vintage, the treading of which is 
every where used-as the figure of wrath, the 
vials are poured out; ver. 8, the temple is filled . 
with smoke from the glory of God ; ‘an allusion.to 
that which occurred at the dedication of Solomon’s 
temple (2 Chron. v. 3. 13, 14;) this was on the first 
day of the feast of tabernacles ;: chap. xxi. 6, the 
expression, ‘‘I will give unto him that is athirst of 
the fountain of the water of life freely,” appears 
to contain an allusion to the last day of the feast 
of tabernacles, when the Jews drew water and . 
poured it out round the altar, and chaunted Isa. 
xii. 3, “ with joy shall ye draw water out of the 


_ wells. of salvation ;” which custom. was counte- | 


Se. 


295 
manced by the Lord Jesus in John vii. 37, where 
it is said in the last day, the great day of the feast, 
“ Jesus stood and cried, saying, if any man thirst, 
let him come unto me and drink ; he that believeth 
on me, as the scripture hath said, out of me 
belly shall flow rivers of living water.” 

Again, as the 144,000 are called, in xiv. 4, first 
fruits, and the great multitude which are men- 
tioned in vii. 9, appear with “palms in their 
hands,” which palms- do not belong to the feast 
of first fruits, but of tabernacles or ingathering, 
it follows that the period described by chap. xiv. 
must occur between ver. 8 and 9 of vii. and the 
great palm-bearing multitude of vii. 9, are doubt- 
less those who, having been gathered in at the 
harvest of xiv. 15, appear now celebrating the 
feast of ingathering or tabernacles. It also 
appears clearly indicated that the seven vials are 
poured out between the events symbolized by the 
first and last days of the feast of tabernacles, 
from comparing xvi. 1, where the vials are first 
to go forth, with chap. xv. 8, which points it out 
to be the first day of the feast; and xxi. 6, which 
declares that ‘“‘ it is done,” and also that it is the 
last day of the feast. You will, perhaps, see it
