Thraso.—Some commentators have taken it for 
granted that the trumpets‘ are included under 


296 


some of the seals, To mé it appears just the re- 
verse, and that the seals and the trumpets bear 
the appearance of being two distinct and diverse 
series of emblems, and that nothing short of an 
imperative necessity should induce us to attempt 
to unite them. The seals and trumpets are no 
doubt primarily intended to shew God’s sove- 
reignty over the powers of the world: that em- 
perors and califs are but his servants or his 
scourges; and that the end of their appointment 
or permission is the advancement or.chastisement 
of his people. For this purpose a brief series of 
the most striking events suffices; it being but a 
catalogue of the different “hammers,” “ battle- 
axes,” and “ weapons of war,” of the Lord of Hosts: 
or the concise ‘‘ Mene, mene, tekel upharsin” to the 
last of the Babylonians. Such being the intention of 
these two series, it does not appear that the right in- 
terpretation of them, with such demonstration as to 
carry conviction, was given till after the great pro- 
phetic period of 1260 had run out at the French re- 
volution. But that the church might never be with- 
out her instruction and consolation, a revelation of 
those things which particularly concern the church 
1s given in the Apocalypse, from chap. x. onwards, 
and the little book in the hand of the angel is 
said to be open, (never having been sealed. to the 
church,) and, by those who have studied this open 
book, the affairs of the church may have been 
always understood, and by the light reflected 
hence in the enlightened periods of the church, 
the saints have understood those secular transac- 
tions which affeeted themselves, through the: in- 


297 


strumentality of the world; whence it comes to 
pass, that there always has been a competent 
witness to the truth in every age. The seals 
are clearly in consecutive order, and on the open- 
ing of the seventh there is silence in heaven, 
which may perhaps indicate that judgment ceases 
in the Western Empire under the seals, while it is 
still going on in the Eastern under the trumpets. All 
the seven angels are then seen at once; their trum- 
pets are all given them, and they all stand prepared 
to sound ; this appears more like the declaration of 
past, than of future events. But before a note is 
blown, the incense and prayers of saints ascend 
before God out of the hand of the angel, (“ for all 
this will I be enquired of by thee,”) not to be an 
efficient cause of bringing down the angels of 
wrath, but to prepare the saints themselves for 
understanding the revelation of God’s judgments, 
now about to be made; and the time of this reve- 
lation was either the time of the earthquake of 
the French revolution, or some time subsequent 
thereto. The division of the empire into eastern 
and western; the diverse circumstances attending 
their ecclesiastical and civil affairs; their distinct — 
languages and customs; and their separate his- 
: torians, and manner of treating history, would all 
combine to make it, humanly speaking, impossible 
to give a clear view of their several revolutions 
under one series of emblems. And when I com- 
pare the two series of the seals and the trumpets, 
I find internal evidence to satisfy me that they do 
not relate to the same portion of the world, but 
that the seals relate to the western, and the trum- 


298 


pets to the eastern portion: Under: the seals the 
Sourth part of men are smitten ; under the trumpets 
the third; for which I know of.no good reason, 
except the division of the Roman Empire: into 
corresponding parts. Moreover, the very emblem 
of the seal and writings, addressed to the eye, 
appears to. agree with the usages of the west, in 
the same exclusive manner as the trumpet, ad- 
dressed to the ear, proclaims its eastern applica- 
tion. In the west, all important events have been 
regulated and fixed by bond and seal,: from the 
Conscript Fathers of old Rome down to the Papal 
bull, and from the feudal charter down to the 
modern bond or lease. All the great questions of 
the west are decided by statute or genealogical 
chart, and fixed by treaty or public record: our 
quarrels are appeased by remonstrance, or, in the 
last resort, a declaration of war.precedes the hostile 
invasion: our armies place their strength in infan- 
try;. and each band rallies round its own conspicu- 
ous standard. The seal also, in other parts of the 
book, as chaps. xiii. and xiv., is exclusively applied, 
by all commentators, I believe, to a people of the 
west. The trumpet is, in all respects, of a cha- 
racter distinct from this. It always conveys an 
eastern and Moorish idea: it carries with it de- 
- fiance, and. seems to recognize no law but force: 
it is the appropriate instrument of cavalry; the 
rallying sound of a scattered plundering troop; 
symbolizing the wrath of Ged and the day of 
doom. A distinction, similar to that which seems 
to be intended in the Apocalypse between the seal 
and the trumpet, is found between the standard 


299 


and the trumpet, in those discursive prophecies 
which relate to the latter time. When.the great 
trumpet is blown for them that are ready to 
perish in the land of Assyria, the standard, or 
ensign, is lifted up on the mountain, to which the © 
Gentiles shall seek, and whose rest shall be 
glorious. In Livy, Sallust, and Terence, inferre 
signa is the common phrase for making an attack ; 
as sounding an alarm, is the oriental signal of 
battle. Many similar analogies and illustrations 
will suggest themselves to you in pursuing. this 
train of investigation; and I think the time is not 
distant, when able men will direct their attention 
to the strengthening Mr. Freres system, by all 
collateral arguments; for 1 am persuaded, that 
though it may have some weak points and minor 
inaccuracies, yet, in the main, it has such firm 
foundation, that it will stand; and these flaws 
will be repaired, either by himself, or by some 
one following the same general plan. 

_ Crito.—An obscurity has hitherto rested on the 
book of the Apocalypse, because it has not been 
sufficiently considered that its visions are to be 
interpreted on the same principle as those of 
Daniel, in all of. which, as. contained in chap. ii. 
vii. viii. and xi., we find that the descriptions of 
the great empires of the world are repeated, ob- 
viously with the intent that each of them may 
form a complete chronological prophetic history, 
commencing from the time then present. Admit 
only that the Apocalypse is equally perfect, and 
no ambiguity will remain in the interpretation of 
any part of it. For instance, with respect to 


300 


the true meaning of the first verse of its eighth 
chapter, believe that one vision begins. with its 
beginning at- the opening of the first seal, Rev. 
vi. 1, and is chronologically continued till it ends 
with its ending in the representation of the palm- 
bearing multitude celebrating the feast of taber- 
nacles, and eternally refreshed with the“ living 
fountains of water,” as described inthe conclusion 
of chap. vii., and you will then see, that at the 
first verse of chap. viii. you must necessarily begin 
a new vision, from the same time as that of the 
first seal. The expression, ‘*‘ when the seventh 
seal had been opened,” can then be understood 
in no other sense than as referring to the whole of 
the preceding series of the seals, and as equiva- 
lent to the expression, when the last of the seals 
had been opened, or when the first series of visions 
was completed ; and the silence which follows can 
also be understood in no other way than as a 
pause in the celestial exhibition, preceding the in- 
troduction of a new series of visions commencing 
from the earliest times. Nothing then will be 
farther required, than that a satisfactory reason 
should be given why the act of the opening of 
the seventh seal should be no where mentioned 
in the Apocalypse in its chronological place, and 
in the usual connexion of the mention of the 
opening of a seal, viz. as introducing the events 
of its period. 

Philalethes.—What explanation can be given of 
a circumstance apparently so extraordinary ? 

Crito.—The vindication of this view of the sub- 
ject is found in the circumstance that the three 


30] 


parallel histories, of which the Apocalypse is sup-: 
posed to consist, are united in the last period ; 
hence the seventh seal and seventh trumpet are 
identical, and it is the very perfection and uni- 
formity of the Apocalypse, which forbids that the 
opening of a seal should be mentioned in its 
chronological place without being followed by the 
events of that seal, and which also forbids that 
the events of the seventh trumpet should be intro- 
duced before those of the first six trumpets have 
been given, which prevents the announcement of 
the opening of the seventh seal where it chro- 
nologically occurs, viz. at the first verse of the 
seventh chapter, immediately after the day of 
wrath has been announced : the true place of its 
announcement, thus deferred, ought then to be in 
chap. xi. 15, which would read thus—And when 
he had opened the seventh seal, and the seventh 
angel had sounded, there were voices in heaven, 
saying, the kingdoms of this world are become 
the kingdoms of our Lord, &c. and the day 
of wrath 1s come;—but a construction so plain, 
and unequivocal would have dissipated all that 
obscurity designed to be maintained by its sym- 
bolical language, and the apparent intricacy of its 
construction, until it should be gradually opened 
to the enquiries of the church, to be fully revealed 
only at that fit time when its revelation should an- 
swer those purposes for which the great Prophet 
of his church originally designed it. | 
Sophron.—After all that has been said, I still 
prefer the scheme of the Apocalypse contained 
in the works of Archbishop Woodhouse, and Mr, 


302 

Cuninghame: but as Mr. Cuninghame has lately 
published a Critical Examination of the Scheme 
of Mr. Frere, and has expressed his willingness, 
if Mr. Irving or Mr. Frere will answer his book, 
either to reply, or to confess himself mistaken, I 
am willing to rest my own ultimate decision on 
the same grounds. 

Philalethes.—Still you have. told me nothing 
about the symBots, so that the interpretation of 
the book, even if the arrangement be correct, is 
as much sealed to me as ever. 

-~ Anastasius.—Although the arrangement of the 
Apocalypse, from internal evidence, does not 
enable us to interpret it, yet it is an indispensable 
preliminary to any attempt at sound interpretation. 
No explanation is worth attending to that does not 
commence with this; nor has any one, since the 
time of Mede, doubted that this was the proper 
foundation. For want of this foundation, every 
writer's application of historical facts to the 
various symbols, seems nearly equally tenable; 
and any Emperor is made to fit any seal, or any 
trumpet, according to the ingenuity of the writer. 
Whereas Bishop Halifax justly observes, that “ the 
very nature of synchronisms requires that, in look- 
ing out for facts to answer them, our search be re- 
strained to particular periods of time, beyond and 
out of which we are not at liberty to recede,” 
p. 324. Thus again Bishop Hurd; ‘the know- 
ledge of this order i is a great restraint on the fancy 
of an expositor, who is not now at liberty to apply 
the prophecies to events of any time to which 
they appear to suit, but to events only falling 


303 


within that time to which they belong in the’ 
course of this predetermined ‘method. . And if to 
this restriction, ‘which is of itself considerable, 
we add another, which arises from the necessity of 
applying not one but many prophecies, that are 
thus shewn to synchronise with each other to 
the same time, we can hardly conceive how an 
interpretation should keep clear of these impedi- 
ments, and make its way through so many in- 
terfering checks, unless it be the true one.” 
Vol. ii. p. 1380. 

_ Philalethes.—How i you prove that the lan- 
guage of the prophecies of Daniel and John are 
symbolical ? 

Anastasius.—That ial must be abie 
which, being taken from material objects, expresses 
things incompatible with the acknowledged pro- 
perties. of those bodies; as for example, where it 
is said that stars fall to the earth; for since the 
stars are larger than the earth they cannot lite- 
rally fall to it. In the Apocalypse, chap. i. 1, it 
is said, that God signified the revelation or dis- 
covery of Jesus Christ; the word søonuavevy mean- 
ing to express by signs or symbols. 

Philalethes.—Are there any fixed rules for the 
interpretation of symbols ? | 

Anastasius.—Undoubtedly symbolic language, 
as Bishop Hurd says, “ is constructed on such 
principles as make it the subject of just criticism 
and rational interpretation. The prophetic style 
was constructed on the symbolic principles of the 
hieroglyphics, which were -not vague uncertain 
things, but fixed and constant analogies, deter- 


304 


minable in their own nature, or from the. steady: 
use that was made of them; and a language 
formed on such principles, may be reasonably 
interpreted upon them.” If you have any doubts 
upon this subject, you can consult at your leisure. 
the Bishop’s Sermon IX., and. also Warburton 
Div: Leg., vol. ii., and Johnston of Holywood. 
It is remarkable, that within these few years a. 
great light has burst in upon our literati respect- 
ing the Egyptian hieroglyphics, who are now 
able to read those hitherto unknown inscriptions, 
The labours of Dr. Young, MM. Champollion, 
Banks, Salt, and others, have shewn us that they 
are written with a Phonetic alphabet, such as 
there is good ground for supposing the Hebrew 
itself was originally. Some light may hence arise 
on our knowledge of symbols. 

_Basilicus.—In Luke xxi. 25, there is a key by 
which you may safely and clearly explain any 
symbols in scripture; and that is by interpreting 
the figure by the plain declaration, and not by 
straining the plain declaration to make it agree 
with the figure. We have, for an example of this 
rule, signs spoken of in this verse as to take place 
in the sun, moon, and stars; this may mean 
either literally those material luminaries them- 
selves; or some other things of which they are 
only symbols: but when we read further, we find 
that there is also distress of nations upon the 
earth with perplexity ; we can have no doubt that 
the latter is literal, and the former figurative. 
We know from the words of our Lord himself, in 
Rev. i. 20, that the stars are symbols of ministers 


305. 


of churches; and the moon therefore, being. a 
homogeneous symbol with stars, must represent 
ecclesiastical rulers and establishments. It is 
amongst these then that we must look for the 
sigos which are here foretold. In the same 
manner we learn that the sea, and the waves 
roaring, represent tumultuous assemblies of the 
people. 

Valerius.—We learn from Gen, xxxvii. 9, 10, 
that the sun, moon, and eleven stars, which made 
obeisance to Joseph were immediately interpreted 
by Jacob to be symbolical of himself, Rachel, and 
his other eleven sons. 

Anastasius.—Mede is therefore fully justified ir in 
saying that “ Heavens mean Regnum Politicum ; 
Sun, Secular Government; Moon, Ecclesiastical 
Government; Stars, Ministers of Religion,” p. 449; 
and again, ‘‘ the Heaven of this political world is 
the Sovereign part thereof, whose host and stars 
are the powers ruling that world. In the highest 
place Gods, or Idols; next Kings, Princes, 
Magistrates, &c., and other such lights shining 
in that firmament. The earth is the Peasantry, 
or vulgus hominum, together with the terrestrial 
creatures serving the use of man,” p. 615. 1 find, 
upon referring to other commentators, that the 
following all agree that ‘ heavens” is the symbol 
for the higher places of the political universe; 
Dr. H. More’s Mystery of Godliness, Daubuz, 
Lancaster, Sykes, Dr. Wall, Vitringa, Lowth, 
Owen, Dean of Christs Church, and Warburton. 
And Sir Isaac Newton says, ‘‘ in sacred prophecy, 
which regards not single persons, the sun is put 

X 


306 


for the whole species and race of kings.” On Apoc. 
p.17. Hence to “ ascend into heaven,” must be, 
as Dr. Goodwin expresses it, ‘‘ to obtain new 
power and glory;” and Daubuz says, “ to ascend 
into heaven” is to obtain rule and dominion, 
That “the sea, and thé waves roaring,” mean 
tumultuous assemblies of the people, and the sea 
by itself, the mass of the people, is manifest from 
many passages. In Isaiah Ix. the Jewish church 
is addressed as at the time of her restoration, 
and it is said to her, “ the abundance of the sea 
shall be converted unto thee, the forces of the 
Gentiles shall come unto thee,” ver. 5.; again, in 
chap. xvii. 12, 13, ‘* Woe to the multitude of 
many people, which make a noise like the noise 
of the seas; and to the rushing of nations, that 
make a rushing like the rushing of mighty waters ; 
the nations shall rush like the rushing of many 
waters.” Again, in viii. 7, “ Behold the Lord 
bringing upon them the waters of the rivers strong 
and many, even the king of Assyria, and all his 
glory,” meaning his army. ‘“‘ As the sun and the 
moon, the stars and the sea, are symbolical ex- 
pressions, to annex a dissimilar interpretation to 
the word earth would be to incur the charge of 
inconsistency.” Illustrations of Prophecy, p. 539. 
The earth seems to be generally put for that over 
which the heavens do rule, but if there be any 
distinction between it and the sea, it is that the 
former represents people in a quiet, and the latter 
in a disturbed state. Thus earthquake must 
mean, as Sir I. Newton observes, p. 17, “ the 
shaking of kingdoms so as to overthrow them ;” 


307 


and Jurieu says, “it is known by all who are 
versed in the prophets, that in the prophetic style 
an earthquake signifies a great commotion of na- 
tions.” Vol. ii. p. 261. See also Lancaster, and 
Vitringa, én voc. ouopoç. 

` Valerius.—In order to obtain a right key to the 
symbols, we must look to their roots rather than 
to their single meaning m detached places; and 
we shall find, that wherever there is a figure ex- 
pressive of one principle, or one set of men under 
the influence of that principle, there is alsoa cor- 
responding figure or symbol representing the op- 
posite character : for example, we have the lion of 
the tribe of Judah, and the lion walking about 
seeking whom he may devour; the soft water of 
Shiloh, or the fountains of living waters, and the 
sea and the waves roaring, or raging waves of 
the sea, Jude 13. The star of Jacob, and wander- 
ing stars; the tree of life, and trees without fruit, 
&e. &c. Thus the same key which will unlock 
the symbols of one part of prophecy, will unlock 
those of all. 

Philalethes.—Authors, in Symbolical Diction- 
aries, frequently give an explanation of a sym- 
bol taken exclusively from the Apocalypse, which 
is arguing in a circle, and taking for granted the 
very point in question. | 

Anastasius.—Undoubtedly it is; but it is very 
easy to find out the true meaning of symbols by 
the Old Testament Scriptures. Lancaster’s Dic- 
tionary, and Jones’s Figurative Language, are use- 
ful aids for that purpose. 7

marked in the Apocalypse do you consider we are.

Anastasius.—From as careful a review as I have. 
been enabled to take of the various opinions ad-

derance of argument seems to me to lay on the 
side of those who consider that the first vial was 
poured out at the French Revolution; that we are 
now living under the sixth; and that we may look 
hourly for the seventh and last, if, indeed, the first 
drop has not already fallen in the landing of the 
English army in Portugal. 
~ Philalethes.—I will not impose so long a task 
upon you, as to request an explanation of all that 
part of the Apocalypse, of which the structure has. 
been given, but I should like much to have an in- 
terpretation of the vials. 

Anastasius.—Whiston says, prop. 15, “ The six 
first seals, and the six first trumpets, are all over 
before the end of the 1260 years of Antichrist’s 
reign; and the seventh trumpet, or seven vials 
contained in it, follow immediately after that time, 
and are cotemporary with the first ages of our 
Saviour’s kingdom, succeeding to it.” In this the 
best modern commentators agree, to whose works 
I must refer you, and assume, without waiting now 
to prove it, that the first vial is the commencement 
of the French Revolution; for as the city of Jericho 
fell after seven times sounding a trumpet, which 
seventh time of sounding was made up of seven 
blasts, so does it appear that the great oppressing 
city of these days is to fall at the sound of the 
seventh trumpet, which is made up of the seven 


309 


blasts of the thunders or vials. ‘‘ The first angel 
poured out his vial upon the earth; and there fell a 
noisome and grievous sore upon the men which 
had the mark of the beast, even them which wor- 
shipped his image.” The principal symbol to con- 
sider in this verse is the “ sore.” A ‘ sore” is used 
by Isaiah, i. 6, as expressive of the total moral cor- 
ruption of the whole mass of the Jewish nation ; and 
nothing can be conceived more appropriate, when 
we call to mind, that a sore breaking out upon any 
part of the human body, is merely a symptom or 
proof that the whole blood and general system is 
in an unhealthy state. This is truly descriptive of 
the state of the French nation, nobles, clergy, and 
people, for a considerable time previous to, and 
particularly at the period of the French Revolution. 
Frivolity, profligacy, and infidelity, pervaded every 
part of the population. The details of the pri- 
vate lives of the court and nobles during the reigns 
of Louis XIV. XV. and the regency of the Duke 
of Orleans, surpass in licentiousness and cruelty 
any thing that has ‘ever taken place since the days 
of the Roman Emperors. The sore of infidelity is 
still seen upon the Papal nations, the Protestant 
subjects of which are-fully as deeply tainted with 
that moral plague as their Popish countrymen. 
No person is free from being influenced, more or. 
less, by the predominant spirit of the times in 
which he lives; and let every Christian man 
seriously examine how far the spirit of infi- 
delity has infected himself; how far he believes 
God simply on his word, or endeavours to explain 
it away, and especially, on the great hope of the 


310 


church in all ages, the coming of the Lord to cast 
Satan out of the earth. 

‘“ The second angel poured out his vial upon the 
sea, and it became as the blood of a corpse; and 
every living soul died in the sea.” We have seen 
that the term “ sea” is used in other parts of Scrip- 
ture to express the people; when it is used in 
connection with the term earth, the latter seems 
to signify the body of the people in a state of rest 
and quiet, or at least under settled regular govern- 
ance: the former, the same people after they are 
thrown into a state of agitation and anarchy, by 
an earthquake, or political revolution; and the 
effect of this vial is, that the sea becomes not blood 
merely, but as the blood of a corpse. Now the 
blood of a corpse is cold blood. There is this pe- 
culiarity about the French Revolution, namely, 
that in other revolutions, the greater part of the 
persons who died, perished in the civil wars, which 
accompanied them; but in this revolution, very 
few persons, comparatively, lost their lives in 
battle; for, except in the province of La Vendée, 
there was no civil war, whilst hundreds of thousands 
were butchered in cold blood by the hands of the 
executioners. 

‘* And the third angel poured out his vial upon 
the rivers and fountains of waters, and they be- 
came blood: and I heard the angel of the waters 
say, Thou art righteous, O Lord, which art, and 
wast, and shalt be, because thou hast judged thus ; 
for they have shed the blood of saints, and pro- 
phets, and thou hast given them blood to drink, for 
they are worthy: and I heard another out of the 


311 


altar say, Even so, Lord God of Hosts, true and 
righteous are thy judgments.” Here, then, we ob- 
serve, that the rivers and fountains of waters mean 
persons, because it is declared of them that they 
have shed blood, &c. We learn from Isa. lv. 1, 
and Ezek. xlvii. and other places, that “‘ waters” 
signify the doctrines and ordinances of religion; 
and the rivers and fountains of these must conse- 
quently be the clergy who dispense them, the 
well-heads and sources of the apostacy. They 
are said to become blood, and in fact the whole 
order was extirpated. Barruel, speaking of the 
sufferings of the French clergy at this. period, says, 
Cent trente huit Eveques ou Archevéques ; soixante 
quatre mille Curés ou Vicaires, condamnés a quitter 
leur sieges, leur paroisses, ou bien à prononcer le 
serment du parjure, et de lapostasie; tous les eccle- 
siastiques, tous les religieux de l'un et l'autre sexe, 
chassés de leur asiles; les temples du Seigneur 
changés en vastes prisons pour ses ministres ; trots 
cent de ses prétres massacrés dans l'espace d'un jour 
dans une seule ville; tous les autres pasteurs tmmolés 
ou chassés de leur patrie, et cherchant à travers mille 
dangers quelque réfuge auprès des nations etran- 
géres ; tel est le spectacle que la revolution Francoise 
vient de donner au. monde. Whatever commisera- 
tion might be shewn to these individuals, it must 
not be forgotten, that considering the Popish 
clergy as one body, they were the inventors 
and executors of all the atrocities of the Inquisi- 
tion; the bloody tortures inflicted through a suc- 
cession of ages on the Waldenses, on the Jews, 
and subsequently on Protestanis: it was the 


312: 


Popish clergy that “ shed the blood of saints and 
prophets ;”” and God, in his righteous retribution, 
“ gave them,” in their turn, “ blood to drink.” 
Without taking any other accounts but the authen- 
tic records told by themselves, it is perfectly clear 
that the Popish clergy have bathed their hands in 
more human blood than the Carthagenians, the 
South Americans, Indians, or the priests of any 
other idolatry which has been ever heard of. See 
the accounts of the Inquisition in Spain, Portugal, 
Goa, Low Countries, La Vallée, Germany, and 
Mexico. In Spain alone, the original documents, 
. published by Llorente, Secretary to the Inquisi-. 
tion, show, that from its establishment, to the 
beginning of the French Revolution, . 34,658 
human beings were burned alive. If the crimes. 
of men merit Divine punishment, wiat did not 
the Popish priests deserve ? 

. . And the fourth angel poured out his vial upon 
the sun, and pé@wer was given him to scorch men 
with fire. And men were scorched with great 
heat, and blasphemed the name of God which had 
power over these plagues, yet they repented not, 
to give him glory.” The pouring out of a vial is 
generally an expression for some evil befalling the 
persons on whom it is poured; but here it says, 
that the consequence of its effusion was the con- 
ferring of power. This would suggest that the 
power given was a power of doing evil. We have 
seen that the sun is the emblem of the temporal 
sovereignty : this was destroyed in the person of 
the unfortunate Louis XVI., but was revived in 
that of Bonaparte, who perfected the most rigorous 


313 


and efficient military tyranny which had existed 
for many centuries, by means of which he marched 
triumphant into the capital of every continental 
kingdom. Yet the calamities of war, the scorching 
which the men of the Popish states received from 
this burning sun, produced no moral or religious 
reformation: they did not turn to the God who 
sent these plagues; they have not repented, nor 
given Him glory. The conduct of England, during 
the life-time of George III., was very different. 
She did, repeatedly, turn unto the Lord, in solemn 
national fastings and prayers; and at the conclu- 
sion of the peace, all the nations of Europe cele- 
brated public thanksgivings: no thanksgivings 
have, however, been offered for its continuance. — 
_ ** And the fifth angel poured out his vial upon 
the throne of the beast; and his kingdom was full 
of darkness: and they gnawed their tongues for 
pain, and blasphemed the God of heaven because 
of their pains and their sores, yet repented not of 
their deeds.” The beast is the symbol used by 
Daniel for each of the powers which have in suc- 
cession oppressed the church of Christ, of which 
the Roman is the last. The Emperor of Austria 
was also Emperor of the Most Holy Roman Em- 
pire. This title was wrenched from him by the 
French armies, and transferred to the Emperor of 
the French, by his assuming the title of King of 
Rome. But the symbol may be equally applied 
to the subsequent destruction of Bonaparte’s dy- 
nasty, as it is by Mr. Irving, Vol. i. p. 229. 

s< The sixth angel poured out his vial upon the 
great river Euphrates, and the water thereof was

dried up, that the way of the kings of the East 
might be prepared.” Two explanations of this 
vial may be given. If the river Euphrates be con- 
sidered symbolical, and part of the symbolical 
Babylon, it must here be taken to have the same 
reference in the figure which it had in reality. 
The river constituted both the support and the 
defence of the city. That which constitutes the 
support and the defence of the Papal Babylon is 
her temporal possessions, worldly rank, tythes, 
property, power, &c. When the river was dried 
up the city fell. Ifthe revenues were taken froma 
the Antichristian church she would fall: and great 
part of the possessions of the church was taken 
from her at the period of the French Revolution, 
which blow is the severest she. has felt, and that 
alone which prevents her acting at present with all 
the violence which she has shown in past times. 
But as the word Euphrates is used in another part 
of this book, chap. ix. 14, where it is clearly to 
be taken literally, it seems more probable that we 
ought to take it literally bere also ; and in this case 
we must consider it as the symbol of the Mahom- 
medan power, which was used as the scourge of 
the apostacy of the Eastern branch of the Empire, 
as the French infidel power was used as the 
punisher of the apostacy of the Western branch. 
This vial then is poured out on the Turkish Em- 
pire. ‘‘ The kings of the East,” or “of the sun- 
rising,” are generally considered, and apparently. 
with reason, to signify the restored Israel, whose

_. “ And I saw three unclean spirits, like frogs,

Philalethes.—I see you have not followed the 
authorized version in this passage. 
Anastasius.—The alterations are trivial, and you 
will find sufficient reason for them by consulting 
Thurston, and Tilloch. From Isa. xxvii. |, and 
Rev. xx. 2, we know that the Dragon is the Devil, 
< the God of this World,” ‘the Prince of the 
power of the Air.” As there is more than one 
beast mentioned in this book, it is not easy to 
shew which is the beast here intended, but it is 
fair to conclude that it is the same power as that 
on whose throne the fifth vial is poured. The 
false prophet is probably the Papacy. That these 
Spirits are principles, seems obvious; and the 
wonder which each is endeavouring to work, is to 
still and quiet this earth, which has been thrown 
into such great confusion by the previous vials. 
The Spirit from Satan goes forth in unsanctified 
knowledge, infidelity, unnatural affections, un- 
tamed passions, resistance to constituted autho- 
rities, contempt of ancient institutions, and aiming 


316 


at introducing a millennium of blessedness, ‘by 
the march of mind, and reformation of civil and 
ecclesiastical governments. The spirit from the 
beast goes forth in rivetting the bands of des- 
potism, increasing standing armies, oppressing 
more heavily the people, refusing them an equality 
of political privileges, and endeavouring to check 
their intellectual advancement. The spirit from 
the false prophet goes forth in re-establishing his 
superstitions and idolatries, which had been over- 
thrown; in multiplying his priests, monks, Jesuits, 
and other emissaries; restoring his blood-thirsty 
inquisition; and separating his tribunals from the 
control of the civil magistrate. Each of these, 
by their several systems, thinks that, if he could 
succeed, he would say, “ Peace, be still,” to the 
troubled earth; instead of which they are only 
preparing to gather the kings of the whole empire 
together, in one great and tremendous and final 
conflict. It is to be observed, that the original 
expression, which is rendered in our version 
“« working miracles,” is rather ‘‘ doing wonders ;” 
onpeov signifies an uncommon event, not contrary 
to the ordinary laws of divine providence; where 
any thing contrary to those ordinary laws is in- 
tended, the word made use of is repara. See a 
Dissertation of Erkhard in Thesaurus Theologico 
—Philologus, vol. ii. 

Philalethes.—Has the effect of one vial com- 
_ pletely ceased before another is poured out ? 

Anastasius. —Not necessarily so by any expres- 
sions in the text; and we see that the same state of 
things exists now, though in a minor degree, which

existed at the beginning of, and all throughout, the

Philalethes.—When may we expect the seventh 
vial ? E 

Anastasius —It is impossible to answer that 
question before the time shall arive; but we have 
lately had remarkable light thrown upon its in- 
terpretation by very unexpected commentators. 
“ And the seventh angel poured out his vial into 
the air.” That the air is a symbol of something 
that is universal, has been concluded by all writers; 
and it appears that under the effusion of this 
seventh vial, that war must take place, to which 
the three unclean spirits are said, in the preceding 
verses, to be gathering the kings of the whole 
empire. Nothing can give a clearer explanation 
of the whole than the following passage in the 
speech of Mr. Canning upon the sending of the 
British troops to Portugal, delivered on Dec. 12, 
1826. ‘I dread it, (war) from an apprehension 
of the tremendous consequences which might arise 
from any hostilities in which we might now be 
engaged. Some years ago, in the discussion of the 
negociations respecting the French War against 
Spain, I took the liberty of adverting to this topic. 
I then stated that the position of this country, 
in the present state of the world, was one of 
neutrality, not only between contending nations, 
but between conflicting principles; and that it was 
by neutrality alone that we could maintain that 
balance, the preservation of which I believed to 


318 


be essential to the welfare of mankind. 1 then 
said, that I feared that the nert war which should 
be kindled in Europe, would be a war not so much 
of armies, as of opinions. Not four years have 
elapsed, and behold my apprehension realised. 
It is, to be sure, within narrow limits, that -this 
war of opinion is at present confined; but it is 
a war of opinion, that Spain, (whether as govern- 
ment or as nation), is now waging against Por- 
tugal; if into that war this country shall be com- 
pelled to enter, we shall enter into it with a sin- 
cere and anxious desire to mitigate rather than 
exasperate; and to mingle only in the conflict 
of arms, not in the more fatal conflict of opinions. 
But I much fear that this country, (however 
earnestly she may endeavour to avoid it,) could 
not, in such case, avoid seeing ranked under her 
banners all the restless and dissatisfied of any 
nation: with which she might come in conflict. 
It is the contemplation of this new power in any 
future war, which excites my most anxious appre- 
hension. The situation of England, amidst the 
struggle of political opinions which agitates more 
or less sensibly different countries of the world, may 
be compared to that of the ruler of the winds, as 
described by the poet : 


‘ Celsé sedet Æolas arce, 

Sceptra tenens ; mollitque animos et temperat iras ; 
Ni faciat, maria ac terras cælumque profundum 
Quippe ferant rapidi secum, verrantque per auras.’ 


The consequence of letting loose the passions, at 
present chained and confined, would be to pro- 


r 


319 


duce.a scene of desolation which no man can con- 
template without horror.”—Such being the senti- 
ments of the most consummate statesman in 
Europe, of the effects likely to be produced by 
the next war, in the development of which senti- 
ments, he makes use of the terms “winds” and 
“air,” as figures for conflicting principles and 
struggling opinions, we cannot have a more per- 
fect explanation of the events to be expected 
under the seventh vial than that which is afforded 
in the speech of Mr. Canning. 

This also throws considerable light upon another 
passage, namely, chap. vii. 1, where the messen - 
gers of the Most High are commanded to restrain 
the winds, whilst some people, symbolized by 
the twelve tribes of Israel, are preserved. Arch- 
deacon Woodhouse, in his Notes on this chapter, 
cites, in illustration of it, the very same passage 
from Virgil, that Mr. Canning, in the extract to 
which I have referred, repeated as so strikingly 
descriptive of the nature of the approaching war. 
The bad principles referred to as producing such 
tremendous effects under the seventh vial, were 
in existence, as we have seen, from the commence- 
ment of the first, and they deluged the whole 
Apocalyptic earth, with the exception of England, 
which nation was alone preserved. They existed 
indeed in this country, as the ‘‘ Corresponding 
Societies” and State Trials of the time abundantly _ 
prove; but they were restrained ; they were not 
allowed to break loose; and this gives consider- 
able force to the suggestion of Mr. Frere, that 
the 144,000 sealed ones are the symbol of that. 


320 


Protestant nation which was so preserved, which 
was the first to break off from the idolatries of 
the papacy, and which is not defiled with that 
woman clothed in scarlet. Speaking of that 
very time, namely, the beginning of the French 
Revolution, Mr. Burke makes use of the same 
figure of air, to express the principle then afloat. 
© When I see the spirit of liberty in action, I see- 
a strong principle at work—the wild gas, the fired 
air is plainly broke loose,” &c. That acute poli- 
tical writer, Mr. Cobbet, also, in commenting 
upon the above-quoted speech of Mr. Canning, 
says, that the very same principles to. which he 
there alludes had been in operation at the be- 
ginning of the Revolutionary war. 

Philalethes.—A reference to the Dares would 
surely determine the meaning of the Symsots 
with perfect accuracy. , > 

Anastasius.—Certainly it. would, if we knew, 
first, what kind of Chronology to use; secondly, 
if we could be certain that that Chronology was 
correct; and thirdly, if we could ascertain the 
proper points from_ whence to commence our 
reckoning. 

Philalethes.—What do you mean by kind of 
chronology ? 

Anastasius. —The chronology which is now in 
universal use throughout Christendom, dates either 
from the supposed time of the birth of our Lord, 
or from that of the creation of the world. But still 
the question is undecided how many years have 
elapsed since either of those events. With respect 
to the former, it was not thought of being fixed 


321 
till about 527 years after it took place; and with 
respect to the latter, Strauchius, in his Breviarium 
Chronologicum, gives a list of 23 authorities, all 
differing from each other as to the age of the globe. 
Attempts have been made to ascertain this from 
astronomical calculations, and these Dr. Kennedy 
asserts, p. 140, have in every instance coincided 
with the dates given in the Hebrew Bibles; and he 
adds, that he has not been “able to discover one 
single various reading in that multitude of numeral 
words and letters, which constitute the scriptural 
series of years, from the creation to the burning of 
the first temple by the Babylonians ; the Jewish 
Hebrew text has never been corrupted in the 
article. of chronology, either by Jew or Pagan, 
either by chance or design.” It is to be observed 
also, that in the Scriptures themselves, no event 
is ever dated by reference to the creation, or to the 
birth of our Lord. Sir Isaac Newton, after men- 
tioning the various days on which different remark- 
able events in the history of the church were cele- 
brated by the early Christians, adds, ‘‘ neither 
was there any certain tradition about ‘the years of 
Christ; for the first Christians make Christ to 
have preached but one year, or at most two; 
neither is there any greater certainty in the opi- 
nions about the time of his birth; therefore, re- 
lating to these things, there is no tradition worth 
considering ;” Obs. on Dan. p.145: he therefore 
proceeds to settle that epoch upon other grounds, 
and establishes the fact as it is now generally 
received. The next difficulty which arises, is one 
which would not be removed by the most exact 
Y 


322 


chronology, and that is the uncertainty of know- 
ing whence to commence the reckoning ; but this 
is a difficulty which has been gradually diminishing 
in every succeeding age of the ehurch, because 
there are ‘only a limited number of events from 
which we-can set oat as starting points. 
Philalethes.—Will you be so good as to men- 
tion some of these points ? | 
Anastasius.—The first to which I will direct 
your attention, is also the first in order, namely, 
that of Dan. vii. 25, where it is said that the 
saints shal be delivered into the hands of the 
little horn, “ wntil a time, times, and the dividing 
of time.” 1 do not stop to shew, that the power 
signified under the emblem of this little horn is 
the papacy ; but taking that for granted, we have 
still to inquire from history, whether at any time 
the saints were delivered into his hands, and 
when that fact took place. For persons to be de- 
livered, or given over, into the hands of any one, 
it is a pre-requisite that they should be first in 
the hands of him who gives them over. -A seizure, 
or unlawful assumption of them, does not justify 
the use of the expression, that they are delivered 
or given over into the hands of him who ‘so -gets 
possession of them; and therefore this is conclu- | 
sive against our dating the period in question from 
the time when any Pope assumed such power, 
title, or dignity of himself, unless the saints were 
given into his hands by some other who was in 
possession of them. Now, no one could be in 
possession of the saints of God, but their lawful 
Governor, the Roman Emperor ; the head of the 


323 


last beast which was ever to oppress them; and 
we do accordingly find extant an edict of Justi- 
nian’s, by which he makes over to the Pope all 
persons as heretics, who should differ from his

Philalethes—Do: you feel quite confident that 
1260 days mean always 1260 years? 

Anastasius.—The question in this place is rather 
what period is meant to be measured by the 
words ‘‘ time, times, and half a time.” The 
argument drawn. from the word “ day,” never 
meaning a “ year” in other parts of scripture, is 
wholly inapplicable here, for the word is “ time,” 
and not “ day;” and certainly there is as much 
right to- assume that the word “time” signifies a 
year, as any other period; and it will hardly be 
contended that in the fourth chapter, ver. 16, of- 
this same prophet, the word signifies only a day, 
when during ‘‘ seven times” Nebuchadnezzar was 
to have his nails grow as long as birds’ claws, his- 
hairs like eagles’ feathers, and he was to be driven 
from man. In chap. xi. 13. we find the expres- 
sion, “ shall certainly come at the end of. times, 
even years,” marg., which alone might settle the 
question. With respect to the word ‘‘ day” not 
being used in symbolical prophecies for a year, 
I know not how this new conceit has got into 
some peoples heads; but since it is there, we 
must revert to our elements of criticism again; 
so true is it in this, as in every thing else, ‘‘ there 
is nothing new beneath the sun.”

Philalethes.—Does not Mr. Frere say that it 

ought to be 2400, and that it is so in the — 
Septuagint 

Sophron.—Yes, he does, but his position is 
quite untenable; and he has subsequently aban- 
doned it; p. 259. The fact is, that our com- 
mon editions of the} Greek Septuagint Bibles are 
printed after a copy in which there was this typo- 
graphical error; an error acknowledged to be 
such, and what no scholar has dreamed of calling 
in question. The edition of the Greek Buble 
which is commonly used, is printed, as you will 
find it stated in Prideaux, and Horne, not after 


327 


that of the 70, but after that of Theodotion, 
made about the end of the second century. 
There are three principal standard editions of 
the Septuagint Bible, all containing the version 
of Daniel by Theodotion; viz. the Complutensian, 
published in 1514; the Aldine, 1518; and the 
Vatican, 1587, from which the last English editions. 
of the 70 have been ¢hiefly taken; to these three, 
we may add a fourth, being that of the Alex- 
andrian text, published between 1707 and 1720, 
Besides these, there is one called the Chisian, 
1772, which contains the Greek text both of 
Theodotion and of the 70. Of all these six 
copies the Vatican alone reads 2400, all the rest 
agreeing with the Hebrew and our English Bibles. 
Moreover, the manuscript itself, in the Vatican, 
from which the edition was printed, has 2300, 
and not 2400, and therefore it is indisputable that 
the number 2400 is nothing but a mis-print. 
Anastasius.—] entirely subscribe to Sophron’s 
opinion, My. Faber is also culpable in taking 
‘another spurious reading from Jerome, namely, 
2200: and itis the more necessary to be on our

Anastasius,—A decree went forth to restore and 
build Jerusalem, by Cyrus, to Ezra, 536 years 
before Christ, the execution of which. occupied a 
considerable number of years ; or rather it is more 
correct to say, four commandments went forth in 
favour of the Jews from three several kings. of 
Persia; ist, from Cyrus; 2d, from Darius Hys- 
taspes; 3d and 4th, from Artaxerxes Longimanus : 
we shall find, hereafter, that many other periods 
terminate in the year A.D. 1847; and if this pe- 
riod end in the same, its commencement must be 
453, or 73 years after the original decree; (pro- 
vided always that our chronology is sufficiently 
correct.) Thus the time of the typical or partial 
cleansing of the sanctuary will be the point from 
which to commence, and the time of the anti- 
typical, or true cleansing, will be the termination 
of that period expressed by 2300. Mr. Mason 
reckons from the year 457, but it is obvious, that 
unless the date of the decree itself be taken, any 
one subsequent year,. during the time that the 
cleansing was in progress, 1s as probable as another. 
. Although this is the best interpretation which has 
hitherto been given of this number, I confess it is 
to me far from satisfactory. It may also be re- 
marked in corroboration of this, that our blessed 
Lord, during his personal ministry on earth, twice 
cleansed the temple; once at the beginning of it, 
John ii. 13, and again at his last entry into Jeru- 
salem, when his ministry was to cease, Matt. xxi. 
12. Itis further worthy of observation, that we 
learn from Josephus, that the second temple, built 
by Zerubbabel, was burnt by the Romans on the

very same day of the very same month that the

The next number is in Dan. xii., where the pro- 
phet having been told of a long list of kings who 
were to be the oppressors of his nation, hears one 
of the angels ask the other, ver. 6, “ How long the 
end of the wonders ?” To which he receives am- 
swer, ver. 7, “ For a time, times, and half; and when 
he shall have accomplished to scatter the power 
of the holy people, all these shall be finished.” 
This term of * time, times, and half,” is one of the 
many forms in which the same peried is expressed, 
namely 1260 years. The prophet did not under- 
stand, for he says, “ E heard but I understood not ; 
then said I, O, my lord, what the end of these?” 
but he is informed, that this is not to be known 
till the time of the end; that none of the wicked 
shall understand, but the wise shall understand. 
The prophet receives for answer, not the same 
number that one of the angels had told to the 
other, and which he had overheard, but literally 
the following words, “ from time it was taken 
away—daily sacrifice, and to give abomimation—~ 
desolating days, 1290; and blessed is he that 
watcheth and cometh to the 1335 days.” Here then 
we have three periods; the second exceeding the 
~ former by 30 days, and the last exceeding that by: 
45. It appears probable, therefore, that though 
the final consummation of the predicted events 
will not exceed the longest of these periods, a be- 
ginning of that consummation may take place at 
the first ; as for example, though the vail of the

temple was rent at the death of Christ, and though: 
the Jews were ta cease to be the exclusive people 
af God. at the coming of Christ, yet their final re- 
jection was not. perfected until the destruction of 
Jerusalem by Titus; so, in like manner, a series of. 
judgments in the last. days may commence at the: 
expiration of the 1260, but nat be completed until 
the end of the 1335. |

Crito.—It is indisputable that certain numbers 
are more applied than others to sacred. purposes ; 
of these the numbers 3 and 7 are of the most ge- 
neral use; they are found varied by different mul- 
tiples; thus, the great period common to Daniel 


om, 


332 


and John is presented ‘to us first, as consisting of 
7. equal parts, in the “time, times, and. half a 
time ;” this again is presented multiplied by .12, 
in the 42 months (7 x 12=84—42=—42), and again 
by 30 in the 1260 days; and the further additions 
made to this period (Dan. xii. 1], 12,) of 30 and 
45 years are also multiples of 3. We find also the 
number 7, and its multiples by 10, in the Seals, 
Trumpets, and Vials; the lamps before the throne, 
which are said to be “ the 7 spirits of God ;” the 
34 days, during which the two witnesses. lie dead ; 
the 7000 who were slain by the. earthquake, and 
various other. periods and symbols of the Apo- 
calypse; also, in the days of the creation, in the 
70.elders of Israel, 70 disciples of our Lord, 70 
years of Babylonish captivity, and 7000 who had 
not bowed the knee to Baal. The number 7, 
which in Hebrew signifies fulness or perfection, 
has the preference, when it is not intended that 
any particular number should be specified; as 
when it is said, the Israelites shall flee before their 
enemies 7 ways; and when our Lord commands 
his disciples to forgive their enemies, not only 


until 7 times, but until 70 times 7. 


Squares of numbers are also frequently used. 
Four, the first or most simple square number, oc- 
curs in the 4 beasts of Daniel; in the 40 days, 
during which the land of Canaan was searched ; 
the 40 years wandering in the wilderness; 400 
years of affliction to Abraham’s seed. The day of 
Pentecost occurred at the expiration of a period 
expressed by the square of the number 7, or 
when 7 Sabbaths were completed. In the: same 


333 


manner was the year of Jubilee appointed; and 
the symbolical Israelitish nation, Rev. vii., re- 
presenting a nation, in these latter times, elected 
and chosen of God, as the 12 tribes were, to 
peculiar national privileges, is described by its 
square, or by 144 multiplied by 1000; by which . 
number it is again represented in chap. xiv. 

Cubes are also sometimes used, as in the- 
measures of Solomon’s temple, and the New 
Jerusalem. 

As geometrical progression, limited to 3 terms, 
produces the perfect number 7, so we find that 4, 
the most simple square number, when multiplied 
by 3, gives another sacred number, to be found in 
the 12 months of which the symbolical year is 
composed ; the 12 tribes of Israel; the 12 Apostles; 
the 12 oxen which supported the molten sea in the 
temple. The number 8 also, the first and most 
simple cube, when multiplied by 3, gives another 
sacred number, in the 24 elders of the Apoca- 
lypse ; 24,000 Levites, with their 24 courses; and 
the 12 courses of 24,000 each, who ministered to 
the king. 

It appears, then, that the number 3, dsame 
progression, the square, and the cube, are to be 
considered as perfect forms of number; whence 
are derived, besides the number 3 itself, those of 
4, 7, 8, 12, and.24, from two of which we obtain 
the larger squares, 49 and 144, and the larger 
cube, 1728, which most perfect number is the 
measure of the New Jerusalem, as given in Rev. 
xxi. 16. The multiplication of any sacred num- 
ber (as a square or a cube) by 10, is only 


334 


a repetition of that number, which gives it a 
higher importance, without attering its primitive 
character. | 

In some few prophetic periods, there is found a 
lesser degree of perfection, as in the “5 months,” 
or the 150 years of the Saracenitc Woe, which ap- 
pears to be formed by the dividing of a month, 
multiplied by 10, in like manner as the ‘time, 
times, and a half” is formed, by the dividing of a 
time multiplied by 7. Soin the 45 years.of Daniel, 
which is composed of a month and the dividing of 
a month; and in the 390 years of Ezekiel, which 
is a year and a month. The square, the cebe, or 
the number 3, are thus found in every prophetic 
or sacred number. 
| The principal subjects of prophetic history will 
also be found to have certain perfect periods of 
years belonging to them, though they are not men- 
tioned in its text. Thus, the sacred times of ‘the 
four Gentile monarchies, described in the viston of 
the great image, commence in the year B. C. 603, 
when that vision was seen, and end A. D. 1798, 
when the Apocalyptic vial of wrath was poured 
out ‘upon Rome, the metropolis of the fourth or 
last, and consist exactly of a period of 49 x 49, or 
2401 years. The addition of a fiftieth, or Jubilee © 
period of another 49 years, bemg made to this 
grand and sacred period of 49 x 49 +49 =2450, will 
extend tt to the year 1847. : 

The times of the Gentile church, commencing 
from the great judgment upon the Jewish Apos- 
tacy, in ‘the destruction of Jerusalem, A. D. 70, 
and consisting of a sacred period of 12x 12 x 12 =

1728, (bemg the measute of the New Jerusalem,) 
will also be found to have expired in the same 
year as the 49 x 49 of the preceding vision, that is,

church. , 

The Mahomedan period also consists, like the 
“Papal, of a sacred number, though it is no where 
specified ; for, from its authorized commencement 
at the Hegira, A. D. 622, to the year 1847, when 
we have otherwise found that the sanctuary of 
Jerusalem is at length to be cleansed from its pol-. 
lution, as a sacred period of 35x 35, or 1225 years.

10, or which is equivalent to it, by the dividing 
of 70. It is also to be observed, that this pe- 
riod of 1225 years, is the dividing of the entire 
period of the 2450 years of the vision of the great 
image. 

So also from the great reformation effected by

half; and from thence to its expiration in the year 
1792, was a like period; making in each an allow-

Valerius.—Another similar period may be ob- 
served between the time that the Israelites re- 
jected the Lord from being their king, 1 Sam. x. 
19, B. C. 1096, and that of their going into- cap- 


337 


tivity, 2 Chron. xxxvi. 21, B, C. 606, which is 70 
sabbaths, or 490 years. The sabbath seems to have 
been a sign of their being in covenant relationship 
with God; for it was only by his giving a miraculous 
double increase on the 6th year that they were 
able to keep the sabbatical year. After their re- 
jection of God, no mention is made of the sab- 
batical year being kept, from whence it would 
seem, that no double increase had been given on 
the sixth. This is further proved by 2 Kings xix. 
29, where the promise of their being able to do so 
is given as a miraculous sign. The land there- 
fore did not enjoy her sabbaths from the year 
1096, B.C., till the period mentioned in Zecha- 
riah, B. C. 606, which is a period of 70 sabbaths, 
or 490 years. 

` Anastasius.—An important practical reflection 
arises from this consideration, which is, that we 
should take good heed as to the manner in which 
we pass the Sabbath-day, as a means of keeping 
up the testimony of our Covenant Tera ONERID 
with God. 

Philalethes.—Does not Mr. Frere still support 
his alteration of the number 2300 to 2400 by this 
theory of sacred numbers ? 

Anastasius.—I am sorry to say that he does; 
for nothing can be more unwarrantable than trifling 
with the text of the Scriptures, and arbitrarily pre- 
suming to alter it to suit any theory or scheme. 
There is no pretext whatever for disputing the au- 
thenticity of the text: not even a various reading. 
It is reasonable to suppose, that all the periods 
mentioned in Holy Writ have some ground for the 

Z 


338 


number assigned to them, and that these numbers 
bear a certain analogy one to the other. With 
respect to the squaring of numbers, it is distinctly 
said in Ezek. xlviii. 20, ‘* All the oblation shall be 
five and twenty thousand by five and twenty thou- 
sand ; ye shall offer the holy oblation /four-square, 
with the possession of the city.” 

Valerius.—The Apostle tells the Thessalonians, 
1. v. 1, that there is no need of his writing to them 
of the times and the seasons, for that they. them- 
selves knew perfectly that the day of the Lord 
comes as a thief in the night ; and that they are not 
in darkness that that day should overtake them as 
a thief. It was then the times and the seasons 
which had given them this knowledge: and I be- 
lieve, that a study of the Jewish ¢imes and seasons 
would lead us into the knowledge of the chronology 
of the Holy Scriptures. The Apostle also tells us, in 
his Epistle to the Colossians, ii. 16, that holy days, 
new moons, and sabbaths, are shadows of things 
which are to come; and it is scarcely to be credited, 
a priori, that the times of each Jewish rite, feast, 
and ceremony, should have been handed down to 
us with such minuteness, unless they also, as well 
as the other Scriptures, were to be profitable to 
us. . Besides, as the seasons refer to the former 
rain, seed-time, latter rain, harvest, and vintage, 
so do the times refer to the three times a year, 
that all the males were obliged to appear before 
the Lord at Jerusalem. The first two periods, 
viz. the Passover, which was at the commence- 
ment of the first feast of first-fruits; and Pente- 
cost, which was at the commencement of the 


339 


second feast of first fruits, were minutely fulfilled 
in the two great events of the sacrifice of Christ, 
our Passover, and the commencement of the 
dispensation of the Spirit at Pentecost. The an- 
titype to the third time of appearance at Jerusa- 
lem, viz. the Feast of Tabernacles, has not yet been 
fulfilled, but will surely be so at the coming of our 
Lord. This Feast, like all others, is both comme- 
morative and typical ; commemorative of the chil- 
dren of Israel tabernacling ‘in the wilderness, and 
typical of Christ’s incarnation (see Mr. Simeon’s Ser- 
mons)—his ‘‘ tabernacling” (Johni. 14. eoxnvwoev) 
amongst us whilst the Church is in her wilderness 
state. It appears to have been first observed, 
when the children of Israel returned to Jerusalem 
from the Babylonish captivity, which is probably 
typical of the future return to Jerusalem, Zech. ii. 
7, when it will be observed again, Zech. xiv. 16, 
when the tabernacle of God is with men: that is, 
when Christ comes again to Jerusalem, prefigured 
by the tabernacle being removed to the temple at 
the Feast of Tabernacles, (1 Kings viii. 2; 2 Chron. 
vii. 9, 10,) which is the Church in her glorious 
state; and this would bring Rev. xv. 8, parallel 
with vii. 9. It is the antitype to this and all the 
other feasts which were appointed to the Ist civil 
month, Tisri, for which we are now to look; be- 
cause the antitypes to those of the 1st Ecclesiasti- 
cal month,’ Abib. have been fulfilled; and the 
ceremonial law having been abrogated, we are re- 
turned to the civil year, as it was at the beginning. 
The year at the creation of Adam commenced with 
the autumn, as we know, amongst other reasons, 
Z 2 


340 


by the trees being first created with their seeds 
upon them. The year was changed at the coming 
up out of Egypt to the spring, Exod: xii. 2, and 
xiii. 3; and all the feasts appointed subsequently 
were arranged according to that year, henee called 
the Ecclesiastical. But the cerénonial law being 
now done away, we are returned to the original 
year, beginning at the autumn, and it is the fulfil- 
ment of those feasts which were otdered to be 
kept.in that month which we have now to expect. 
The offering of Cain and Abel seems to have been 
on the new-year’s day (compare Gen, iv. 8, margin, 
with. 3 Sam. xiv:.26.) The feast of trumpets was 
dlso on new-year's day. It is, perhaps, in this 
sense, therefore, that the blessing promised to 
those who study the Apocalypse is to be under- 
stood, namely, because it is not possible to under- 
stand its symbolical language; and chronological 
indications, but by an accurate study of the cere- 


monial law; not only in the books of Moses, where _ 


the law is detailed, but in the writings also of the pro- 
phets, where so many figures alluding to it are found. 

Anastasius.—Such is also the reflection of Sir 
I. Newton, who says, “the event will prove the 
Apocalypse; and this prophecy, thus proved and 
understood, will open the old prophets ;—for he 
that will understand the old prophets must begin 
with this,’"—p. 252, according to the well known 
remark, Veteri testamento novum latet; novo tes- 
tamento vetus patet.
