Valerius.—There is not one of the prophets, 
particularly the lesser prophets, in whose books 
we shall not find this chronology; but since one 
must be selected, let us take that of Joel. In the 
first chapter, the perfect barrenness of the Jewish 
nation is described, as bringing forth no fruit unto 
God. In chap. ii. 1, the trumpet is blown with 
an alarm, which was done when the people were 
to move forward (Numb. x. 5, 6), indicative here 
of their return into their own land, and restoration 
to God’s favour. In ver. 15, the trumpet is again 
blown, with the expression, ‘* sanctify a fast,” but 
without an alarm, indicating the ushering in of the 
day of atonement, the solemn assembly, and sanc- 
tified congregation, (Numb. x. ver. 7,) at some 
period subsequent to their moving forward, and 
doubtless when they are returned into their land; 
and look on him whom they have pierced; which 
they will never do collectively, as the whole na- 
tional assembly, till that time. The feast of trum- 
pets was on the Ist day of the first month of the 
civil year; the solemn assembly ten days after- 
wards; in ver. 23, we have an allusion to the for- 
mer rain, which was in the 3d month, and to the 
latter rain, which was in the 7th month; in ver. 
28, the feast of first-fruits is mentioned, the second 
of which was in the 9th month. In chap. iii. 18; 
the harvest.and vintage are alluded to; the former 
of which takes place in the 10th month, and the 
latter three months later. I have already referred 
you to a similar arrangement in the Apocalypse. 
I have drawn up tbe annexed table, which con- 
tains some of these chronological indications, de- 


342 


rived from the feasts, arranged according to the 
months. 

Philalethes.—But does not the Apostle, in Acts 
ii. 16, say, that the passage in Joel, to which you 
have referred, was fulfilled at Pentecost ? 

Valerius.—The passage in Joel includes ‘‘ the 
great and terrible day of the Lord ;” consequently, 
every commentator finds it necessary to make a 
halt somewhere in the middle of it. It seems as 
if the most correct view to take of the subject is, 
to suppose that this, like many other prophecies 
which we have examined, has a series of fulfil- 
ments, each more complete than its predecessor, 
and that this prediction had only a partial comple- 
tion at Pentecost. In the text we find expressions 
indicating that the accomplishment was incom- 
plete ; for in Joel ii. 23, it is said, ‘“ he hath given 
you the former rain moderately.” Peter quotes 
from the Septuagint, exyew aro rs rvevparoc ps, “ I will 
pour out of my spirit;” i.e. a portion of, see 
Horne, ii. 409. The persons who “ gladly received 
the word,” Acts ii. 41, in consequence of this effu- 
sion, were in number about ‘three thousand,” 
being “ Jews, devout men, of every nation under 
heaven.” St. James seems to be writing to the 
same people when he addresses his epistle ‘to the 
twelve tribes which are scattered abroad,” i. 1., 
whom he afterwards calls ‘“‘ a kind of first fruits,” 
18, apparently alluding to their conversion on the 
second feast of first-fruits. 

Philalethes.—Can you mention any historical 
events, as well as feasts, that contain a chro- 
nology? 


343 


Valerius.—Jones, of Nayland, points out the 
fall of Sisera, and Gideon’s victory as typical, 
but the chronology of the former is not so distinct, 
though it may point out the ten kingdoms men- 
tioned in Ps. Ixxxiii. (for the children of Lot are 
mentioned twice, Gen. xix. 37, 38,) as the 10 
anti-christian powers that are said to be destroyed 
by Christ’s having ascended on high, and led 
captivity captive. With respect to Midian, Isaiah 
points out the time very exactly at ix. 3, 4, which 
may be considered as key verses; and this also 
was the same time as that of Gideon's victory; 
Judges vii. 2, viii. 2, and this is the great time of 
slaughter, the day of atonement, 10th Tisri, or 
the time of vintage. The day of atonement does 
not prefigure Christ’s atonement, for the passover 
did that, but the day of vengeance, Isa. lxiii. 4: 
and the expression “‘ the year of my redeemed,” 
would also point out‘the year of Jubilee, which 
commenced on this very day, Lev. xxv. 9. But 
this may refer to the end of the millennium ; when 
Christ gives up the kingdom to the Father, which 
js perhaps pointed out by the expression, ‘ mine 
ear hast thou bored ;” consequently he could not 
be released till the year of Jubilee: this, however, 
does not seem clear, for the release may be consi- 
dered to take place when Satan is bound, &c. We 
must take care, likewise, not to confound two pe- 
riods together, as the Jews did of old, who mixed 
the humiliation of Jésus with his kingdom, and as 
the Christians do now, who mix his kingdom with 
his heavenly state. | 


: 344 


Anastasius.—This view of internal scriptural 
chronology, as now unfolded by our Christian 
Brother Valerius, and derived from the study of 
the Jewish feasts, appears to be very important, 
and well worthy of our most attentive investiga- 
tion. Dr. Kennedy observed long ago that the 
most remarkable events in the-New Testament 
had occurred on days memorable also, and no 
doubt therefore typical,. under the old dispensa- 
tion; and he says, ‘“‘ it deserves our attention, 
that whenever the modern Jews celebrate their 
feast of tabernacles, which they never fail to do 
every year in its appointed season, they at the 
same time celebrate not only the birth-day of the 
world, but of the promised Messiah too; for the 
Messiah was born on the first day of the feast 
of tabernacles, and was circumcised on the eighth, 
or great day of the same,” p. 393. Again, he 
says, in another place, and upon another set of 
astronomical calculations, that “it is undeniably 
certain that our Lord rose from the dead on the 
morrow after the 15th of Nisan,” p. 446. 

Valerius.—The morrow after the 15th of Nisan, 
that is the 16th, you will find in the preceding 
table to be the day on which the wave-offering 
of the first fruits was made; on this day our 
blessed Lord arose and became the first fruits 
_ from the dead of them that slept, agreeably to 
the Apostle’s declaration in 1 Cor. xv. 

Philalethes.—Is it not contended that our Lord 
rose on the true Sabbath, according to. its original 
institution ? 


345 


Anastasius.—Dr. Kennedy seems to establish 
this point very clearly, but as it would require 
much time to go into the question fully, I can 
only now refer you to his work, Diss. V., the 
whole of which is well worth perusing, particu- 
larly his calculation on the 70 weeks of Da- 
niel. Sir Isaac Newton interprets the Apo- 
calypse by using the Jewish feasts as its key, 
chap. 11. _ | | 

Valerius.—-It is clear also from the journeyiugs 
of the Israelites, that the days of keeping the 
Sabbath was changed from that on which it was 
originally instituted ; you will see this alluded to 
in Jennings’ account of the Jewish feasts. 

Thraso.—The succession of events brought into 
the order of the Jewish ‘‘ times and seasons,” by 
our brother Valerius, receives confirmation from a 
reference to those prophecies of the Old Testa- 
ment which relate to the latter times. Thus the 
“ present brought to the Lord of Hosts,” (Psalm 
xlv. 15; lxvii. 29; Is. xvii. 7; see Horsley 
in loco; Is. lv. 12; Zeph. ii. 10,) is in the 
Hebrew identified with the Jubilee, the word 
“ brought” being derived from the same root as 
jubilee, and signifying flowing back in its original 
course; answering more immediately to our word 
restore. The jubilee began on the day of atone- 
ment (10th, 7th month, Tisri); it was, therefore, 
preceded by the harvest and vintage, and suc- 
ceeded by the feast of tabernacles. Now, where- 
ever this “ present brought” in the day of jubilee 
is mentioned, it is always preceded by expressions 


346 


announcing the indignation and fierce anger of 
God; and succeeded by the rebuilding of Zion, 
answering to the feast of tabernacles, and called, 
in Amos ix. 11, raising the tabernacle or booth 
nap of David, which brings in the blessedness of 
the earth, even as life from the dead. This 
tabernacle bears an evident allusion to the feast 
of tabernacles. The feast of tabernacles, on 15th 
= Tisri, was the last of the three great annual 
assemblies, and held five days after the day of 
atonement and jubilee. But I have already 
observed, that the day of atonement and jubilee 
is that on which the ‘ present is brought of a 
people scattered and peeled wonderful from their 
beginning hitherto,” and that it was preceded by 
the feasts of trumpets ten days, (which occurred 
on Ist Tisri, and was commemorative of the crea- 
tion of the world,) and that before that time both 
harvest and vintage had ended. In Gen. i. 1. 
the earth is said to have been wa wn without form 
and void; and in the day of vengeance it is said, 
that the Lord will reduce a portion of it to its 
primitive chaos, stretching out upon it the line of 
confusion, yn, and the stones of emptiness, ya, 
Is. xxxiv. 8. 11. From this desolation, like a 
new creation, Jerusalem arises, Is. xxiv. xxv. xxvi. 
And Is. lxv. 17, ‘* I create new heavens and a new 
earth, and the former shall not be remembered 
nor come into mind; but be glad and rejoice for 
ever in that which I create, for behold I. create 
Jerusalem a rejoicing, and her people a joy :” 
then comes the time for restoring the tabernacle 


347 


of David, when “ Jerusalem shall be a crown of 
glory in the hand of the Lord, and a royal diadem 
in the hand of thy God.” The three great feasts 
on which every Israelite was-obliged to appear 
before the Lord in Jerusalem, may be typical and 
commemorative of the return from the three great 
captivities of Egypt, Babylon, and Rome. The 
first of these representing man’s natural bondage 
and slavery under sin, for deliverance from which 
the passover and sacrifice of Christ provide: the 
` second, representing man’s slavery to, and idolatry 
of sense, and the good things of this world, 
punished by the Babylonish captivity, that the 
land might enjoy its Sabbaths, and purified at 
Pentecost by the outpouring of the Holy Spirit; 
the first effect of which was to expel selfishness 
and love of the world, Acts ii. 45, instanced by 
the early Christians selling their possessions and 
goods: and the third, representing man’s intellec- 
tual apostacy, or idolatry of intellect, the most 
severely visited of all; beginning and closing in 
vengeance; but issuing in the ingatherings, 
Hosanna Rabba, and water of Siloam, palm 
branches of triumph, and horn of jubilee. — 
Anastasius.—A true fundamental chronology, 
founded on the period of the Jubilee, was pub- 
lished by Frank in 1774. A good account of this 
very learned and elaborate work is given by Mr. 
Granville Penn, in his “ Comparative Estimate 
of the Mineral and Mosaical Geologies ;” you will 
there find that his treatise sets forth the powers 
of the Jus1Lzawn Penriop as a rule for computing 
and harmonising time in its divisions of years, 


348. 


months, and weeks. The author shews that the 
Jubilean Cycle is, and must ever continue to be, 
perpetually recurrent. The Jubilean Period is 
a cycle of 7 x 7 = 49 luni-solar years, which, by 

means of Epacts, marks out years, months, and 
days, according to the courses of the sun and 
moon, in perpetual and equal revolution. That 
period has this pre-eminence above all others, 
that it was taught by God himself to the people 
of Israel, and was delivered to them from Him by 
Moses. Out of the difference between solar and 
lunar years arose the controversy which lasted 
two centuries, whether the Jubilee was to be kept 
on the 49th or 50th year. Now as 49 solar years 
are equal to 502 lunar years, there is both a lunar 
and a solar progress to be considered in deter- 
mining the year of the Jubilee. -And as God fixed 
the commencement of the Jewish year according 
to the lunar index, but fixed its festivals according 
to the solar season, regard must be had to both 
computations in attempting to determine the ques: 
tion of the 50th year; since the computation of 
the festivals by lunar time, but their determina- 
tion by solar time necessarily implies some 
method of equalising the differenee between the 
two, it may therefore be that the numeratioa of 
the 50th year may not have relation to the compu- 
tation of the lunar years, considered in them- 
selves, nor to that of 49 solar years, but to that 
of the lunar years in their equation with the solar. 
He shews that this period of civil restitution was 
also a period of the restitution of time to its pri- 
mitive order, by equation of the differences ao- 


349 


cruing from the varying progressés of thé suh and 
moon ; both which luminaries had been originally 
and expressly appointed by God to ‘be for 
years.” I must refer you to the work itself, or 
at least to Mr. Penn’s abstract of it, for further 
very curidus and ithportant details, and only add 
the practical conclusion at which he arrives in the 
establishing of this great truth, namely, that the 
chronology of the Bible is most perfect, and truly 
fundamental, and is clear from all errors and real 
contradictions ; the establishment of which truth 
must unquestionably add very considerable weight 
to the evidences which prove thé divine origin of 
the Sacred Scripture. 

According to the computations of this laborious 
calculator, Frank, the birth of our Lord took 
place in the year of the world 4181, conformably 
to the computations of Pétavius and some other 

later chronologists; that is, in the 16th year of 
' the 30th Judaical, or 86th Mundane, Jubileean 

Cycle. The following year, 4182, is the epocha, 
or first year of the Christian era, consequently, 
by the same computation, we are now (1827) in 
the 30th year of the 123d Jubilean Cycle from 
the creation ; and as A.D. 1812 was A.M. 6000, 
we are now ih the 8th year of the seventh mille- 
nary of the world. 

-© Philalethes.—I can say nothing to this state- 
ment without examining the calculations on which 
it is founded ; it is somewhat remarkable, how- 
ever, that the sum of the numeration of the cycles, 
. that is 123, should be exactly equal to the number 
of millenaries since the world has been created, 


350. 


namely 6: but let me refer to a former part of 
this conversation, and enquire what extraordinary 
event is said to have taken place which marks 
the end of the 1290 years, as well as the 
1260. | 
Anastasius.—We cannot judge, a priori, what 
years are, or are not, of sufficient importance for 
. God to designate; if the three periods in Daniel 
xii. begin to be reckoned from the same date, 
and the 1260 days concluded at the beginning 
of the French revolution in 1789, or 1792, the 
period of 1290 days must have terminated in 
1819, or 1822; but on reference to the works of 
all the living commentators, you will find that 
they are much perplexed to discover an event of 
sufficient importance to signalize this period. 
Philalethes.—What is the most striking event 
which they have found ? | 
_ Anastasius.—I do not think that they have 
found any, nor that, even supposing the com- 
mencement of the above periods to be correct, 
it is necessary that they should. Within those 
30 years, according to this view, the whole of 
the first five vials were poured out. We are 
now under the sixth. During some part of the 
remaining 45 years the seventh vial will doubt- 
less be poured out, and during this whole 
period the church is called upon to ‘ watch:” 
45 is the 8th part of 360; a watch was the Sth 
part of the day; and it is during this last watch 
of 45 years that we are to expect our Lord with 
all the concomitant circumstances attending -his - 
coming. To cut off these 45 years from all other 


351 

years, it would be necessary to mark their com- 
mencement as well as their close, the one being 
1335, and the other 1290. A very striking passage 
relating to the present time of pause, occurs in the 
25th chapter of Jeremiah. From the Ist to the 
13th verses the Prophet is speaking of the 70 
years of the Babylonian captivity, but in order 
that we may not limit his warnings to that cap-. 
tivity only, the Lord declares, “ also I will bring 
upon the earth, nxn, all my words which I have 
pronounced against it, even all that is written in 
this book, which Jeremiah hath prophesied against 
all the nations.” The wine-cup of fury is then 
given to the nations, above 20 of whom, 18—27, 
are enumerated, upon whom ¢he sword is to be 
sent, ver. 16; and all the kingdoms of the world 
which are upon the face of the earth.—26, Upon 
these the curse is pronounced, rise no more, because 
of the sword which I will send among you. Then 
follows a passage which seems to foretell that 
these nations will be very unwilling to go into 
this war, contrary to the usual alacrity which they 
generally testify to enter upon this state, for it is 
said, ver. 28, if they refuse to take the cup at thine 
hand to drink, then shalt thou say unto them, thus 
saith the Lord of Hosts, Ye shall certainly drink 
-Jor Iwill call for a sword upon all the inha- 
bitants of the earth. The chronology of this war 
is also marked by the simile of the vintage in 
ver. 30; and in ver. 32, it is tied to the time of 
the 7th vial, by the words, behold, evil shall go forth 
from nation to nation, and a great WHIRLWIND 
shall be raised up from the coasts of the earth. 


352 


Philalethes.—As the term of 1260 years closed 
with so remarkable an event as the French Revo- 
lution, should we not expect that the period of its 
commencement would be marked by something 
equally striking ? 

Anastasius.—The whole of Justinian’s- reign 
was pervaded by a series of prodigies; as if God 
would not only by his word, but also by his pro- 
vidence, warn the great ones of the earth not to 
dare to touch His peculiar treasure, His Church, 
for whose sake alone all the empires of the world 
exist, whether to support or to chastise, as He 
sees most conducive to his own glory in Christ 
Jesus, and to her benefit. In the xliid chapter 
of Gibbon you will find several pages of the In- 
fidel Historian devoted to the “comets, the earth- 
quakes, and the plagues which astonished or 
afflicted: the age of Justinian.” These began -in 
the year 531, and continued without interruption 
to 594. Speaking of the earthquakes, he says, 
‘ This fever of the earth raged with uncommon 
violence during the reign of Justinian. Each year 
i marked by the repetition of earthqaakes of such 
duration, that Constantinople has been shaken 
above forty days: of such extent, that the shock 
has been communicated to the whole ‘surface of 
the globe, or at least of the Roman Empire. An 
impulsive or vibratory motion was felt: enormous 
chasms were opened; huge and heavy bodies 
were discharged into the air; the sea alter- 
nately advanced and retreated beyond its ordi- 
fary bounds; and a mountain was torn from 
Libanus, and cast into the waves.” Speaking of 


‘358 


the plague, he says, “ Such was the universal 
corruption of the air, that the pestilence which 
burst forth in the 15th year of Justinian was not 
checked nor alleviated by any difference of the 
seasons. In time its first malignity was abated 
or dispersed; the disease alternately languished | 
and revived ; but it was not till the end of a cala- 
mitous period of fifty-two years that mankind 
recovered their health, or the air resumed its 
pure and salubrious quality. No facts have been 
preserved to sustain an account, or even a con- 
jecture, of the numbers that perished in this ex- 
traordinary mortality, The triple scourge of war, 
pestilence, and famine, afflicted the subjects of 
Justinian, and ‘this reign is disgraced by a visible 
decrease of the human species, which has never 
been repaired, in ‘some of the finest countries of 
the globe.” Thus we find the events of the be- 
ginning of the 1260 years fully as awful as those 
of its close: but the nations have eyes and see 
not, ears but hear not, &c. 

Philalethes.—You have supported the opinions 
you have laid before me this day with many 
references to different writers; had you any object 
iù doing so? 

Anastasius.—I was willing to furnish you with 
the means of refuting the ignorance of the persons 
who have called the expectations of our Lord’s 
coming in judgment a novel opinion.

men who are so unmindful of the remark, that 


Ten censure wrong for one who writes amiss,”

whose talents and whose colossal strength of mind 
could have borne him aloft to the most arduous

gumentation ;” and whe, after writing 16 pages of 
scurrility, under pretext of reviewing sentiments 
contained in a book, conclude with the disgust-

when these sentiments are contained in what is

nals would not contain such language; but in pro-

A candid statement of different opinions would 
deserve by us as patient an investigation as we

in search for truth, is that we are desirous of 
hearing sentiments opposite to our own, in order 
that we may try them by the standard of the 
Word of God.

There are many Dissenters whom I know to be“ 
disgusted with the mode in which such periodicals: 
are conducted ; and it.is unfortunate for them that 
these journals claim to be the representatives of the 
opinions of the Dissenting Interest. But their most 
popular preachers, instead of warning their congre-: 
gations against the evil principles of the reviews, 
are frequently found contributing to their support.. 
With respect to those of a better spirit, I men- 
tioned, in a former conversation, that riches were 
as dangerous to a community, or to a church as 
to-an individual. This danger is not so much ` 
fromthe time which-‘must be necessarily’ em- 
ployed in the management of- secular affairs, 
leaving an. insufficiency: for prayer, reading,-and 
meditation; or-from,-their producjng.so great a 


308 


fulness of enjoyment to the carnal man, as to 
tempt: him to desire nothing beyond the present: 
scene, (although this is the view of their danger 
which is commonly taken,) as it is from the habit: 
thereby contracted of looking involuntarily more 
at second causes than at the First, and gradually 
inducing us, even against our better convictions, 
to live by sight rather than by faith. All the evil 
and worldly-mindedness, which rules in the Com- 
mittees of Bible and Missionary: Societies, arises” 
from this cause. Many of the individuals who 
conduct the Societies, as well as some of those 
who write in the Reviews, are men of piety and 
prayer; but they carry on, or rather attempt to 
carry. on, the work of God upon the same prin- 
ciples, and in the same manner, that they would 
conduct and carry on any worldly office. Hence 
the faith of the church is so low, as to be almost 
extinct. Knowledge of the doctrines of salvation, 
which the extension of general information has 
caused to be acquired with facility, is mistaken 
for faith in the word of God ; and a very large por- 
tion of the Bible is as completely disbelieved, or 
perverted, and twisted into nothing by the majority 
of professing religious people, as it is by avowed 
infidels. From this state of practical infidelity 
which pervades the religious world, nothing can 
rouse and save Ged’s people but calling their 
attention to the things which the word of God 
reveals to be coming on the earth. Mr. Wolff 
declared to me, and desired me to make it every 
where known, that in conversing with one of the 
principal managers of the Church Missionary 


389 


: Society, respecting preaching the Gospel iu 
Persia, he-was told by him that the Gospel ought © 
not to be preached there, because it was dan- 
gerous for men to believe it; and that Russia 
ought to carry nominal Christianity into Persia, 
first of all, before the Persians were. instructed in 
vital Christianity. Now this opinion is diametri- 
cally opposed to the precept, and example of the 
Word of God; and contrary to all experience; as 
it is notorious that vital Christianity is much more 
reviled in nominally Christian countries, and 
particularly in Russia, than it is in other parts of 
the world. Yet this is the doctrine of too many 
of the chief supporters of Missionary Societies.

one common hope will help him on his road; sti-

there will be.none with us. Expect little from

Philalethes.— Does not the tone and temper of 
the Dissenting Magazines differ materially from 
what they were twenty or thirty years ago ? 
Justus.—You will easily convince yourself of 
this by reference to. the earlier numbers; and 


363 


the fact argues a melancholy decline in the spiri- 
tuality of that body. Nor is it unobserved by: 
others, although it is by themselves. I met lately 
a French Gentleman, who had not been in this 
country for nearly thirty years, and he told me he 
was much pained at the great change he per- 
ceived. Their periodical publications were never 
distinguished for much learning or talent; but 
they possessed the more valuable qualifications of 
uncompromising adherence to the grand truths of 
the Gospel; a spirit of candour towards their 
brethren; and a zeal for the glory of God, which, 
in spite of other defects, rendered them truly 
estimable. They are, of late, greatly altered for 
the worse. New Magazines have arisen, and old 
ones have fallen into the hands of a new genera- 
tion. Loose principles are maintained; and the 
same doctrines which were strenuously asserted 
by their predecessors, as the true sayings of God, 
have been branded as Antinomian, and held up to 
ridicule. With respect to the subject of the In- 
spiration of the Scriptures, on which they betrayed 
their ignorance in the Apocryphal controversy, it 
is but justice to remember, that some of the most 
eminent dissenting ministers, in different parts of. 
England, were so disgusted with it, that they felt 
it necessary to write to the Edinburgh Bible So- 
ciety, begging that it might not be imagined that 
these reviews fairly represented the whole body. '

Aristo.—-The idolatry of these Societies ex- 
ceeds all conception. Their supporters are for 
ever prophesying blessings from them, whilst even 
worldly people, who are acute in their several 
ways, can perceive that a shaking of the nations is 
at hand. Idolaters of Societies, and the partakers 
of the good things proceeding from them, and the 
manufacturers of reports alone, are blinded by 
their vanity, and misleading the people. We 
say that the word of God prophesies judgments: 
they prophesy blessings: let the people consult 
their Bibles, and decide between us. As Michal, 
the worldly-minded daughter of Saul, mocked 
and laughed at David, to whom she was so nearly 
related, when she saw him throw off his outer- 
most garments, and exulting with great joy before 
the ark, on its coming up to Jerusalem; so do 
worldly-minded Christians of this day join with 
the openly profane, in mocking and laughing at 
those who are their brethren, when they see them 
casting off all outward systems of expediency and 
policy, and exulting with great joy in the expecta- 
tion of the speedy coming back of the Lord to 
Jerusalem. Michal was cursed with barrenness 
for her unholy levity; and neither shall they who 
imitate her in these days produce fruit unto God,

` Anastasius.—The grand maxim of the con- 
ductors of these societies is ‘‘ prudence;” by 
which they mean trusting to visible things, such as 
wealth and influence, rather than the promises of 
the word of God. They are exactly like the 
Israelites, when the prophet told them not to seek 
help from Egypt: but yet it was very “ pru- 
dent” to get a rich and powerful king to support 
them; and they despised the prophet. ‘ Where- 
fore came this mad fellow to thee?” (2 Kings ix. 
11,) they cry out on another occasion. In every 
instance, throughout the history given by the 
prophet Jeremiah, the people took the ‘ prudent” 
course, that is, they relied upon visible power.

may snatch the few, Who. by cutting off a right 
hand, and plucking out a right eye, are contented

cast out ; whilst of the eldest line of the Gentile

the injustice which has been practised towards 
the Papists of England and Ireland: many privi- 
leges are withheld from them which ought to 
be granted; and the Orange faction, which has 
trampled upon and insulted them in Ireland, is as - 
little Christian as they are: but there is a wide 
difference between persecuting and encouraging 
apostacy, as we should do by paying its priests, or 


- - encouraging its schools, such as Maynooth, &c. 


_Philalethes.—I thought your explanation of the 
- vials seemed very clearly to indicate the period in 
-which we are. 


376 
_ Anastasius.—However ‘clear it may seem, you - 
must remember the maxim with which I set out, 
that in things to come all we can say is, that pro- 
bability stands in place of a demonstration, until a 
greater probability can be brought to shoulder it out. 
You must by no means admit that interpretation 
of the vials, unless you shall find it agree with 
some arrangement of the Apocalypse founded on 
its own internal evidence; this is espécially ne- 
cessary with respect to events so near our own 
days, although these are the very events which 
it is of most importance for us to examine. To 
hold fast, as far as we have already ascertained, _ 
and at the same time to be ready to abandon any | 

preconceived opinion, is the proper state of mind 
to be in. We should read the Scriptures on this 
matter not only in the spirit, but as much as 
possible in the very act, of prayer. If by closer 
examination, the true structure hereafter shall 
be found to depend upon the chronological indi- 
cations of the Jewish feasts, as Valerius supposes, 
the events expressed by the seven vials, answer- 
ing to the seven last days of the feast of Taber- 
nacles, cannot have taken place, unless we can 
also point out some events answering to the 
former and latter rain, harvest, vintage, &c.; and — 
if he is farther right, that the feast of Tabernacles, 
during which the seven vials are to be poured out, 
is to be celebrated by the church in the pre- 
sence of her Lord at his coming, as Mr. Simeon 
is also said to have lately preached, then there 
is reason to suppose, that we are not so far ad- 
vanced in prophetic time as has been imagined. 


377 


I will also throw out for your consideration, but 
merely as hints for further investigation, whether 
the diffusion of the Scriptures by the Bible So- 
ciety, which was very happily termed, by the 
' amiable and pious Bishop of Lichfield, a`“ Pen- 
tecostal effusion,” may not have been the latter 
tain; and the great increase of the church, which 
has taken place within the last twenty years, 
the harvest of the same, chap. xiv. But whe- 
ther this be so or not, I do not think that 
the opinon of Ben Ezra, Mr. Maitland, and 
others, is to be overlooked, which considers, that 
the greater part of the Apocalypse is yet to 
be fulfilled in a literal period of 1260 days: 
nor is this opinion so opposed to the other as 
may at first sight appear: for as all the pro- 
phecies of the Old Testament had an inchoate 
accomplishment first, and a more perfect fulfilment 
, afterwards, so it is not impossible that this great 
prophecy of the New Testament. may have had a 
partial application during the whole time of the 
Gentile dispensation, and will have a more full and 
literal completion in the days which accompany 
the coming of our Lord. Many of these events 
may be fulfilled by our Lord in person. For, if 
during the time of his personal ministry at his 
first advent, which was three years and a half, 
or 1260 days, he. fulfilled in his own person all 
the things which the church had performed 
personally, yet typically, herself, from the calling 
of Abraham to that period ; it seems fair to con- 
clude, that he will likewise fulfil, in his own 
person, at the time of his second advent, all the 


378 


‘things which his church shall have performed 
from the time of her first calling out from amongst 
the Gentiles. For “this prophecy,” says Ben- 
gelius, “is like a cloud, richly full of fructifying 
rain, which spreads over a large extent of land, 
„and which sheds some part of its waters on every 
ground in its turn by specks or spots. What 
“belongs to each particular time, the believers of 
that age may turn to their advantage in a special 
manner,” p. 124. And it is well observed by 
the wisest of uninspired men, that ‘divine pro- 
phecies being of the nature of their Author, with 
whom a thousand years are but as one day, 
and therefore are not fulfilled punctually at once, 
but have springing and germinant accomplishment 
throughout many ages; though the height or 
fulness of them may refer to some one age.” 
Advancement of Learning, vol. 1. b. 2. p. 88. 
Philalethes.—I have been very anxious to find, 
that your apprehensions of the low state of faith 
in the church was exaggerated; but as there is 
no means of ascertaining this but by outward 
works, I confess, the more I examine it, the 
worse it appears: for I see, 1. Trifling with the 
subject of inspiration by the Bible Society not 
_ only considered as a slight offence by its Direc- 
tors, but justified by many of the religious jour- 
nals. 2. Worldly patronage, the chief thing 
sought after, and men of zeal repressed as im- 
prudent. 3. Love of the brethren almost ex- 
tinct, and men of power preferred before them. 
4. False statements of the proceedings of Societies 
published by the Committee, and fraudulent sa- 


379 


laries paid to their servants. 5. General scoffing at 
the doctrine of the coming of the Lord. 6. Ex- 
plaining away the plainest declarations of the 
Scriptures, under the pretence of making them 
More spiritual. Surely the ordinary graces of the 
spirit have ceased, as well as the miraculous gifts 
which were manifest in the first ages. 

Anastasius —The miraculous gifts of the Spirit 
ceased not, because they were no longer of use 
to the church ; but because the faith of the church 
grew cold and dead. The Romish Church main- 
tains, that we Protestants have no ground what- 
ever for saying, that miracles were ever to cease, 
and that we can produce no warrant from Scrip- 
ture for so saying: in this she is right; and if 
the faith and purity of the church burned as 
. bright now as it did in the days of the Apostles, 
She would again exhibit that light to the world. 
Not that miracles produce conviction to any 
who will not be convinced without them, as we 
see by the conduct of Pharaoh, of God’s own 
. people. in the wilderness, and by the 4th and dth 
chapters of the Acts of the Apostles. The error 
of the Church of Rome was, in supposing she 
“could, like Simon, command the Holy Ghost 
by wealth; work miracles without special faith ; 
and at whatever time she pleased; whereas the 
Apostles themselves had not power to do so 
but at particular times. The best instruction 
respecting the present state of the church, is 
to be derived from the history of her former 
situation as revealed in the Old Testament; for 
as every prophecy must have had a partial 


380 


immediate accomplishment, in - order : that - the 
inspiration of the prophet. might be proved, 
(Deut. xviii. 22,) -so has the . church . had 
always an immediate object. of faith proposed 
to it, by the belief or-rejection of which, her faith, 
in more distant events, might be proved: Thus 
we are no where told the number. of professors 
of: religion ‘at the time. of. Noah, nor how many | 
were spiritual worshippers, looking through the 
bl6od of their sacrifices, to a better blood than 
that of bulls and of goats; but. we are told that 
only eight persons were saved from that judg- 
ment. The Israelites had: faith sufficient to walk 
_ through the sea; and some had sufficient to look 

at the brazen: serpent, and deliver themselves from 
the destruction of that time; but when the period 
arrived to enter into the promised possession, 
some despised it; some said it was not so good as 
God declared it to be; all reviled. the men who 
gave a good report of it, and after having endured 
the hardships of forty years travel in the burning 
sands, only two souls entered in of all who had 
left Egypt, ‘‘ because of unbelief.” The church 
is exactly in that state now; and it must be a 
generation which shall arise. out of the. present’ 
professing churches, both High Church and Evan- 
gelical, “a very elect,” out of the present “ elec» 
tion,” that shall enter in with their Joshua to the 
long looked-for Canaan. It is the office of the 
Holy Ghost to lead God’s people into aut truth ; 
not into a portion of it merely: and there is an es- 
sential difference to be made betwixt those who 
are ignorant of any branch of truth, because they 


381 


be. 


have never heard it, and those who, having heard 
_ it, shew.such wilful antipathy to it, as to refuse to 
search the Scriptures to see whether it be so or 
not. I am willing to rest the whole question upon 
the answer to this one historical fact; namely, 
In every judgment which God has brought upon 
the earth, did not the majority of those who pro- 
fessed to be his peculiar worshippers at the time 
disbelieve the judgment, and abuse those who 
warned them of it?: and we are every-where told 
in the New Testament, that a similar state of 
things shall be found on the earth, when our Lord 
comes again in power and in great glory, 

The temple had been so neglected and polluted, 
that in the days of Hezekiah, even the inner part 
itself required to be cleansed, (2 Chron. xxix. 16,) 
which when that righteous king attempted, they 
who called: themselves.the Lord’s people, laughed 
him to scorn. (2 Chron. xxx. 10.) . It is truly a 
subtle device of Satan to allow of such an en- 
crease to the outer court as has taken place of late 
years, at the same time that he has been defiling 
the inner court. l 

Let us strive to live very near to God in prayer, 

to be instant in season and out of season, for an 
increased measure of faith; for the Holy Spirit 
to dwell more abundantly in us; to separate us 
more from the world ; to lead us into more of God’s 
revealed truth; and to shew us things to come; 
to prepare-us for the coming of the bridegroom ; 
that we may not only hold pure doctrine as 
‘“ chaste virgins espoused to Christ,” evinced by. 
the cleanness of our white garments, and by having 


in our hand the lamp of our profession of expect- 
ing him at midnight, when ‘all the world is asleep, 
but have the true oil of the sanctuary in it like- 
wise. Great exertions are now using to circu- 
late Mr. Stewart’s pamphlet on the Necessity of 
Prayer for the outpouring of the Holy Spirit; 
may they be abundantly crowned with success! 
It is somewhat extraordinary, however, that one 
especial mode by which the Holy Spirit was to act 
as the Comforter is totally omitted in the twenty- 
five subjects which Mr. S. has suggested as proper 
subjects for discourses on the offices of the Spirit, 
and that is, “‘ He shall shew you things to come ;” 
and the greater part of the passages of Scripture 
which he has quoted to shew that the Lord has 
promised in the latter days to pour out his spirit 
in an unusual manner, prove also by their contexts 
that that effusion is to take place at the time, or 
not till after he has visited the world with his 
judgments; the very point which the majority of 
professors disbelieve. There is so remarkable a 
confusion in the minds of many pious men upon 
this subject, from the inattention with which they 
read the Scriptures, and wrest insulated passages 
from their context, that I must detain you a few 
minutes whilst I point out one of these, and I will 
take the very first which Mr. Stewart adduces in 
proof that the Scriptures predict a day when the 
Holy Spirit shall be given in a very abundant man- 
ner, and that this blessing will be preceded ‘by 
earnest prayer. He begins to quote Joel ii. at the 
28th verse, “ And it shall come to pass afterward’ 
that I will pour out my spirit upon all flesh,” &c. 


383 


after what, or after when? and with what propriety 
ean any piece of a statement be taken from the. 
middle of it, without referring to the scope of the 
whole? The prophet distinctly says at the begin- 
ning of the chapter, “ let all the inhabitants of the 
land tremble, for the day of the Lord cometh, for 
it is nigh at hand,” &c.; again at the 11th verse, 
after describing the ministers of his vengeance, 
“ the day of the Lord is great and terrible, who can 
abide it;” at this time the people of Zion are 
desired to “call a solemn assembly ;” and “‘ then 
will the Lord be jealous for his land, and pity his 
people,” giving them corn, wine, and oil, &c.; 
and “it shall come to pass afterward that I will 
pour out my spirit,” for behold in those days and 
at that time when I shall bring again the captivity 
of Judah and Jerusalem, ‘“ I will also gather all 
nations, and will bring them into the valley of 
Jehoshaphat, &c. Multitudes, multitudes in the 
valley of threshing, for the day of the Lord is near 
the valley of threshing,” &c. &c. Valerius has 
already pointed out the partial fulfilment of the 
passage which Mr. Stewart quotes at the time of 
Pentecost, when the Holy Spirit was poured out 
upon the Jews exclusively. 

Philalethes.—Mr. Lewis Way has well shewn 
the misapplication of Scripture by Mr. Stewart 
in his pamphlets on the “ Latter Rain.” It is cer- 
tainly like teaching us to expect snow in summer, 
to call upon us to look-out for the fulfilment of 
promises now, which God has declared shall 
not be till other things have intervened. 

Anastasius.—Undoubtedly it is, and tending to 


384 


mislead the church to her destruction, for the 
judgment will be coming upon her whilst she is 
expecting something very different. I do not in 
the least object to Mr. Stewart’s proposal; on 
the contrary, I will practise what he recommends, 
and exhort all others to do so likewise; but I 
protest against a misapplication of Scripture, and 
against wresting from its context to support 
any measure however excellent. I look for the 
greatest benefits to the church as likely to arise 
from the adoption of more earnest prayer for ‘the 
outpouring of the Holy Spirit; and particularly 
in separating the principles and practices of real 
Christians, from the worldly principles and prac- 
tices. on which religious societies have been 
conducted. There is another pamphlet which 
deserves also to be quite as widely circulated, 
entitled, Thoughts on Christian Devotedness. 
Let us trust that these exertions may prove an 
additional instance of the spirit of Ehas, being 
come amongst us by calling out for blessings from, 
heaven at the close of so many years disbelief 
of the church in the kingdom, and power, and 
great glory of her coming Lord. 


Why then in sad and wintry time, 

Her heavens all dark with doubt and crime, 
Why lifts the Church her drooping head 
As though her evil hour were fled ? 

Is she less wise than leaves of spring, 

Or birds that cower with folded wing? 
What sees she in this lowering sky 

To tempt her meditative eye? 


` She has a charm, a word of fire, 
A pledge of love that cannot tire ;. 


385 


By tempests, earthquakes, and by wars, 
By rushing waves and falling stars, 

By every sign her Lord foretold, 

She sees the world is waxing old, 

And through that last and direst storm 
Descries by faith her Saviour’s form. 


Not surer does each tender gem, 

` Set in the fig-tree’s polished stem, 
Foreshow the summer season bland, 
Than these dread signs thy mighty hand ; 
But oh! frail hearts, and spirits dark ! 
The season’s flight unwarn'd we mark, 
But miss the Judge behind the door, 
For all the light of sacred lore. 


See a volume of exquisite poetry, entitled, The Christian Year ; p. 13.

the crane, and the swallow, observe the time of 
their coming ; but my people know not the judg- 
„ment of the Lord, Jer. viii. 7.
